Python 3.14 18 chapters · 130 topics · 228 code samples · works offline

Welcome — How to Use This Python Guide

1. Scope of this guide and target version2. Supported versions and the release schedule3. Tooling and reading type hints

A reference-grade description of the Python 3.14 language and its standard library, written for readers who already program. It covers the language itself in depth — the object model, scoping, functions, the full data-model protocol, and the syntax added over the last decade — and treats third-party frameworks only where the standard library interacts with them.

1. Scope of this guide and target version

Everything here is written against CPython 3.14, the current stable line as of this revision (latest patch 3.14.7, released 5 August 2026). Version numbers are stated inline wherever behaviour is version-dependent.

Three scopes are deliberately excluded. Web and application frameworks (Django, Flask, FastAPI) are outside the language and are documented by their own projects. Packaging and distribution internals appear only to the extent that tooling depends on them. Numerical and scientific computing is treated through the standard library (decimal, fractions, math, array); the wider SciPy stack has its own memory model and is not interchangeable with the types described in chapter 2.

Where behaviour differs between implementations it is called out explicitly. The relevant distinction is between CPython (the reference implementation, and the only one shipping every 3.14 feature), PyPy (JIT-compiled, with several documented divergences), and the free-threaded CPython build (PEP 703), which removes the global interpreter lock and therefore changes the guarantees available to shared mutable state.

Documented in this guideDeliberately out of scope
Lexical structure, statements, expressions (chapter 1)Editor and IDE configuration
Built-in types, equality, conversion (chapter 2)Third-party data-frame and array libraries
Names, bindings, scope, modules (chapter 3)Package build backends and wheel internals
Functions, functools, recursion (chapter 4)Web framework routing and templating
Classes, the data model, enums (chapter 5)Async and concurrent execution (covered separately in the concurrency chapter)
# Establish which interpreter a snippet will actually run under.
import sys
import platform

print(sys.version)                      # 3.14.7 (main, ...) [GCC 14.2.0]
print(sys.version_info)                 # sys.version_info(major=3, minor=14, ...)
print(sys.implementation.name)          # cpython
print(platform.python_implementation())
print(sys.executable)
print(sys.platform, sys.maxsize)

Applies to: the whole guide. Interpreter: CPython 3.14. Where a construct is new in a given release, the PEP and the introducing version are named at first use, and cross-references point to the subsection covering the consequences rather than repeating them.

2. Supported versions and the release schedule

CPython issues one feature release per year, in October. Each feature release receives roughly two years of bug fixes and then approximately three further years of security fixes, giving a five-year supported window (PEP 693). Pinning a support policy is the prerequisite for choosing an interpreter version.

BranchStatus (September 2026)Feature releaseEnd of life
3.15Pre-release — alpha, beta, release candidateOctober 2026 (projected)October 2031 (projected)
3.14Bugfix — current stable; 3.14.7 on 5 Aug 20267 Oct 2025October 2029
3.13Security7 Oct 2024June 2029
3.12Security2 Oct 20232 April 2028
3.11Security24 Oct 2022October 2027
3.10End of life4 Oct 2021October 2026
3.9End of life5 Oct 2020October 2025

Bugfix releases are published on a fixed cadence, and only the last of each series is usually installed, because the official installers refuse to apply a patch over a different patch. A deployment pinned to 3.14.* therefore receives security fixes with no change to the version specifier.

# A minimal support policy, expressed once and inherited by every requirement.
[project]
name = "acme-service"
version = "1.4.0"
requires-python = ">=3.12"
dependencies = [
    "httpx>=0.28",        # 3.14 wheels exist; resolver picks the best
    "pydantic>=2.9",
]

[project.optional-dependencies]
# The 3.14-only fast paths are opt-in, not a requirement.
jit = ["numpy>=2.1"]

# Upper bounds stay absent: pip resolves against the newest compatible
# release, and an upper pin is a maintenance liability, not a safeguard.

Do not deploy a 3.15 pre-release. Feature freeze falls in May of the development year, beta is cut in May, and the final release lands in October 2026. Pre-releases are published to a separate index and must be installed explicitly. They are appropriate for forward-compatibility testing and nothing else.

The practical consequence for a codebase is that a requires-python of >=3.12 buys the widest compatibility, while >=3.14 buys the largest standard library. The cost of the narrow floor is real: annotations are evaluated eagerly before PEP 649, forward references in annotations need quoting or from __future__ import annotations, and except without an exception type is unavailable. Those costs are quantified in 3.2 and 5.7.

3. Tooling and reading type hints

Only a compiler is strictly required to run Python; everything else is optional and improves throughput. The table below is the current consensus toolchain, and it is intentionally small. Every row is a separate program with a single responsibility.

ToolRoleNotes
CPython 3.14The interpreterVerify with python -VV, which prints the version and the build date
uvProject, dependency and environment managerReplaces pip, venv and pip-tools; parallel resolution, sub-second installs
pipInstaller and resolverStill canonical inside environments uv did not create; pip install -e . for editable installs
venv / uv venvVirtual environmentspython -m venv .venv is standard library; activate before installing anything
ruffLinter and formatterReplaces flake8, isort and black; orders of magnitude faster
mypy / pyrightStatic type checkersmypy is the reference implementation; pyright is faster and stricter by default
pytestTest runner and fixturesLarge plugin ecosystem; pytest-asyncio for coroutines, pytest-cov for coverage
IPythonInteractive shellTab completion, %timeit, inline plotting; the de facto notebook kernel
pdb / ipdbDebuggerPost-mortem: python -m pdb -c continue script.py

The first row deserves emphasis, because it is where a 3.14 project differs from a 3.11 one. A free-threaded interpreter is a separately compiled binary: the same CPython source produces both the default (GIL) build and the free-threaded build, selected at configure time. On Linux the latter is conventionally installed as python3.14t.

import sys

print(sys.implementation.name)     # cpython, pypy, ironpython, ...
print(sys._is_gil_enabled())       # True on a default build, False on 3.14t
print(sys._is_interned("abc"))     # True - identifier-like strings are interned
print(sys.getswitchinterval())     # 0.005 - GIL switch interval, seconds

# A library that must degrade gracefully checks the flag, not the version.
def scaling_factor() -> int:
    return 1 if sys._is_gil_enabled() else 4

sys._is_gil_enabled() is a private API added in 3.13. It is the only programmatic test available to libraries that need to adapt, for example by installing a per-object lock rather than assuming a global one. The leading underscore is a deliberate signal that no compatibility guarantee attaches.

On type hints: this guide writes annotations in the modern form, which requires Python 3.10 or later for built-in generics and 3.12 for the type statement. Where a construct needs a newer version, the version is named inline. As of Python 3.14, PEP 649 makes annotation evaluation lazy by default, so a forward reference to a class defined later in the same file no longer needs quoting.

Read annotations as documentation written by the author, not as a contract enforced at runtime. Python does not check them. Their value comes from a static checker reading the same source, and a checker can only verify what the annotation says — an Any or a bare object erases the guarantee completely.

Exercises 0

  1. Install CPython 3.14 in a virtual environment created by uv, and record sys.version, sys.implementation.name and sys._is_gil_enabled().
  2. Determine which branch in section 2 your interpreter belongs to and state its end-of-life date.
  3. Write a pyproject.toml declaring requires-python = ">=3.12", and confirm the resolver refuses a distribution that requires 3.14.
  4. Configure ruff check and ruff format against a scratch directory, then run mypy and pyright on the same code and reconcile the differences in their output.
  5. Reproduce a post-mortem session with python -m pdb -c continue on a script that raises an uncaught exception.

1 · Lexical Structure & Syntax

1.1 Indentation is syntax, not formatting1.2 Tokens, keywords and line joining1.3 Comprehensions and inlined scopes1.4 Structural pattern matching1.5 f-strings, format specs and t-strings1.6 Assignment forms, literals and Ellipsis

Python’s tokenizer turns a character stream into a token stream, and the block structure of a program is a property of that token stream rather than of delimiter characters. This chapter covers the consequences: what a suite is, which tokens exist, how a comprehension is scoped, and the syntax added since 3.8.

1.1 Indentation is syntax, not formatting

A suite is the group of statements that follows a compound-statement header. Its extent is determined by the leading whitespace on each line, which the tokenizer converts into INDENT and DEDENT tokens. There is no delimiter character, so a missing colon or a misaligned indent is a syntax error rather than a style violation.

The eight keywords that open a suite are if, elif, else, for, while, try, except, finally, with, def, class and match/case. Each is followed by a colon and then either a single simple statement on the same line, or a newline plus an indented suite.

# Both forms are legal and equivalent in meaning.
total = 0
for n in range(1, 11):
    total += n
print(total)                                # 55

total = 0
for n in range(1, 11): total += n            # suite on one line
print(total)                                # 55

# A suite may contain compound statements to arbitrary depth.
def classify(n):
    if n > 0:
        if n % 2 == 0:
            return "positive even"
        return "positive odd"
    return "non-positive"

print(classify(4), classify(3), classify(-1))

The one-line form is legal for any compound statement, including ones nested inside others. It is used in this guide only in single-line if guards, because it defeats every subsequent structural reformatting.

The convention is four spaces per level. This is not merely stylistic: the tokenizer compares indentation widths for equality, and mixing tabs with spaces inside one block is a hard error.

>>> if True:
...     print("four spaces")
... 	print("one tab")
TabError: inconsistent use of tabs and spaces in indentation

>>> if True:
...         print("over-indented")
IndentationError: unexpected indent

>>> if True:
IndentationError: expected an indented block after 'if' statement on line 1

A literal tab cannot be typed here as reliably as four spaces, which is the entire argument for spaces: the correct form is reproducible in a file, in an editor, in a diff and in a review. Nothing distinguishes them to the parser once a file is tab-only, and a tab-only file still breaks every other tool in the ecosystem.

A suite cannot be empty. The body of a compound statement must hold at least one statement, and pass exists to satisfy that requirement when there is genuinely nothing to do. pass is a no-op statement, not a block: it occupies one statement position and leaves indentation unchanged.

Requirements: use spaces, never tabs, and configure the editor to insert four spaces for a tab and to convert tabs to spaces on save. Caveats: TabError is reported only when a tab and spaces are compared within one block, so a file indented entirely with tabs parses cleanly and remains unreadable to every formatter. form feed characters reset the column count, which is why they are legal in source at all.

1.2 Tokens, keywords and line joining

Python has 35 hard keywords in 3.14 and a set of soft keywords that are ordinary identifiers until a particular construct needs them. Line continuation has two distinct mechanisms, and a third case that is neither: a backslash inside a string literal.

Identifiers match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]* and are case-sensitive. Unicode normalisation is applied to identifiers only, as NFKC, which collapses some visually distinct characters into one identifier. The distinction is invisible in a diff and has caused real defects.

import keyword
import unicodedata

# Hard keywords are always reserved and never usable as a name.
print(len(keyword.kwlist))                    # 35
print(keyword.iskeyword("match"))              # False - match is a SOFT keyword
print(keyword.issoftkeyword("match"))          # True
print(keyword.softkwlist)                      # ['_', 'case', 'match', 'type']

# The two sets are disjoint: soft keywords are absent from kwlist.
print(sorted(set(keyword.kwlist) & set(keyword.softkwlist)))   # []

# A soft keyword is a perfectly good identifier.
match = "an ordinary variable"
case = {"_": "also ordinary"}
type = 3                                        # shadowed, but legal
print(match, case, type)

# NFKC normalisation applies to identifiers only.
a, b = "\u00e9", "e\u0301"
print(a != b, unicodedata.normalize("NFKC", b) == a)   # True True

Line joining has two mechanisms. Implicit joining applies inside an open bracket, inside a triple-quoted string, and inside a parenthesised with or import statement. Explicit joining uses a backslash as the final character of a physical line, and that backslash may be followed by nothing at all — not even a comment or trailing whitespace.

# Implicit: brackets are open, so newlines are insignificant.
total = (1 + 2 +
         3 + 4)
coords = [
    (0, 0), (1, 1),
    (2, 2),
]

# Explicit: a trailing backslash. No comment is permitted after it.
total += 5 + \
          6
print(total, coords)                            # 21 [(0,0), (1,1), (2,2)]

# A backslash inside a STRING is not a continuation - it is a character.
path = "C:\\Users\\temp"
print(len(path), repr(path))                   # 15 'C:\\Users\\temp'

Prefer implicit joining. An explicit backslash is invisible to most editors, admits no trailing comment, and swallows the newline and its indentation, which makes tracebacks point at an unhelpful line. A formatter cannot reflow around it.

String literals accept four prefix letters: r (raw), b (bytes), u (str, redundant since 3.3) and f (formatted, section 1.5). Python 3.14 adds t for templates. Prefixes are case-insensitive and combinable except where contradictory, so rb is valid and br is not.

raw = r"C:\new\table"           # backslashes literal, 14 chars
esc  = "C:\\new\\table"          # escaped backslashes, 14 chars
blob = rb"\x00\xff"             # raw + bytes
print(raw == esc)                            # True
print(type(blob), blob)                       # <class 'bytes'> b'\\x00\\xff'

# Adjacent string literals concatenate at compile time, prefixes permitting.
name = "world"
msg = "hello " "there, " "dear " name
print(msg)                                   # hello there, dear world

# Escape sequences are consumed by the tokenizer, before parsing.
print("tab:\there", "quote:\"", "backslash:\\")
print(len("C:\\temp"), len(r"C:\temp"))       # 8 7

The last rule matters when moving text between a browser and a source file: the HTML parser decodes entities before anything else sees the document, so an ampersand that Python is meant to see must be written escaped in HTML source. Only the f-string format specification is decoded by Python itself, and only into a type or format character.

1.3 Comprehensions and inlined scopes

A comprehension is a single expression that builds a container. Before Python 3.12 (PEP 709) each comprehension was compiled to its own code object; as of 3.12 it is inlined into the enclosing function or class body, and Python 3.13 (PEP 667) fixed the observable consequences of that inlining, including the exact semantics of locals() and frame.f_locals.

# The four comprehension forms, plus the generator expression.
squares = [n * n for n in range(6)]
evens   = {n for n in range(6) if n % 2 == 0}
lookup  = {n: n * n for n in range(4)}
pairs   = [(a, b) for a in "ab" for b in (1, 2)]
lazy    = (n * n for n in range(6))

print(squares)      # [0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25]
print(evens)        # {0, 2, 4}
print(lookup)       # {0: 0, 1: 1, 2: 4, 3: 9}
print(pairs)        # [('a', 1), ('a', 2), ('b', 1), ('b', 2)]
print(type(lazy).__name__, list(lazy))      # generator [0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25]

The scoping rules are subtle and are why a comprehension is not simply a loop written on one line. The leftmost iterable is evaluated eagerly in the enclosing scope. Everything after it — remaining iterables, the condition, and the result expression — is evaluated in a scope of its own.

threshold = 2
data = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
n = "the enclosing binding"

# The leftmost iterable sees the enclosing scope; `n` here is the
# comprehension's own loop variable and does not touch the outer `n`.
kept = [n for n in data if n > threshold]
print(kept)                                  # [3, 4, 5]
print(n)                                     # the enclosing binding

# A name bound inside a comprehension is not visible outside it.
_ = [x for x in data]
print(x if "x" in dir() else "x is not a module global")   # x is not a module global

The comprehension’s iteration variable was never a leak — it was a separate scope in 3.11 too. What PEP 709 changed is that the comprehension no longer creates a code object, so it is faster, and tracemalloc and profiler output now attribute the allocations to the enclosing function. A walrus assignment inside a comprehension does bind in the enclosing scope, which is the one genuine leak.

# A comprehension body cannot see a class-body name, and that restriction
# survives PEP 709 deliberately.
class Report:
    debug = True
    rows = ["a", "bb", "ccc"]
    # kept = [r for r in rows if debug]   <-- NameError: name 'debug'
    _debug = debug                          # resolve it here, before the loop
    kept = [r for r in rows if _debug]

print(Report.kept)                          # ['a', 'bb', 'ccc']

# The same restriction applies to a method body reading a class attribute
# by bare name, which is exactly why methods take `self`.
class Widget:
    label = "w"
    def show(self):
        # return label        <-- NameError
        return self.label
print(Widget().show())

Caveats: nested comprehensions and long conditions are legal but unreadable, and a comprehension cannot break, continue or return — which forces a two-pass idiom where a single loop would express the intent in one pass. Beyond roughly two levels of nesting, or any condition needing a comment, use a loop. Under PEP 667, frame.f_locals became a write-through proxy and an independent mutation of it now raises RuntimeError rather than silently doing nothing.

1.4 Structural pattern matching

The match statement, added in Python 3.10, dispatches on the shape of a value rather than on the result of a comparison. It reads like a switch, but its semantics are those of a destructuring expression language with guards, not a jump table.

class Command:
    def __init__(self, name, args=()):
        self.name, self.args = name, args

def handle(subject) -> str:
    match subject:
        case Command(name="quit"):
            return "exiting"
        case Command(name="move", args=(x, y)):
            return f"move to {x},{y}"
        case Command(name=other):
            return f"unknown command: {other}"
        case [a, b, c]:
            return f"triple {a + b + c}"
        case {"type": "ping", "seq": seq}:
            return f"ping #{seq}"
        case str() as s if s.startswith("#"):
            return "comment"
        case 1 | 2 | 3:
            return "small int"
        case _:
            return "no match"

The pattern kinds, in the order the reference implementation checks them:

PatternMatches whenBinds
Value: Color.REDsubject == pattern, compared as a dotted attribute pathNothing
Literal: 0, "ok", Nonesubject == pattern; None means is NoneNothing
Capture: xAlwaysx, by name
Wildcard: _AlwaysNothing; at most one per sequence pattern
Sequence: [a, b], (a, *rest)Subject is a list, tuple or a registered collections.abc.Sequence — but not str, bytes or bytearrayAll captures
Mapping: {"k": v}Subject is a Mapping; the pattern is a subset testAll captures
Class: Point(x=0, y=y)isinstance holds, then keyword patterns are read as attributes, or positional patterns via __match_args__All captures
Alternative: A() | B()Either side matchesOnly captures present on both sides
Guard: case p if condp matches and cond is then trueAs p; the guard runs after binding

Two properties deserve emphasis. A mapping pattern is a subset test: extra keys in the subject are ignored, which is the opposite of a dict comparison. And matching is not exhaustively checked — a match with no applicable case completes silently, exactly as an unmatched if chain does.

from typing import NamedTuple, assert_never

class Point(NamedTuple):
    x: int
    y: int

def quadrant(p: Point) -> str:
    match p:
        case Point(0, 0):
            return "origin"
        case Point(0, y):
            return "on the y axis"
        case Point(x, 0):
            return "on the x axis"
        case Point(x, y) if x == y:
            return "on the diagonal"
        case _:
            return "off-axis"

# assert_never turns an incomplete match into a runtime error, by exhaustion.
def parse(raw: str | int) -> str | int:
    match raw:
        case str():
            return raw.strip().lower()
        case int():
            return raw
        case _:
            assert_never(raw)          # TypeError if a third type is added

print(quadrant(Point(1, 1)), parse("  OK  "))

Applies to: Python 3.10 and later. match and case are soft keywords, so an existing program that uses match as an identifier keeps parsing — the match = re.match(...) idiom is safe. Caveats: a sequence pattern deliberately excludes str, bytes and bytearray, so case [a, b]: will not match a two-character string. A capture name may not shadow a name already bound in the same pattern, and an alternative pattern needs as to be given a name. A dotted value pattern is an attribute lookup, not a name lookup, so it cannot see a local variable.

1.5 f-strings, format specs and t-strings

The f-string prefix arrived in 3.6 and has been substantially revised twice: = in 3.8, and PEP 701 in 3.12, which permitted reuse of the same quote, plus backslashes and comments inside a replacement field. Python 3.14 adds the t-string, which separates the template from its data.

name, score, ratio = "Ada", 1234567.891, 0.123456

# Conversions !r !s !a, then the specification mini-language.
print(f"{name} scored {score:.2f} ({ratio:.1%})")
print(f"{name!r} {score!s} {ratio!a}")
print(f"{name:>10}|{name:<10}|{name:^10}|")   # align, default space fill
print(f"{score:14.3f}")                            # width then precision
print(f"{score:,}")                                # grouping separator
print(f"{score:+,.2f}")                            # explicit sign
print(f"{ratio:+.3e}")                              # scientific
print(f"{5:0=6b}")                                 # fill=, width, binary
print(f"{score:#010.2f}")                           # alternate form, zero-pad

The specification grammar is [[fill]align][sign][z][#][0][width][grouping_option][.precision][type]. Width and precision may be nested replacement fields, and a literal brace inside a specification must be doubled. In Python 3.14 the f-string is parsed by a C implementation built on the new string.formatter_parser(), and the string.Formatter class is deprecated in its favour.

rows = [("widget", 7), ("gadget", 1234)]
w = max(len(label) for label, _ in rows)

# Dynamic width from a nested field; doubled braces survive as literals.
for label, qty in rows:
    print(f"| {label:{w}} | {qty:>6} | {{literal}} |")

# The = specifier reproduces the expression and its value, for debugging.
def f(x: int = 3) -> int:
    return x * 2
print(f"{f()=}")
print(f"{f(x=5)=}")

PEP 701 (Python 3.12) removed the restrictions that made single-quoted f-strings awkward: the same quote character may be used inside the replacement field, and a backslash or a comment may appear there.

user = {"name": "Grace", "home": r"C:\Users"}
n = len(user)

# Same quote reused inside the field, plus a backslash in the expression.
print(f"{user['name']} at {user["home"]}")
print(f"{n = } fields, ratio {1 / n:.3f}")
print(f"{ "hi" if n > 1 else "bye" }")

# Before 3.12 the first line above was a SyntaxError: the outer double
# quotes collided with the inner ones and a backslash was forbidden.

Caveats: an f-string evaluates its fields at construction time, so it is not a deferred formatting facility — that is what the t-string provides. A malformed specification is not a compile-time error, so a typo raises ValueError at runtime. Nesting is legal under PEP 701 rules and unreadable beyond one level.

Python 3.14 adds t-strings (PEP 750). A t-string evaluates to a string.templatelib.Template holding the constant fragments and the interpolated values in separate sequences, which makes it safe to defer, escape, audit or translate without ever formatting untrusted input.

from string.templatelib import Template
import html

user = "grace"
count = 3

tpl = t"user {user!r} has {count} logins"
print(tpl)                       # user 'grace' has 3 logins
print(type(tpl).__name__)        # Template
print(tpl.strings)               # ('user ', ' has ', ' logins')
print(tpl.values)                # ('grace', 3)
print(tpl.attributes)            # one dict per interpolation, in order

# Nothing is formatted until asked, so the raw data can be escaped first.
safe = Template(tpl.values, tpl.strings, tpl.attributes)
print(safe)                      # user &#x27;grace&#x27; has 3 logins

# Adjacent t-strings merge at compile time, exactly as plain literals do.
merged = t"a" t"b" t"{user}"
print(merged.strings, merged.values)       # ('ab',) ('grace',)

Template(values, strings, attributes=None) takes its arguments positionally in that order, and attributes carries each interpolation’s flags and conversion, which is why passing it back preserves the !r. Because the data is separated from the template, a t-string is the correct type for anything reaching a log, a trace or a message queue. A format string is not: a caller who can supply the format string can trigger attribute access on arbitrary objects.

1.6 Assignment forms, literals and Ellipsis

Beyond plain binding, Python has an assignment-expression operator, extended unpacking that tolerates length mismatches, numeric literals with digit separators and explicit bases, and the Ellipsis singleton.

# Extended unpacking: a starred target absorbs any number of items.
head, *middle, tail = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
print(head, middle, tail)                    # 1 [2, 3, 4] 5

first, *rest = "abc"                         # strings unpack too
print(first, rest)                           # a ['b', 'c']

# Starred targets on both sides require at least one item on each.
a, *b, c = [1]
print(a, b, c)                               # 1 [] 1

# Starred expressions in calls, in displays and as assignment targets.
def g(p, q, r): return p + q + r
print(g(1, *[2, 3]))                        # 6
first, *others = [*range(3), 9]
print(first, others)                         # 0 [1, 2, 9]

# `_` is an ordinary name; repeated assignment overwrites it.
x, _, z = 1, 2, 3
print(x, z)                                  # 1 3
_ = "now it holds the last discarded value"

The assignment expression (the walrus operator, :=) arrived in 3.8. It binds a name and yields its value, which removes the need to assign and then read across two statements.

import re

# The canonical use: removing a redundant lookup in a loop.
lines = ["id=17 ok", "garbage", "id=42 ok"]
ids = []
for line in lines:
    if (m := re.search(r"id=(\d+)", line)) is not None:
        ids.append(int(m.group(1)))
print(ids)                                   # [17, 42]

# Comprehension conditions and while conditions both accept it.
d = {"a": 1, "b": 2}
print({k for k, v in d.items() if (n := len(k)) > 0})

n = 0
while (chunk := "abc"[n:n + 2]):
    n += len(chunk)
    if n >= 3:
        break

# A bare target with a lower-precedence right-hand side: legal, rarely wanted.
x = y := 0
print(x, y)                                  # 0 0

Caveats: a bare assignment expression used as a statement requires parentheses, otherwise it is a SyntaxError. A walrus inside a comprehension binds in the enclosing scope — useful occasionally, surprising frequently, and the reason n survives the comprehension above. := may not appear in the leftmost iterable of a comprehension, because that part is evaluated in the enclosing scope before the target exists.

Numeric literals accept underscores as digit separators anywhere within the digits, and an explicit base prefix: 0o octal, 0x hexadecimal, 0b binary. A leading 0o, 0x or 0b may itself be followed by an underscore.

n = 1_000_000
mask = 0xFF_FF
perms = 0o755
flags = 0b1010_1010
print(n, mask, perms, flags)
print(1_000.000_1, 1e6, 1_0e2)               # 1000.0001 1000000.0 10.0
print(3j, 3.5j)                              # complex literals
print(0xFF, 0o17, 0b1011)                    # 255 15 11

# Conversion of a numeric string honours the same separators and prefixes.
print(int("1_000"), int("0x1f", 0), int("1_0", 2))

Ellipsis is a built-in singleton, written ..., of type ellipsis. It is not a placeholder for missing code; it is a real object with three established uses.

print(...)                                    # Ellipsis
print(type(...) is type(Ellipsis))        # True

# 1. Slicing: `...` as the start means "from the beginning", as the stop
#    means "leave it unchanged".
data = list(range(10))
print(data[2:5], data[...:3], data[3:...])        # [2,3,4] [0,1,2] [3..9]

# 2. Typing: the conventional spelling of a variable-length tuple type.
from typing import Tuple
def coords() -> tuple[int, ...]: ...
def coords2() -> Tuple[int, ...]: ...
print(coords.__annotations__["return"], coords2.__annotations__["return"])

# 3. Stub bodies: `...` is the no-implementation body a stub declares.
class NotImplementedYet:
    def method(self) -> None: ...

Applies to: ... as a slice endpoint follows from slice.indices() clamping, which treats None as “the end” and therefore lets Ellipsis mean “leave it unchanged”. As a type it denotes a homogeneous tuple of any length, which is distinct from tuple[int, int] (exactly two) and from tuple[int, *tuple[str, ...]] (a heterogeneous prefix, PEP 646, Python 3.11).

Exercises 1

  1. Write a function containing nested if/for/with suites six levels deep, then confirm with tokenize that each level produces exactly one INDENT token.
  2. Demonstrate all four line-joining mechanisms, including the case where a backslash inside a string is not a continuation.
  3. Write a comprehension inside a class body that reads a class attribute, observe the NameError, and repair it. Explain why the restriction exists.
  4. Implement a dispatcher with match handling a mapping payload, a class payload, a sequence payload and a guarded capture, then prove exhaustiveness with assert_never.
  5. Produce a t-string holding a SQL template with two parameters, then render it through Template with the values HTML-escaped, and confirm the raw value never appears unescaped.
  6. Write one expression that parses a log line, binds the match object and filters on it using :=, then remove the walrus and show the two-statement equivalent.

2 · Built-in Types & the Object Model

2.1 Numbers, precision and rounding2.2 Text, bytes and buffers2.3 Sequences, mappings and sets2.4 Identity, equality and hashability2.5 Conversion, testing and truthiness

Python has no user-visible primitive types. Everything is an object with a type, and every type is a class with methods. This chapter covers the types every program uses, the operations available on each, and the small set of rules that govern equality, identity and truth.

2.1 Numbers, precision and rounding

Four numeric types ship in the core. int is arbitrary-precision, float is IEEE 754 binary64, complex is a pair of binary64 values, and bool is a subclass of int. The decimal and fractions modules supply exact decimal and exact rational arithmetic.

TypeStoragePrecisionUse for
intArbitrary size; 30 bits per internal digitExactCounts, identifiers, arbitrary-precision arithmetic
floatIEEE 754 binary64, 8 bytesAbout 15–17 significant decimal digitsScientific computation, interop with C
boolSubclass of intNot applicableTruth values only, never arithmetic intent
complexTwo binary64 valuesSame as floatSignal processing, roots of polynomials
decimal.DecimalBase-10, arbitrary sizeExact, then rounded to a context precision (default 28)Money, invoicing, regulated quantities
fractions.FractionExact numerator and denominatorExactRational arithmetic, unit conversion
import sys

# int is unbounded. Conversion to TEXT is bounded by policy, not by size.
print(2 ** 1000)
print(sys.int_info.bits_per_digit, sys.int_info.default_max_str_digits)

try:
    str(10 ** 5000)
except ValueError as e:
    print("ValueError:", str(e)[:44])         # 4300-digit limit, added in 3.11
sys.set_int_max_str_digits(0)
print(len(str(10 ** 5000)))
sys.set_int_max_str_digits(4300)

n = 255
print(n.to_bytes(2, "big"), int.from_bytes(b"\x00\xff", "big"))
print(0xFF.bit_count(), 0b1010.bit_count())               # 8 2   (3.10+)

# float is binary64. The classic demonstration is not a bug in Python.
print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3)                                  # False
print(0.1 + 0.2)                                         # 0.30000000000000004
print((0.1 + 0.2).hex())                                 # 0x1.3333333333334p-1
print(float("inf"), float("-inf"), float("nan"))
print(sys.float_info.max, sys.float_info.epsilon)

The 4300-digit conversion limit was added in 3.11 as mitigation for CVE-2020-10735. sys.set_int_max_str_digits(0) lifts it, which is safe for a value the process produced itself and unsafe for one received from untrusted input.

bool is a subclass of int, so True == 1 and isinstance(True, int) is True. That compatibility is convenient and is a recurring source of bugs, because {1: "a", True: "b"} is a one-entry dictionary — True and 1 hash and compare equal.

from decimal import Decimal, localcontext, getcontext, ROUND_HALF_UP
from fractions import Fraction
import math

# round() uses banker's rounding: a tie goes to the nearest EVEN neighbour.
print(round(0.5), round(1.5), round(2.5), round(-0.5))    # 0 2 2 0

# round() on a float is a formatting operation, not exact arithmetic.
print(round(2.675, 2))                      # 2.67 - 2.675 is really 2.67499...
print(round(Decimal("2.675"), 2))            # 2.68 - exact decimal input
print(round(Decimal("2.5"), 0, ROUND_HALF_UP))          # 3

# Never round money in binary floating point.
total = sum(Decimal("0.1") for _ in range(3))
print(total, float(total))                   # 0.3 0.3  (float for display only)

# Fraction is exact and interoperates with int and float.
r = Fraction(1, 3) + Fraction(1, 6)
print(r, float(r), r.limit_denominator(100))            # 1/2 0.5 1/2

# math: isclose for comparison, fsum for accurate summation.
print(math.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3, rel_tol=1e-9))       # True
print(sum([0.1] * 10), math.fsum([0.1] * 10))           # differ
print(math.prod([1, 2, 3, 4]), math.comb(10, 3), math.gcd(12, 18))
print(math.nextafter(1.0, math.inf) == 1.0000000000000002)   # True

The decimal context is a thread-local stack, so precision is a property of the executing context rather than of the value. localcontext is the only thread-safe way to change it.

print(getcontext().prec, getcontext().rounding)   # 28 ROUND_HALF_EVEN
with localcontext() as ctx:
    ctx.prec = 6
    print(Decimal(1) / Decimal(3))                       # 0.166667
print(Decimal(1) / Decimal(3))                           # 0.3333333333333333333333333333

# A Decimal built from a float has already inherited that float's error.
bad = Decimal(0.1)
print(bad, bad == Decimal("0.1"))          # False - use Decimal("0.1")

Caveats: Decimal and fractions.Fraction do not interoperate implicitly with float; mixing them raises TypeError, which is deliberate. float("nan") != float("nan") is true, so NaN never compares equal to anything including itself — test with math.isnan. Sorting a sequence containing NaN produces an implementation-dependent order, because a comparison against NaN is always false and so no element ever moves past it.

2.2 Text, bytes and buffers

str is a sequence of Unicode code points, immutable and indexed by integer. bytes is a sequence of integers in 0..255, also immutable; bytearray is its mutable counterpart, and memoryview exposes the memory of any buffer without copying it.

Indexing returns an object of the same type, negative indices count from the end, and a negative step returns the elements in reverse. An out-of-range index raises IndexError; an out-of-range slice silently clamps.

s = "Python"
print(s[0], s[-1], len(s))                  # P n 6
print(s[1:4], s[::-1], s[:-2])              # yth nohtyP hon
print(repr(s[100:200]))                     # '' - a slice clamps, an index raises
print(s[::2])                               # Pto

try:
    s[0] = "J"
except TypeError as e:
    print("immutable:", e)

b = b"bytes"
print(b[0], b[:3], type(b[0]))              # 98 b'by' <class 'int'>

# str counts code points: not bytes, and not grapheme clusters.
e = "e\u0301"                                # 'e' plus a combining acute accent
print(len(e), len(e.encode("utf-8")))        # 2 3
MethodSignatureBehaviour
splitsplit(sep=None, maxsplit=-1)sep=None splits on runs of whitespace and discards leading and trailing empties; maxsplit limits the count
rsplitrsplit(sep=None, maxsplit=-1)As split but from the right; with sep=None the limit applies differently
joinsep.join(iterable)Called on the separator; raises TypeError if any element is not a str
partitionpartition(sep), rpartitionAlways returns a 3-tuple; ("", "", self) when not found
replacereplace(old, new, count=-1)Non-overlapping; count limits replacements from the left
translatetranslate(table)Maps code points through a dict from str.maketrans; a value of None deletes the character
stripstrip(chars=None)chars is a set of characters, not a prefix or a substring
ljust rjust center*(width, fillchar=" ")Never truncate; returns self when already long enough
casefoldcasefold()Full Unicode case folding; the correct comparison key, unlike lower()
titletitle()Word boundaries are not linguistic; avoid it for display
startswith endswith*(prefix_or_tuple)Accepts a tuple; the empty string is a prefix of everything
removeprefix removesuffix*(p)Added in 3.9; remove one occurrence, return self when absent
find index*(sub, start, end)find returns -1; index raises ValueError
countcount(sub, start, end)Counts non-overlapping occurrences
format_mapformat_map(mapping)Applies {} placeholders; a missing key raises KeyError
raw = "  user_id=42; name=Ada Lovelace  "
fields = dict(p.split("=", 1) for p in raw.strip().split(";"))
print(fields)                        # {'user_id': '42', 'name': 'Ada Lovelace'}

# strip(chars) takes a SET of characters and strips from both ends.
print("xxhelloxx".strip("x"))         # hello
print("hello".strip("lo"))            # he - not "hel"

# translate for character-level rewriting, including deletion.
table = str.maketrans({"a": "4", "e": "3", "z": None})
print("laziness".translate(table))    # l4in3ss

# Case-insensitive comparison must use casefold, not lower.
print("Stra\u00dfe".casefold() == "STRASSE".casefold())      # True
print("Stra\u00dfe".lower() == "STRASSE".lower())            # False

# prefix/suffix helpers, and find versus index.
print("v1.2.3".removeprefix("v"), "v1.2.3".removesuffix(".3"))
print("abcabc".count("abc"), "abcabc".find("z"), "abcabc".index("bc"))
try:
    "abcabc".index("z")
except ValueError as e:
    print("ValueError:", e)

Conversion between text and bytes is explicit, and encoding errors originate here. Both str.encode and bytes.decode default to UTF-8 with errors="strict".

s = "caf\u00e9 \U0001F600"
b = s.encode("utf-8")
print(b, len(s), len(b))
print(b.decode("utf-8") == s)               # True

for errors in ("strict", "replace", "ignore", "backslashreplace", "surrogateescape"):
    try:
        out = b"\xff\xfe ok".decode("utf-8", errors=errors)
    except UnicodeDecodeError as e:
        out = f"UnicodeDecodeError: {e.reason}"
    print(f"{errors:16} {out!r}")

# memoryview: a zero-copy window onto a buffer (PEP 688, Python 3.12).
mv = memoryview(bytearray(b"abcd"))
mv[0] = 65
print(bytes(mv), mv.hex(), mv.tolist(), mv.nbytes)
print(mv.tolist() == [65, 98, 99, 100])
TypeMutableElement typeTypical use
strNostr of length 1Text
bytesNoint in 0..255Serialised data, hashes, file blobs
bytearrayYesint in 0..255Accumulating output, binary protocols
memoryviewView only, unless the target isDepends on formatZero-copy slices, buffer-protocol interop

Requirements: pass encoding="utf-8" explicitly to open(). The default is locale-dependent, so the same code reads a different encoding on a different machine; UTF-8 mode is scheduled to become the default in Python 3.15. Caveats: bytes has no encode and str has no decode, so mixing them up raises AttributeError, which is the intended failure. Indexing a str builds a new one-character string, which is why s[0] in big_text in a hot loop is worth replacing with "x" in big_text.

2.3 Sequences, mappings and sets

The built-in containers cover almost every case: a general-purpose mutable sequence, a general-purpose immutable sequence, an insertion-ordered mutable mapping, an insertion-ordered immutable mapping, and two set types. Each has a documented ordering and a documented mutability.

TypeMutableOrderedHashableNotes
listYesInsertion orderNoIndexable, sliceable, accepts duplicates
tupleNoInsertion orderYes, if every element isThe default for a heterogeneous fixed record
dictYesInsertion order, guaranteed since 3.7NoKeys must be unique and hashable
types.MappingProxyTypeNoSame as the sourceNoA read-only view; source changes are visible through it
setYesNot defined — do not rely on itNoMembership in constant time; no duplicates
frozensetNoNot definedYes, if elements areUsable as a dict key or a set element
# dict preserves insertion order, and its views support set operations.
d = {"b": 2, "a": 1}
e = {"a": 1, "c": 3}
print(d, list(d), list(d.items()))
print(d.keys() & e.keys(), d.keys() | e.keys(), d.keys() - e.keys())
print({**d, **e})                 # merged, right side wins on a key clash
d2 = d | e                       # PEP 584, Python 3.9+
d2 |= {"d": 4}                   # in-place union, also 3.9+
print(d2)
print(d.popitem(), d.setdefault("z", 0), d.get("nope", "default"))

# Views are live: they reflect later mutations of the source.
view = d.keys()
d["new"] = 1
print("new" in view)              # True

# Set operations, and the subset relation.
s = {1, 2, 3}
print(s | {4}, s & {2, 9}, s ^ {3, 4}, s - {1}, s <= {1, 2, 3, 4})

# list and tuple are Sequences, compared lexicographically.
print([1, 2] < [1, 3], (1, 2) < (1, 2, 0), [1, 2, 3][1:], list("abc"))

range is a lazy Sequence, not an iterator. It supports len() and indexing, it is re-iterable, and it computes its length arithmetically rather than by counting. zip, by contrast, is a one-shot iterator over tuples, and zip(..., strict=True) (added in 3.10) raises ValueError on a length mismatch rather than truncating silently.

r = range(10, 0, -2)
print(r, list(r), len(r), r[0], r[-1], 7 in r)

names = ["ada", "grace"]
ids = [1, 2, 3]
print(list(zip(names, ids)))                     # silently truncated
try:
    list(zip(names, ids, strict=True))
except ValueError as e:
    print("ValueError:", e)

for i, name in enumerate(names, start=1):
    print(i, name)

print(sorted(names, key=len, reverse=True))
print(min(names, key=len), max(ids), max(names, key=len, default="none"))
print(sorted(zip(names, ids), key=lambda p: p[1]))
print(any(x == 0 for x in ids), all(x > 0 for x in ids), sum(ids, 0))

The collections module supplies the specialised containers the built-ins do not cover.

from collections import Counter, deque, defaultdict, ChainMap, namedtuple

counts = Counter("abracadabra")
print(counts.most_common(3), counts["z"], counts.total())   # total() is 3.10+

# A deque with maxlen is a bounded queue that discards from the left.
q = deque(["a", "b", "c"], maxlen=3)
q.append("d")
print(q, q.popleft(), q.rotate(1))

# DEFECT: defaultdict(list) passes the SAME list object every time.
bad = defaultdict(list)
for k in ("x", "y"):
    bad[k].append(k)
print(list(bad.values()))         # [['x', 'y']] - one shared list

# FIX: a setdefault, a comprehension, or a factory function.
d_get = {k: [] for k in ("x", "y")}
for k in ("x", "y"):
    d_get[k].append(k)
print(d_get)                      # {'x': ['x'], 'y': ['y']}

P = namedtuple("P", "x y")        # 3.7+: also class syntax via typing.NamedTuple
print(P(1, 2)._asdict(), P._fields)
print(list(ChainMap({"a": 1}, {"a": 2, "b": 3}).items()))   # [('b', 3), ('a', 1)]

Caveats: the defaultdict(list) pattern is the single most common collections defect, and it stays invisible until two distinct keys are used. Set ordering and set iteration order are explicitly unspecified and vary between runs when PYTHONHASHSEED changes, so never rely on them. OrderedDict is redundant on 3.7 and later, where dict already guarantees order; its only remaining advantages are move_to_end and popitem(last=False).

2.4 Identity, equality and hashability

is compares object identity and answers a question about memory addresses. == asks for value equality and dispatches to __eq__. The two are unrelated, and confusing them is the origin of a long list of subtle defects.

import sys

a, b = [1, 2], [1, 2]
print(a == b, a is b, a is not b)          # True False True
print(a is a, id(a) == id(a))              # True True

# None is a singleton. Test it with `is`, never with `==`.
print(None is None, [] is None, [] == None)        # True False False
missing = None
if missing is None:
    print("absent")

# Small integers and identifier-like strings are interned. That is an
# implementation detail, not a language guarantee.
x, y = 1000, 1000
print(x is y)                              # True on CPython
s1, s2 = "user_name", "user" "_name"
print(s1 is s2, s1 is sys.intern("user_name"))       # True True

Interning small integers from -5 to 256, and interning identifier-like strings, are CPython memory optimisations. The same expression on PyPy, or under a different implementation, may answer differently. sys.intern is the only portable way to request interning; it returns the canonical instance for that interpreter run.

id() reports a memory address, which is unique only among live objects. An object freed by the collector releases its address for reuse, so an id() observed earlier may belong to a different object later.

# Hashability: a type is hashable if it supplies a usable __hash__.
print(hash(1), hash("a"), hash((1, 2)), hash(frozenset({1})))
for value in ([1], {1}, {1: 2}, bytearray(b"x")):
    try:
        hash(value)
    except TypeError as e:
        print(f"unhashable: {type(value).__name__}")

# Defining __eq__ without __hash__ sets __hash__ to None automatically.
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y): self.x, self.y = x, y
    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.x, self.y) == (other.x, other.y)

try:
    hash(Point(1, 2))
except TypeError as e:
    print("Point is unhashable:", e)
print(Point.__hash__)                       # None

The contract is a single obligation: if a type overrides __eq__, it must also declare __hash__, and the two must agree — objects that compare equal must hash equal. The default object.__hash__ is derived from id, so leaving it in place produces a type whose instances compare equal yet land in different buckets, silently breaking dict and set lookups.

from dataclasses import dataclass

# The correct pattern: declare both, and make them agree.
@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Pt:
    x: int
    y: int
    def __hash__(self) -> int:
        return hash((self.x, self.y))

p1, p2 = Pt(1, 2), Pt(1, 2)
print(p1 == p2, hash(p1) == hash(p2), {p1: "a"}[p2])   # True True a

# Rich comparison returns NotImplemented to defer to the reflected operand,
# which is what allows an unrelated type to define the other half.
class Always:
    def __eq__(self, other): return NotImplemented
    def __hash__(self): return 0
print(Always() == 3, 3 == Always())        # False False, and no exception

Requirements: a type used as a dict key or a set element needs __hash__, consistent with __eq__. The standard library supplies the pattern in functools.total_ordering and in dataclasses; attrs generates both correctly. Caveats: == falls back to identity when __eq__ is absent, and is on floats, small integers and interned strings tests the interpreter rather than the program. Comparing a float for identity is almost always a defect; use math.isclose (section 2.1).

2.5 Conversion, testing and truthiness

A conversion function is the constructor of the corresponding type, so a class may replace a built-in and inherit its conversion protocol. isinstance consults the MRO and honours __instancecheck__; issubclass does the same through __subclasshook__.

# int() accepts str, bytes and bytearray, with an explicit base.
print(int("42"), int("0x1f", 0), int("0b1010", 0), int("1_000"))
print(int(b"\xff", 16), int(bytearray(b"12")))
print(int("z", 36))                       # 35 - bases up to 36 are valid

# int() on a float truncates toward zero, and rejects non-finite values.
print(int(3.99), int(-3.99))               # 3 -3
for bad in (float("nan"), float("inf")):
    try:
        int(bad)
    except (ValueError, OverflowError) as e:
        print(type(e).__name__)

# float() accepts numeric strings and raises on anything else.
print(float("  1e3  "), float("nan"), float("1_000.5"))
try:
    float("1,000")
except ValueError as e:
    print("ValueError:", e)

# str() uses __str__; repr() targets the developer and is usually eval-able.
print(str(1e16), repr(1e16))               # 1e+16 1e+16
print(str(10), repr(10), str([1]), repr([1]))
print(f"{10:#o}", bin(10), oct(10), hex(255))

Truth testing follows a documented two-step rule, and both steps are overridable. bool(x) calls x.__bool__() if it exists; failing that it calls x.__len__() and compares the result against zero; failing that the object is always true.

class AlwaysEmpty:
    def __len__(self): return 0
class Explicit:
    def __bool__(self): return False
    def __len__(self): return 100           # __bool__ wins

print(bool(0), bool(0.0), bool(""), bool([]), bool({}), bool(None), bool(set()))
print(bool([0]), bool("0"), bool(AlwaysEmpty()), bool(Explicit()))
print(bool(1e-300), bool(10 ** 1000), bool(float("nan")))   # all True
print(bool(1), bool(-1))                     # any non-zero number

# isinstance accepts a tuple of types and honours __instancecheck__.
print(isinstance(1, int), isinstance(True, int), isinstance(1, (str, float)))
print(issubclass(bool, int), issubclass(int, object), isinstance("s", str))
try:
    isinstance(1, int | str)
except TypeError as e:
    print("TypeError:", e)                  # unions are for annotations only

import abc
import numbers

# __instancecheck__ is how an ABC intercepts isinstance.
class Even(abc.ABC):
    @classmethod
    def __instancecheck__(cls, obj):
        try:
            return obj % 2 == 0
        except TypeError:
            return False

print(isinstance(4, Even), isinstance(5, Even), isinstance("x", Even))

# The numeric tower: int and float register with the numbers ABCs.
print(isinstance(2, numbers.Number), isinstance(1, numbers.Integral))
print(isinstance(True, numbers.Integral))
print(isinstance(10.0, numbers.Real), isinstance(3j, numbers.Complex))
print(isinstance(3j, numbers.Real))         # False

Write if x is not None: when testing for absence, never if x != None:. The latter calls __ne__, which a custom class may have overridden, and it is also false for a container that merely contains None. The is not None form is also faster on CPython, since no comparison object is built.

Exercises 2

  1. Compute 2 ** 1000 as an int and as a float, and account separately for the OverflowError and for the precision loss.
  2. Exercise every str method in the table above, including the two counter-intuitive ones: strip(chars) as a character set, and count non-overlap semantics.
  3. Reproduce the defaultdict(list) shared-object defect, then write the same logic three other ways and compare readability.
  4. Write a class that overrides __eq__ only, observe the resulting TypeError on hash, then add a consistent __hash__.
  5. Implement __instancecheck__ so that isinstance(x, Prime) holds exactly for primes below 1000, and time it against a set lookup.
  6. Enumerate every value in the bool table, and write a class whose __len__ and __bool__ disagree to show which one wins.

3 · Names, Bindings & Scope

3.1 Binding, rebinding and deletion3.2 The LEGB rule and the two special scopes3.3 global, nonlocal and module state3.4 Closures and late binding3.5 Modules, __name__ and the single-pass model

A name in Python is a reference to an object, held in a mapping. Which mapping a name is read from is decided by a four-step rule, and the two non-obvious scopes in that rule — the comprehension scope and the class scope — are the source of most name-resolution surprises in the language.

3.1 Binding, rebinding and deletion

Assignment binds a name in the current scope’s mapping. It never modifies the object, and that distinction is the whole of Python’s copy semantics. Rebinding a name is invisible to every other holder of it; mutating an object is visible to all of them.

original = [1, 2, 3]
alias = original
alias.append(4)                 # mutates the shared object
print(original)                  # [1, 2, 3, 4] - visible through both names

rebound = original
rebound = [9]                    # rebinds only this name
print(original, rebound)         # [1, 2, 3, 4] [9]

# del removes the NAME, not the object.
x = object()
y = x
del x
print(y is not None)             # the object survives with one reference

# int is immutable, so "mutation" is always rebinding.
a, b = 1, 1
a += 1
print(a, b)                      # 2 1

The += case is the one to internalise. For a list, lst += other is an in-place operation equivalent to lst.extend(other), mutating the shared object; for an integer it is int.__add__ followed by a rebinding. The same operator therefore means different things depending on the static type of the target.

import copy

shared = [[0]] * 3                       # ONE list, referenced three times
shared[0].append(1)
print(shared)                             # [[0, 1], [0, 1], [0, 1]]

independent = [[0] for _ in range(3)]    # three distinct lists
independent[0].append(1)
print(independent)                        # [[0, 1], [0], [0]]

# A shallow copy copies references to the elements.
original = [[0], [1]]
shallow = copy.copy(original)
deep = copy.deepcopy(original)
original[0].append(99)
print(shallow)                            # [[0, 99], [1]] - inner list shared
print(deep)                               # [[0], [1]] - fully independent

# A tuple containing a list is still not hashable.
try:
    hash(([1],))
except TypeError as e:
    print("TypeError:", e)

Caveats: [x] * n and [[x]] * n are the two most frequent sources of shared mutable state in Python code. copy.deepcopy goes through __deepcopy__ and the __reduce__ protocol, so it fails on objects holding a lock, a socket or an open file — and it fails at copy time, not at construction time. Deleting a name while iterating the dict that holds it raises RuntimeError.

3.2 The LEGB rule and the two special scopes

A name is resolved by searching four namespaces in order — Local, Enclosing, Global, then Builtins — and the first match wins. Two of these are not what their names suggest: the comprehension scope is a genuine scope, and the class scope is not an enclosing scope at all.

import builtins

value = "global"
print(value)                     # G - found in globals

def outer():
    value = "enclosing"
    def middle():
        def inner():
            print(value)         # L: no; E: middle then outer -> found here
        return inner()
    return middle()
outer()

def uses_global():
    print(value)                 # L: no; E: no; G: found
uses_global()

def uses_builtin():
    print(len, print)            # L: no; E: no; G: no; B: found
uses_builtin()

print(hasattr(builtins, "value"))  # False - module globals are not builtins

A function body introduces a local scope for the whole body, decided at compile time. A name assigned anywhere in a function is local to the entire function, so reading it before the assignment is an UnboundLocalError even when a same-named global exists.

count = "module"

def reads_before_binding():
    print(count)                 # the assignment below makes `count` LOCAL,
    count = "local"              # so this raises at run time
reads_before_binding()
    # UnboundLocalError: local variable 'count' referenced before assignment

def correct():
    return count                 # no local assignment -> reads the global
print(correct())

# Blocks do NOT introduce scopes: if, for, while, with, try, match.
def blocks_do_not_scope():
    if True:
        inner = "visible"
    for _ in range(1):
        other = "also visible"
    return inner, other
print(blocks_do_not_scope())

Two scoping rules differ from what an experienced reader expects, and both follow from how the compiler builds the enclosing scopes.

# Difference 1: a comprehension body cannot read a class-body name,
# and that restriction survives PEP 709 deliberately.
class Config:
    debug = True
    options = ["a", "bb", "ccc"]
    # kept = [r for r in options if debug]   <-- NameError: name 'debug'
    _debug = debug                 # resolve it here, before the comprehension
    kept = [r for r in options if _debug]

print(Config.kept)                 # ['a', 'bb', 'ccc']

# Difference 2: a method body also skips the class scope entirely, which is
# exactly why the first parameter exists.
class Tally:
    total = 0
    def __init__(self): self.n = 0
    def add(self) -> int:
        total = self.n + 1         # a LOCAL named total, not Tally.total
        return total
    def add_to_class(self) -> None:
        type(self).total += 1      # the correct way to mutate a class attribute

t = Tally()
t.add(); t.add_to_class(); t.add_to_class()
print(Tally.total, t.n)            # 2 0

The restriction exists because a class body executes as ordinary top-level code while the nested scopes inside it are compiled ahead of time. Anything the body computes — methods, comprehensions, nested functions — cannot depend on a name that does not yet exist when its code object is compiled. Method bodies avoid the problem through the mandatory self parameter (section 5.1).

Python 3.13 (PEP 667) made these rules precise. frame.f_locals is now a write-through proxy rather than a snapshot, and mutating it independently — by assigning through frame.f_locals()[name] or by exec — no longer has any effect. Attempting it from a frame that does not own the proxy raises RuntimeError.

3.3 global, nonlocal and module state

Two statements change where a name is bound. global redirects a binding into the module namespace; nonlocal redirects it to the nearest enclosing function scope that already binds the name. Both are declarations and must precede any use of the name in the block.

counter = 0

def bump_global() -> None:
    global counter               # binding lives in this name's module
    counter += 1
bump_global()
print(counter)                    # 1

def read_global() -> int:
    return counter                # reading needs no declaration
print(read_global())

def make_accumulator():
    total = 0
    def add(n: int) -> int:
        nonlocal total           # bind in make_accumulator's scope
        total += n
        return total
    def peek() -> int:
        return total
    return add, peek

add, peek = make_accumulator()
add(5); add(7)
print(peek())                     # 12

nonlocal requires an existing binding in an enclosing function scope. Naming a module-level variable with nonlocal, or a name that exists nowhere, is a SyntaxError raised at compile time:

#   def outer():
#       def inner():
#           nonlocal module_level
#           # SyntaxError: no binding for nonlocal 'module_level' found
#       inner()
#   module_level = 1

Module-level state remains the correct home for genuinely process-wide configuration. The correct way to expose it is a frozen value object with a leading underscore on private fields and an explicit __all__.

# settings.py
from dataclasses import dataclass, field

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Settings:
    host: str = "127.0.0.1"
    port: int = 8080
    tags: tuple[str, ...] = field(default_factory=tuple)
    _secret: str | None = None      # private: not part of the public surface

__all__ = ["Settings", "configure"]

_ACTIVE: Settings | None = None

def configure(host: str | None = None, **kw) -> Settings:
    """Install process-wide settings and return the active Settings."""
    global _ACTIVE
    base = _ACTIVE or Settings()
    if host is not None or kw:
        _ACTIVE = Settings(host=host or base.host,
                           port=base.port, tags=base.tags)
        _ACTIVE = _ACTIVE.__class__(**{**_ACTIVE.__dict__}) if False else _ACTIVE
    return _ACTIVE

The awkward line above is deliberate evidence for the point: a global-mutating function is hard to write cleanly because the merge semantics between the existing value and the supplied keyword arguments are not expressible. Pass the whole object, or pass the override as a dataclass, and the function becomes a pure transformation.

Why globals are discouraged: a global declaration turns a name into a hidden parameter of every function in the module. Nothing in the signature advertises it, so the function cannot be tested in isolation, cannot be called concurrently without external locking, and cannot be reasoned about by reading its body. A function that accepts its state and returns a value is strictly more useful and strictly more testable.

3.4 Closures and late binding

A function defined inside another function may reference the enclosing function’s locals, and the compiler promotes those locals into cell variables. The resulting inner function is a closure, and it keeps those cells alive for as long as it exists.

def counter(start: int = 0):
    n = start
    def increment(step: int = 1) -> int:
        nonlocal n
        n += step
        return n
    def current() -> int:
        return n
    return increment, current

inc, cur = counter(10)
print(inc(), inc(5), cur())        # 11 16 16

# The closure mechanism is introspectable.
def make_adder(k: int):
    def add(x: int) -> int:
        return x + k
    return add

a3 = make_adder(3)
print(a3.__closure__[0].cell_contents)     # 3
print(a3.__code__.co_freevars)             # ('k',)
print(a3.__code__.co_cellvars)             # () - `k` is free, not a cell here

Every reference in a function body to a name bound in an enclosing function scope promotes that name to a cell variable, and the cell is shared by every closure over the same scope. That is the mechanism behind both the counter above and the following defect.

# DEFECT: late binding. All three functions share one `i` cell.
bad = [lambda: i for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in bad])                  # [3, 3, 3]

# FIX 1: bind the value now, with a default argument.
good_default = [lambda i=i: i for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in good_default])         # [0, 1, 2]

# FIX 2: bind now, in a factory function. Preferred - the intent is explicit.
def capture(i: int):
    return lambda: i
good_factory = [capture(i) for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in good_factory])         # [0, 1, 2]

# FIX 3: store the value in an object, so each instance has its own slot.
class Cell:
    def __init__(self, i: int): self.i = i
    def get(self) -> int: return self.i
print([c.get() for c in (Cell(i) for i in range(3))])    # [0, 1, 2]

The default= idiom works for the wrong reason: it is correct only because the default is evaluated in the enclosing scope at definition time. It also leaves a spurious parameter in the signature that the reader must mentally discount. A factory function or a class makes the binding explicit and survives a later change to the loop variable.

Applies to: nested functions only, and a lambda assigned in a loop is a nested function. Caveats: a closure keeps every cell it references alive, which is a real memory cost for a long-lived callback holding a large buffer. A closure that references self creates a reference cycle, and the cycle collector must break it, which delays release. functools.partial covers the “capture one argument” case without a nested def and is faster.

3.5 Modules, __name__ and the single-pass model

A module is a namespace held in a dictionary, executed exactly once per interpreter. The dictionary is populated by the module’s top-level statements in source order, and the module object is cached in sys.modules for the life of the process.

import sys

__name__          # the fully qualified module name, or "__main__"
__file__          # the path the module was loaded from
__spec__          # the import spec, None under some exec() forms
__package__       # the package for relative imports, "" at top level
__doc__           # the module docstring
__annotations__   # module-level annotations (lazily, PEP 649, Python 3.14)
__all__           # the names exported by `from module import *`

# The single-pass model: a module-level name must exist before it is read.
CONFIG_VALUE = 42                # define first ...

def read_later() -> int:
    return CONFIG_VALUE          # ... a function body resolves at call time

print(read_later(), CONFIG_VALUE)          # 42 42

# Swap those two statements and the module raises
#   NameError: name 'CONFIG_VALUE' is not defined
# at import time, on the last line.

# A second import does not re-execute the module.
import app                      # noqa: F401
print("app" in sys.modules)                 # True

The distinction between executed now and resolved at call time is the single most important fact about module-level code. It is why a function definition may precede the definition it depends on, and why a forward reference at module level is an error while the same reference inside a function body is not.

# app.py
def main() -> int:
    from config import SETTINGS          # deferred until called
    print("starting", SETTINGS)
    return 0

if __name__ == "__main__":               # the conventional guard
    raise SystemExit(main())

#   python app.py      -> __name__ == "__main__", main() runs,
#                         sys.path[0] is the script's own directory
#   python -m app      -> __name__ == "__main__", main() runs,
#                         sys.path[0] is the CWD, __spec__.name == "app"
#   import app         -> __name__ == "app",    main() does NOT run

Prefer python -m package.module to python path/to/module.py. The former puts the current working directory on sys.path and sets __package__ correctly, so absolute and relative imports both resolve. The latter puts the module’s own directory first, which is a frequent cause of an ImportError for a sibling module in the same package.

from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:                     # a type checker sees the real imports
    from .report import render

_LAZY = {"render": ("report", "render"), "parse": ("parser", "parse")}

def __getattr__(name: str):
    """PEP 562, Python 3.7: a module-level hook for lazy re-export."""
    if name in _LAZY:
        import importlib
        mod, attr = _LAZY[name]
        return getattr(importlib.import_module(f".{mod}", __package__), attr)
    raise AttributeError(f"module {__name__!r} has no attribute {name!r}")

def __dir__() -> list[str]:
    return sorted(list(globals()) + list(_LAZY))

Caveats: a circular import does not always raise. It silently binds a partially initialised module, and the failure surfaces later as an AttributeError on a name that "should" exist. The remedies, in order of preference: move the shared definitions into a third module; import the module rather than the name, so that a.b is read at call time rather than at import time; or use a module-level __getattr__ as above.

Exercises 3

  1. Demonstrate that a += 1 on a list mutates a shared object while on an int it rebinds, and explain the difference from the type’s perspective.
  2. Write a function that raises UnboundLocalError, then repair it two ways — by renaming the local, and by declaring global.
  3. Show that a comprehension and a method body both fail to read a class-body name, and document the mechanism that makes the method case work with self.
  4. Build a counter closure, then inspect __closure__, co_freevars and cell_contents at each step.
  5. Reproduce the late-binding defect from a lambda list, repair it with all three fixes from section 3.4, and time each.
  6. Create a two-module package that imports circularly, produce the partial-initialisation failure, and repair it twice.

4 · Functions

4.1 Definition, return and docstrings4.2 Parameter kinds and defaults4.3 Functions as values4.4 functools: partial, cache, dispatch4.5 Recursion and the recursion limit

A def statement creates a function object and binds it to a name. That object is a first-class value with attributes, so the parameter-passing rules, the callable protocol and the standard-library helpers for manipulating functions are all that separate a function from any other object.

4.1 Definition, return and docstrings

A def statement binds a function object at execution time. The body does not run then; the object is created with a reference to its code object, to the globals it will search, and to default values that have already been evaluated.

def greet(name: str, excited: bool = False) -> str:
    """Return a greeting for *name*.

    Args:
        name: Who to greet.
        excited: Append an exclamation mark.

    Returns:
        The formatted greeting.

    Raises:
        TypeError: If *name* is not a string.
    """
    if not isinstance(name, str):
        raise TypeError("name must be str")
    return f"Hello, {name}{'!' if excited else ''}"

print(greet("Ada", excited=True))
print(greet.__name__, greet.__qualname__)
print(greet.__doc__.splitlines()[0])
print(greet.__module__, greet.__defaults__)
print(greet.__annotations__["return"])

Reaching the end of a function body is equivalent to return None. That is not an error and is frequently correct; it becomes a problem only when a caller assumes a value.

import inspect

def no_return_path(x):
    if x > 0:
        return "positive"
    # falls off the end -> returns None

def explicit_none() -> None:
    return None                            # equivalent, and clearer

def undocumented(x):
    return x

print(no_return_path(-1), explicit_none())
print(type(no_return_path(-1)).__name__)   # NoneType
print(undocumented.__doc__)                # None

class Api:
    def call(self):
        """Summary line.

        Body paragraph, indented by the method.
        """

# getdoc() additionally dedents and cleans the raw docstring.
print(inspect.getdoc(Api.call).splitlines()[0])

There is no enforcement of any kind. A >>> line after a def is an IPython convention that never reaches the file, and help() formats whatever __doc__ holds. The convention that survives scrutiny is a one-line imperative summary, a blank line, then detail — the shape that renders correctly in a terminal and in generated documentation alike.

4.2 Parameter kinds and defaults

Python has five parameter kinds, distinguished by a marker in the signature. Two markers make a section positional-only or keyword-only, and two collect surplus arguments into a tuple and a dict. Defaults are evaluated once, at definition time, and that single fact is the origin of the mutable-default defect.

KindSyntaxReceivesCaller may
Positional-onlydef f(a, /)Positional arguments onlyPass positionally only
Positional-or-keyworddef f(a, b)EitherUse either form
Var-positionaldef f(*args)Surplus positional arguments, as a tuplePass any number
Keyword-onlydef f(*, k)Surplus keyword arguments, by namePass by name only
Var-keyworddef f(**kw)Surplus keyword arguments, as a dictPass any keyword
def render(path, /, *, mode: str = "r", encoding: str = "utf-8",
           **options) -> str:
    """*path* is positional-only; everything else is keyword-only."""
    return f"{path}|{mode}|{encoding}|{sorted(options.items())}"

print(render("a.txt", mode="rb", errors="ignore", newline=""))

try:
    render(path="a.txt")
except TypeError as e:
    print("TypeError:", e)                # path is positional-only

def collect(*args, **kw) -> None:
    print(type(args).__name__, args)
    print(type(kw).__name__, kw)
collect(1, 2, a=3, b=4)

# A bare star separator is legal with no var-positional parameter.
def strict(a, *, b): return a + b
print(strict(1, b=2))

Positional-only parameters are enforced at call time and exist so a name stays free to change. Built-ins adopted them in 3.8 for exactly that reason: dict.copy, str.replace and print now declare the arguments whose names are not part of the contract, and only the ones that are may be passed by keyword.

Defaults are evaluated once, when the def statement executes. A mutable default is therefore shared by every call for the life of the process, and an expensive default is paid for whether or not the argument is ever supplied.

# DEFECT: the list is created once and reused forever.
def append_wrong(item, bucket=[]):
    bucket.append(item)
    return bucket
print(append_wrong(1), append_wrong(2))     # [1] [1, 2]

# FIX 1: a None sentinel. The idiomatic solution.
def append_right(item, bucket=None):
    if bucket is None:
        bucket = []
    bucket.append(item)
    return bucket
print(append_right(1), append_right(2))      # [1] [2]

# FIX 2: an immutable default is safe to share.
def append_tuple(item, bucket=()):
    return bucket + (item,)
print(append_tuple(1), append_tuple(2))      # (1,) (1, 2)

# The evaluation-once rule is observable for an expensive default too.
calls = 0
def make_default() -> object:
    global calls
    calls += 1
    return object()

def expensive(default=make_default()):
    return default
expensive(); expensive()
print("default evaluated", calls, "time(s)")

# The default is frozen at definition time, whatever happens later.
limit = 10
def frozen(n: int, cap: int = limit) -> int:
    return min(n, cap)
limit = 99
print(frozen(50))                            # 10

inspect.signature reports the full contract, including the kinds the markers imply, and it is the correct tool for validating arguments inside a decorator or a framework hook.

import inspect

def api(a, b=1, *args, c, d=2, **kw): ...

sig = inspect.signature(api)
print(sig)
for name, p in sig.parameters.items():
    print(f"{name:6} {p.kind.name:24} default={p.default!r}")
print(sig.bind(1, 2, 3, c=4, e=5).arguments)
try:
    sig.bind(1)
except TypeError as e:
    print("TypeError:", e)
print(sig.replace(parameters=[p.replace(default=9) for p in sig.parameters.values()]))

4.3 Functions as values

A function is an object. It can be stored in a container, passed as an argument, returned from a function and given attributes. Higher-order functions are therefore ordinary code, and the map/filter/reduce trio is three specific built-ins rather than a language feature.

def double(x): return x * 2
def is_small(x): return x < 10

ops = {"double": double, "identity": lambda x: x}

def apply_all(values, fn):
    return [fn(v) for v in values]

def adder(k):
    def add(x): return x + k
    return add

print(apply_all([1, 2, 3], double))
print(adder(10)(5))
print([ops[name](n) for n in (1, 2)])

# A function object carries its own attributes.
double.calls = 0
double.calls += 1
print(double.calls, double.__name__, double.__qualname__)

# Any class with __call__ is a function-like value.
class Scale:
    def __init__(self, factor): self.factor = factor
    def __call__(self, x): return x * self.factor
print(list(map(Scale(3), [1, 2])), Scale(3).__call__(4))

from functools import reduce
import operator

data = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]

# The built-in trio: all lazy, all iterator-consuming.
print(list(map(double, data)))
print(list(filter(is_small, data)))
print(reduce(operator.add, data))                 # 15
print(reduce(lambda a, b: a * b, data))           # 120

# The comprehension equivalents, usually clearer and usually faster.
print([double(x) for x in data])
print([x for x in data if x < 10])
print(sum(data))

# map with several iterables zips them; the shortest one wins.
print(list(map(operator.add, [1, 2, 3], [10, 20])))       # [11, 22]

# map and filter are single-pass: the second traversal yields nothing.
m = map(double, data)
print(list(m), list(m))                        # [2, 4, 6, 8, 10] []
ConstructEquivalentPrefer the construct when
map(f, xs)[f(x) for x in xs]Never, for a single sequence: the comprehension avoids the intermediate iterator and is faster
filter(pred, xs)[x for x in xs if pred(x)]Never, for the same reason
reduce(f, xs)functools.reduce or a loopThere is no comprehension form; use reduce, or the C-level sum/min/max where they apply
map(f, xs, ys)[f(a, b) for a, b in zip(xs, ys)]Never: zip(..., strict=True) is strictly better and detects a length mismatch

Wrapping map or filter in list() is a correctness statement, not a style choice: the second traversal of the iterator object yields nothing, so a function that maps once and then inspects twice silently sees an empty sequence. In CPython a list comprehension with a local function is measurably faster, because it is a single loop with no per-item function-object lookup and no intermediate iterator.

4.4 functools: partial, cache, dispatch

The functools module holds the four utilities every Python codebase eventually needs: argument binding, decorator metadata preservation, memoisation and generic dispatch. All four are implemented in C, and each has version-specific availability.

from functools import partial, wraps, lru_cache, cache, reduce, total_ordering

def connect(host, port, *, timeout=5.0, retries=1) -> str:
    return f"{host}:{port} timeout={timeout} retries={retries}"

# partial binds leading positional arguments, or any subset by keyword.
fast = partial(connect, "db.internal", timeout=0.5)
print(fast.keywords, fast(5432, retries=3))
print(fast.func is connect, fast.args)
