Caveats: asyncio.Semaphore created before Python 3.10 bound its value at construction and silently accepted Semaphore(-1) as unbounded; both were fixed, and a negative value is now a ValueError. An unbounded asyncio.Queue in front of a slow consumer is a memory leak, not a buffer. Free-threaded builds: the queue’s internal counters are protected by locks in a 3.14 free-threaded build, so qsize() is a genuine snapshot rather than an approximation; the ordering guarantees of gather are unchanged.

6.6 Choosing the right tool

Four constructs produce a sequence: a list, a generator, a coroutine and a plain function. They differ in when work happens, whether the result can be re-read, whether other work can proceed meanwhile, and what happens on abandonment.

NeedConstructWork startsRe-iterableOther tasks progressAbandonment
A finite result read more than once, indexed or measuredList or list()At the callYesNoNothing to clean up
A large or unbounded result read onceGenerator function or (... for ...)On first next()NoNoGeneratorExit at the yield; use try/finally
Concurrent I/O with a bounded number of in-flight operationsasync def with TaskGroup and a SemaphoreOn schedulingResults are values, not iteratorsYesTask.cancel() → CancelledError
One blocking call inside an otherwise synchronous programPlain functionAt the callYesNo — the thread is blockedNothing to clean up
CPU-bound work, or a library that blocks the GILProcessPoolExecutorOn submissionYesYes, in another processExecutor shutdown
# All four shapes, side by side, on one data set.
from pathlib import Path
import asyncio
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor

LOG = Path("app.log")


def eager_count(path: Path) -> int:
    """Plain function. Reads the whole file; nothing is deferred."""
    return len(path.read_text(encoding="utf-8").splitlines())


def lazy_lines(path: Path) -> object:
    """Generator. Holds the file open until exhaustion or close()."""
    with path.open(encoding="utf-8") as handle:
        for line in handle:
            if line.strip():
                yield line


async def async_count(path: Path) -> int:
    """Coroutine. Uses the loop's default thread pool for the blocking read."""
    return await asyncio.to_thread(eager_count, path)


def cpu_bound(n: int) -> int:
    total = 0
    for k in range(n):
        total += k * k
    return total


async def main() -> None:
    print(eager_count(LOG))
    print(sum(1 for _ in lazy_lines(LOG)))
    print(await async_count(LOG))

    # CPU-bound work: threads do not help under a GIL; processes do.
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    with ProcessPoolExecutor(max_workers=2) as pool:
        results = await asyncio.gather(
            loop.run_in_executor(pool, cpu_bound, 3_000_000),
            loop.run_in_executor(pool, cpu_bound, 3_000_000),
        )
    print(results)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())

Four rules that resolve nearly every case. (1) Never block the loop. A time.sleep, a synchronous requests.get or a subprocess.run inside a coroutine stalls every other task; use asyncio.sleep, an async client, or asyncio.to_thread. (2) CPU-bound work belongs in a process, not a thread and not a coroutine; under a free-threaded 3.14 build threads become viable, but a process still avoids paying for interpreter-level parallelism inside the GIL-free build’s per-object locks. (3) Bound every fan-out with a semaphore. An unbounded gather over ten thousand coroutines that each open a socket will exhaust file descriptors before it exhausts memory. (4) Cancel cleanly or not at all: a finally that awaits during cancellation must be short, because the task is already in the process of being cancelled.

Applies to: this chapter’s guidance is written for CPython 3.14, including the production-ready free-threaded build. Caveats on other implementations: PyPy ships a GIL-reduced interpreter with a background thread for the asyncio loop; blocking calls stall the loop there too, so rule (1) holds universally. Requirements for the async path: every awaited coroutine must either complete or be cancellable, and no coroutine may block the loop. Caveats: asyncio.to_thread (3.9+) is the shortest correct bridge for one blocking call; it is not correct for a blocking call that must be cancelled, because a thread cannot be interrupted — the to_thread future is cancelled, but the thread keeps running to completion. Where cancellation of the underlying work matters, use a native async API or a process.

Exercises 6

  1. Implement a re-iterable Range3 class and a single-pass Range3Iter class with __iter__ and __next__, then show with list() twice that the first is repeatable and the second is not. Confirm iter(it) is it for the iterator.
  2. Write a generator that reads a file in fixed-size byte chunks, and prove with tracemalloc that peak memory is independent of file size. Then close the generator early and show the finally block still closes the handle.
  3. Build a three-stage generator pipeline that reads, normalises and yields, and confirm nothing executes before the first next() by printing a sentinel from each stage.
  4. Write a coroutine built on .send() that implements a two-way accumulator, then drive it with .throw() to simulate a mid-transaction abort, and with .close() to simulate abandonment. Record which cleanup ran in each case.
  5. Measure any and all over a generator that prints every element it yields, and confirm short-circuiting. Then demonstrate the empty-iterable identities: any([]) is False and all([]) is True.
  6. Compare list(map(f, xs)) with [f(x) for x in xs] and with list(f(x) for x in xs) over ten million elements using timeit, and account for the difference in the first two cases.
  7. Implement a TaskGroup pipeline of five stages connected by bounded asyncio.Queue objects, then cancel it from outside mid-run and verify every task reached a terminal state and every queue producer observed shutdown.
  8. Write an @asynccontextmanager that acquires a permit from a Semaphore(1) and a resource from a pool, and prove the permit is released when the body raises, using a with statement in the body to show the ordering of teardown.

7 · Decorators and Metaclasses

7.1 Function decorators7.2 Decorators with arguments and functools.wraps7.3 Class decorators7.4 Attribute interception and __slots__7.5 Class preparation hooks7.6 Metaclasses

A decorator is a callable that takes a callable and returns a callable. That is the whole specification, and everything in this chapter follows from it: the ordering rules, the need for functools.wraps, the class-creation hooks, and the fact that a metaclass is simply the type of a class. Read alongside section 5.1, which covers the attribute lookup these mechanisms intercept.

7.1 Function decorators

Decorated syntax is sugar for assignment. @d immediately above def f(...) binds f = d(f), and the decorated name is bound only after the decorator has returned. There is no runtime difference beyond that, and no special support from the compiler beyond the convenience.

# These two definitions produce an identically named, differently
# introspectable function object.

def plain(f):
    return f


@plain
def a() -> None: ...

def b() -> None: ...
b = plain(b)

print(a.__name__, b.__name__)       # 'a' 'b' -- plain returns the same object
print(a is plain(a))


# The real form: the decorator returns a NEW function.
import functools
import time
from typing import Callable, TypeVar, ParamSpec

F = TypeVar("F", bound=Callable[..., object])
P = ParamSpec("P")


def logged(func: "Callable[P, F]") -> "Callable[P, F]":
    """Wrap func so each call is timed. Returns a different object."""
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args: "P.args", **kwargs: "P.kwargs") -> object:
        start = time.perf_counter()
        try:
            return func(*args, **kwargs)
        finally:
            elapsed = (time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000
            print(f"{func.__name__} took {elapsed:.3f}ms")
    return wrapper  # type: ignore[return-value]


@logged
def work(n: int) -> int:
    """A function whose metadata must survive decoration."""
    return n * 2


print(work(21))
print(work.__name__, work.__doc__, work.__wrapped__ is not None)
print(work.__wrapped__ is not work)   # __wrapped__ points at the original

Decoration is evaluated bottom-up within a single stacked block, and applied outermost-last at call time. Both orderings matter and are routinely confused, so they are stated separately below.

def tag(name: str):
    def decorate(func):
        def wrapper(*a, **k):
            print(f"[{name}] enter")
            out = func(*a, **k)
            print(f"[{name}] exit")
            return out
        return wrapper
    return decorate


@tag("outer")
@tag("inner")
def work() -> None:
    print("body")


print("--- decoration order ---")
work()
print("--- construction order: inner applied first, outer second ---")
# The decorators are CALLED bottom-up: tag("inner") runs, then tag("outer").
# The resulting wrapper chain is outer(inner(work)), so at CALL time the
# outer wrapper's "enter" prints first and its "exit" prints last.

A decorator is applied once, at definition time, and the two orderings are distinct. Definition order is bottom-up: the decorator closest to def receives the raw function first. Call order follows the resulting nesting and is outermost-first on entry, innermost-first on exit. This is ordinary function composition; the only way it is surprising is that the decoration order is not visible in the source.

Decorators that lose metadata are the most common defect in the category. A wrapper is a new function object, and a new function object has a new __name__, a new __doc__ and a new __module__, all inherited from the wrapper’s own definition. Without functools.wraps, a traceback names wrapper, help() shows the wrapper’s docstring, and a framework that dispatches on __name__ or reads a signature sees the wrapper’s *args, **kwargs.

import functools
import inspect


def broken(func):
    def wrapper(*a, **k):
        return func(*a, **k)
    return wrapper


def fixed(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)          # copies 5 attributes, sets __wrapped__
    def wrapper(*a, **k):
        return func(*a, **k)
    return wrapper


def sample(x: int, y: str = "a") -> int:
    """A documented function with a real signature."""
    return len(y) + x


for deco in (broken, fixed):
    wrapped = deco(sample)
    print(deco.__name__, wrapped.__name__, str(inspect.signature(wrapped)))
    print("   doc:", (wrapped.__doc__ or "").strip()[:20])

# What functools.wraps actually copies.
print(sorted(functools.WRAPPER_ASSIGNMENTS))
print(functools.WRAPPER_UPDATES)      # ('__dict__',)
# __wrapped__ is what makes inspect.signature report the original.

functools.wraps is not optional for any decorator that wraps. It copies __module__, __name__, __qualname__, __doc__ and __annotations__, updates the wrapper’s __dict__ from the wrapped function’s, and sets __wrapped__. Because __wrapped__ is set, inspect.signature follows it and reports the original signature — which is what a validating framework needs, and which is also why a decorator that changes the calling convention must additionally override __signature__ explicitly, since the copied one will then be a lie.

Three standard-library decorators cover most legitimate cross-cutting needs, and each exists for a specific reason.

DecoratorModulePurposeCost at call timeNotes
@functools.cache3.9+Memoise on the argument tupleO(1) dictionary lookup plus a tuple hashUnbounded; see the staleness failure in section 4.4
@functools.singledispatch3.4+Dispatch on the first argument’s typeA registry cache lookupThe correct generic-function tool
@propertybuilt-inTurn a method into an attributeA function call plus descriptor protocolSee section 5.2
@functools.cached_property3.8+Memoise a property in the instance dictO(1) after the first readNon-data descriptor; needs a mutable instance dict
@dataclasses.dataclass3.7+Generate __init__, __repr__, __eq__None beyond the generated methodsSee section 5.4
@functools.total_ordering3.4+Derive the other comparison operators from oneNoneRedundant as of 3.12 for enum; a documented trap elsewhere

Applies to: all implementations. Requirements for a wrapping decorator: call the wrapped function exactly as many times as the wrapper is called unless the decorator is explicitly documented as caching or retrying; forward positional and keyword arguments unchanged; and return the result unless the contract says otherwise. Caveats: a decorator that changes the return type is a decorator that needs a different name. A decorator applied to a staticmethod or classmethod in the wrong order — @staticmethod above the custom decorator rather than below — produces a staticmethod wrapping a function, which no longer binds. A decorator applied to a generator function that is not itself generator-aware turns the result into a generator object returned from a normal function, which still iterates correctly but breaks yield from delegation and asyncio type detection.

7.2 Decorators with arguments and functools.wraps

A decorator that takes arguments is three nested functions: the outer builds a decorator, the middle is the decorator, and the inner is the wrapper. Each level adds a frame that persists only for the duration of one application, and the naming convention — deco, decorator, wrapper — exists to keep the call sites readable.

import functools
import time
from typing import Any, Callable, TypeVar

F = TypeVar("F", bound=Callable[..., Any])


def retry(times: int = 3, delay: float = 0.0,
          exceptions: tuple = (Exception,)) -> "Callable[[F], F]":
    """Retry the wrapped callable. The outer call returns a decorator."""

    def decorator(func: F) -> F:
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args: Any, **kwargs: Any) -> Any:
            last: BaseException | None = None
            for attempt in range(1, times + 1):
                try:
                    return func(*args, **kwargs)
                except exceptions as exc:
                    last = exc
                    if attempt < times and delay:
                        time.sleep(delay)
            raise last            # re-raise the final failure, not None

        return wrapper  # type: ignore[return-value]

    return decorator


@retry(times=3, delay=0.01, exceptions=(KeyError,))
def unstable(cache: dict, key: str) -> int:
    """Fails until the third attempt."""
    if key not in cache:
        cache[key] = 0
    if not hasattr(unstable, "_n"):
        unstable._n = 0
    unstable._n += 1
    if unstable._n < 3:
        raise KeyError(key)
    return cache[key]


print(unstable({}, "a"), unstable._n)
print(unstable.__name__, unstable.__wrapped__.__name__)

The three-level shape is unavoidable because the syntax is fixed: @retry(times=3) is an expression that must evaluate to a single callable before unstable exists. The outer function is called immediately with times=3, so it cannot possibly wrap anything; it returns the decorator, which is then called with the function, so it cannot possibly be the wrapper; the wrapper is the only one that ever sees the arguments of the decorated call.

# Stacked decorators with arguments: order is still bottom-up at
# definition time and outermost-first at call time.

def trace(label: str):
    def deco(func):
        @functools.wraps(func)
        def inner(*a, **k):
            print(f"  [{label}] call")
            return func(*a, **k)
        return inner
    return deco


def once(func):
    """Run at most once; later calls return the first result."""
    sentinel = object()
    cache = [sentinel]

    @functools.wraps(func)
    def inner(*a, **k):
        if cache[0] is sentinel:
            cache[0] = func(*a, **k)
        return cache[0]
    return inner


@trace("A")
@once
@trace("B")
def compute(n: int) -> int:
    print("  body")
    return n * 10


print(compute(2))
print(compute(2))
print("--- second call ---")
print(compute(3))
# Definition order: trace("B") then once then trace("A").
# Nesting: A(once(B(compute))).
# First call:  A traces, once runs -> B traces, body runs.
# Second call: A traces, once short-circuits -> B never traced, body skipped.

A decorator that must remain transparent to the type checker should be typed with ParamSpec, which preserves the exact parameter list of the wrapped callable through the wrapper. Without it, every decorated function is typed as Callable[..., Any] and the checker stops verifying the body.

# A fully typed wrapping decorator. Run mypy --strict on this file.

from __future__ import annotations
import functools
from collections.abc import Callable
from typing import ParamSpec, TypeVar

P = ParamSpec("P")
R = TypeVar("R")


def audited(func: Callable[P, R]) -> Callable[P, R]:
    """Preserve the signature exactly: same parameters, same return type."""
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args: P.args, **kwargs: P.kwargs) -> R:
        return func(*args, **kwargs)
    return wrapper


@audited
def parse(raw: str, base: int = 10) -> int:
    return int(raw, base)


reveal_type(parse)                  # (raw: str, base: int =) -> int
print(parse("ff", base=16))

Applies to: ParamSpec since 3.10; Concatenate since 3.10; TypeVarTuple since 3.11 — see section 9.3 for the full generic-parameter treatment. Requirements: a decorator that adds a parameter must be typed with Concatenate, because ParamSpec alone cannot express "the same parameters plus one". Caveats: a decorator written before 3.10 must fall back to Callable[..., R], which silently disables checking of the decorated function’s arguments; a # type: ignore on the return wrapper line is the conventional cost. functools.wraps requires the wrapped object to have __name__; applying it to a class works because classes have one, and applying it to a functools.partial object raises AttributeError since 3.12 strictness tightened — use functools.update_wrapper with assigned=() if that is genuinely required.

7.3 Class decorators

A class decorator receives a class object, which is an ordinary callable, and returns whatever it likes. A decorator that returns the class unchanged is an inspection pass; one that returns a replacement is a factory in disguise. The distinction is invisible at the call site and must be documented, because the two have different observable behaviours on subclassing and on isinstance.

import functools
from typing import Any, TypeVar

T = TypeVar("T", bound=type)


def add_repr(cls: T) -> T:
    """Inspection pass: returns the SAME class object."""
    fields = sorted(
        n for n in vars(cls)
        if not n.startswith("_") and isinstance(vars(cls)[n], (int, float, str))
    )
    def __repr__(self) -> str:
        inner = ", ".join(f"{n}={getattr(self, n)!r}" for n in fields)
        return f"{type(self).__name__}({inner})"
    cls.__repr__ = __repr__          # type: ignore[method-assign]
    return cls


@add_repr
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x: int, y: int) -> None:
        self.x, self.y = x, y


print(repr(Point(1, 2)))


# A validating decorator: the class is not usable until it is valid.
def validate(cls: T) -> T:
    """Wrap __init__ so post-construction invariants are checked."""
    original = cls.__init__

    @functools.wraps(original)
    def __init__(self: Any, *args: Any, **kwargs: Any) -> None:
        original(self, *args, **kwargs)
        cls.__post_validate__(self)

    cls.__init__ = __init__          # type: ignore[method-assign]
    return cls


@validate
class Interval:
    def __init__(self, low: float, high: float) -> None:
        self.low, self.high = low, high

    def __post_validate__(self) -> None:
        if self.low > self.high:
            raise ValueError(f"low {self.low} exceeds high {self.high}")

    def __repr__(self) -> str:
        return f"Interval({self.low}, {self.high})"


print(Interval(0.0, 1.0))
try:
    Interval(5.0, 1.0)
except ValueError as exc:
    print("ValueError:", exc)

The registration pattern is the other major use, and it is the one that a plugin system, a serialisation framework and a command dispatch table all share.

# Registry pattern: a decorator as a side-effecting registrar.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Callable

REGISTRY: dict[str, type] = {}


def register(name: str) -> Callable[[type], type]:
    """A decorator whose primary job is its side effect, not its return."""
    def decorator(cls: type) -> type:
        if name in REGISTRY:
            raise ValueError(f"duplicate registration: {name}")
        REGISTRY[name] = cls
        cls.registry_name = name      # type: ignore[attr-defined]
        return cls
    return decorator


class Handler:
    """The abstract base. Not itself registered."""
    registry_name: str = ""
    def handle(self, payload: str) -> str:
        raise NotImplementedError


@register("upper")
class UpperHandler(Handler):
    def handle(self, payload: str) -> str:
        return payload.upper()


@register("reverse")
class ReverseHandler(Handler):
    def handle(self, payload: str) -> str:
        return payload[::-1]


print(sorted(REGISTRY))
h = REGISTRY["reverse"]()
print(h.handle("python"), h.registry_name)

try:
    @register("upper")
    class Again(Handler): ...
except ValueError as exc:
    print("ValueError:", exc)

Applies to: dataclass(slots=True, frozen=True) returns a new class object rather than mutating the original, which makes the class decorator form the only place where a frozen or slotted transformation can be applied to an already-declared class. typing.dataclass_transform (3.11+) is a decorator for decorator authors: applied to a custom @dataclass-like decorator, it tells a static checker to apply dataclass semantics to whatever the decorated class would have produced, so field annotations are understood even though the checker did not generate the methods. Requirements: a class decorator that mutates in place must return the class; one that returns a replacement must be documented, and callers who wrote @dec above a class they also intend to subclass by name must be aware that the name is bound to the return value. Caveats: a class decorator cannot add a __slots__ entry to a class that already has instances, and adding __slots__ to a class that inherits from a non-slotted class has no memory benefit because the base still supplies __dict__.

7.4 Attribute interception and __slots__

Attribute access is dispatched through __getattribute__, which every type inherits from object and which almost no code should override. __getattr__ is different: it is called only after normal lookup has failed, and is the correct hook for a fallback.

The lookup order for obj.name is: a data descriptor on the type; then the instance dictionary; then a non-data descriptor or a plain class attribute, searching the MRO; and finally type(obj).__getattr__ if it is defined. __getattr__ is the last resort, and the reason it is so widely used for delegation is precisely that it fires only when nothing else matched.

# __getattr__ is the fallback: called only when normal lookup fails.

class Lazy:
    def __init__(self, **values: int) -> None:
        self._values = values

    def __getattr__(self, name: str) -> int:
        # Reached for EVERY name, including dunders looked up by the
        # interpreter and names misspelled by the caller.
        try:
            return self._values[name]
        except KeyError:
            raise AttributeError(
                f"{type(self).__name__!r} object has no attribute {name!r}"
            ) from None


o = Lazy(a=1, b=2)
print(o.a, o.b)
try:
    o.missing
except AttributeError as exc:
    print("AttributeError:", exc)
print("a" in o.__dict__, "_values" in o.__dict__)

# has() and getattr() with a default both depend on raising AttributeError.
print(hasattr(o, "nope"), getattr(o, "nope", "default"))
print(getattr(o, "a", "absent"))         # 'a' is found by __getattr__: 1
print(getattr(o, "b", "absent"))         # 'b' is found by __getattr__: 2
print(getattr(o, "zzz", "absent"))       # __getattr__ raises: the default

The raise ... from None in the example above is not cosmetic. A KeyError propagating out of __getattr__ produces a chained traceback whose first frame is a dict lookup, which is noise. More importantly, the exception type is part of the protocol: hasattr, getattr with a default, copy.copy, pickle and every getattr(obj, "__something__", None) idiom in the standard library test for AttributeError specifically. A __getattr__ that raises something else breaks all of them.

# __getattribute__ runs on EVERY access. Overriding it is expensive
# and rarely correct; override __getattr__ where that will do.

class Audited:
    def __getattribute__(self, name: str) -> object:
        # object.__getattribute__ must be called explicitly, or this
        # recurses to the recursion limit.
        value = object.__getattribute__(self, name)
        if not name.startswith("_"):
            print(f"  read {name}")
        return value

    def __init__(self) -> None:
        object.__setattr__(self, "_ready", True)

    def __setattr__(self, name: str, value: object) -> None:
        print(f"  set {name}")
        object.__setattr__(self, name, value)

    def __delattr__(self, name: str) -> None:
        print(f"  del {name}")
        object.__delattr__(self, name)


a = Audited()
a.value = 1
print(a.value)
del a.value
print(object.__getattribute__(a, "_ready"))

__slots__ removes the instance dictionary, which removes __dict__, which makes __getattr__ both more useful and more dangerous: a __getattr__ that references self.__dict__ raises AttributeError from inside itself, and a __setattr__ that stores into self.__dict__ fails for the same reason. Slotted classes must keep their state in slot names, and a __getattr__ must not assume a dictionary exists.

# A proxy that delegates everything, and the __slots__ constraint.

class _Service:
    def ping(self) -> str:
        return "pong"


class Proxy:
    __slots__ = ("_target",)

    def __init__(self, target: object) -> None:
        # object.__setattr__ bypasses any custom __setattr__; with __slots__
        # a plain assignment is equally correct here.
        object.__setattr__(self, "_target", target)

    def __getattr__(self, name: str) -> object:
        return getattr(object.__getattribute__(self, "_target"), name)

    def __repr__(self) -> str:
        t = object.__getattribute__(self, "_target")
        return f"Proxy({type(t).__name__})"


p = Proxy(_Service())
print(p.ping(), p)                     # delegates and does not crash
print("__dict__" in dir(Proxy), Proxy.__slots__)

# A slotted class with a __getattr__ that touches __dict__ breaks.
class Slotted:
    __slots__ = ("a",)
    def __getattr__(self, name: str) -> object:
        return self.__dict__[name]     # AttributeError, recursively

try:
    Slotted().b
except AttributeError as exc:
    print("AttributeError:", exc)

Two __getattr__ failure modes that surface far from their cause. (1) The dunder probe. The interpreter looks up __wrapped__, __name__, __iter__ and a dozen other special names on objects it merely inspects. A __getattr__ that eagerly imports, opens a file or starts a network call will be entered by all of them, once per probe, and often during a failure report. (2) The infinite recursion. If __getattr__ reads an attribute that does not exist, it re-enters __getattr__ until RecursionError. Always access state through object.__getattribute__(self, ...) or self._field where _field is guaranteed to exist, and initialise every field __getattr__ might reach for before anything can ask for it.

Applies to: __getattr__, __getattribute__, __setattr__, __delattr__ and __slots__ are language-level and identical in CPython, PyPy and free-threaded builds. Requirements for __setattr__: a frozen or validated type must call object.__setattr__ to store, and a __slots__ type must not assign to a name that is not a slot. Caveats: __slots__ accepts a single string as a shorthand for a one-element tuple, and a __slots__ entry whose name duplicates a class attribute is a ValueError at class creation. A class with __slots__ and no __dict__ cannot be weak-referenced unless __weakref__ is added to __slots__. __getattr__ is consulted for every failed lookup including on the class itself, which is why a module-level __getattr__ exists as a documented lazy-import feature — see section 8.6.

7.5 Class preparation hooks

Python calls two hooks on the class being defined, in a fixed order, before the class statement’s body finishes binding: __init_subclass__, which runs on the parent, and __set_name__, which runs for every descriptor created in the class body. A third, __class_getitem__, controls subscripting of the class object itself.

The order within a class statement is: the metaclass __prepare__ produces the namespace; the body executes and binds names into it; __set_name__ is called for every namespace value that defines it; the metaclass __new__ and __init__ build the class; and only then does __init_subclass__ run on the nearest base. The two orderings that matter most are that __set_name__ runs before the class exists, and that __init_subclass__ runs after it does.

from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Any, ClassVar

# __set_name__ runs on the descriptor, during class creation, with the
# attribute name it was bound to. It cannot use the class.
class Field:
    def __set_name__(self, owner: type, name: str) -> None:
        self.public_name = name
        self.storage_name = f"_{name}"
        self.owner_name = owner.__name__       # available: the namespace

    def __get__(self, obj: object, objtype: type | None = None) -> object:
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return getattr(obj, self.storage_name, None)

    def __set__(self, obj: object, value: object) -> None:
        setattr(obj, self.storage_name, value)


class Model:
    registry: ClassVar[dict[str, type]] = {}
    id = Field()                      # __set_name__ sees the name "id"

    def __init_subclass__(cls, /, *, table: str | None = None,
                          **extra: Any) -> None:
        super().__init_subclass__(**extra)
        cls.table = table or cls.__name__.lower()
        Model.registry[cls.__name__] = cls
        print(f"  registered {cls.__name__} -> table {cls.table}")


# __set_name__ has already run, so the descriptor knows its own name.
print(Model.id.public_name)           # 'id'
print(Model.id.storage_name)          # '_id'
print(Model.id.owner_name)            # 'Model': the namespace, not an instance


class User(Model, table="users"): ...


class Audit(Model, table="audit_log"): ...


print(sorted(Model.registry))         # ['Audit', 'User']
print(User.table, Audit.table)        # users audit_log


# The descriptor is shared with every subclass and keeps reporting the
# BASE class's attribute name. Only a new Field() in the subclass body
# produces a different public_name.
class Timestamped(Model):
    created = Field()                 # a second, independent descriptor

print(Timestamped.created.public_name)  # 'created'
print(Timestamped.id.public_name)       # 'id': the same Field instance

Two facts in that block are worth separating. __set_name__ is the only hook through which a descriptor learns the attribute name it was bound to, and it fires during class creation — before any instance exists, and before __init_subclass__ runs. A decorator applied to a class body has therefore already run by the time __set_name__ fires, and cannot rely on that name unless it reads it back from the descriptor afterwards. The second fact is that a descriptor declared in a base is shared: Timestamped.id and Model.id are the same object, permanently bound to the name "id".

# A clean registry built on __init_subclass__, with keyword
# configuration passed through the class statement's own header.

from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Any, ClassVar, Iterator


class Plugin:
    """Base class. Importing a module that defines a subclass registers it."""

    registry: ClassVar[dict[str, type[Plugin]]] = {}
    kind: ClassVar[str] = "generic"

    def __init_subclass__(cls, /, kind: str | None = None,
                          **extra: Any) -> None:
        super().__init_subclass__(**extra)
        if kind is not None:
            cls.kind = kind
        if cls.__name__ in Plugin.registry:
            raise ValueError(f"plugin {cls.__name__} already registered")
        Plugin.registry[cls.__name__] = cls

    def run(self, value: str) -> str:
        raise NotImplementedError


class Reader(Plugin, kind="reader"):
    def run(self, value: str) -> str:
        return f"read:{value}"


class Writer(Plugin, kind="writer"):
    def run(self, value: str) -> str:
        return f"wrote:{value}"


def discover(base: type = Plugin) -> Iterator[type[Plugin]]:
    for name, cls in sorted(base.registry.items()):
        if cls is not base:
            yield cls


for cls in discover():
    print(cls.__name__, cls.kind, cls().run("x"))


# A grandchild IS registered: __init_subclass__ is looked up on the parent,
# and Reader inherits Plugin's, so it is found.
class LoudReader(Reader, kind="reader"): ...


print("LoudReader" in Plugin.registry, LoudReader.kind)
print(len(Plugin.registry))

Applies to: __init_subclass__ since 3.6 and implicitly made a classmethod, with cls bound to the new subclass; the explicit / in the signature is conventional and documents that cls is not a normal first parameter. __set_name__ since 3.6. Requirements: every keyword in the class header after the bases must be consumed by __init_subclass__, or forwarded through super().__init_subclass__(**extra), or the class statement raises TypeError. Caveats: __init_subclass__ is looked up on the parent, so a class that defines its own and does not call super() silently severs the chain for its own subclasses, and a grandchild registered through an inherited hook is indistinguishable in the registry from a direct child. A base that inspects cls.__dict__ sees only what the subclass body has bound so far, because the hook runs while the body is still executing. __set_name__ is not called for a descriptor that is not in the class body namespace, including one added to the class after creation — in that case the descriptor has no public_name and its __get__ will raise AttributeError. A descriptor created in a base class is bound to the base's attribute name and is shared by all subclasses; it never receives the subclass’s name.

7.6 Metaclasses

Every class is an instance of some class, and that class is its metaclass. For a class declared with no explicit metaclass, the metaclass is chosen from the bases: the most derived metaclass among them, failing that type. A metaclass is therefore an ordinary class with ordinary methods, and overriding __call__, __new__ or __init__ on it intercepts class creation, not instance creation.

from typing import Any


class Meta(type):
    """A metaclass. Instances of Meta are classes."""

    def __new__(mcls, name: str, bases: tuple, ns: dict,
                **kw: Any) -> "Meta":
        # ns is the class namespace: methods, class attrs, __qualname__,
        # __module__, and the slot descriptors created by __slots__.
        print(f"  creating {name} with {len(ns)} namespace entries")
        return super().__new__(mcls, name, bases, ns, **kw)

    def __init__(cls, name: str, bases: tuple, ns: dict, **kw: Any) -> None:
        super().__init__(name, bases, ns, **kw)
        print(f"  initialised {name}")

    def __call__(cls, *args: Any, **kwargs: Any) -> object:
        # Intercepts Foo(...) -- construction of an INSTANCE of cls.
        print(f"  calling instance of {cls.__name__}")
        return super().__call__(*args, **kwargs)

    def __prepare__(mcls, name: str, bases: tuple, **kw: Any) -> dict:
        # Runs BEFORE the class body. The returned mapping receives it.
        ns = super().__prepare__(name, bases, **kw)
        print(f"  preparing {name}")
        return ns

    def __repr__(cls) -> str:
        return f"<class {cls.__module__}.{cls.__qualname__}>"


class Widget(metaclass=Meta):
    def __init__(self, n: int) -> None:
        self.n = n


class SubWidget(Widget):              # inherits the metaclass
    pass


w = Widget(3)
print(type(Widget), type(SubWidget), isinstance(w, Widget))
print(repr(Widget))
print(Widget.__mro__)

The five hooks in the example are the entire practical surface, and they fire in a strict order: Meta.__prepare__ before the body, Meta.__new__ after the body, then Meta.__init__, then — on the first Widget(...) — Meta.__call__. __call__ on a metaclass is not instance construction; it is the call that creates an instance of the class, and it delegates to type.__call__, which itself calls __new__ then __init__ as described in section 5.1.

# __prepare__ controls the namespace mapping. An OrderedDict gives
# definition order, which is why pre-3.6 code needed it.
import collections
from typing import Any


class Ordered(type):
    def __prepare__(name: str, bases: tuple, **kw: Any) -> dict:
        return collections.OrderedDict()


class Config(metaclass=Ordered):
    a = 1
    b = 2
    c = 3


print([k for k in vars(Config) if not k.startswith("__")])
print(list(vars(Config)))
print(Config.__prepare__.__qualname__)


# The metaclass is chosen from the bases, and a conflict is a TypeError.
class MA(type): pass
class MB(type): pass

class Both1(MA, MB): ...              # MRO: Both1, MA, MB, type, object
print(Both1.__mro__)

# Two unrelated metaclasses have no most-derived candidate, so the class
# statement fails at the import line and names neither library's intent.
try:
    class Both2(MA, metaclass=MB): ...
except TypeError as exc:
    print("TypeError:", type(exc).__name__)
    print("  ->", str(exc)[:64])


# abc.ABCMeta is a shipped metaclass: abstractness is a creation-time
# concern, which is why it works with __slots__ and with ABCs.
import abc

class AbstractThing(abc.ABC):
    __slots__ = ()
    @abc.abstractmethod
    def go(self) -> None: ...

print(type(AbstractThing).__name__, AbstractThing.__abstractmethods__)
try:
    AbstractThing()
except TypeError as exc:
    print("TypeError:", str(exc)[:60])
RequirementCorrect toolIncorrect toolWhy
Intercept instantiation of subclasses__init_subclass__Metaclass __call__A metaclass is inherited and would apply to the base as well
Reject instantiation of incomplete subclassesabc.ABC + @abstractmethodMetaclassShipped, tested, and composes with __slots__
Add methods to every subclass of a baseMix-in base classMetaclassExplicit, greppable, and inherited predictably
Intercept an attribute read that failed__getattr__ on the classMetaclass __getattr__Applies to instances of the class, not to the class object
Register subclasses in a registry__init_subclass__Metaclass __new__The registry data does not belong to the type hierarchy
Intercept the class body before it executesMetaclass __prepare__Nothing elseThere is no other hook that runs first

Why a metaclass is almost always the wrong tool. (1) It is the most powerful hook in the language with the least readable cost: the reader must know that type is involved to follow what happens on a class statement, and nothing in the syntax signals it. (2) It is inherited implicitly, so a metaclass added for one subclass silently changes the creation of every subclass of that subclass. (3) It conflicts: two libraries that each want a metaclass on a common base produce a TypeError at import time that names neither library’s intent. (4) It duplicates existing machinery — abc, enum.EnumMeta, EnumType, Protocol's runtime machinery and dataclass all use one, and all of them are more thoroughly tested than anything a project would write. Reach for a metaclass when the requirement is genuinely "before the class body executes", which in practice means namespace control or a namespace-level transformation nothing else offers.

Applies to: abc.ABCMeta since 3.4; enum.EnumMeta, renamed EnumType in 3.11 and aliased in both directions. Implementation divergence: on PyPy, abc and enum still use their documented metaclasses, so type(x) is EnumMeta and type(x) is ABCMeta hold there too; a custom metaclass written against CPython’s type.__prepare__ contract is not exercised by PyPy’s test suite and should be verified separately. Free-threaded builds: a metaclass is a shared object, so a mutable class-level counter incremented in Meta.__new__ is a genuine data race in a 3.14 free-threaded build; use a lock or an atomic counter. Caveats: __prepare__ must return a mapping, and returning anything else is a TypeError at class creation. A metaclass that returns a non-class from __new__ makes the class statement bind that object, and everything downstream that assumes a class will then fail in a confusing place.

Exercises 7

  1. Write a @logged decorator with functools.wraps, apply it twice to the same function, and show with inspect.signature and __doc__ that the metadata survives; then remove wraps and record the four attributes that are lost.
  2. Write a @memo(maxsize) decorator with arguments using the three-nested-def shape, implement a real eviction, and prove the eviction order with a maxsize of 2 and five distinct keys.
  3. Stack three decorators with arguments over one function and predict the printed call order from the source before running it, then confirm or correct the prediction.
  4. Write a class decorator that registers a handler in a module-level dict, rejects a duplicate name with a ValueError, and demonstrates that a subclass created with @handler("name") is a different name if the decorator returns a subclass rather than the original.
  5. Implement __getattr__ on a lazy module proxy, and enumerate the six attribute names the interpreter probes on an object during a failing repr(), showing each one entering __getattr__.
  6. Write a descriptor using __set_name__ that rejects an unrecognised value, then subclass the class that uses it and show that the descriptor reports the base class’s attribute name rather than the subclass’s.
  7. Build a registry on __init_subclass__ that accepts kind= and priority= keyword arguments, and prove that an unrecognised keyword raises TypeError at the class statement rather than later.
  8. Write a metaclass that records the order in which classes are created in a list, subclass a class that already has a metaclass, and show that __init_subclass__ alone reproduces the same registry with no metaclass at all.

8 · Modules, Packages and the Import System

8.1 Modules, packages and the import machinery8.2 Namespace packages and __path__8.3 __all__ and re-export conventions8.4 Circular imports8.5 Execution once, caching and reloading8.6 Plugins, entry points and dynamic import

The import system is a documented, four-stage pipeline with a cache, a finder, a loader and a spec object. It is also the source of two failures that appear in almost every large codebase — the circular import and the import-time side effect. This chapter describes the machinery precisely, then treats both failures. The single-pass execution model described in section 3.5 is assumed throughout.

8.1 Modules, packages and the import machinery

A module is a namespace in a dictionary, backed by a file whose name is the module’s name. A package is a module whose namespace contains __path__, which tells the import system where to look for its submodules. The import of a dotted name is resolved one component at a time, from the left.

Four stages run in order, and each is replaceable: find asks the meta-path finders for a ModuleSpec; load asks the spec’s loader to produce the module; execute runs the module’s code in its own namespace; and cache binds the result into sys.modules. The default finder chain is the built-in finder, the path finder, and the frozen importer; sys.meta_path is where a third-party finder inserts itself.

import importlib
import importlib.util
import sys

# The three import forms, and what each binds.
import os                       # binds the module object in this namespace
import os.path                  # binds os, and ensures os.path is imported
from os import path             # binds the submodule object as `path`
from os import path as p       # binds it under a different local name
import xml.etree.ElementTree    # binds `xml` only
from xml.etree import ElementTree  # binds the leaf module

print(os is sys.modules["os"])
print(p is os.path, p is sys.modules["os.path"], path is p)
print("xml.etree.ElementTree" in sys.modules)
print(xml is sys.modules["xml"], ElementTree is sys.modules["xml.etree.ElementTree"])
print(ElementTree.fromstring("<a/>").tag)

# Everything a module object exposes.
mod = sys.modules["os"]
print(type(mod), mod.__name__, mod.__package__)
print(mod.__spec__.name, mod.__spec__.origin)
print(mod.__spec__.loader)
print(mod.__loader__ is mod.__spec__.loader)

# A module's namespace is a plain dict, and it is mutable.
print(sorted(k for k in vars(mod) if k.startswith("_"))[:6])

The rule that governs the three forms is narrow and worth memorising: import a.b.c binds the leftmost component, from a.b import c binds the rightmost. This is why import xml.etree.ElementTree followed by ElementTree.fromstring(...) is a NameError, and why the correct spelling is from xml.etree import ElementTree. The bound name is not a convenience; it is the only name that exists.

# Relative imports: the number of leading dots is the level, and the
# level is resolved against __package__ -- never against the cwd.
#
#   pkg/__init__.py
#   pkg/core.py        -> from . import core          (same package)
#   pkg/sub/__init__.py
#   pkg/sub/deep.py    -> from .. import core        (parent package)
#                      -> from ...core import x      is only legal when
#                         pkg is itself a package on the path root's
#                         parent, and is almost never what was meant

# Absolute vs relative is a style decision with a correctness cost. The
# three forms below are a syntax reference: the relative ones name
# siblings of `pkg`, so they are shown rather than executed.
#
#   from collections.abc import Iterable   # absolute: a stdlib module
#   from .utils import normalise          # relative: pkg/utils.py
#   from ..shared import constant         # relative: the parent package
#   from ...top import thing              # three dots: rarely intended

# `from __future__ import annotations` is NOT a relative import. It is a
# compiler directive and must be the first statement in the file:
#
#   from __future__ import annotations
#
# In 3.14 it is redundant for most purposes: PEP 649 already defers
# annotation evaluation (see section 9.1). It remains the portable
# spelling for code that must also run on 3.9 to 3.13.

print(__package__ is not None)

The two genuinely useful programmatic entry points are find_spec, which answers "can this be imported, and from where" without importing it, and util.module_from_spec, which creates an uninitialised module from a spec. Every dynamic-import idiom in the standard library is built from those two.

import importlib.util
import sys

# find_spec resolves the parent package, so it is not free of side effects.
spec = importlib.util.find_spec("json.encoder")
print(spec.name, spec.origin is not None, spec.loader)
print(spec.submodule_search_locations)          # None: not a package

# Building a module by hand, without executing anything.
spec2 = importlib.util.find_spec("base64")
mod = importlib.util.module_from_spec(spec2)
print("base64" in sys.modules)                  # False: not registered
print(hasattr(mod, "b64encode"))               # False: not executed
sys.modules["base64"] = mod
spec2.loader.exec_module(mod)                   # now it is executed
print(mod.b64encode(b"hi"))

# A genuinely synthetic module, with no file behind it.
synthetic = types.ModuleType("synthetic")
synthetic.answer = 42
synthetic.__doc__ = "Created in memory, never on disk."
print(synthetic.answer, synthetic.__spec__)

Applies to: the import system is specified in PEP 451 (3.3) and extended by PEP 690 (3.13) for ModuleSpec.origin and related fields. importlib.resources supersedes the older pkg_resources and pkgutil.get_data for reading packaged data files. Requirements: a module that is intended to be imported must be reachable from sys.path, which means the package must be installed, be on the path as a source checkout, or be the directory containing the top-level package. Running a script by path puts that script’s directory on sys.path, not the project root, which is the cause of most "works in the shell, fails in tests" import failures. Caveats: from package import name first tries an attribute of the package, then tries a submodule, so a submodule whose name collides with a package attribute — for example a submodule named __version__ — resolves to the attribute and the submodule is never imported. import a.b when a is already in sys.modules but a.b is not still executes a.b; the cache is per fully-qualified name, not per root package.

8.2 Namespace packages and __path__

A regular package has an __init__.py and therefore has a single module object with real code in it. A namespace package has no __init__.py; it is a bare directory, and the import system fabricates a module whose only content is a __path__ with several entries. It exists so that two distributions can each contribute a subdirectory of the same top-level package.

import importlib.util
import tempfile
from pathlib import Path

with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
    root = Path(tmp)

    # Two independent roots, each contributing a subdirectory of `app`.
    (root / "vendor_a" / "app" / "plugins").mkdir(parents=True)
    (root / "vendor_b" / "app" / "handlers").mkdir(parents=True)
    (root / "app").mkdir()                        # no __init__.py: namespace
    (root / "vendor_a" / "app" / "plugins" / "__init__.py").write_text("", encoding="utf-8")
    (root / "vendor_b" / "app" / "handlers" / "__init__.py").write_text("", encoding="utf-8")

    import sys
    sys.path[:0] = [str(root / "vendor_a"), str(root / "vendor_b"), str(root)]

    try:
        import app
        print(type(app).__name__)                 # module
        print(app.__file__)                        # None: a namespace package
        print(app.__spec__.origin)                 # 'namespace'
        print(app.__path__)                        # a _NamespacePath, not a list
        print(len(app.__path__))                   # two contributions

        spec = importlib.util.find_spec("app.plugins")
        print(spec.origin)                         # .../vendor_a/app/plugins/__init__.py
        print(spec.submodule_search_locations is not None)   # a real package
    finally:
        sys.path[:3] = []
        for name in [k for k in list(sys.modules) if k == "app" or k.startswith("app.")]:
            del sys.modules[name]

A namespace package is a real package and a directory at the same time, which is the source of both its utility and its traps. Its __path__ is a _NamespacePath object that recomputes itself whenever sys.path changes, so a namespace package tracks later path modifications; a regular package’s __path__ is a plain list fixed at creation. The failure mode is that sys.path order decides which contribution wins: if two installed distributions both provide app/plugins, the first on the path takes it and the second is silently invisible, with no warning at any level.

# __path__ is a normal list on a regular package, and it is writable.
# Editing it is legal, discouraged, and occasionally necessary.
import os
import os.path

print(isinstance(os.path, list), len(os.path.__path__))
os.path.__path__.insert(0, "/extra/roots/go/here")
print(os.path.__path__[0])
os.path.__path__.pop(0)

# A zip archive on sys.path is a real import root: the zipimporter
# provides both a finder and a loader for a path prefix.
import zipfile
import tempfile
import sys

with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
    zpath = os.path.join(tmp, "lib.zip")
    with zipfile.ZipFile(zpath, "w") as z:
        z.writestr("shaded/__init__.py", "NAME = 'shaded'\nVERSION = '1.0'\n")
        z.writestr("shaded/core.py", "def run():\n    return 'from a zip'\n")

    sys.path.insert(0, zpath)
    try:
        import shaded
        import shaded.core
        print(shaded.NAME, shaded.core.run())
        print(shaded.__file__)                  # '.../lib.zip/shaded/__init__.py'
        print(shaded.core.__loader__.__class__.__name__)   # zipimporter
    finally:
        sys.path.remove(zpath)
        for name in [k for k in list(sys.modules) if k.startswith("shaded")]:
            del sys.modules[name]

Three namespace-package defects that are invisible until production. (1) Editable installs and namespace packages do not mix. A pip install -e of a namespace package requires the build backend to emit the correct .pth; with several editable namespace packages installed at once the path entries can shadow one another, and the shadowing is order-dependent. (2) A namespace package has no __init__.py, so it has no code: no attributes, no __all__, no module docstring, and __version__ must be supplied by a submodule, which forces from app import __version__ to work through the submodule fallback. (3) Because the path is recomputed, installing a distribution after import time makes new submodules visible but does not make new attributes of the namespace module itself visible, since the module object was already created.

Applies to: namespace packages are specified in PEP 420 (3.3). zipimport is implemented as a finder plus loader pair on the sys.path_hooks list; zipimport.zipimporter is the loader class. Requirements: a namespace package must not have an __init__.py in any contributing root, and every root must be a plain directory on sys.path. Caveats: a zipped package cannot be written to, cannot contain a C extension that must be loaded from the filesystem, and its resource access goes through zipfile.Path rather than pathlib, so code using importlib.resources.files() returns different types under a zip. Tooling note: type checkers and IDEs resolve namespace packages differently from the runtime; an unresolved-import diagnostic in a tool but a working runtime is a tooling limitation, not a code defect, and the fix is a py.typed marker plus a correct packages declaration rather than adding an __init__.py that would convert it to a regular package.

8.3 __all__ and re-export conventions

__all__ is a module-level list of strings that overrides what from module import * exports, and nothing else. It does not restrict from module import name, does not restrict module.name, and is not consulted by any other part of the language. Its second, more valuable use is as a declaration of a module’s public surface for readers, linters and type checkers.

# A package facade: the only module a consumer should import.
# mylib/__init__.py

"""Public interface of mylib. Import from here, not from submodules."""

from .core import Engine, EngineError
from .config import Settings, load_settings
from ._version import __version__

__all__ = [
    "Engine",
    "EngineError",
    "Settings",
    "load_settings",
    "__version__",
]


# Without __all__, `from mylib import *` would also export the submodule
# objects `core`, `config` and `_version`, and every name the re-exported
# modules themselves export. __all__ makes the surface explicit and is
# what a linter reads to decide which names are public.

# What the two forms actually bind.
import types
m = types.ModuleType("demo")
exec("__all__ = ['a', 'b']\na = 1\nb = 2\n_hidden = 3\nc = 4\n", m.__dict__)
print(sorted(n for n in m.__dict__ if not n.startswith("__") and n != "_hidden"))
ns: dict = {}
exec("from demo import *", {"__builtins__": __builtins__}, ns)
print(sorted(k for k in ns if not k.startswith("__")))

The exec construction in that example is the honest way to demonstrate the rule: without __all__, from demo import * binds every name in the module’s namespace that does not begin with an underscore — here that includes a, b, c and _hidden is excluded only because of the underscore. With __all__ present, only the listed names are bound, including names that begin with an underscore, and excluding public names that were not listed.

# The `as` re-export idiom, and why the redundant alias exists.
#
#   from .core import Engine as Engine
#
# is not a mistake and not redundant. It is the signal that tells a type
# checker (and a reader) that this name is re-exported deliberately, so
# the name is not reported as unused. Ruff, mypy's --no-implicit-reexport
# and pyright's reportPrivateImport all key off it.

# mylib/facade.py -- an explicit, checked facade.
from .core import Engine as Engine
from .core import EngineError as EngineError
from .config import Settings as Settings
from .config import load_settings as load_settings

__all__ = ["Engine", "EngineError", "Settings", "load_settings"]

# The alternative: a module-level __getattr__ for lazy re-export. The name
# is resolved on first access, and the submodule import is deferred.
from typing import Any

_LAZY: dict[str, str] = {
    "Engine": ".core",
    "EngineError": ".core",
    "Settings": ".config",
    "load_settings": ".config",
}


def __getattr__(name: str) -> Any:
    """PEP 562 module-level __getattr__: lazy attribute resolution."""
    if name in _LAZY:
        import importlib
        module = importlib.import_module(_LAZY[name], __name__)
        value = getattr(module, name)
        globals()[name] = value          # cache: runs only once
        return value
    raise AttributeError(f"module {__name__!r} has no attribute {name!r}")


def __dir__() -> list:
    """Keep introspection and tab-completion honest alongside __getattr__."""
    return sorted(set(globals()) | set(_LAZY))

Applies to: PEP 484 defines __all__ as the re-export declaration; PEP 562 (3.7) adds the module-level __getattr__ and __dir__. Requirements: a lazy __getattr__ must raise AttributeError for every name it does not handle, and must write the resolved value into globals() so the second access is a plain lookup. A lazy __getattr__ that does neither turns every hasattr probe into an import attempt. Caveats: a module-level __getattr__ makes the module’s interface invisible to a static checker that does not read __all__, so a lazy facade must still declare __all__, and for the best inference should additionally use if TYPE_CHECKING: imports. from module import * inside a function is a SyntaxError in Python 3, so the construct is only usable at module scope — which is where the shadowing it causes is also least likely to be noticed. Free-threaded builds: a lazy __getattr__ that mutates globals() without a lock can run twice concurrently under PEP 703; both computations are idempotent here, but a non-idempotent one is not safe.

8.4 Circular imports

A circular import fails for exactly one reason: the single-pass execution model. A module’s body runs top to bottom, so a name must exist before it is read at module level. In a cycle, one of the two modules is always read before it has been written, and the failure is a NameError or ImportError raised from whichever module happened to be entered second.

# BEFORE -- a cycle between two modules in one package.
#
#   analytics/report.py          analytics/queries.py
#   --------------------------   -------------------------
#   from .queries import fetch    from .report import render
#   def summarise(): ...         def fetch(): ...
#
# Import analytics.report first:
#   1. report starts executing, binds the name `fetch` from .queries
#   2. queries starts executing, tries `from .report import render`
#   3. sys.modules['analytics.report'] exists but is only PARTIALLY
#      executed: `render` is not bound yet
#   4. ImportError: cannot import name 'render' from partially
#      initialized module 'analytics.report'
#
# The error names the symptom, not the cause: nothing in the message
# mentions the cycle.

# AFTER -- option 1: invert the dependency. The lowest-level module
# imports nothing from the layer above it.
#
#   analytics/queries.py   -- no import of report
#   analytics/render.py    -- imports from queries
#   analytics/report.py    -- imports from queries and from render
#
# Option 2: defer the import to the point of use, inside the function.
def summarise(period: str) -> str:
    # Deferred: the cycle is broken because analytics.render is fully
    # executed by the time summarise is called. The cost is an import
    # statement executed on every call, which sys.modules makes cheap.
    from .render import render
    return render(period)


# Option 3: break the cycle for the type checker only, with a guard.
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:                      # False at runtime, True to mypy/pyright
    from .render import render as RenderFn
else:                                  # the runtime sees this instead
    RenderFn = object


def render_period(period: str) -> str:
    fn = RenderFn                      # a real function at runtime
    return fn(period)                  # a type error, caught statically

A TYPE_CHECKING guard is a checker technique, not a runtime repair. It makes the cycle invisible to the type checker while leaving it entirely intact at runtime, so the module-level NameError still happens. It is correct only in the third position in the list above — when the real cycle has already been broken structurally and the guard exists to avoid importing a module purely for a type.

# A worked before/after that runs. The cycle is real, in one process.
import sys
import types


def make_modules() -> tuple:
    """Build the before-case: a genuine cycle that fails on import."""
    a = types.ModuleType("cycle_a")
    b = types.ModuleType("cycle_b")
    src_a = "from cycle_b import helper\nVALUE_A = 1\n"
    src_b = "from cycle_a import VALUE_A\nhelper = lambda: VALUE_A\n"
    a.__dict__.update(__name__="cycle_a", __file__="cycle_a.py")
    b.__dict__.update(__name__="cycle_b", __file__="cycle_b.py")
    sys.modules["cycle_a"], sys.modules["cycle_b"] = a, b
    return a, b, src_a, src_b


a, b, src_a, src_b = make_modules()
try:
    exec(compile(src_a, "cycle_a.py", "exec"), a.__dict__)
except ImportError as exc:
    print("ImportError:", type(exc).__name__)
    print("  partially initialised:", "partially initialized" in str(exc))
finally:
    del sys.modules["cycle_a"], sys.modules["cycle_b"]


# AFTER -- restructure so the dependency is acyclic. `core` knows nothing
# about `ui`; `ui` knows about `core`. A DAG needs no guards.
import types

core = types.ModuleType("demo_core")
ui = types.ModuleType("demo_ui")
core.__dict__.update(__name__="demo_core")
exec("def total(values):\n    return sum(values)\n", core.__dict__)
ui.__dict__.update(__name__="demo_ui", core=core)
print(ui.core.total([1, 2, 3]))

Four legitimate fixes, in order of preference. (1) Restructure — move the shared type or the shared constant into a third, lower-level module that neither side imports the other from. This is the only fix that removes the coupling. (2) Invert — if the cycle exists because a low-level module needs something from a high-level one, the high-level module is depending on the wrong abstraction; pass the value in as a parameter instead. (3) Local import — move the import inside the function or method that needs it; legal, cheap, and the standard pragmatic answer when the cycle is genuinely an artefact of convenience rather than of design. (4) TYPE_CHECKING guard — only for a cycle that exists purely in the type graph, and only after the runtime cycle is already gone. Never: wrapping the import in a bare try/except ImportError and continuing. That converts a loud failure into a NameError later, in a different file, at a different time.

Applies to: the failure mode is a consequence of the single-pass model and therefore identical in every implementation. Requirements: a module imported by two others must be importable on its own; the most reliable way to test that is a test file that imports nothing but the module in question. Caveats: a cycle through a package rather than through modules is not a cycle at all — from pkg import a in pkg/__init__.py is legitimate as long as pkg/a.py does not import from pkg itself, because pkg is already in sys.modules and partially executed by design. The partially initialized module message names the module that was entered second, which is not necessarily the module whose author created the cycle; the cycle is symmetric and blame is symmetric, so the fix belongs in whichever of the two modules has the higher-level responsibility. Detection: a plugin architecture, or any two packages that must not know about each other, is best protected by a test that asserts the import graph is acyclic — for example by parsing every module’s ast and asserting a topological order exists.

Use TYPE_CHECKING for types, and a function-local import for values. The two techniques solve the same circular-import symptom and are not interchangeable. A local import defers a value the module needs at run time; a TYPE_CHECKING guard removes a type the checker needs and the runtime does not. Applying the guard to a real runtime dependency produces a NameError; applying a local import to a type leaves the checker blind to the annotation.

8.5 Execution once, caching and reloading

The first import of a module executes its body and stores the resulting module object in sys.modules under its fully-qualified name. Every subsequent import returns that same object without re-executing anything. The cache is checked before the finders run, which is why a stale module is impossible to refresh without importlib.reload.

import sys
import types

counter = types.ModuleType("demo_counter")
counter.__dict__.update(__name__="demo_counter", value=0)
sys.modules["demo_counter"] = counter

# First import: the finder is consulted and the body runs.
import demo_counter
print(demo_counter.value)
demo_counter.value += 1

# Second import: the cache is consulted first. No finder, no execution.
import demo_counter
print(demo_counter.value, demo_counter is sys.modules["demo_counter"])

# sys.modules is the cache, and it is an ordinary mutable dict.
print(type(sys.modules).__name__, len(sys.modules) > 100)
print("json" in sys.modules, "definitely_not_a_module" in sys.modules)
print(sys.modules["json"] is __import__("json"))

# __import__ vs importlib.import_module: which name is bound.
returned = __import__("xml.etree.ElementTree", fromlist=["ElementTree"])
print(returned.__name__)          # 'xml' -- the ROOT package
import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET2
import importlib
print(importlib.import_module("xml.etree.ElementTree") is ET2)
print(returned is not ET2, returned is sys.modules["xml"])

del sys.modules["demo_counter"]

# importlib.reload: re-executes the body INTO the existing module object.
# The object identity is preserved; the namespace is NOT cleared.
import sys
import types
import importlib

m = types.ModuleType("live")
m.__dict__.update(__name__="live", kept="preserved", v=1)
sys.modules["live"] = m

# Give it a file so reload has something to read.
import tempfile
from pathlib import Path

with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
    p = Path(tmp) / "live.py"
    p.write_text("v = 2\nadded = 'new'\n", encoding="utf-8")

    # Reload needs __spec__ with a real origin.
    import importlib.machinery
    spec = importlib.machinery.ModuleSpec("live", None, origin=str(p))
    spec.loader = importlib.machinery.SourceFileLoader("live", str(p))
    m.__spec__ = spec
    m.__loader__ = spec.loader

    before = m
    importlib.reload(m)
    print(m is before)             # True: the same object
    print(m.v, m.added)            # 2 new
    print(m.kept)                  # 'preserved': reload does not clear
    print(sorted(k for k in vars(m) if not k.startswith("__")))

del sys.modules["live"]

Four properties of reload matter in practice. The module object identity is preserved, so existing references to it stay valid and see the new attributes. The namespace is not cleared, so a name deleted from the file survives the reload — which is why reload is unreliable for a module that has had names removed. Objects that captured the old values — subclasses, decorators already applied, entries in a functools.singledispatch registry — are not updated, so a reloaded module can leave a half-old, half-new object graph. And reload does not reload the module’s imports; a dependency that also changed must be reloaded explicitly, bottom-up. Its legitimate uses are interactive development, module_forcing in a test harness, and a plugin reloader; it is not a production configuration mechanism.

# Import-time side effects: the thing that makes a library untestable.
import sys
import types

# A module whose body connects to a database. Importing it, in a test,
# in a CLI --list, or in a documentation build, performs the connection.
side = types.ModuleType("demo_side")
exec(
    "import sys\n"
    "print('  [side effect] opening the connection pool')\n"
    "POOL = object()\n"
    "print('  [side effect] running the migration check')\n",
    side.__dict__,
)
sys.modules["demo_side"] = side
print("POOL is set:", hasattr(side, "POOL"))

# The repair, in full. The body declares; a separate function connects.
clean = types.ModuleType("demo_clean")
exec(
    "POOL = None\n"
    "def connect(dsn: str):\n"
    "    global POOL\n"
    "    print('  connecting:', dsn)\n"
    "    POOL = object()\n"
    "    return POOL\n",
    clean.__dict__,
)
print("import is silent:", clean.POOL is None)
clean.connect("host=localhost dbname=app")
print("explicit:", clean.POOL is not None)

The four side effects that must never appear at module level in a library. (1) Opening a network or database connection — it makes the module unimportable in an offline environment, including in a test suite that never touches it. (2) Reading a configuration file or an environment variable that must exist — the module becomes order-dependent within the process, so a test that sets the variable after importing gets the old value from a cached module. (3) Registering signal handlers — the handler cannot be unregistered, and a test that imports the module twice has one handler that no test can remove. (4) Registering with a global framework, plugin registry or atexit handler — the registration is idempotent only by accident, and it runs on every import in every process that imports the module, including the one running the tests.

Applies to: importlib.reload since 3.4 and unchanged in 3.14; importlib.invalidate_caches since 3.3 is the companion call, and is required after adding a directory to sys.path at run time because the path finders cache directory contents with a timestamp. Requirements for a reloadable module: a real __spec__.origin and a working __loader__; a module created by types.ModuleType or by exec with no file has neither and cannot be reloaded. Caveats: deleting a name from sys.modules and re-importing produces a different module object, so every object that already holds a reference to the old one keeps it; that is a memory leak and a correctness hazard in a long-running process, and it is why sys.modules manipulation is a testing tool rather than a lifecycle tool. sys.modules entries whose value is None are a deliberate, documented convention meaning "this import is known to fail" — the import system raises ImportError for a None entry rather than attempting the import, which is how a package makes an optional dependency genuinely optional.

8.6 Plugins, entry points and dynamic import

Two mechanisms make a package extensible at run time: a module-level __getattr__ (PEP 562), which resolves a name by importing a submodule on first access, and entry points, which let a distribution advertise an object under a named group for a consumer to discover. The first is intra-package laziness; the second is inter-distribution extensibility.

# A lazy facade module: every name is a deferred submodule import.
#
#   mylib/__init__.py
#
from typing import Any

_SUBMODULES: dict[str, str] = {
    "Engine": ".engine",
    "Settings": ".settings",
    "compile_query": ".query",
}

__all__ = sorted(_SUBMODULES) + ["__version__"]

__version__ = "2.1.0"            # a real attribute, resolved immediately


def __getattr__(name: str) -> Any:
    """Import the owning submodule the first time `name` is read."""
    if name in _SUBMODULES:
        import importlib
        module = importlib.import_module(_SUBMODULES[name], __name__)
        value = getattr(module, name)
        globals()[name] = value              # cache: the next read is direct
        return value
    raise AttributeError(f"module {__name__!r} has no attribute {name!r}")


def __dir__() -> list:
    return sorted(set(globals()) | set(_SUBMODULES))


# The consequences, stated precisely:
#   * `import mylib` does not import mylib.engine. Import time is O(1).
#   * `mylib.Engine` triggers exactly one import, then an attribute read.
#   * `mylib.Engine is mylib.Engine` is True: both read the cached global.
#   * `from mylib import Engine` also works, via the same __getattr__.
#   * `hasattr(mylib, "Nonexistent")` is False and imports nothing.
#   * A circular import between the facade and a submodule is still a
#     cycle: the facade's globals() is incomplete until the submodule
#     finishes, and a submodule that does `from mylib import X` at module
#     level will not find it.

# The checker-side companion: annotations and re-exports must be visible
# to mypy and pyright, which do not execute __getattr__.
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from .engine import Engine
    from .settings import Settings
    from .query import compile_query

# Entry points: the packaging side of a plugin contract.
#
#   [project.entry-points."mylib.formats"]
#   csv  = "mylib_csv:Reader"
#   json = "mylib_json:Reader"
#
# The consumer never imports the plugin modules; it asks the packaging
# metadata which objects implement the contract.
from importlib.metadata import entry_points, version, PackageNotFoundError


def discover(group: str = "mylib.formats") -> dict:
    """Return {name: object} for every advertised entry point.

    Nothing is imported until the attribute is read: EntryPoint.load()
    is a lazy attribute lookup on the object returned here.
    """
    found: dict = {}
    for ep in entry_points(group=group):        # 3.10+ keyword form
        try:
            found[ep.name] = ep.load()
        except Exception as exc:                # a broken plugin is data
            print(f"  plugin {ep.name!r} failed to load: "
                  f"{type(exc).__name__}: {exc}")
    return found


def available(group: str = "mylib.formats") -> list:
    """Names only. No plugin code executes. Safe on a hostile path."""
    return sorted(ep.name for ep in entry_points(group=group))


# Asking the metadata for a version is the portable way to do it.
try:
    print("mylib version:", version("mylib"))
except PackageNotFoundError:
    print("mylib is not installed as a distribution")

# The two APIs, and why the modern one is used:
#   entry_points(group=...)     3.10+, returns EntryPoints, no deprecation
#   entry_points()["group"]     3.8/3.9, returns a plain dict; calling the
#                               result selects ALL entry points across all
#                               groups, which is a common and severe bug
print(hasattr(entry_points(group="console_scripts"), "select"))

# A complete registry over dynamically imported modules, with the
# failure policy made explicit. This is the shape a plugin system takes.
import importlib
import pkgutil
from dataclasses import dataclass
from types import ModuleType
from typing import Callable, Protocol


class Formatter(Protocol):
    name: str
    def render(self, rows: list[dict]) -> str: ...


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Loaded:
    name: str
    factory: Callable[..., Formatter]
    error: str | None = None


def scan(namespace: str) -> tuple[dict[str, Loaded], list[Loaded]]:
    """Import every submodule of `namespace` and collect its factories."""
    ok: dict[str, Loaded] = {}
    bad: list[Loaded] = []

    for info in pkgutil.iter_modules(importlib.util.find_spec(namespace).__path__):
        full = f"{namespace}.{info.name}"
        try:
            module: ModuleType = importlib.import_module(full)
        except Exception as exc:              # never let one plugin abort
            bad.append(Loaded(info.name, None, f"{type(exc).__name__}: {exc}"))
            continue

        factory = getattr(module, "create", None)
        if not callable(factory):
            bad.append(Loaded(info.name, None, "no callable `create`"))
            continue
        ok[full] = Loaded(info.name, factory)
    return ok, bad


# The contract, documented once:
#   A plugin module must define a module-level callable named `create`
#   taking no required arguments and returning an object with `.name`
#   and `.render(rows)`. Anything else is a configuration error and is
#   reported, not raised.

# Two conforming plugins, discovered by the scan above.
import types as _t
_csv = _t.ModuleType("formats.csv")
exec("def create():\n    return Csv()\n"
     "class Csv:\n    name = 'csv'\n"
     "    def render(self, rows):\n        return '\\n'.join(str(r) for r in rows)\n",
     _csv.__dict__)
sys.modules["formats.csv"] = _csv
_fmt = _t.ModuleType("formats")
_fmt.__path__ = []                            # pretend it is a package
_fmt.__spec__ = importlib.machinery.ModuleSpec("formats", None, is_package=True)
sys.modules["formats"] = _fmt

try:
    good, broken = scan("formats")
    for name, item in sorted(good.items()):
        print(name, "->", item.factory().name)
finally:
    for k in [k for k in list(sys.modules) if k.startswith("formats")]:
        del sys.modules[k]

The except Exception around import_module is the load-bearing line of that block, and it is a deliberate policy statement: a plugin is third-party code, one of them will raise something unexpected on import, and the process must survive it. The pkgutil.iter_modules walk is the older discovery mechanism and requires the namespace to be importable as a package; entry points are the better contract because the plugin advertises itself through packaging metadata rather than through a directory layout the consumer must guess.

Applies to: importlib.metadata replaced importlib_metadata in the standard library in 3.8; the keyword form entry_points(group=...) is 3.10+ and the dict form is deprecated since 3.10 and removed in 3.12. EntryPoint.load is lazy; EntryPoint.load() imports the target module. Requirements: a plugin must be an installed distribution; entry-point discovery reads the metadata of installed distributions and will not find a module that merely exists on sys.path. Caveats: entry points are read at call time and are not cached by the standard library, so available() on a hot path re-reads every distribution’s metadata; cache the result. A module-level __getattr__ facade does not remove a genuine circular import, because the facade’s globals() is incomplete while the submodule executes. pkgutil.iter_modules requires find_spec to return a spec with submodule_search_locations, which a namespace package provides and a bare module does not.

Exercises 8
