  1. Create a two-level package on disk with __init__.py in each directory, import it with all four spellings, and print __name__, __package__, __spec__.name and __path__ from both modules.
  2. Write a relative import that crosses one level up and one that crosses two, and show in each case that __package__ is what determines the resolution rather than the file’s location on disk.
  3. Build a namespace package from two sys.path roots, print __path__ before and after adding a third root, and show that _NamespacePath recomputes while a regular package’s __path__ does not.
  4. Write a facade module with a module-level __getattr__ over three submodules, then measure len(sys.modules) before and after import facade to prove nothing was loaded, and again after first attribute access to prove it was.
  5. Reproduce a circular import with two modules created by exec and registered in sys.modules, catch the ImportError, and then break the cycle three ways: by inverting the dependency, by a function-local import, and by a TYPE_CHECKING guard — showing that the third does not fix the runtime failure.
  6. Import a module that appends to a list at module level, then import it again and show the list has one entry; then use importlib.reload and show the list has two, and that an attribute deleted from the file survives the reload.
  7. Write a plugin contract module with a create() factory, plus one conforming plugin and one that raises on import, and show that scan reports the failure without propagating it.
  8. Enumerate the entry points in the console_scripts group, load exactly one of them, and confirm that entry_points() without a group returns all groups rather than the one requested.

9 · Typing and Static Analysis

9.1 The typing module and annotation evaluation9.2 Union, Literal, Final and friends9.3 Type variables and generics9.4 PEP 695 type parameter syntax9.5 TypedDict, Protocol, TypeGuard and TypeIs9.6 Checkers, configuration and library markers

Type hints are documentation that a program reads. Python does not enforce them; a static checker does, and the checker’s value is bounded by the precision of what the annotation actually says. This chapter covers the typing surface that a professional codebase uses, the PEP 695 syntax that replaced most of the boilerplate, and the configuration that makes a checker useful on a real project.

9.1 The typing module and annotation evaluation

Everything in the type system except the four special forms — Annotated, TypeAlias, TypeGuard, TypeIs, LiteralString — lives in typing. collections.abc and re are also recognised as type sources. All of it is available in typing for compatibility, and importing from collections.abc is the modern spelling for the abstract base classes.

Until Python 3.13, an annotation was an ordinary expression evaluated eagerly at the point it was written, in the scope where it appeared. That had two consequences: a forward reference had to be a string literal, and an annotation that named an expensive object paid for it at import time. Python 3.14 changes this: PEP 649 and PEP 667 make annotation evaluation deferred and lazy, through the __annotate__ function on the defining object.

import sys
import typing
from typing import get_type_hints

# PEP 649: an annotation is compiled into a function named __annotate__
# on the module or class, and is evaluated only when something asks.
print("Python:", sys.version_info[:2])
print(hasattr(sys.modules[__name__], "__annotate__"))

# Forward references no longer need to be quoted.
class Node:
    def link(self, other: Node | None) -> Node:      # 3.14: legal
        return other or self

# The classic form still works, and is still required before 3.14.
class Legacy:
    def link(self, other: "Legacy | None") -> "Legacy":
        return other or self

# Forcing evaluation: get_type_hints resolves every forward reference.
print(get_type_hints(Node.link)["other"])
print(get_type_hints(Legacy.link)["other"])


# What deferred evaluation buys: the cost is not paid at import time.
import time

def build() -> "Expensive":
    print("  building the annotation object")
    return list


class Deferred:
    def f(self, x: build()) -> None: ...      # not evaluated at def time

print("class body done; nothing built yet")
t0 = time.perf_counter()
hints = get_type_hints(Deferred.f)
print(f"resolved in {(time.perf_counter() - t0) * 1000:.3f}ms -> {hints}")


# __annotations__ is now a lazy view, not a dict.
print(type(Deferred.__annotations__).__name__)
print(Deferred.__annotations__)
try:
    Deferred.__annotations__["x"] = str
except Exception as exc:
    print(type(exc).__name__, "- the mapping is not writable")


# The portable spelling for 3.9 to 3.13 code, and still legal in 3.14.
from __future__ import annotations          # must be the first statement

print(typing.get_origin(list[int]) is list)
print(typing.get_args(dict[str, int]))
print(typing.get_type_hints is not None)

The important consequence of PEP 649 is not the syntax; it is that an annotation may now name something that does not exist yet without being a string, and that a circular reference between two modules' annotations no longer requires quoting. The compatibility cost is that a checker that only reads the AST must be taught about __annotate__; mypy and pyright both are, for 3.14. The from __future__ import annotations directive is now redundant in most code but remains the only spelling that works on every supported version, so a library with a 3.9 floor should keep it.

Any is the escape hatch that erases checking. It is not "no type"; it is "the checker will accept anything here and give up on the expression’s type from this point outward", and the effect propagates through every call that touches it.

from typing import Any


def bad(x: Any) -> int:
    return x + 1                 # no error: Any silences the checker


def worse(x: Any) -> None:
    first = x                    # x: Any
    second = x + 1               # Any
    third = x.attribute          # Any -- no attribute error either
    print(first, second, third)


def honest(x: object) -> int | str:
    # object forces a narrowing decision, which is the point.
    if isinstance(x, int):
        return x + 1
    if isinstance(x, str):
        return x * 2
    raise TypeError(f"unsupported: {type(x).__name__}")


print(honest(1), honest("ab"))

# A precise shape instead of Any, when the shape is what matters.
from typing import TypedDict


class Row(TypedDict):
    id: int
    name: str


def total(rows: list[Row]) -> int:
    return sum(r["id"] for r in rows)     # r["id"] is int, not Any


print(total([{"id": 1, "name": "a"}, {"id": 2, "name": "b"}]))
# total([{"id": "1"}])   -> a checker error: str is not int, and "name"
#                           is missing. With list[dict[str, Any]] both
#                           defects are invisible.

Applies to: PEP 649 and PEP 667 are 3.14 features; on 3.13 and earlier, annotations are evaluated eagerly and a forward reference must be quoted. typing.get_type_hints, get_origin and get_args are unchanged. Requirements: any code that reads cls.__annotations__ and expects a dict must be updated for 3.14, where it is a lazily-evaluated mapping; typing.get_type_hints(cls) is the portable accessor. Caveats: on a free-threaded 3.14 build the __annotate__ function is memoised per object, and the memoisation is protected, but a class whose __annotate__ has side effects will still run them exactly once regardless of thread count. Any is not equivalent to object for a checker: object rejects every operation without narrowing, and Any accepts every operation without checking. Cross-reference: the object-based exhaustiveness pattern in section 1.4 is the runtime half of the same discipline.

Read annotations as documentation written by the author, and choose object over Any by default. The two differ in what the checker does next: object rejects every operation until the code narrows, which forces a decision; Any accepts every operation and ends the checking of the whole expression. Any belongs in exactly one place — the signature of a function that parses untrusted input — and from there the code should return a concrete type so that the region of unchecked code does not grow.

9.2 Union, Literal, Final and friends

The qualified forms in typing cover four needs: a value of one of several types, a value from a closed set, a name that must not be rebound, and a name that is a type rather than a value. The NewType form covers a fifth: a distinct type that is indistinguishable at runtime from its source.

from typing import (
    Annotated, ClassVar, Final, Literal, Never, NoReturn, NewType,
    Optional, TypeAlias, Union, assert_never,
)

# Union: the 3.10+ form is the operator, and it is the preferred spelling.
def parse(raw: str) -> int | float | None: ...
def parse_old(raw: str) -> Optional[Union[int, float]]: ...   # equivalent

# Optional[T] is exactly Union[T, None]. | None is the modern spelling.
def maybe() -> list[str] | None:
    return None

# Literal: a closed set of values, checked exactly, not by type.
Mode = Literal["r", "w", "a", "x"]
Level = Literal[1, 2, 3]


def open_file(path: str, mode: Mode = "r") -> None: ...


def set_level(n: Level) -> None: ...


open_file("x", "rb")        # a checker error: "rb" is not a Mode


# Final: this binding happens once. The name is a constant thereafter.
TIMEOUT: Final = 30
NAME: Final[str] = "service"          # the type may be given explicitly


def timeout() -> int:
    return TIMEOUT


# ClassVar: an attribute belongs to the class, not to instances.
class Config:
    DEBUG: ClassVar[bool] = False     # one value for every instance
    name: str                         # per instance

    def __init__(self, name: str) -> None:
        self.name = name


print(Config.DEBUG, Config("a").name)
# Config().DEBUG = True             -> a checker error: ClassVar is read
#                                      through the class, not an instance


# Annotated: a type plus metadata. The metadata is inert at runtime.
Port = Annotated[int, "must be 1-65535", "checked by the validator"]


def listen(port: Port) -> None: ...


print(Port.__metadata__)
print(Port.__origin__)


# NewType: a distinct type that is the same object at runtime.
UserId = NewType("UserId", int)


def load(uid: UserId) -> str:
    return f"user:{uid}"


uid = UserId(42)
print(load(uid), uid + 0)             # 42 arithmetic still works
# load(7)                          -> a checker error: int is not UserId

NewType has no runtime behaviour whatsoever: UserId(42) is 42 in the sense that the call returns its argument unchanged. Its entire value is to make a checker refuse to pass a bare int where an identifier is required, which converts a whole class of silent mix-ups into compile-time errors. The cost is that a NewType must be constructed at every boundary where the distinction matters, and that it does not compose with isinstance as a distinct type — isinstance(x, UserId) raises TypeError, because a NewType is a function, not a class.

# The exhaustiveness pattern, which is what Never and assert_never
# are for. Adding a member to the enum makes the checker fail here.
from enum import StrEnum
from typing import Callable, TypeAlias, assert_never


class Verdict(StrEnum):
    APPROVED = "approved"
    REJECTED = "rejected"
    DEFERRED = "deferred"


def label(v: Verdict) -> str:
    match v:
        case Verdict.APPROVED:
            return "accepted"
        case Verdict.REJECTED:
            return "refused"
        case Verdict.DEFERRED:
            return "later"
        case _:
            # REJECTED by a checker if Verdict gains a member and the
            # match above is not updated. Never is the bottom type:
            # nothing inhabits it, so nothing can be assigned to it.
            assert_never(v)
            raise AssertionError("unreachable")


def never_returns(x: int) -> NoReturn:
    raise SystemExit(1)


def always_raises() -> Never:
    raise ValueError("always")


def impossible(x: int | str) -> int:
    if isinstance(x, int):
        return x
    if isinstance(x, str):
        raise TypeError("no int here")
    return x                 # a checker error: Never is uninhabited


# TypeAlias: says explicitly that a name IS a type.
Vector: TypeAlias = list[float]
Matrix: TypeAlias = list[Vector]
Handler: TypeAlias = "Callable[[int], None]"

print(Vector([1.0, 2.0]), Matrix([[1.0]]))
print(Handler)
FormAddedCheckedRuntime representationUse when
T | U3.10Yestypes.UnionTypeThe default for a union
Optional[T]3.5Yestyping.OptionalInteroperating with 3.9 code
Literal[...]3.8Yes, by valuetyping.LiteralA closed string or int set
Final3.8YesNothing; the name is a plain bindingA module constant, a literal-default marker
ClassVar3.3YesNothing at class creationA class-level attribute shared by all instances
Annotated[T, ...]3.9T onlytyping.Annotated with __metadata__Attaching a constraint or a docstring to a type
NewType3.10YesA function returning its argumentDistinguishing two same-shaped types
Self3.11YesNothingAny method that returns its own class
Never / NoReturn3.11 / 3.6YesNothingUninhabited; exhaustiveness and dead branches
TypeAlias3.10YesNothingDeclaring a name that is a type
# Self: a method returns the same class, whatever the subclass.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Self


class Builder:
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._parts: list[str] = []

    def add(self, part: str) -> Self:      # 3.11+
        self._parts.append(part)
        return self

    def optional(self, part: str | None) -> Self | None:
        return self if part is None else self.add(part)


class AuditBuilder(Builder):
    def build(self) -> str:
        return f"AUDIT[{'+'.join(self._parts)}]"


# Without Self, the return type would be Builder and the chain would
# stop: AuditBuilder(...).build() would be an error.
print(AuditBuilder().add("a").add("b").build())
print(Builder().optional(None) is not None)
print(Builder().optional("x") is not None)

Applies to: | unions and TypeAlias since 3.10; Self, Never, LiteralString and assert_never since 3.11; LiteralString is the correct annotation for a parameter that must be a literal — which is what makes a t-string or an f-string safe to log. Annotated since 3.9. Requirements: Annotated metadata is not enforced; a validator that reads __metadata__ at run time is a separate mechanism, and Annotated[int, "1-65535"] on its own checks nothing at run time and nothing beyond int in a checker. Caveats: Final is checked by the checker only when the name is given an explicit type; an un-annotated module constant is not protected, which is why the two forms above differ. ClassVar and Final are mutually exclusive on the same name, and combining them is a checker error. In a dataclass, a ClassVar field is not a field at all and is excluded from __init__; a Final field in a non-frozen dataclass is rejected by the checker. Cross-reference: the enum exhaustiveness pattern is the same one used for match in section 1.4.

9.3 Type variables and generics

A TypeVar names a type that is decided by the caller. A bound restricts it; a constraint enumerates the permitted types and selects between overloads; Generic[T] makes a class parameterisable. Variance determines which of the four positions a parameter may occupy, and getting it wrong is the most common source of spurious errors in a generic API.

from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Generic, TypeVar

# A bound: T must be a subtype of Number.
T = TypeVar("T")

Num = TypeVar("Num", bound="int | float | complex")


def total(values: list[Num]) -> Num:
    """Sum any sequence of numbers, preserving the element type."""
    result = values[0]
    for v in values[1:]:
        result = result + v
    return result


print(total([1, 2, 3]), total([1.5, 2.5]), total([1j, 2j]))
# total(["a"])                 -> a checker error: str is not a Number


# A constraint list is different: it selects an overload, and it is only
# honoured by the checker, never at run time.
AnyStr = TypeVar("AnyStr", str, bytes)


def join(sep: AnyStr, parts: list[AnyStr]) -> AnyStr:
    return sep.join(parts)


print(join("-", ["a", "b"]), join(b"-", [b"a", b"b"]))
# join("-", [b"a"])           -> a checker error: the two must match


# A bound that constrains a container, not an element.
TKey = TypeVar("TKey")
TVal = TypeVar("TVal")


class Registry(Generic[TKey, TVal]):
    """A typed mapping. TKey and TVal are unrelated type variables."""

    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._data: dict[TKey, TVal] = {}

    def put(self, key: TKey, value: TVal) -> None:
        self._data[key] = value

    def get(self, key: TKey) -> TVal:
        return self._data[key]

    def keys(self) -> list[TKey]:
        return list(self._data)


r: Registry[str, int] = Registry()
r.put("a", 1)
print(r.get("a"), r.keys())
# r.put("b", "text")           -> a checker error: str is not int
r2: Registry[int, str] = Registry()
r2.put(1, "one")
print(r2.get(1))

Variance is a property of the parameter, and it is what determines whether a generic class can be used in a given position. A parameter declared with TypeVar("T", bound=..., covariant=True) may only appear in a return type; contravariant=True may only appear in a parameter; and the default invariant T may appear in both, and is the correct choice unless there is a specific reason.

from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Callable, Generic, TypeVar, Iterable, Iterator

T_co = TypeVar("T_co", covariant=True)
T_contra = TypeVar("T_contra", contravariant=True)
T_inv = TypeVar("T_inv")


class Producer(Generic[T_co]):
    """Covariant: only produces T. Iterable[Dog] is a valid Iterable[Animal]."""
    def __init__(self, item: T_co) -> None:
        self._item = item
    def produce(self) -> T_co:
        return self._item


class Consumer(Generic[T_contra]):
    """Contravariant: only consumes T. Consumer[Animal] eats Consumer[Dog]."""
    def __init__(self, handler: Callable[[T_contra], None]) -> None:
        self._handler = handler
    def handle(self, item: T_contra) -> None:
        self._handler(item)


class Cell(Generic[T_inv]):
    """Invariant: the default. Both reads and writes, so no widening."""
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._value: T_inv | None = None
    def set(self, v: T_inv) -> None:
        self._value = v
    def get(self) -> T_inv:
        assert self._value is not None
        return self._value


# The two safe widenings.
p: Producer[Dog] = Producer(Dog())
q: Producer[Animal] = p                 # covariant: fine
print(type(q.produce()).__name__)

# The safe narrowing, in the other direction.
cs: Consumer[Animal] = Consumer(lambda a: print("ate", type(a).__name__))
cs.handle(Dog())                          # contravariant: fine


# Sequence is covariant; MutableSequence is invariant; that is why
# MutableSequence[Dog] is NOT a valid Sequence[Dog] subtype chain and
# List[Dog] is not assignable to List[Animal].
def feed(items: Iterable[Animal]) -> int:   # accepts Iterable[Dog]
    return sum(1 for _ in items)


def total_it(items: Iterator[int]) -> int:  # Iterator is covariant
    return sum(1 for _ in items)


print(feed([Dog(), Dog()]))
print(total_it(iter([1, 2, 3])))

Variance is not decoration; it is a soundness condition. A Consumer[Animal] can be used wherever a Consumer[Dog] is expected, because anything that can handle a Dog can handle an Animal. The reverse is unsound, and Python’s list rejects it: MutableSequence[Dog] is not a MutableSequence[Animal], because the latter promises to accept an Animal and the former cannot. Sequence is covariant precisely because it promises only to read.

# ParamSpec: preserves the exact parameter list of a callable.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Callable, Concatenate, ParamSpec, TypeVar

P = ParamSpec("P")
R = TypeVar("R")
Q = TypeVar("Q")


def logged(func: Callable[P, R]) -> Callable[P, R]:
    """The decorator from 7.2, typed. Same parameters, same return."""
    import functools

    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args: P.args, **kwargs: P.kwargs) -> R:
        print("  ->", func.__name__, args, kwargs)
        return func(*args, **kwargs)
    return wrapper


def traced(func: Callable[Concatenate[str, P], R]) -> Callable[P, R]:
    """Drops a leading `name` parameter from the public signature."""
    import functools

    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args: P.args, **kwargs: P.kwargs) -> R:
        print("  [traced]")
        return func("default", *args, **kwargs)
    return wrapper


@logged
def compute(a: int, b: str = "x", *, flag: bool = False) -> int:
    return a + len(b)


print(compute(1, "yy", flag=True))
reveal_type(compute)      # (a: int, b: str = ..., *, flag: bool = ...) -> int


# TypeVarTuple and Unpack: a variable number of type parameters.
from typing import TypeVarTuple, Unpack

Ts = TypeVarTuple("Ts")


def first(values: tuple[Unpack[Ts]]) -> tuple[Unpack[Ts]]:
    """The identity at the type level, for any arity."""
    return values


print(first((1, "a", True)))
reveal_type(first((1, 2)))       # tuple[int, int]

Applies to: ParamSpec and Concatenate since 3.10; TypeVarTuple and Unpack since 3.11; TypeVar defaults (T = TypeVar("T", default=int)) since 3.13. Requirements: a covariant TypeVar may appear only in return positions, a contravariant one only in parameter positions, and an invariant one in both; violating this is a checker error and is not a runtime error, so it will not be caught by a test. Generic[T] or the PEP 695 class C[T] form is required for a class to be parameterisable; subscripting a class that inherits neither is a TypeError at run time. Caveats: a bound is a subtype constraint, while a constraint list is an enumeration — the two are not interchangeable, and a TypeVar with constraints cannot be solved for at run time. NewType is invariant, so a NewType over int is not accepted where int is expected; a function declared to take int accepts a UserId at run time but a checker will reject it unless the parameter is declared as the NewType or as int with a widening rule. Implementation divergence: the annotations are inert; a checker that does not understand ParamSpec silently treats the decorated function as untyped.

9.4 PEP 695 type parameter syntax

PEP 695, added in Python 3.12, put type parameters into the language. A TypeVar is declared in a bracket immediately after the def, the class or the type keyword, and scoped to that declaration. It also added TypeVar defaults and made variance a property of the type parameter rather than of the TypeVar object.

# PEP 695: the TypeVar is scoped to the function, and it is not a name
# in the module namespace at all.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Callable


def first[T](items: list[T]) -> T:
    """The type variable is declared here, and only here."""
    return items[0]


print(first([1, 2, 3]), first(["a"]))
# first(1)                       -> a checker error: int is not list[int]

# There is no module-level `T` to collide with, and no need to restate
# the bound inline.
def largest[T: float](values: list[T]) -> T:
    return max(values)


print(largest([1, 5, 3]))


# A generic class, without inheriting Generic[T].
class Box[T]:
    __slots__ = ("_value",)

    def __init__(self, value: T) -> None:
        self._value = value

    def get(self) -> T:
        return self._value

    def map[U](self, fn: Callable[[T], U]) -> Box[U]:
        """A method may declare its own type parameters."""
        return Box(fn(self._value))


b = Box(1)
print(b.get(), b.map(str).get())


# A generic alias, with its own parameters, and lazy evaluation.
type Pair[T] = tuple[T, T]
type Registry[K, V] = dict[K, list[V]]
type Result[T] = tuple[bool, T | None]

p: Pair[int] = (1, 2)
r: Registry[str, int] = {"a": [1]}
print(p, r)

# A generic function alias: a full signature, and it is deferred.
type Handler = Callable[[int, str], bool]


def parse(raw: str) -> Result[int]:
    try:
        return True, int(raw)
    except ValueError:
        return False, None


print(parse("42"), parse("nope"))

# Type parameter defaults: 3.13+, and they make a partial
# parameterisation legal.
def with_default[T = int](x: T) -> T:
    return x


print(with_default("s"))

# The runtime representation: each declaration produces a real object.
print(type(first).__name__, first.__type_params__)
print(Box.__type_params__)
print(Box[int], type(Box[int]).__name__)

PEP 695 removes four pieces of boilerplate: the Generic[T] base, the module-level TypeVar definitions, the TypeAlias annotation, and the covariant=True argument. It also fixes a real bug: two modules each defining T = TypeVar("T") at module level could pick up each other’s T through a wildcard import, silently changing what a function meant. With PEP 695 the T in def f[T] exists only inside f.

Pre-3.12 spellingPEP 695 spellingAddedWhat disappears
T = TypeVar("T") + def f(x: T) -> Tdef f[T](x: T) -> T3.12The module-level name and its scope
class C(Generic[T])class C[T]3.12The Generic base and the import
Vec: TypeAlias = list[float]type Vec = list[float]3.12The TypeAlias annotation; aliases are now lazy
class C(Generic[T_co])class C[+T]3.12The covariant argument
T = TypeVar("T", bound=int)class C[T: int]3.12The bound declaration
n/adef f[T = int](x: T)3.13—
TypeAliasType(...)type A[T] = ...3.12The manual lazy-alias construction
# The `type` statement is a lazy alias: the right side is not evaluated
# at the point of the statement. This is the fix for forward references
# in aliases, which is why PEP 695 aliases are the 3.12+ recommendation.
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from collections.abc import Sequence


type Slow = Sequence[list[dict[str, int]]]     # never evaluated
type Missing = DoesNotExist                     # legal: never evaluated
type Concrete = list[int]                       # evaluated only on use


def build() -> type:
    print("  building")
    return list


type Lazy = build()                            # legal: built on first use


print("module fully imported, nothing built")
print(Lazy[int], Concrete, Slow)
print("__value__ is the evaluated right-hand side:")
print(Lazy.__value__, Concrete.__value__)
print("type_params:", Slow.__type_params__)

Three migration hazards in PEP 695. (1) The syntax is version-gated. def f[T] and type A = ... are SyntaxError on 3.11 and earlier, so a library with a 3.9 floor cannot use them in the same file as its supported syntax; a conditional import does not help, because the parser is not conditional. (2) The type parameters are not module names. A decorator or a helper that previously received T from the module namespace must now receive f.__type_params__[0], and this is the only supported way to introspect them. (3) A type alias is not a class. isinstance, subclassing and __mro__ do not apply to a TypeAliasType; an alias cannot be used as a base class, and isinstance(x, MyAlias) raises TypeError. Use a NewType or a Protocol where a runtime-checkable value is required.

Applies to: PEP 695 in 3.12; TypeVar defaults and __type_params__ exposure in 3.13; fully lazy evaluation of type aliases in 3.14. Requirements: on 3.14, PEP 649 means a type alias’s right-hand side and a deferred annotation are both evaluated only on demand, so get_type_hints on a function annotated with a PEP 695 alias resolves it correctly without a string. Caveats: typing.get_type_hints on 3.13 and earlier cannot resolve a TypeAliasType defined by the type statement unless the alias is in scope; that was fixed as part of the 3.14 annotation work. __type_params__ is a tuple of real type-parameter objects with their own bounds, defaults and variance, and is the supported replacement for reaching into a module’s TypeVar names. Implementation divergence: PEP 695 syntax is implemented by all three 3.12+ implementations; on PyPy and on a free-threaded build the runtime objects are the same and no interpreter-level cost is attached to a type parameter.

9.5 TypedDict, Protocol, TypeGuard and TypeIs

Four forms describe a shape rather than a class. TypedDict describes a dictionary with a fixed set of keys of known types. Protocol describes an object that has certain members, whether or not it inherits anything. TypeGuard and TypeIs let a function tell the checker what it has established.

from __future__ import annotations
from typing import TypedDict, NotRequired, Required, Literal


class UserRow(TypedDict):
    id: int
    name: str
    email: NotRequired[str]         # 3.11+: the key may be absent
    role: Required[Literal["admin", "user"]]


def label(row: UserRow) -> str:
    # A TypedDict is a precise dict: row["id"] is int, not Any, and
    # row["nope"] is a checker error, not a KeyError at run time.
    suffix = " (admin)" if row["role"] == "admin" else ""
    return f"{row['name']}{suffix}"


print(label({"id": 1, "name": "Ada", "role": "admin"}))
# label({"id": 1, "name": "Ada"})   -> a checker error: "role" is Required

# total = {"sum": ...} is NOT a TypedDict: the types are fixed values.
# A total=True TypedDict requires every key, and says nothing about
# the types being literal.

# TypedDict and dataclass are not interchangeable:
#   TypedDict  -> untyped at run time; a plain dict, validated by nothing
#   dataclass  -> a real class, validated in __init__, slower to build

# Protocol: structural typing. No inheritance required.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Protocol, runtime_checkable


class SupportsWrite(Protocol):
    """Anything with a binary write method."""
    def write(self, data: bytes, /) -> int: ...


def dump(obj: SupportsWrite) -> int:
    """obj is now known to have .write, with no isinstance anywhere."""
    return obj.write(b"hello")


class PlainFile:
    def write(self, data: bytes) -> int:
        return len(data)


class Unrelated:
    pass


print(dump(PlainFile()))
# dump(Unrelated())            -> a checker error: no `write` attribute


# runtime_checkable enables isinstance(), with an important limitation.
@runtime_checkable
class HasName(Protocol):
    name: str


class Named:
    name = "x"


class Anon:
    pass


print(isinstance(Named(), HasName))     # True
print(isinstance(Anon(), HasName))      # False
# isinstance(5, HasName)               -> a TypeError: only @runtime_checkable
#                                          protocols may be used here


# A protocol with a default implementation: a mix-in that is also a
# contract. Subclassing is optional but gets the default for free.
class Named(Protocol):
    name: str

    def describe(self) -> str:
        return f"named {self.name}"


class Thing:
    name = "thing"

    def describe(self) -> str:          # overrides the protocol default
        return f"a thing called {self.name}"


def show(item: Named) -> str:
    return item.describe()


print(show(Thing()))

@runtime_checkable checks presence of members and nothing else. isinstance against a runtime-checkable protocol returns True for an object that has the right attribute names with the wrong types, and for an object whose attributes are all None because they are properties that raised. A data protocol — one with a non-method member such as name: str — can be used with isinstance only because the check is presence-based; that is a documented limitation and it is why runtime_checkable is applied sparingly.

# TypeGuard narrows to the declared type but says nothing about the
# negative branch. Use it for membership tests and parsed values.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import TypeGuard, TypeIs


# TypeGuard narrows to the declared type but says nothing about the
# negative branch. Use it for membership tests and parsed values.
def is_str_list(value: list[object]) -> TypeGuard[list[str]]:
    return all(isinstance(v, str) for v in value)


def show_guard(v: list[object]) -> None:
    if is_str_list(v):
        print(v[0].upper())          # v is list[str] in this branch only


# TypeIs narrows BOTH branches: the else branch is narrowed to the
# complement. It requires the narrowed type to be a subtype of the
# parameter type, which TypeGuard does not.
def is_str(value: object) -> TypeIs[str]:
    return isinstance(value, str)


def show_is(v: str | int) -> None:
    if is_str(v):
        print(v.upper())
    else:
        print(v.bit_length())        # v is int here: TypeIs, not TypeGuard


# The parse-and-validate pattern, which is where TypeGuard earns its
# keep: a try/except that narrows an untrusted value.
def parse_port(raw: str) -> TypeGuard[int]:
    """Narrow a str to an int port, or return False."""
    if not raw.isdigit():
        return False
    n = int(raw)
    return 1 <= n <= 65535


def connect(raw: str) -> str:
    if parse_port(raw):                # raw is int in this branch
        return f"connecting to {raw}"
    return f"invalid port {raw!r}"


print(connect("8080"), connect("99999"))

# Annotated[TypeGuard[...]] is rejected: the guard must be the return
# annotation itself, not a nested form.

Applies to: NotRequired, Required and ReadOnly since 3.11; TypeIs since 3.13; TypeGuard since 3.10. Requirements: a TypeIs[T] function must return bool, its parameter must be a supertype of T, and the function must be narrow in both directions — returning True for a value that is not an instance of T makes every downstream inference wrong. A TypeGuard returning True for a non-T is merely unsound in the negative branch. Caveats: neither form has any run-time effect; both are erased. A checker may not narrow through a TypeGuard that is called behind a non-bool intermediate, and narrowing does not survive being stored in a variable typed Callable without an explicit annotation. Protocol members declared without a value are invariance-checking attributes: a protocol with name: str is not satisfied by a class with name: str | None. A protocol method is checked covariantly in its parameters and contravariantly in its return, and a protocol with a __call__ member is a callable protocol — which is how a non-function callable is annotated. Cross-reference: a runtime Protocol is the same concept as the structural interface described for abstract bases in section 5.6, with the inheritance requirement removed.

9.6 Checkers, configuration and library markers

A checker is only as good as its configuration and its inputs. Three things determine the result: the strictness level, the treatment of unannotated code, and whether the package declares itself typed. The last of these is a one-file decision with a large downstream effect and is documented by PEP 561.

# pyproject.toml -- the configuration is the API of a typed project.
#
# [tool.mypy]
# python_version = "3.14"
# strict = true
# warn_unreachable = true
# disallow_any_explicit = false
# show_error_codes = true
# plugins = ["pydantic.mypy"]
# exclude = ["^build/", "^dist/"]
#
# [[tool.mypy.overrides]]
# module = ["some_untyped_vendor.*"]
# ignore_missing_imports = true
#
# [tool.pyright]
# include = ["src"]
# pythonVersion = "3.14"
# typeCheckingMode = "strict"
# reportMissingTypeStubs = false
# reportImplicitOverride = true
# reportUnnecessaryTypeIgnoreComment = true
#
# A `strict = true` mypy run applies all of these:
#   disallow_untyped_defs      every def needs annotations
#   disallow_incomplete_defs    every *partially* annotated def is an error
#   disallow_untyped_calls      calling an unannotated def is an error
#   no_implicit_reexport       a re-export needs the `as` form
#   warn_return_any             returning Any from a typed function is an error
#   warn_unused_ignores         a stale # type: ignore is an error
#   strict_equality             == between non-overlapping types is an error
#   disallow_any_generics       bare `list` is an error; `list[str]` is not
#
# Gradual typing: the three levels a real migration uses.
#
#   1. Untyped.  def f(x): ...        -- no checking anywhere
#   2. Partial.  def f(x: int): ...   -- checked where annotated
#   3. Strict.   def f(x: int) -> int: -- checked everywhere
#
# A module is only fully checked in level 3. A single unannotated
# parameter in a function body makes the whole function unchecked, which
# is why `disallow_incomplete_defs` matters more than it looks.

# What gradual typing looks like in practice, in one file.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Any


def fully_typed(raw: str) -> int:
    return len(raw)


def partially_typed(raw: str) -> int:
    helper(raw)               # an unannotated call: not checked
    return len(raw)


def helper(value) -> None:   # one unannotated parameter
    print(value)


def fully_typed_boundary(payload: dict[str, Any]) -> int:
    """Any is confined to a single boundary, on purpose.

    The parse-and-validate step is where untrusted data becomes trusted
    data, and that is the only legitimate home for Any in a codebase.
    """
    if not isinstance(payload, dict):
        return 0
    out = 0
    for key, value in payload.items():
        if isinstance(key, str) and isinstance(value, int):
            out += value
    return out


print(fully_typed("abc"), fully_typed_boundary({"a": 1, "b": "x", "c": 2}))

# The TYPE_CHECKING pattern: an import that only the checker sees.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    # Cost-free at run time; fully visible to mypy and pyright.
    import pandas
    from pandas import DataFrame
    import numpy.typing as npt

    ArrayLike = "numpy.typing.ArrayLike"


def summarise(frame: DataFrame) -> int:
    return int(frame.shape[0])


# The alternative when the type is only needed at run time too:
# a string alias, evaluated lazily since 3.14.
type Frame = DataFrame


# reportUnusedImport is not a setting most projects want, because the
# TYPE_CHECKING import IS the re-export declaration. The correct pair
# is:
#
#   if TYPE_CHECKING:
#       from .model import Order as Order      # the `as` form
#
# which mypy --no-implicit-reexport and pyright both accept as a public
# re-export, and which costs nothing at run time.

# A forward reference in a TYPE_CHECKING block, for a class defined
# later in the same file.
class Node:
    def children(self) -> list["Node"]:
        return []


print(Node().children())

# Common strict-mode errors and their repairs, in the order they
# appear in a migration:
#
#   "Returning Any from function declared to return int"
#     -> the annotation is a promise the body cannot keep. Narrow the
#        value, or change the annotation to what it actually returns.
#   "Argument 1 has incompatible type dict[str, int]; expected str"
#     -> a real defect. Insert the key: d["k"].
#   "Incompatible return value type (got list[int], expected int)"
#     -> a real defect: the function returns a container where the
#        contract says a scalar.
#   "Missing type parameters for generic type list"
#     -> disallow_any_generics. Write list[int].
#   "Unused 'type: ignore' comment"
#     -> a stale suppression, usually because a base annotation was
#        widened. Delete it.
#   "Untyped decorator makes function untyped"
#     -> an unannotated decorator from a third-party library. Write
#        the decorator with ParamSpec (see 7.2) or suppress it once,
#        centrally, in a stubs package.
#   "Cannot find implementation or library stub for module named X"
#     -> the package is untyped. Either add py.typed upstream, or
#        install types-X, or add a one-line stub in a local stubs/
#        directory that overrides the Any.

# PEP 561: publishing types. The marker file is one line, and without
# it every consumer sees `import mylib` as Any.
#
#   mylib/
#     py.typed          <-- empty file, and this is the whole contract
#     __init__.py
#     core.py
#
# In the distribution metadata, with a build backend that supports it:
#
#   [build-system]
#   requires = ["hatchling"]
#   build-backend = "hatchling.build"
#
#   [tool.hatch.build.targets.wheel]
#   packages = ["src/mylib"]
#   # hatchling includes py.typed automatically when it is inside the
#   # package directory.
#
# The three states a consumer can observe:
#
#   py.typed present       -> the package is typed; annotations are used
#   py.typed absent        -> the package is untyped; all imports are Any
#   py.typed + partial     -> PEP 561 allows a partial stub package to
#                             declare only the modules it covers, with
#                             py.typed at the root and no py.typed in
#                             the individual sub-stubs

# The consumer-side check: a local package is typed or it is not.
import importlib.util
import pathlib
import sys

root = pathlib.Path("/tmp/example-pkg/mylib")
root.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
(root / "__init__.py").write_text("def f(x: int) -> int: return x\n", encoding="utf-8")
(root / "py.typed").write_text("", encoding="utf-8")

sys.path.insert(0, str(root.parent))
try:
    import mylib
    print("resolved:", mylib.__file__)
    print("py.typed present:", (pathlib.Path(mylib.__file__).parent / "py.typed").exists())
    spec = importlib.util.find_spec("mylib")
    print("origin:", spec.origin is not None)
    print("submodule_search_locations:", spec.submodule_search_locations is not None)
finally:
    sys.path.remove(str(root.parent))
    for k in [k for k in list(sys.modules) if k == "mylib" or k.startswith("mylib.")]:
        del sys.modules[k]

The five mistakes that make a strict configuration impractical, and the repair for each. (1) Starting at strict = true on an existing codebase — begin with the default, fix what surfaces, and raise one flag at a time; a migration that begins with maximum strictness does not get merged. (2) Annotating signatures but not bodies — the function is still unchecked, because mypy and pyright both infer the body only when every parameter and the return are annotated; disallow_incomplete_defs is the flag that catches this. (3) Using # type: ignore as a comment — it must be bare # type: ignore or carry a specific code, and with warn_unused_ignores a stale one becomes an error. A blanket ignore_errors in configuration is worse than no configuration, because it removes the signal without removing the defect. (4) Letting Any spread — every function that touches an Any is unchecked, and the region grows from the boundary outward; contain it in a parse-and-validate function and return a concrete type. (5) Assuming an installed package is typed — without py.typed, every annotation in it is invisible, and the correct fix is either to publish the marker or to install a maintained stub package.

Applies to: mypy and pyright both fully support 3.14 and PEP 695 as of their current releases. py.typed is PEP 561 (3.7). Requirements: a typed package needs the marker file inside the package directory — not beside it, and not at the project root — and it must be included in the built wheel and sdist; a MANIFEST.in or a package-data entry is required with some backends. Caveats: the marker is a promise, not a verification; a package that ships py.typed and incomplete annotations is worse for a consumer than one that ships no marker, because the consumer’s checker now reports errors on annotations the author never intended as a contract. A .pyi stub in a stubs/ directory is the override mechanism for a third-party package that cannot be fixed, and it must be named exactly as the module it replaces. disallow_any_explicit is worth enabling only once Any has been confined to boundaries. Tooling: the type checker and the runtime are independent; a correct annotation that the runtime would reject is a defect in the annotation, and a run-time guard that the annotation already implies is redundant code.

Exercises 9

  1. Write a module that annotates every signature, run get_type_hints on each function and class, and show that on 3.14 the __annotate__ function exists and the __annotations__ mapping is lazy rather than a plain dict.
  2. Replace every Any in a small module with object plus explicit narrowing, and record how many new checker errors appear at each replacement, then fix them.
  3. Build a Verdict StrEnum with three members and a match whose final branch is assert_never; add a fourth member and confirm the checker reports the missing branch.
  4. Implement a generic Registry[K, V] and then a covariant Producer[T] and a contravariant Consumer[T], and demonstrate with assignments which widenings a checker accepts and which it rejects.
  5. Convert a module from TypeVar plus Generic[T] to PEP 695, then convert a TypeAlias annotation to a type statement, and note every import that became unnecessary.
  6. Write a TypeGuard validator for a JSON-shaped value and a TypeIs for a primitive, then show the difference in what the checker knows in the negative branch of each.
  7. Take a module with no annotations, run mypy with its default settings, then with disallow_incomplete_defs = true, and record which functions changed from unchecked to checked.
  8. Build a distribution-shaped directory with and without a py.typed marker, import it from a second directory, and show that a checker treats the two cases differently while the runtime treats them identically.

10 · Exceptions and Error Handling

10.1 The exception hierarchy10.2 try, except, else and finally10.3 Exception chaining and tracebacks10.4 Designing custom exceptions10.5 Exception groups and notes10.6 assert and the -O flag10.7 Suppression, warnings and conventions

Python has one exception mechanism, one hierarchy rooted at BaseException, and a small set of conventions that distinguish a library from a script. The mechanism is straightforward; the conventions are what this chapter is about, because they are what determines whether a failure is diagnosable six months later.

10.1 The exception hierarchy

Every exception derives from BaseException. The four direct subclasses of BaseException that are not Exception are the ones that terminate a program or a frame for reasons unrelated to the code’s logic, and which must never be caught by application code.

# The hierarchy, from the root. Indentation is inheritance.

BaseException
  +- BaseExceptionGroup   (3.11+; also derives from Exception)
  +- GeneratorExit
  +- KeyboardInterrupt
  +- SystemExit
  +- Exception
     +- ArithmeticError
     |  +- ZeroDivisionError
     |  +- OverflowError
     |  +- FloatingPointError
     +- AssertionError
     +- AttributeError
     +- BufferError
     +- EOFError
     +- ImportError            (also derives from NameError)
     |  +- ModuleNotFoundError
     +- LookupError
     |  +- IndexError
     |  +- KeyError
     +- MemoryError
     +- NameError
     |  +- UnboundLocalError
     +- OSError
     |  +- BlockingIOError
     |  +- ChildProcessError
     |  +- ConnectionError
     |  |  +- BrokenPipeError
     |  |  +- ConnectionAbortedError
     |  |  +- ConnectionRefusedError
     |  |  +- ConnectionResetError
     |  +- FileExistsError
     |  +- FileNotFoundError
     |  +- InterruptedError
     |  +- IsADirectoryError
     |  +- NotADirectoryError
     |  +- PermissionError
     |  +- ProcessLookupError
     |  +- TimeoutError
     +- ReferenceError
     +- RuntimeError
     |  +- NotImplementedError
     |  +- RecursionError
     +- StopAsyncIteration
     +- StopIteration
     +- SyntaxError
     |  +- IndentationError
     |     +- TabError
     +- SystemError
     +- TypeError
     +- ValueError
     +- UnicodeError
     |  +- UnicodeDecodeError
     |  +- UnicodeEncodeError
     |  +- UnicodeTranslateError
     +- Warning
     |  +- BytesWarning
     |  +- DeprecationWarning
     |  +- EncodingWarning       (3.10+; also a SyntaxWarning)
     |  +- FutureWarning
     |  +- ImportWarning
     |  +- PendingDeprecationWarning
     |  +- ResourceWarning
     |  +- RuntimeWarning
     |  +- SyntaxWarning
     |  +- UnicodeWarning
     +- ExceptionGroup         (3.11+, the ordinary form)

# The two structural facts that drive every catch clause.
print(issubclass(ImportError, NameError))    # True: an odd but real edge
print(issubclass(ZeroDivisionError, ArithmeticError))
print(issubclass(ConnectionError, OSError))
print(issubclass(KeyboardInterrupt, Exception))   # False
print(issubclass(SystemExit, Exception))         # False
print(issubclass(GeneratorExit, Exception))      # False
print(issubclass(BaseExceptionGroup, Exception)) # True, since 3.11

The hierarchy contains several deliberate surprises, and each of them has a practical consequence. ImportError is a NameError, so a bare except NameError swallows a failed import; catch ImportError when you mean it. ConnectionError is an OSError, so except OSError is the correct broad clause for network code, and except ConnectionError is the correct narrow one. TimeoutError is an OSError in Python 3 — since 3.10 it is also the built-in raised by asyncio, so one clause covers both a socket timeout and an expired deadline. RecursionError is a RuntimeError, so except RuntimeError catches stack exhaustion as well as the other runtime failures, and code that must not do that must list RecursionError first.

# The four BaseException-only exceptions, and why each must propagate.
import sys
import time

# SystemExit: raised by sys.exit() and by an uncaught exception in a
# thread's default handler. It carries a code and reaches the interpreter,
# which turns it into an exit status. Catching it swallows the request
# to terminate.
def parse_args(argv: list[str]) -> int:
    if not argv:
        sys.exit("usage: prog [--verbose]")
    return 0

# KeyboardInterrupt: SIGINT. The user asked to stop. Catching it and
# continuing produces a process that ignores its operator.
def poll() -> None:
    try:
        time.sleep(10)
    except KeyboardInterrupt:
        print("interrupted; cleaning up and re-raising")
        raise                 # ALWAYS re-raise after cleanup

# GeneratorExit: thrown into a generator by close() and by garbage
# collection. A generator that swallows it and yields again raises
# RuntimeError. Never catch it except to release resources and re-raise.
# See section 6.2 for the correct shape.

# BaseExceptionGroup: the group form. Since 3.11 it is BOTH a
# BaseException and an Exception, which is the whole point: except*
# can unpack it while except Exception can still catch it as a whole.
print(issubclass(BaseExceptionGroup, BaseException), BaseException)

# The one legitimate broad catch: the top of a process, where the job
# is to report and exit rather than to recover.
def run() -> int:
    """The real work. Replaced by the program."""
    return 0


def main() -> int:
    try:
        return run()
    except KeyboardInterrupt:       # listed first as a matter of style,
        return 130                  # even though it is not an Exception
    except Exception:
        import traceback
        traceback.print_exc()
        return 1                    # not `raise`: a CLI must exit with a code
ExceptionMeaningRecoverable by application codeCorrect clause
ValueErrorRight type, unacceptable valueYes, with a different valueexcept ValueError
TypeErrorWrong type for the operationUsually a programming defectexcept TypeError at a boundary only
KeyError / IndexErrorAbsent key / out-of-range indexYes, with a default or a guardexcept (KeyError, IndexError)
AttributeErrorNo such attributeUsually a programming defectNarrow: getattr with a default is better
NotImplementedErrorAn abstract method was calledNo — a class-design defectNever; fix the class
OSError familyOperating-system failureYes, with a policyThe narrowest subclass that fits
TimeoutErrorA deadline expiredYes — usually with a retryexcept TimeoutError
RecursionErrorStack exhaustedNo — see section 4.5Never; raise the limit or restructure
MemoryErrorAllocation failedAlmost neverOnly to release and re-raise
SystemExit, KeyboardInterrupt, GeneratorExitControl flow, not failureNoNever, except to re-raise

Applies to: the hierarchy is part of the language specification and is identical in CPython, PyPy and free-threaded builds. Requirements: every except clause in application code names a subclass of Exception; the three control-flow exceptions and a bare except BaseException are reserved for a process-level boundary. Caveats: a raise of an exception class rather than an instance — raise ValueError — is legal and Python instantiates it with no arguments, but it discards the information a message would have carried. StopIteration raised inside a generator body is converted to RuntimeError (PEP 479), so the only way to terminate a generator is return; the original StopIteration is available as __cause__. Base exceptions in threads: a BaseException that escapes a thread’s target is not propagated to the main thread; the thread’s excepthook prints it and the thread dies, so a KeyboardInterrupt delivered to a non-main thread is effectively lost unless the thread records it explicitly.

10.2 try, except, else and finally

The try statement has four clauses, not two. else runs when no exception was raised; finally always runs. Their existence is not a convenience: without else, the only way to write "handle the error, then continue" puts the continuation inside the try block, where it is also covered by the except clauses.

import json
from typing import Any


class ConfigError(Exception):
    """A configuration file could not be read, parsed or understood."""


def load_config(path: str) -> dict:
    """The correct shape: only the risky operation is inside `try`."""
    try:
        raw: str = open(path, encoding="utf-8").read()   # may raise
    except OSError as exc:
        raise ConfigError(f"cannot read {path}") from exc

    # Parsing and validation are OUTSIDE the try, so a ValueError from
    # the parser is not misreported as an OSError.
    try:
        data: Any = json.loads(raw)
    except json.JSONDecodeError as exc:
        raise ConfigError(f"{path} is not valid JSON: {exc}") from exc

    if not isinstance(data, dict):
        raise ConfigError(f"{path} must contain an object")
    return data


# What `else` is for.
def read_number(raw: str) -> float:
    try:
        value = int(raw)              # the only risky step
    except ValueError:
        return 0.0                    # handle the failure
    else:
        return value * 1.5            # runs only on success


# Without `else`, the continuation is inside the try and is therefore
# covered by the except clause -- the classic defect.
def read_number_broken(raw: str) -> float:
    try:
        value = int(raw)
        return value * 1.5            # a ValueError here would be caught
    except ValueError:
        return 0.0


# finally runs on every path: normal, exceptional, and a return.
def always() -> str:
    order = []
    try:
        order.append("try")
        return f"{order} then finally"
    except Exception:
        order.append("except")
        return "unreachable"
    finally:
        order.append("finally")


print(load_config.__doc__ is not None)
print(read_number("4"), read_number("x"))
print(always())

The rule that follows is short and worth restating: the try block should contain the smallest possible region that can raise the exception you are catching. A try that spans a function call chain catches the exceptions of every function in that chain, and reports all of them as if they came from the one you were thinking of. The else clause exists so that the successful path can be written outside that region without duplicating the try.

# except ordering: first match wins, so the most specific comes first.
import json


def parse(raw: str) -> dict:
    return json.loads(raw)


def classify(raw: str) -> str:
    try:
        return str(parse(raw))
    except json.JSONDecodeError:              # a subclass of ValueError
        return "malformed"
    except ValueError:                        # reached only if the first
        return "bad value"                    # clause did not match
    except Exception:                         # last resort, documented
        return "unexpected"


# What a bare `except:` catches: everything, including the three
# control-flow exceptions and KeyboardInterrupt. It is almost always
# wrong, and it is fatal in a library because it prevents the caller
# from ever interrupting the process.
def dangerous(fn) -> None:
    try:
        fn()
    except:                     # noqa: E722 -- do not write this
        print("something went wrong")

# The two acceptable forms of a broad catch.
def acceptable_top_level(fn) -> int:
    try:
        fn()
    except KeyboardInterrupt:
        return 130
    except Exception:
        import traceback
        traceback.print_exc()
        return 1

# Except as e, and the lifetime of e. Python 3 deletes the name at the
# end of the except clause, which prevents the classic leak.
def lifetime(raw: str) -> str:
    try:
        int(raw)
    except ValueError as e:
        captured = str(e)     # str() it here: e is deleted after the block
    return captured

print(classify("{"), classify("[]"), classify("1"))
print(lifetime("x"))
# print(e)                   -> NameError: e is unbound

finally has one sharp edge worth knowing: a return, break or continue in a finally block discards the exception in flight or the pending return. It is legal, and it silently swallows control flow.

# The two finally defects, both silent.

def swallows_exception() -> str:
    try:
        raise ValueError("important")
    finally:
        return "replaced"       # the ValueError is discarded entirely


def swallows_return() -> str:
    result = "original"
    try:
        return result
    finally:
        result = "changed"      # legal, and has no effect on the return


print(swallows_exception())     # 'replaced': the exception is gone
print(swallows_return())        # 'original': the local change is discarded

# The rule: a finally block may run cleanup, and may raise a DIFFERENT
# exception deliberately. It may not return, break or continue.

Four defects in try usage that pass a code review and fail in production. (1) A bare except: or except BaseException: in a library, which makes the process uninterruptible and hides real bugs. (2) An except that logs and continues without changing state, so the caller receives a wrong result as if it were right — at minimum, re-raise. (3) Catching Exception around a block that includes a bare raise-free body, so the retry logic cannot distinguish a transient failure from a permanent one. (4) A return inside finally, which erases the exception the caller needed to see. Requirements: every except clause either recovers fully, re-raises, or raises a different, more specific exception with from; and every finally block contains cleanup only.

Applies to: try/else/finally since 2.5, unchanged in 3.14. Requirements: the exception variable named by except ... as e is deleted at the end of the clause, so a reference needed after the block must be extracted. Caveats: a finally that performs I/O can itself raise, and that exception replaces the one in flight — which is why cleanup in a finally should itself be guarded, for example with contextlib.suppress or a nested try. On a free-threaded build, a finally that mutates state another thread also touches is a data race, and the language provides no protection for it. The except* form (section 10.5) may not be combined with except in the same try.

10.3 Exception chaining and tracebacks

An exception carries two chain attributes. __context__ is set automatically to whatever exception was being handled when this one was raised. __cause__ is set only by an explicit raise ... from .... The traceback printer shows __cause__ as "The above exception was the direct cause" and __context__ as "During handling of the above exception, another exception occurred".

# Implicit chaining: __context__ is filled in automatically.
def parse_port(raw: str) -> int:
    return int(raw.split(":")[1])


def connect(spec: str) -> int:
    try:
        return parse_port(spec)
    except IndexError as exc:               # handled here...
        raise ValueError(f"malformed spec: {spec!r}")   # ...new one raised
    # No `from`: __context__ is IndexError, __cause__ is None.
    # The traceback shows BOTH, the second under the first.


def connect_explicit(spec: str) -> int:
    try:
        return parse_port(spec)
    except IndexError as exc:
        raise ValueError(f"malformed spec: {spec!r}") from exc
    # __cause__ is IndexError. The traceback shows the cause first, then
    # "The above exception was the direct cause of..." and the new one.


def connect_suppressed(spec: str) -> int:
    try:
        return parse_port(spec)
    except IndexError:
        raise ValueError(f"malformed spec: {spec!r}") from None
    # __cause__ is None AND __suppress_context__ is True: the IndexError
    # is hidden. The traceback shows only the ValueError.


for fn in (connect, connect_explicit, connect_suppressed):
    try:
        fn("localhost")
    except ValueError as exc:
        print(f"{fn.__name__}: cause={type(exc.__cause__).__name__} "
              f"context={type(exc.__context__).__name__} "
              f"suppress={exc.__suppress_context__}")
Form__cause____context__Traceback showsUse when
raise E(...) inside exceptNoneThe handled exceptionBoth, chained as "during handling"The new error is a consequence, not a translation
raise E(...) from excexcThe handled exceptionBoth, chained as "direct cause"The new error is a translation of exc
raise E(...) from NoneNoneSet, but suppressedOnly the new oneThe new error is a complete replacement and the old one is noise
raise exc (bare)UnchangedUnchangedThe original traceback plus the current frameRe-raising after cleanup; adds a frame
raise (bare)UnchangedUnchangedThe original traceback onlyRe-raising after cleanup, from inside the handler

Reading a traceback is a skill, and the rules are short. Read from the last frame backwards: the last line is the message and the exception type, the line above it is where it was raised, and each preceding block is one frame of the call chain, innermost first. Chained exceptions appear below the exception that references them, and the "above exception" wording refers to what is printed above in the terminal.

# A full traceback, printed, so the structure is visible.
import traceback


def level3() -> None:
    raise RuntimeError("the actual failure")


def level2() -> None:
    level3()


def level1() -> None:
    level2()


try:
    level1()
except RuntimeError:
    tb = traceback.format_exc()
    for line in tb.strip().splitlines():
        print(line)

print("--- the structured form, for programmatic handling ---")
try:
    level1()
except RuntimeError as exc:
    frames = traceback.extract_tb(exc.__traceback__)
    print([(f.name, f.lineno, f.line) for f in frames])
    print("cause:", exc.__cause__, "context:", exc.__context__)
    print("args:", exc.args)
    print("str():", str(exc))
    print("repr():", repr(exc))
    print("with_traceback round-trip:", exc.with_traceback(None) is exc)

Applies to: raise ... from ... since 3.0; traceback.TracebackException (3.10+) formats an exception chain without rendering it, and ExceptionGroup.traceback (3.11+) is the group equivalent. Requirements: a library that translates a low-level exception into a domain exception should use from exc, because that is the assertion "this is a translation of that"; a library that re-raises a completely different failure should use from None, because the original is not informative to the caller. Caveats: __context__ is assigned for any exception raised while another is being handled, including one raised inside an except clause’s own cleanup — so it is frequently set to something irrelevant, and only __cause__ should be treated as meaningful. A bare raise exc where exc is the caught exception appends the current frame to the traceback, which is useful in a handler that adds information and misleading in a handler that does not. traceback.print_exc() writes to sys.stderr by default and returns None; traceback.format_exc() returns a string and is what a test or a log handler should use.

10.4 Designing custom exceptions

A library’s exception hierarchy is its public interface for failure. Three properties matter: it derives from an Exception subclass that is specific enough to catch selectively; it carries structured attributes rather than only a message; and its docstring states the conditions under which it is raised.

# A domain hierarchy: one base, specific subclasses, structured data.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Any


class AppError(Exception):
    """Base for every error this package raises deliberately.

    Raised when: never directly. Catch this to handle any expected
    failure from this library without catching unrelated exceptions.
    """

    def __init__(self, message: str, **context: Any) -> None:
        super().__init__(message)
        self.message = message
        self.context: dict[str, Any] = context

    def __str__(self) -> str:
        if not self.context:
            return self.message
        detail = " ".join(f"{k}={v!r}" for k, v in sorted(self.context.items()))
        return f"{self.message} ({detail})"


class ConfigError(AppError):
    """The configuration is missing, malformed, or internally inconsistent.

    Raised when: a required key is absent, a value cannot be coerced to
    its annotated type, or two keys contradict each other.
    """


class ValidationError(AppError):
    """Input failed a domain rule. The caller's data is at fault.

    Attributes:
        field: the offending field name, or None for a whole-object rule.
        rule: a short stable identifier, safe to branch on.
    """

    def __init__(self, message: str, *, field: str | None = None,
                 rule: str = "invalid", **context: Any) -> None:
        super().__init__(message, **context)
        self.field = field
        self.rule = rule


class ResourceError(AppError):
    """An external resource was unavailable or refused the request.

    Raised when: a connection failed, a file could not be opened, a
    remote call timed out. Never raised for a programming defect.
    """


# The hierarchy is the contract: catch at the level of intent.
def load_config(data: dict) -> dict:
    if "name" not in data:
        raise ConfigError("name is required", keys=sorted(data))
    if not isinstance(data["name"], str):
        raise ValidationError("name must be a string",
                              field="name", rule="type", got=type(data["name"]).__name__)
    if "limit" in data and not isinstance(data["limit"], int):
        raise ValidationError("limit must be an int",
                              field="limit", rule="type", got=type(data["limit"]).__name__)
    return data


for payload, expected in [({}, ConfigError), ({"name": 1}, ValidationError)]:
    try:
        load_config(payload)
    except AppError as exc:
        print(type(exc).__name__, "->", str(exc))
        print("  caught as AppError:", isinstance(exc, expected))
        print("  field:", getattr(exc, "field", None), "rule:", getattr(exc, "rule", None))

# Interoperating with exception groups from 3.11, and re-raising a
# group so the caller can unpack it. The two definitions from the block
# above are repeated so this one runs on its own.
from __future__ import annotations
from typing import Any


class AppError(Exception):
    def __init__(self, message: str, **context: Any) -> None:
        super().__init__(message)
        self.message = message
        self.context: dict[str, Any] = context

    def __str__(self) -> str:
        detail = " ".join(f"{k}={v!r}" for k, v in sorted(self.context.items()))
        return f"{self.message} ({detail})" if detail else self.message


class ConfigError(AppError):
    """The configuration is missing, malformed, or inconsistent."""


class ValidationError(AppError):
    """Input failed a domain rule."""

    def __init__(self, message: str, *, field: str | None = None,
                 rule: str = "invalid", **context: Any) -> None:
        super().__init__(message, **context)
        self.field = field
        self.rule = rule


def load_config(data: dict) -> dict:
    if "name" not in data:
        raise ConfigError("name is required", keys=sorted(data))
    if not isinstance(data["name"], str):
        raise ValidationError("name must be a string",
                              field="name", rule="type",
                              got=type(data["name"]).__name__)
    return data


def validate_all(rows: list[dict]) -> list[dict]:
    """Validate every row; report every failure, not just the first."""
    errors: list[ValidationError] = []
    good: list[dict] = []

    for index, row in enumerate(rows):
        try:
            good.append(load_config(row))
        except ValidationError as exc:
            exc.add_note(f"row index {index}")     # 3.11+, see 10.5
            errors.append(exc)

    if errors:
        # One group, raised once. The caller unpacks with except*.
        raise ExceptionGroup("row validation failed", errors)
    return good


def summarise(rows: list[dict]) -> str:
    try:
        validate_all(rows)
    except* ValidationError as group:
        return f"{len(group.exceptions)} invalid: " + ", ".join(
            sorted(f"{e.field}" for e in group.exceptions)
        )
    except* ConfigError as group:
        return f"{len(group.exceptions)} misconfigured"
    return "all valid"


print(summarise([{"name": "a"}, {}, {"name": 3}, {"name": 1}]))

# A group can also be raised, split and re-raised, which is the only
# way to add context to a group without losing its structure.
def with_context(rows: list[dict]) -> list[dict]:
    try:
        return validate_all(rows)
    except ExceptionGroup as eg:
        raise ExceptionGroup(eg.message, eg.exceptions) from eg

The design rules in the second block are the ones that separate a usable hierarchy from a decorative one. A str override that appends structured context means the exception is legible in a log without the caller having to know the attribute names. A stable rule identifier is what a caller should branch on, because messages are for humans and change. add_note attaches context to an existing exception without re-raising it, which is exactly what a per-item failure needs. And a group is raised once with all of its members, so the caller learns about every failure in one pass rather than one per repair cycle.

PropertyGoodBadWhy
Base classOne Exception subclass per libraryDeriving from BaseExceptionCallers need one clause to handle the library
DataAttributes plus a message that includes themMessage string onlyThe caller cannot parse a message reliably
DocstringStates "Raised when:"Describes the class onlyCallers need the conditions, not the definition
ConstructorAll arguments after message are keyword-onlyPositional extrasAdding a field cannot break a caller
RaisingTranslate with from excRe-raise the original from a deeper layerEach layer owns one level of abstraction
ScopeOne hierarchy per distributable packageOne exception per functionA caller cannot catch 200 exceptions selectively

Applies to: add_note and ExceptionGroup since 3.11; both are also available on BaseExceptionGroup. Requirements: a custom exception should define __init__ with a message first parameter and keyword-only extras, and should call super().__init__(message) so that args remains a one-tuple — several standard-library facilities and every serialisation of an exception depend on that shape. Caveats: exceptions are not picklable unless every constructor argument is recoverable from args; with **context in __init__ the extras are lost on unpickling, so a custom exception that must cross a process boundary should reconstruct itself in __reduce__. A __slots__ on an exception subclass prevents the instance dictionary the interpreter adds for args, so exception classes should not declare __slots__. Catching AppError in application code is correct; re-raising a translated exception from inside a library function is correct; defining a new exception type in a function body on every call is not, because except cannot catch a type it has never seen.

10.5 Exception groups and notes

ExceptionGroup, added in Python 3.11, is a single exception that contains a list of others. The except* clause unpacks it: it selects the sub-exceptions matching the clause’s type, runs the body for them, and re-raises the rest. It is the only construct in the language that can catch several exceptions of different types in one place.

import asyncio


# The canonical producer: a structured-concurrency block whose children
# all failed. The group appears whether one child or all of them raised.
async def main() -> None:
    async def ok(n: int) -> int:
        return n

    async def bad(kind: type[Exception], n: int) -> None:
        raise kind(f"task {n}")

    try:
        async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:
            tg.create_task(ok(1))
            tg.create_task(bad(ValueError, 2))
            tg.create_task(bad(KeyError, 3))
            tg.create_task(bad(ValueError, 4))
    except* ValueError as eg:
        print("value errors:", [str(e) for e in eg.exceptions])
    except* KeyError as eg:
        print("key errors:", [str(e) for e in eg.exceptions])
    # Two clauses, two types, one pass. Nothing is lost and nothing
    # needs to be re-raised by hand.


asyncio.run(main())


# The same shape, synchronously, for a batch validator. ValidationError
# is the one defined in 10.4.
def run_batch(items: list[int]) -> list[int]:
    """Fail-fast for programming errors, fail-complete for data errors."""
    results: list[int] = []
    data_errors: list[ValidationError] = []

    for item in items:
        if item < 0:
            data_errors.append(
                ValidationError("negative value", field="value", rule="range", value=item)
            )
            continue
        results.append(item * 2)

    if data_errors:
        raise ExceptionGroup("batch validation", data_errors)
    return results


def report(items: list[int]) -> str:
    try:
        return f"ok: {run_batch(items)}"
    except* ValidationError as eg:
        bad = [e.context.get("value") for e in eg.exceptions]
        return f"rejected {len(eg.exceptions)}: {bad}"


print(report([1, 2, 3]))
print(report([1, -1, 2, -3]))


# Splitting and regrouping: the only supported way to add context.
def regroup(eg: ExceptionGroup) -> ExceptionGroup:
    extra = [type(e)(str(e)) for e in eg.exceptions]
    merged = eg.exceptions + extra
    return ExceptionGroup(f"{eg.message} (x2)", merged)


# add_note: attach context to an exception already in flight.
def explain(err: Exception, detail: str) -> None:
    err.add_note(detail)          # 3.11+: appends to __notes__
    raise err


try:
    explain(ValueError("bad input"), "while parsing row 7")
except ValueError as exc:
    print(exc.__notes__)

# Notes are additive and unlimited, and they are printed after the
# message in the traceback.
try:
    raise RuntimeError("root cause")
except RuntimeError as root:
    root.add_note("while handling the request")
    try:
        raise LookupError("missing row")
    except LookupError as e:
        e.add_note("while resolving the primary key")
        e.add_note("table=users id=42")
        raise
except LookupError as final:
    print(final.__notes__)

The two cases where a group is the right shape, and the two where it is not. A group is right when several independent operations failed and the caller needs to know about all of them, and when concurrent children fail and structured concurrency has already collected them. A group is not right for a single failure, for failures that must be handled in a specific order, and for a chain of nested causes — that is raise ... from ..., which is a different relationship entirely.

Applies to: ExceptionGroup, BaseExceptionGroup, except* and add_note since 3.11. Requirements: except* may not be combined with a plain except in the same try, and a try with only except* clauses and no match re-raises the unmatched remainder automatically — there is no need for a trailing bare except. The body of an except* clause receives a group, so eg.exceptions is the list; if that list has one member the exception is re-raised unwrapped for that case only. Caveats: a group is a single object, so a caller that catches ExceptionGroup directly and then inspects .exceptions must handle arbitrarily deep nesting, because nested groups are legal and except* does not flatten them into the clause body. except* imposes a real run-time cost: it builds a filtered group and an unhandled remainder for every clause, so it belongs on an error path, not a hot one. BaseExceptionGroup derives from BaseException and is raised automatically by a TaskGroup when a child raises a BaseException that is not an Exception; catching it with a plain except Exception will not work, and the correct handling is except* BaseException or a deliberate propagation.

10.6 assert and the -O flag

assert is a debugging aid whose entire statement is removed when the interpreter runs with optimisation. It is documented as such: the manual states that assertions should not be used for validation, because -O and PYTHONOPTIMIZE remove them. A library that depends on an assertion for correctness is a library with a correctness mode switch.

# assert: removed entirely under -O. Never use it for validation.
def divide(a: float, b: float) -> float:
    assert b != 0, "b must be non-zero"      # GONE under -O
    return a / b


print(divide(1, 0))      # ZeroDivisionError, whether or not -O is set


# The difference, side by side, in a form that can be run both ways.
# Nothing needs importing: `__debug__` is a builtin constant that is
# False under -O, and `__debug__` is the whole mechanism.


def checked(x: int) -> int:
    assert x > 0, "x must be positive"
    return x


def validated(x: int) -> int:
    if x <= 0:
        raise ValueError("x must be positive")
    return x


print("__debug__ is", __debug__, "(False means -O is active)")
for fn in (checked, validated):
    try:
        fn(-1)
        print(f"{fn.__name__}: returned normally")
    except ValueError as exc:
        print(f"{fn.__name__}: ValueError {exc}")

# Under `python -O demo.py`:
#   __debug__ is False
#   checked:   returned normally     <-- the check vanished
#   validated: ValueError x must be positive


# The valid uses of assert.
def process(rows: list[int]) -> list[int]:
    assert isinstance(rows, list), "rows must be a list"

    # An internal invariant the type checker cannot express.
    for i in range(1, len(rows)):
        assert rows[i - 1] <= rows[i], "rows must be sorted"

    # A developer-facing sanity check, not a contract.
    assert all(isinstance(r, int) for r in rows), "rows must be ints"
    return [r * 2 for r in rows]


print(process([1, 2, 3]))


# A property whose precondition is also a postcondition benefit.
class Buffer:
    def __init__(self, size: int) -> None:
        assert size > 0, "size must be positive"     # internal invariant
        self._size = size
        self._data = bytearray(size)

    def write(self, offset: int, payload: bytes) -> None:
        assert 0 <= offset <= self._size, "offset in range"
        self._data[offset:offset + len(payload)] = payload
        assert len(self._data) == self._size, "size invariant preserved"

# The mechanical conversion: every assert becomes a raise.
import ast
import textwrap
