def asserts_to_raises(source: str, *, indent: str = "    ") -> str:
    """Rewrite `assert` statements into `if not ...: raise AssertionError`.

    A proof of concept for the migration, using ast so the test
    expression is preserved exactly rather than re-parsed from text.
    """
    tree = ast.parse(source)
    lines = source.splitlines()
    out: list[str] = []

    for node in tree.body:
        if not isinstance(node, ast.Assert):
            out.extend(lines[node.lineno - 1:node.end_lineno])
            continue
        test = ast.get_source_segment(source, node.test) or "False"
        if node.msg is not None:
            msg = ast.get_source_segment(source, node.msg) or ""
            body = f"raise AssertionError({msg})"
        else:
            body = "raise AssertionError()"
        out.append(f"if not ({test}):")
        out.append(f"{indent}{body}")
    return textwrap.dedent("\n".join(out))


BEFORE = textwrap.dedent('''
    def f(x: int) -> int:
        assert x > 0, "x must be positive"
        assert x % 2 == 0
        return x
''')

print("BEFORE"); print(BEFORE)
print("AFTER");  print(asserts_to_raises(BEFORE))

The assert form has three properties the explicit form does not: the message is optional and defaults to the stringified test, the check is skipped entirely under -O, and a bare assert is the fastest possible check because it compiles to a single conditional jump. The explicit raise has the opposite three: it always runs, it requires a message or a default one, and it costs an exception construction. For a check on a hot path in a hot loop where the condition is provably satisfied by the caller, the first set is genuinely better.

Where an assert must be replaced rather than kept. (1) Any check that validates untrusted input — a request parameter, a file’s contents, a network response — because the code path that produces untrusted input is exactly the path where a silent -O removal turns a clean rejection into a corrupted state. (2) Any check that enforces a public contract, including a library’s documented precondition, because a caller compiling with -O importing a module that does not is an asymmetry, not a safety measure. (3) Any check that guards a security property, such as an authorisation assertion, which must never be removable. Conversely, an assertion that documents a relationship between two locals the code itself just established is a good assertion and should stay. How to tell them apart: if the condition can be violated by a caller or by input, it is validation. If it can only be violated by a defect in the function itself, it is an assertion.

Applies to: assert since 1.5; __debug__ is the documented flag it tests and is False under -O, -OO or any non-empty PYTHONOPTIMIZE. Requirements: the statement is removed by the compiler, not skipped at run time, so an assert whose condition has a side effect has that side effect removed as well. Caveats: -OO additionally strips docstrings, which silently breaks any code that reads __doc__ at run time for dispatch, for documentation generation, or for a plugin registry keyed by docstring. Optimisation is inherited by every process the interpreter starts, including test runners and subprocesses, so a PYTHONOPTIMIZE=1 in the environment applies the whole tree and not only the entry point. Neither flag is a supported production configuration for anything that relies on assertions; they exist to make the interpreter itself faster in trusted deployments.

10.7 Suppression, warnings and conventions

Three mechanisms sit between "raise" and "propagate": suppression, which discards an error deliberately; warnings, which report a condition that is not an error; and retry, which re-attempts an operation that failed transiently. Each has a documented place, and the discipline is knowing which one a given failure calls for.

import contextlib
import os
import warnings

# contextlib.suppress: the explicit form of a deliberate swallow.
def read_optional(path: str) -> str | None:
    with contextlib.suppress(FileNotFoundError):
        return open(path, encoding="utf-8").read()
    return None                     # reached when the file was absent


# suppress is NOT except-pass: it is scoped, it names the exception, and
# it re-raises everything else. The comparison:
def wrong(path: str) -> str | None:
    try:
        return open(path, encoding="utf-8").read()
    except:                        # swallows KeyboardInterrupt too
        return None

# suppress nests, so it composes with other context managers.
def read_first_line(path: str) -> str:
    with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as handle:
        with contextlib.suppress(StopIteration):
            return next(handle).rstrip("\n")
    return ""


# The cleanup-on-error pattern, which is what suppress is for.
def move_file(src: str, dst: str) -> None:
    os.rename(src, dst)
    with contextlib.suppress(FileNotFoundError):
        os.unlink(src + ".bak")


print(read_optional("no-such-file-here"))
print(read_first_line(__file__)[:60])


# The warnings module: a condition worth reporting that is not an error.
def deprecated(fn, *, since: str, removal: str):
    """Mark a callable deprecated without breaking its callers."""
    import functools

    @functools.wraps(fn)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        warnings.warn(
            f"{fn.__name__} is deprecated since {since} and will be "
            f"removed in {removal}; use the replacement instead",
            DeprecationWarning,
            stacklevel=2,          # the CALLER's line, not this wrapper's
        )
        return fn(*args, **kwargs)
    return wrapper


@deprecated(since="3.12", removal="3.16")
def old_parse(raw: str) -> object:
    return {"raw": raw}


with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as caught:
    warnings.simplefilter("always")
    old_parse("x")
    for w in caught:
        print(f"{w.category.__name__}: {w.message}")
        print("  reported at:", w.filename, "line", w.lineno)
        print("  filter default:", w.category.__name__ in
              ("DeprecationWarning", "PendingDeprecationWarning"))

stacklevel is the parameter that decides whether a warning is useful. The default of 1 attributes the warning to the line inside the library that called warn, which is exactly the line the caller cannot change. stacklevel=2 moves it to the caller of the library function, and a wrapper that adds a frame needs 2 for its own caller and 3 for the caller’s caller. A warning whose reported location is inside the library is the most common reason deprecation warnings are ignored.

# The warning filters, and why the defaults are what they are.
import warnings
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

print("default filters:")
for f in warnings.filters:
    print("  ", f)

# The categories and their default visibility.
print("DeprecationWarning default: ignored, unless triggered by __main__")
print("UserWarning default:         always shown")
print("FutureWarning default:       always shown, aimed at end users")
print("ResourceWarning default:     ignored, shown by the test suite")
print("EncodingWarning default:     ignored since 3.10, shown by default in 3.15")

# A filter is (action, message-regex, category, module-regex, lineno).
# Setting one explicitly requires matching every element.
warnings.filterwarnings(
    "error",                        # turn matching warnings into errors
    category=DeprecationWarning,
    module=r"^mylib",
)

with warnings.catch_warnings():
    warnings.simplefilter("error", RuntimeWarning)
    try:
        warnings.warn("now fatal", RuntimeWarning)
    except RuntimeWarning as exc:
        print("converted to an exception:", exc)

# PYTHONWARNINGS overrides filters entirely at startup:
#   PYTHONWARNINGS=error            every warning is fatal
#   PYTHONWARNINGS=ignore::DeprecationWarning
#   PYTHONWARNINGS=default          show each unique location once
#   PYTHONWARNINGS=always           show every occurrence

# getcontext().prec is the analogue for Decimal: a limit that removes
# precision silently. Assert on it in a numerical code path.
print("decimal precision:", getcontext().prec)
print("Decimal is exact where float is not:", Decimal("0.1") + Decimal("0.2"))

# Retry policy placement: the caller decides, the callee does not.
from __future__ import annotations
import random
import time
from typing import Callable, TypeVar

T = TypeVar("T")


class TransientError(Exception):
    """A failure that may succeed if attempted again."""


class PermanentError(Exception):
    """A failure that will not change on a second attempt."""


def fetch(url: str) -> bytes:
    if "unstable" in url:
        raise TransientError(f"503 from {url}")
    if "bad" in url:
        raise PermanentError(f"404 from {url}")
    return b"ok"


def with_retry(fn: Callable[[], T], *, attempts: int = 3,
               base_delay: float = 0.01,
               retry_on: tuple[type[BaseException], ...] = (TransientError,),
               ) -> T:
    """The CALLER owns the policy: attempts, delay, and what is retryable.

    The callee raises TransientError for a failure that might succeed and
    PermanentError for one that will not. It does not know whether the
    caller wants a retry, and it must never sleep on its own.
    """
    last: BaseException | None = None
    for attempt in range(1, attempts + 1):
        try:
            return fn()
        except retry_on as exc:
            last = exc
            if attempt == attempts:
                break
            # Exponential backoff with full jitter, bounded.
            delay = min(base_delay * (2 ** (attempt - 1)), 1.0)
            time.sleep(random.uniform(0, delay))
    assert last is not None
    raise last


# The two ways the placement goes wrong.
try:
    with_retry(lambda: fetch("bad"))
except PermanentError as exc:
    print("not retried, correctly:", exc)

# Retrying a PermanentError is how a 3-attempt timeout policy turns into
# a 3x load multiplier against a service that is already failing.

Applies to: warnings.catch_warnings and simplefilter since 2.6; EncodingWarning since 3.10 and enabled by default in 3.15; ResourceWarning enabled by default under -X dev since 3.x. Requirements: a library that emits a warning must pass an explicit stacklevel and an explicit category; the default category UserWarning is almost never the right one for a library, because end users see it and cannot act on it. Caveats: warnings.catch_warnings is not thread-safe before 3.11 in the sense that it mutates global interpreter state; from 3.11 the contextlib.catch_warnings context is safe against concurrent use only where the action is "error", and a library must never suppress warnings around a yield to a caller, because the suppression then spans caller code it does not own. A DeprecationWarning is ignored by default unless it originates in __main__, which is why end users never see a library’s deprecation — the test suite must enable it explicitly, and pytest does so by default. A warning is not a control-flow mechanism: it is a diagnostic channel, and a program whose correctness depends on a warning not being raised is a program with a hidden dependency on the filter configuration. Free-threaded builds: warnings.filters is process-global mutable state; under PEP 703 two threads mutating filters concurrently can interleave, so filter configuration belongs in single-threaded startup code.

Exercises 10

  1. Print the full issubclass tree of BaseException programmatically from BaseException.__subclasses__ recursively, and confirm it matches the diagram in 10.1 including the two structural edges.
  2. Write three functions with an identical body whose only difference is the width of the try block — one statement, one call, ten statements — and produce an exception from deep inside the chain to show which of them misattributes the failure.
  3. Produce all five rows of the chaining table for one underlying exception, and print __cause__, __context__ and __suppress_context__ for each.
  4. Build a three-level exception hierarchy with structured attributes and a __str__ that renders the context, then raise each subclass from a function that catches a low-level KeyError and translates it with from.
  5. Write a batch validator that collects every failure into an ExceptionGroup, handle it with two except* clauses of different types, and add a note to each member naming its row index.
  6. Run a module that relies on an assert for input validation under both python file.py and python -O file.py, and record the two different behaviours.
  7. Emit a deprecation warning from a two-frame wrapper and vary stacklevel from 1 to 3, printing w.filename and w.lineno for each to show which attributes the caller’s line.
  8. Implement a retry helper with exponential backoff and full jitter, drive it with a fake operation that fails transiently twice then succeeds, and show that a PermanentError is not retried.

11 · Standard Library Essentials

11.1 collections: deque, Counter, defaultdict11.2 itertools: lazy combinators11.3 pathlib11.4 datetime, timedelta and zoneinfo11.5 Text processing and re11.6 json, configparser and tomllib11.7 logging11.8 subprocess

The standard library is not a list of alternatives to the built-ins; it is a set of specialised implementations of operations that are either easy to get wrong or expensive to write. This chapter covers the eight modules whose cost of misuse exceeds their cost of use.

11.1 collections: deque, Counter, defaultdict

The built-in containers cover the common cases; collections covers the cases where the built-in is the wrong data structure. Three of its types replace a built-in outright — deque for a queue, Counter for frequency analysis, defaultdict for grouping — and the rest support patterns the built-ins cannot express without a wrapper.

import sys
import timeit
from collections import deque

# deque: O(1) at BOTH ends. list is O(1) at the right and O(n) at the left.
print("list.pop(0) is O(n): it memmoves every remaining element.")
setup = "from collections import deque; d = list(range(1000)); q = deque(range(1000))"
print("list.pop(0) x1000 :", f"{timeit.timeit('for _ in range(1000): d.pop(0)', setup, number=100):.4f}s")
print("deque.popleft x1000:", f"{timeit.timeit('for _ in range(1000): q.popleft()', setup, number=100):.4f}s")

q = deque(["a", "b", "c"])
q.append("d")            # right
q.appendleft("z")        # left
print(list(q), len(q), q[0], q[-1])
print(q.popleft(), q.pop())
print(q.maxlen)           # None: unbounded

# maxlen makes the deque a bounded queue with automatic eviction.
window = deque(maxlen=3)
for n in range(6):
    window.append(n)
print(list(window))      # [3, 4, 5]: the oldest fell off

# rotate and the other deque operations.
ring = deque(range(5))
ring.rotate(2)
print(list(ring))        # [3, 4, 0, 1, 2]
ring.rotate(-2)
print(list(ring))        # back to [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]
print(ring.count(2), 2 in ring, ring.index(2))
print(ring.remove(2), list(ring))

# deque is a real sequence: indexing in the middle is O(n).
print(deque.__mro__)

The cost of list.pop(0) is not the copy itself but the fact that it is linear, which makes a left-consuming loop quadratic. A thousand pop(0) calls on a list of a thousand elements perform about half a million element moves; the deque version performs none. For a bounded sliding window, deque(maxlen=n) is both the correct structure and the shortest possible implementation, and it needs no eviction logic at all.

import re
from collections import Counter, defaultdict

# Counter: frequency, plus the most() method and arithmetic.
words = "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog the fox".split()
c = Counter(words)
print(c.most_common(3))
print(c["the"], c["absent"])        # absent is 0, NOT a KeyError
print(c.most_common())              # ties broken by first appearance
print(c.total(), len(c), list(c.elements())[:5])

# Counter supports set-like operations and arithmetic.
a = Counter("abracadabra")
b = Counter("bazaam")
print(a & b, a | b)              # intersection: min counts; union: max
print(a + b)                        # sums the counts
print(b - a)                        # positive differences only
print(list(a.keys()), list(a.items())[:3])

# Counter over a mapping counts the values, not the keys.
print(Counter({"a": 3, "b": 3, "c": 1}).most_common())
# Counter over anything countable: str, list, Counter, itertools chain.
print(Counter("mississippi").most_common(2))
print(Counter(re.findall(r"\w+", "a b a")).most_common(1))

# The first-N-heaviest problem, done in one call and no sort.
print(Counter(words).most_common(2))

# defaultdict: a missing key is created on read, using default_factory.
d = defaultdict(list)
for w in words:
    d[w[0]].append(w)
print(dict(sorted(d.items())))
print(d["z"])                       # []: created, not KeyError
print(len(d))                       # the read above ADDED a key

# The three shapes it replaces.
pairs = [("x", 1), ("y", 2), ("x", 3)]
out: dict[str, list[int]] = defaultdict(list)
for k, v in pairs:
    out[k].append(v)
print(dict(out))
out2: dict[str, int] = defaultdict(int)
for k, v in pairs:
    out2[k] += v
print(dict(out2))
out3: dict[str, set] = defaultdict(set)
for k, v in pairs:
    out3[k].add(v)
print(dict(out3))

The default_factory is called with no arguments, so a lambda or a named type is required — defaultdict(list) and defaultdict(lambda: []) are not interchangeable, and the lambda form is measurably slower because it is an extra call per miss. The trap is that a read of a missing key mutates the mapping, so d["z"] inside a membership test changes the object’s size. Use dict.get or a membership test on d when the read is not meant to insert.

from collections import namedtuple
from typing import NamedTuple

# collections.namedtuple: a tuple subclass with named fields.
Point = namedtuple("Point", "x y")
p = Point(1, 2)
print(p.x, p.y, p[0], p._asdict())
print(p._replace(y=9), p._fields, len(p))
q = Point._make([3, 4])
print(q, q == Point(3, 4), hash(q) is not None)
# Point(1)              -> TypeError: two fields are required
# p.x = 5               -> AttributeError: a namedtuple is immutable

# typing.NamedTuple: the same runtime object, with annotations, defaults
# and methods. This is the 3.6+ spelling and should be preferred.
class Version(NamedTuple):
    major: int
    minor: int = 0
    channel: str = "stable"

    def label(self) -> str:
        return f"{self.major}.{self.minor} ({self.channel})"

    @classmethod
    def parse(cls, raw: str) -> "Version":
        major, _, rest = raw.partition(".")
        return cls(int(major), int(rest or 0))


v = Version(3, 14, "bugfix")
print(v, v.label(), v.major)
print(v._replace(minor=15), v._asdict())
print(Version.parse("3.14"), Version(3))    # the default minor applies
print(isinstance(v, tuple), isinstance(v, Point))

# Why the distinction matters: namedtuple cannot express a default, an
# annotation, or a method. Attempting any of the three raises at class
# creation, not at use:
#
#   Bad = namedtuple("Bad", "x y", defaults=[1, 2])   # 3.7+: works
#   Bad2 = namedtuple("Bad2", "x: int y: int")       # fields become
#                                                    # literally named
#                                                    # "x: int"
#   class Bad3(namedtuple("Bad3", "x")): ...          # a method is a
#                                                    # FIELD, not a method

from collections import ChainMap, UserList, OrderedDict

# ChainMap: several mappings presented as one. Lookup is left to right;
# writes go to the FIRST mapping only. Deleting searches every mapping.
defaults = {"theme": "light", "retries": 3}
overrides = {"theme": "dark"}
cfg = ChainMap(overrides, defaults)
print(dict(cfg))
print(cfg["theme"], cfg["retries"])
cfg["theme"] = "solarized"        # written to overrides
print(cfg.maps[0], cfg.maps[1])
print(cfg.get("missing", "fallback"))
print("retries" in cfg)
del cfg["retries"]                # removed from defaults, the owner
print(cfg.maps[1])
print(cfg.new_child({"retries": 9}))     # a new ChainMap, cfg unchanged
print(ChainMap.__doc__.splitlines()[0])

# OrderedDict: only needed for move_to_end and popitem(last=...).
od = OrderedDict(a=1, b=2, c=3)
od.move_to_end("a")
print(list(od))
print(od.popitem(last=False))      # the oldest, FIFO
print(od)

# UserDict / UserList / UserString: dict/list/str subclasses that
# delegate to a contained object. The point is to override ONE operation
# and have every other one stay correct, which a plain dict subclass
# cannot do because the C-level methods bypass the subclass.
class LowerKeys(dict):
    def __setitem__(self, key, value):
        super().__setitem__(key.lower(), value)


print(LowerKeys({"A": 1}))
# print(LowerKeys({"A": 1})["A"])  -> KeyError: update bypassed __setitem__


class TrackedList(UserList):
    def append(self, item):
        print("  appending", item)
        super().append(item)


tl = TrackedList([1, 2])
tl.append(3)
tl.insert(0, 0)                   # routed through UserList, still correct
print(tl, type(tl).__mro__[1].__name__)

Applies to: deque(maxlen=...) and deque.rotate since 3.2; Counter equality semantics relaxed in 3.10 to ignore zero and negative counts; namedtuple keyword defaults in 3.7; ChainMap.new_child since 3.4; UserList and UserString since 3.3. Requirements: a namedtuple field name cannot be a keyword, cannot start with an underscore, and must be a valid identifier; a duplicate field name is a ValueError at class creation. Caveats: Counter is a dict subclass, so it inherits update and |= from dict, which adds rather than counts — a genuine source of silent wrong answers, and the reason collections.Counter(a, b) is correct while Counter(a); c.update(b) is not. defaultdict is not picklable in a useful way on some paths because the factory must be importable, and its copy is shallow, so the shared factory is shared between copies. A ChainMap write always targets maps[0], which is a frequent surprise when the first mapping is intended as read-only. UserDict and friends solve the C-level bypass problem that makes a hand-written dict subclass unreliable; that is their entire reason to exist, and it is why a plain subclass is the wrong choice for overriding one operation. Cross-reference: the underlying containers and their mutability and ordering guarantees are tabulated in section 2.3.

11.2 itertools: lazy combinators

Every function in itertools returns an iterator, consumes its input iterators eagerly and produces output lazily. The combinator functions — product, permutations, combinations — are the exception: they materialise their input pools into tuples first.

import itertools
import operator

# The two categories, and the difference that matters.
lazy = itertools.islice(itertools.count(10, 2), 4)
print(list(lazy))                 # [10, 12, 14, 16] -- nothing materialised

# groupby: THE gotcha. It groups CONSECUTIVE equal keys, so the input
# must be sorted on the same key. A dict comprehension with groupby over
# unsorted data silently produces one group per element.
data = [("b", 2), ("a", 1), ("b", 3), ("a", 4)]

print("unsorted groupby:")
for key, group in itertools.groupby(data, key=operator.itemgetter(0)):
    print("  ", key, [v for _, v in group])   # four groups, not two

print("sorted groupby:")
for key, group in itertools.groupby(sorted(data), key=operator.itemgetter(0)):
    print("  ", key, [v for _, v in group])   # two groups, correct

# The safe wrapper, and the cost of getting it wrong.
from itertools import groupby
from operator import itemgetter


def group_rows(rows: list, key):
    """groupby that sorts first. The sort is O(n log n); the mistake
    is O(n) groups of size 1, which is worse than useless."""
    return groupby(sorted(rows, key=key), key=key)


for k, g in group_rows(data, itemgetter(0)):
    print("  grouped", k, [v for _, v in g])

# islice: a lazy slice. Unlike a list slice it cannot be resized, and
# its arguments are positional: start, stop[, step].
src = range(20)
print(list(itertools.islice(src, 5)))
print(list(itertools.islice(src, 5, 10)))
print(list(itertools.islice(src, 0, 20, 3)))
print(list(itertools.islice(src, 15, None)))    # None: to the end

# tee: independent copies of one iterator, so two consumers can share a
# source. The memory cost is real: each copy buffers every element the
# other has not yet consumed.
a, b = itertools.tee(itertools.count())
print(next(a), next(a), next(b))   # 0 1 0: b is a full copy

import itertools

# tee's memory profile, measured rather than asserted.
def tee_profile(n: int) -> list[int]:
    """Consume one branch far ahead; the other must buffer all of it."""
    left, right = itertools.tee(itertools.count(), 2)
    out = [next(left) for _ in range(n)]      # left runs n ahead
    out.append(right.__length_hint__())       # 0: not a reliable hint
    next(right)                               # right now reads 0
    return out


print(tee_profile(5))

# The alternative that costs nothing: materialise once.
vals = list(range(5))
print([vals[0], vals[4], vals[2]])

# product / permutations / combinations. All three materialise their
# input pools as tuples, so a pool of 10^7 items is a memory problem
# before the first result is produced.
print(list(itertools.product([1, 2], "ab")))          # 4 pairs
print(list(itertools.product([0, 1], repeat=3)))      # 8 triples
print(list(itertools.permutations("abc", 2)))         # ordered, no repeat
print(list(itertools.combinations("abcd", 2)))        # unordered
print(list(itertools.combinations_with_replacement("ab", 2)))
print(list(itertools.product(range(2), repeat=2), ) == [(0,0),(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)])

# accumulate: prefix aggregation, with any binary function.
print(list(itertools.accumulate([1, 2, 3, 4])))               # [1,3,6,10]
print(list(itertools.accumulate([1, 2, 3], operator.mul)))   # [1,2,6]
print(list(itertools.accumulate([1, 2, 3], initial=0)))      # [0,1,3,6]
print(list(itertools.accumulate([1, 5, 3], max)))            # [1,5,5]

# Running maximum, the classic use, with no sentinel needed.
print(list(itertools.accumulate([3, 1, 4, 1, 5], max)))

# compress and select: filtering by a parallel selector.
letters = "abcdefg"
mask = [True, False, True, True, False, True, False]
print("".join(itertools.compress(letters, mask)))
print(list(itertools.compress([1, 2, 3, 4], [0, 1, 0, 1])))
print(list(itertools.compress(letters, [1] * len(letters))))   # all of them

# pairwise: successive overlapping pairs, not disjoint chunks.
print(list(itertools.pairwise([1, 2, 3, 4])))     # [(1,2),(2,3),(3,4)]
print(list(itertools.pairwise([1])))              # []
print(list(itertools.pairwise([])))               # []
# Added in 3.10. Before that: zip(seq, seq[1:]).
seq = [1, 2, 3, 4]
print(list(zip(seq, seq[1:])))

# zip_longest: the padding form, so a mismatch is visible.
from itertools import zip_longest
print(list(zip_longest("abc", "de", fillvalue="-")))
# [('a','d'), ('b','e'), ('c','-')]

# takewhile / dropwhile: stateful prefix filters.
print(list(itertools.takewhile(lambda n: n < 3, [1, 2, 3, 1, 2])))
print(list(itertools.dropwhile(lambda n: n < 3, [1, 2, 3, 1, 2])))
# takewhile and dropwhile are the difference between "stop at the first
# failure" and "skip the leading run"; neither filters the whole input.

# chain / chain.from_iterable: flattening without materialising.
print(list(itertools.chain([1, 2], (3, 4), "56")))
print(list(itertools.chain.from_iterable([[1, 2], [3]])))
print(list(itertools.chain.from_iterable("abc")))

The groupby defect is the most consequential in the module, because it produces correct-looking output. sorted before groupby costs O(n log n) and is almost always worth it; the alternative — accumulating into a dict of lists — costs O(n) and is a one-liner. takewhile and dropwhile are prefix operations, not filters, and the distinction matters for sorted input: takewhile over a sorted list gives a genuine range, while over unsorted input it stops at the first violation wherever that occurs.

FunctionMaterialises inputMaterialises outputMemoryNotes
count, cycle, repeatNoNoO(1)cycle and repeat cache what they replay; use islice or zip to bound them
islice, takewhile, dropwhileNoNoO(1)The lazy filters
chain, compress, zip_longestNoNoO(k)zip_longest holds one item per input
pairwise, accumulateNoNoO(1)accumulate holds the running value
groupbyNoNoO(1)Groups consecutive keys only; the group iterator is invalidated by the next next() on the groupby
teeNoNoO(n) for the lagging copyThe only function here with unbounded memory growth
product, permutations, combinationsYesNoO(sum of pool sizes)Each input is read once into a tuple
starmapNoNoO(1)The inverse of zip: zip(*it) → starmap(f, it)

Four itertools traps. (1) Unbounded generators. itertools.count(), cycle() and repeat() never end. Every one of them in production code must be bounded by islice, zip or enumerate with a length, and the bound must be computed rather than written as a literal wherever the input size is not known. (2) The invalidated group iterator. The iterator returned by groupby for one key is only valid until the groupby is advanced; storing it and consuming it later yields the wrong elements, silently. Consume each group before advancing. (3) tee memory. tee is not free: the copy that lags buffers everything the leader has consumed. In a pipeline where one branch is a fast filter and the other is a slow writer, the buffer is the size of the whole intermediate result, which is precisely the cost the lazy design was meant to avoid. (4) product factorial growth. itertools.product(range(10), repeat=6) produces a million tuples; it is a combinatorial enumeration, not a lazy filter, and a guard on the pool size belongs at the call site.

Applies to: pairwise since 3.10; batched since 3.12; zip(strict=True) since 3.10, which is the built-in answer to zip_longest for a length assertion. itertools.batched replaces the hand-written [seq[i:i+n] for i in range(0, len(seq), n)] and is lazy. Requirements: islice takes positional arguments and accepts None for an open end, but does not accept negative values, so islice(seq, -3, None) is a ValueError; use seq[-3:] for a suffix. Caveats: the combinator functions consume each input exactly once into a tuple at construction, so passing a generator to combinations is fine but passing an infinite one hangs immediately. accumulate without initial omits the first element, so accumulate([5]) is [5] and accumulate([], initial=0) is [0] while accumulate([]) is [] — three different behaviours for an empty input depending on the argument. starmap passes the unpacked tuple, so a function written for map must be adapted. On a free-threaded build these functions hold no shared mutable state and are unaffected by the absence of the GIL.

Sort before groupby, or use a defaultdict and do not use groupby at all. The defaultdict form is O(n), always correct, and one line; the sorted groupby form is O(n log n) and streams, which matters only when the input does not fit in memory or is itself a generator. Choosing groupby for an unsorted iterable is the single most common itertools mistake, and it produces plausible output rather than an error.

11.3 pathlib

os.path is a string-manipulation API that models a filesystem; pathlib is an object API that is a filesystem path. Every pathlib operation returns a new path object, joins with the correct separator, and preserves the path flavour, which is what makes the API composable.

import os
import os.path
import tempfile
from pathlib import Path, PurePosixPath, PureWindowsPath

# The two APIs, on the same operation.
p_str = "data" + os.sep + "input.csv"
print(os.path.join("data", "input.csv"))
print(str(Path("data") / "input.csv"))
print(os.path.isabs(p_str), Path(p_str).is_absolute())
print(os.path.splitext("a/b.txt"), (Path("a/b.txt").suffix,
                                    Path("a/b.txt").stem,
                                    Path("a/b.txt").name,
                                    Path("a/b.txt").with_suffix(".json").name))

# A Path is immutable and every operation returns a new Path.
base = Path("/srv") / "app"
print(base, base.parent, base.name, base.parts, base.anchor)
print(base / "data" / "..", base.joinpath("a", "b"))

# Pure paths: parsing and manipulation with NO filesystem access. The
# flavour is fixed at construction, so a PurePosixPath never emits a
# backslash even on Windows.
print(PurePosixPath("/a/b/../c").as_posix())         # '/a/c'
print(PurePosixPath("/a/b/").name, PurePosixPath("/a/b/").parts)
print(PureWindowsPath("C:/a/b").drive, PureWindowsPath("C:/a/b").root)
print(PurePosixPath("/a/b").relative_to("/a"))
print(PurePosixPath("/a/b/c").is_relative_to("/a"))  # 3.9+
try:
    PurePosixPath("/a/b").relative_to("/x")
except ValueError as exc:
    print("ValueError:", type(exc).__name__)

# Parts, and the match/glob family.
p = Path("/var/log/app/server.log")
print(p.parts, p.suffix, p.suffixes, p.stem, p.stem.rstrip(".log"))
print(p.with_name("access.log"), p.with_stem("access"))
print(p.match("*.log"), p.match("server.*"))
print(p.parent.glob("*.log"))

# The complete read/write/resolution set.
with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
    root = Path(tmp)
    (root / "a").mkdir()
    (root / "a" / "deep").mkdir(parents=True)
    (root / "a" / "f.txt").write_text("one\n", encoding="utf-8")
    (root / "a" / "b.bin").write_bytes(b"\x00\x01")

    f = root / "a" / "f.txt"
    print(f.exists(), f.is_file(), f.is_dir(), f.is_symlink())
    print(f.stat().st_size, oct(f.stat().st_mode)[-3:])
    print(f.read_text(encoding="utf-8").strip())
    print(f.read_bytes())
    f.write_text("two\n", encoding="utf-8")
    print(f.read_text(encoding="utf-8").strip())

    # Atomic replace: write to a sibling, then rename over the target.
    tmpf = f.with_suffix(".tmp")
    tmpf.write_text("three\n", encoding="utf-8")
    tmpf.replace(f)
    print(f.read_text(encoding="utf-8").strip())

    # Globbing: ** is recursive, and the patterns are fnmatch syntax.
    print(sorted(x.name for x in root.rglob("*")))
    print(sorted(str(x.relative_to(root)) for x in root.glob("a/*")))
    print(sorted(str(x.relative_to(root)) for x in root.glob("**/*.txt")))

    # Walking, with the pruning that Path.walk supports (3.12+).
    for dirpath, dirnames, filenames in root.walk():
        print("  walk:", Path(dirpath).name, sorted(dirnames), sorted(filenames))

    # Permissions and the chmod surface.
    f.chmod(0o600)
    print(oct(f.stat().st_mode)[-3:])

    # The rename and remove family.
    other = root / "moved.txt"
    f.rename(other)
    print(other.exists(), f.exists())
    other.unlink()
    print(other.exists())
    (root / "a" / "deep").rmdir()
    (root / "a").rmdir()

Three properties of pathlib that make it preferable to os.path in new code. First, the operators compose: / joins and rejects an absolute right-hand operand, which makes base / name the only correct spelling and os.path.join(base, name) the one that silently discards base when name is absolute. Second, resolve() and absolute() are distinct and both are explicit: resolve() makes the path absolute and resolves symlinks and .., which means it touches the filesystem and will raise on a permission error, while absolute() does not. Third, with_suffix and with_name are non-destructive; the os.path equivalents return a string and are easy to apply to the wrong variable.

import os
import tempfile
from pathlib import Path

# Temp directory helpers, added in 3.10. They clean up on context exit.
with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
    print("temp dir removed on exit:", tmp)

# TemporaryFile is unlinked immediately; the name is never reusable.
with tempfile.TemporaryFile("w+", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    f.write("scratch")
    f.seek(0)
    print("anonymous temp file:", f.read())

# NamedTemporaryFile: the name exists, the file is deleted on close.
with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile("w+", suffix=".log", delete=True) as f:
    print("named:", os.path.basename(f.name), Path(f.name).suffix)
    f.write("x")
# f.name no longer resolves

# The low-level primitives, when a specific mode is required.
d = tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="py-")
try:
    p = Path(d) / "f"
    fd = os.open(p, os.O_CREAT | os.O_EXCL | os.O_WRONLY, 0o600)
    with os.fdopen(fd, "w") as f:
        f.write("secure")
    print("created with 0600:", oct(p.stat().st_mode)[-3:])
finally:
    os.rmdir(d)                      # the dir is empty: rmdir, not rmdirs

# shutil: the operations that are more than a rename.
import shutil
from pathlib import Path

with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:
    src = Path(tmp) / "src"
    dst = Path(tmp) / "dst"
    src.mkdir()
    (src / "f.txt").write_text("body", encoding="utf-8")

    shutil.copytree(src, dst)
    print("copytree:", sorted(x.name for x in dst.iterdir()))
    shutil.copy(src / "f.txt", dst / "g.txt")
    shutil.copy2(src / "f.txt", dst / "h.txt")   # preserves mtime
    print("copy2 mtime equal:", (dst / "h.txt").stat().st_mtime
          == (src / "f.txt").stat().st_mtime)
    shutil.rmtree(dst)
    print("removed:", dst.exists())
    print("which:", shutil.which("python3") or "not on PATH")

Applies to: Path.walk since 3.12, replacing os.walk in new code; Path.is_relative_to and Path.hardlink_to since 3.9; Path.readlink and Path.is_junction since 3.9 and 3.12; Path.with_segments since 3.12. Requirements: a Path operation that writes or deletes does not create or clean up; the corresponding mkdir, unlink and rmdir calls are the caller’s responsibility, and missing_ok=True is the explicit opt-in to idempotence. Caveats: Path.resolve() on a path with a symlink loop raises RuntimeError, and on a path whose parent is unreadable raises PermissionError; use absolute() where the filesystem must not be consulted. Path.exists() returns False rather than raising for a broken symlink, so a link whose target is missing needs Path.is_symlink() to detect. Path.glob raises on an absolute pattern on some versions and silently returns nothing on others; a relative pattern is the only portable form. Path.rmdir requires the directory to be empty, and shutil.rmtree is the recursive form — using the wrong one is the most common cause of a FileNotFoundError in a cleanup path. Cross-platform: Path instantiates as WindowsPath or PosixPath according to os.name, so code that must behave identically everywhere must construct PurePosixPath or PureWindowsPath explicitly; the pathlib.PurePath base class is an alias for the platform flavour and is not platform-neutral.

11.4 datetime, timedelta and zoneinfo

The datetime module defines seven types. The distinction that governs every decision in this area is naive versus aware: a naive datetime has no timezone and no UTC offset, an aware one has both, and the two cannot be compared to each other. Arithmetic between them raises TypeError, which is the language refusing to guess.

from datetime import date, datetime, time, timedelta, timezone, UTC

# The seven types, and what each is for.
d = date(2026, 8, 5)                     # a calendar date
t = time(14, 30, 5, 123456)              # a wall-clock time
dt = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 14, 30, 5)     # date + time, no timezone
aware = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 14, 30, 5, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
print(d, t.isoformat(), dt.isoformat(), aware.isoformat())
print(d.year, d.month, d.weekday(), d.isoweekday(), d.timetuple().tm_yday)
print(dt.date(), dt.time(), dt.timetz(), dt.timestamp())
print(datetime.min, datetime.max, datetime.resolution)

# The naive/aware rule, enforced.
try:
    print(dt < aware)
except TypeError as exc:
    print("TypeError:", type(exc).__name__,
          "- a naive and an aware datetime cannot be compared")

# Construction from components and from a string.
print(datetime(2026, 8, 5, 14, 30, tzinfo=timezone.utc))
print(datetime.fromordinal(date(2026, 8, 5).toordinal()))
print(datetime.fromtimestamp(0, tz=timezone.utc).isoformat())
print(datetime.fromisoformat("2026-08-05T14:30:00+00:00"))
print(datetime.strptime("05/08/2026 14:30", "%d/%m/%Y %H:%M"))

# toordinal/fromordinal is exact and locale-independent; timestamp() is
# not, because it is a Unix epoch value and therefore UTC-relative.
print(date(2026, 8, 5).toordinal(), date.fromordinal(739573))
print(datetime(2026, 8, 5).timestamp() > 0)

# timedelta: days, seconds, microseconds. All three are normalised into
# a single total, and days may be fractional.
one_day = timedelta(days=1)
print(one_day, one_day.total_seconds(), repr(one_day))
print(timedelta(hours=25), timedelta(days=1.5))
print(timedelta(weeks=1) + timedelta(hours=1))
print(timedelta.max, timedelta.min)

# Arithmetic: datetime +/- timedelta is a datetime; datetime - datetime
# is a timedelta. Subtraction between a naive and an aware value fails.
print(datetime(2026, 8, 5) + one_day)
print(datetime(2026, 8, 6) - datetime(2026, 8, 5))
try:
    datetime(2026, 8, 6, tzinfo=UTC) - datetime(2026, 8, 5)
except TypeError as exc:
    print("TypeError: mixing naive and aware in arithmetic")

from datetime import datetime, timezone
from zoneinfo import ZoneInfo, ZoneInfoNotFoundError, available_timezones

# zoneinfo: the IANA database, read from the system. No dependency on
# the operating system's own timezone configuration, unlike time.tzset.
try:
    london = ZoneInfo("Europe/London")
    tokyo = ZoneInfo("Asia/Tokyo")
except ZoneInfoNotFoundError as exc:
    print("ZoneInfoNotFoundError:", type(exc).__name__)
    london = tokyo = timezone.utc

london_dt = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 12, 0, tzinfo=london)
tokyo_dt = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 12, 0, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
print(london_dt.isoformat())
print(london_dt.astimezone(tokyo).isoformat())
print(london_dt.utcoffset(), (tokyo_dt - london_dt).total_seconds() / 3600)

# The available set is the tz database, not a hard-coded list.
print(len(available_timezones()) > 400)
print("Europe/London" in available_timezones())
print("UTC" in available_timezones(), "utc" in available_timezones())

# Aware comparison now works, and a fold is resolved by the value.
a = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 12, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
b = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 12, tzinfo=london)
print(a < b, a == b, abs((a - b).total_seconds()))

# strftime and the platform-C-library caveat.
print(london_dt.strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S %Z%z"))
print(london_dt.strftime("%j %U %G %V"))      # day-of-year, week, ISO week
# %e, %-d and other glibc extensions are NOT portable to Windows.

# The three ISO 8601 forms and which parser accepts which.
from datetime import date
print(date.fromisoformat("20260805"))            # 3.11+: basic format
print(date.fromisoformat("2026-W32-1"))          # 3.11+: ISO week date
print(date.fromisoformat("2026-08-05"))
print(datetime.fromisoformat("20260805T143005+0000"))   # 3.11+

# now() and today(): the correct way, and the wrong one.
print(datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(timespec="seconds"))
print(datetime.now().astimezone().isoformat(timespec="seconds"))
print(datetime.utcnow())        # DEPRECATED in 3.12: returns a NAIVE value
# datetime.now().isoformat()   # has no offset: unusable for storage

The naive/aware distinction is a correctness trap rather than a style preference, and the three failure modes are all silent. datetime.now() returns a naive value carrying the machine’s local time, so a record written on a server in one zone and read in another is wrong by an unknown offset. Comparing a naive value to an aware one raises TypeError, which is at least loud. And a value produced by datetime.utcnow() — deprecated in 3.12 because it returns a naive value whose name claims otherwise — is a UTC time labelled as if it were local. The rule is: a datetime that crosses a process boundary is always aware, and the only place a naive value is acceptable is a value that never leaves the function that made it.

TaskCorrectIncorrectConsequence
Now, in UTCdatetime.now(timezone.utc)datetime.utcnow()Naive value; deprecated in 3.12
Now, localdatetime.now().astimezone()datetime.now()No offset attached
Store a momentISO 8601 with offsetstr(dt)str() omits the offset for an aware value in some paths and is not a documented format
Compare two momentsBoth aware, then astimezone(UTC)Stripping tzinfoCompares wall-clock labels, not instants
A date with no timedatedatetime at midnightA midnight datetime in a DST-observing zone can be a non-existent local time
Parsedatetime.fromisoformatstrptime for ISO inputstrptime is locale-aware and slow
Human-readable datestrftimeA hand-rolled month tableLocale handling and %b correctness
Timezone datazoneinfotime.tzset, a fixed UTC offsetSystem-dependent, and a fixed offset has no DST
# A correct duration helper, and the arithmetic that is easy to get
# wrong: "one month" is not a number of days.
from datetime import date, datetime, timedelta, timezone
LAST_DAY = (31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31)


def days_in_month(year: int, month: int) -> int:
    if month == 2 and (year % 4 == 0 and (year % 100 != 0 or year % 400 == 0)):
        return 29
    return LAST_DAY[month - 1]


def add_months(when: date, months: int) -> date:
    """Add whole months, clamping the day to the month's length."""
    total = (when.year * 12 + when.month - 1) + months
    year, month = divmod(total, 12)
    month += 1
    return date(year, month, min(when.day, days_in_month(year, month)))


print(add_months(date(2026, 1, 31), 1))     # 2026-02-28: clamped
print(add_months(date(2024, 1, 31), 1))     # 2024-02-29: leap year
print(add_months(date(2026, 12, 15), 1))    # 2027-01-15: year rollover
print(days_in_month(2024, 2), days_in_month(2026, 2))

# A duration between two aware instants, in the correct unit.
start = datetime(2026, 8, 5, 9, 0, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
end = datetime(2026, 8, 7, 11, 30, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
delta = end - start
print(delta, delta.days, delta.seconds, delta.total_seconds())
print("human:", f"{delta.days}d {delta.seconds // 3600}h "
                f"{(delta.seconds % 3600) // 60}m")
print("weeks:", divmod(delta, timedelta(weeks=1))[0])

Applies to: zoneinfo since 3.9 and PEP 615. datetime.UTC, the Z suffix in isoformat, date.fromisoformat accepting the basic and week-date forms, and datetime.fromisoformat accepting more ISO 8601 shapes — all since 3.11. datetime.utcnow() is deprecated since 3.12 and emits a DeprecationWarning. Requirements: zoneinfo requires an IANA database: the operating system provides it on Linux and macOS, and on Windows it requires the separate tzdata package, without which every ZoneInfo lookup raises ZoneInfoNotFoundError and zoneinfo.ZoneInfo("UTC") also fails — a deployment failure that appears only at first use, not at import. Caveats: a tzinfo from zoneinfo is correct only if the instant is correct; ZoneInfo computes the offset from the wall-clock fields, so a nonexistent local time (the spring-forward hour) is assigned a pre-transition offset and an ambiguous local time (the autumn hour) requires fold. Daylight saving boundaries are why a date is a different type from a datetime for anything contractual. Arithmetic on a timedelta is exact and never overflows until the field range is exceeded, at which point it is an OverflowError and not a wrap. Free-threaded builds: ZoneInfo instances are cached and are safe to share; the cache is protected. Cross-reference: the interval-arithmetic helper is the same shape as the timedelta table in section 2.5.

11.5 Text processing and re

Regular expressions are the wrong tool for a surprising share of text problems, and the right tool for a specific and narrow set. The decision rule is: if the pattern has a fixed structure and the data is trusted, a str method is faster, clearer and safer; if the pattern describes a shape in untrusted data, a regular expression is the correct instrument.

import re

# The str methods first. Most "parse this field" problems are these.
raw = "  user_id=42; name=Ada Lovelace; active=true  "
print(raw.strip())
parts = dict(kv.split("=", 1) for kv in raw.strip().split("; "))
print(parts)
print(raw.split("=", 1)[1].split(";")[0])
print("42".isdigit(), "4.2".isdecimal(), "".isspace())

# removeprefix/removesuffix: 3.9+, and they do not remove repeatedly.
s = "prefix-payload-suffix"
print(s.removeprefix("prefix-"), s.removesuffix("-suffix"))
print("xxy".removeprefix("x"))          # 'xy': once only, not 'y'

# partition and rpartition: a guaranteed three-part split.
head, sep, tail = "key=value=extra".partition("=")
print(head, repr(sep), tail)

# The regex equivalents, and why they are worse.
LOG = 'ERROR 2026-08-05T14:30:00Z db=primary msg="connection refused"'

m = re.search(r'msg="(?P<value>[^"]*)"', LOG)
print(m["value"], m.span(), m.start(), m.end(), m.re.pattern)
print(m.groupdict(), m.lastindex, m.lastgroup)

# Named groups, including the duplicate-name rejection.
try:
    re.compile(r"(?P<x>a)|(?P<x>b)")
except re.error as exc:
    print("re.error: a group name may not be duplicated")

# The flag set that changes the language, not the pattern.
print(re.findall(r"[A-Z]+", "AB cd EF"))
print(re.findall(r"(?s)a.b", "a\nb"))              # DOTALL: . matches \n
print(re.findall(r"(?m)^b", "a\nb\nb"))            # MULTILINE: ^ per line
print(re.findall(r"(?i)ab", "AB"))                 # IGNORECASE
print(re.findall(r"\w+", "a_1 \u00e9", re.UNICODE)[:3])
print(re.findall(r"(?x) a  b  # a comment is allowed here", "ab"))

# VERBOSE, which is what makes a real pattern readable.
PATTERN = re.compile(r"""
    ^(?P<ts>\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}T[\d:.]+Z)     # ISO 8601 UTC timestamp
    \s+(?P<level>DEBUG|INFO|WARN|ERROR)      # a closed level set
    \s+(?P<rest>.*)$                        # the rest of the line
""", re.VERBOSE)

for line in [
    "2026-08-05T14:30:00Z ERROR db=primary msg=refused",
    "2026-08-05T14:30:01Z INFO  heartbeat",
]:
    m = PATTERN.match(line)
    print(m["level"], m["ts"], m["rest"][:20])
print(PATTERN.match("not a log line"))       # None: no exception

re.VERBOSE ignores whitespace in the pattern, so a pattern must escape a literal space as \ or as [ ]. The comment character is #, which means a pattern matching a literal hash needs \#. These are the two errors that appear in every hand-written verbose pattern, and both surface as a pattern that does not match what its author expected rather than as an exception.

import re
import time

# Catastrophic backtracking: the defining property is AMBIGUITY. Two
# parts of a pattern that can partition the same text in more than one
# way, with nested quantifiers, produce exponential work.
#
# BAD:  ^(a+)+$ against a non-matching string of n 'a's.
# GOOD: ^a+$ -- linear, because there is only one way to match.

BAD = re.compile(r"^(a+)+$")
GOOD = re.compile(r"^a+$")
NON_MATCH = "a" * 24 + "b"

t0 = time.perf_counter()
print("GOOD on 25 chars:", GOOD.match(NON_MATCH) is None,
      f"{(time.perf_counter() - t0) * 1e6:.1f}us")

# BAD is not timed: 2**25 backtracks is minutes, not milliseconds.
# A bounded demonstration: 18 characters is already 260k steps.
BAD_SMALL = re.compile(r"^(a+)+$")
t0 = time.perf_counter()
BAD_SMALL.match("a" * 18 + "b")
print("BAD on 19 chars: ", f"{(time.perf_counter() - t0) * 1e3:.1f}ms")

# The other ReDoS shapes.
PATTERNS = {
    r"^(a|a)*$":                  "alternation inside a quantifier",
    r"^(\w+\s?)*$":               "a repeated group with a quantifier",
    r"^(.*,)*x":                  "a lazy-looking star that is greedy",
    r"^([a-zA-Z]+)*$":            "a quantified character class",
    r"^(a{1,10}){1,10}$":         "nested bounded quantifiers",
}

# The repairs.
REPAIRS = {
    r"^a+$":                      "one way to match: no ambiguity",
    r"^[\w ]+$":                  "a character class, not a group",
    r"^(?:[a-zA-Z]+ )*[a-zA-Z]+$": "an atomic-ish shape: no empty iteration",
    r"^(?:a|ab)c$":               "alternation ordered longest-first",
}

# The two structural repairs, in order of preference:
#   1. Eliminate the ambiguity. An atomic group (3.11+) or a possessive
#      quantifier removes the backtracking without a timeout.
#   2. Bound the input. A length check before the match turns an
#      exponential into a linear scan that simply fails.
import sys

if sys.version_info >= (3, 11):
    ATOMIC = re.compile(r"^(?>[a-zA-Z]+)+$")   # possessive: no backtrack
    print("atomic group compiles:", ATOMIC.match("abcabc"))
    print("possessive quantifier:", re.match(r"a++b", "aaab") is not None)

# Timeout is not available in the `re` module. The `regex` third-party
# module and `re` in a separate process are the two ways to bound it.
def safe_match(pattern: str, text: str, limit: int = 4096) -> bool:
    """Bound the input, then match. Cheap, and it removes the ReDoS."""
    return len(text) <= limit and re.match(pattern, text) is not None


print(safe_match(r"^(a+)+$", "a" * 24 + "b"))    # False without hanging

The defining property of catastrophic backtracking is not the presence of a quantifier; it is ambiguity — two or more ways for the same part of the pattern to match the same text. ^(a+)+$ is ambiguous because the inner a+ and the outer group can each absorb any split of the run of as. ^a+$ matches the same language with one way to do it and is therefore linear. The practical defences, in order: eliminate the ambiguity; make the quantifier possessive or wrap the group in an atomic group (3.11+); bound the input length before matching; and, where neither is possible, match in a process that can be killed.

import re

# Substitution, and the two forms that are not interchangeable.
print(re.sub(r"\s+", " ", "a   b  \n c"))          # count omitted: all
print(re.sub(r"\s+", " ", "a b c", count=1))       # only the first
print(re.subn(r"\s+", " ", "a   b  c"))            # (new, n)
print(re.sub(r"(\w+)@(\w+)", r"\2 at \1", "user@host"))
print(re.sub(r"x", lambda m: m.group().upper(), "x"))

# A function replacement is the only safe way to insert text that may
# itself contain a backslash or a group reference.
print(re.sub(r"a", r"\\n", "a"))                    # a LITERAL \n, not a newline
print(re.sub(r"a", lambda m: "\\n", "a"))           # a real newline

# The re module has no lookbehind of unbounded width; the workaround.
print(bool(re.match(r"(?<=USD )\d+", "total USD 42")))
try:
    re.compile(r"(?<=a*)b")
except re.error as exc:
    print("re.error: look-behind requires a fixed width")

# split, and the capture-group form that keeps the delimiters.
print(re.split(r",\s*", "a, b,c"))
print(re.split(r"(,)", "a,b"))                      # keeps the separator
print(re.split(r"(\s*,\s*)", "a , b"))

# finditer versus findall: match objects versus strings.
for m in re.finditer(r"\d+", "a1 b22 c333"):
    print("  ", m.group(), m.span())

# The translation form, and the fact that re is not needed for it.
print(re.sub(r"[aeiou]", "*", "the quick brown fox"))
print(str.maketrans("aeiou", "*****"), "the quick".translate(
    str.maketrans("aeiou", "*****")))

# fullmatch, and the anchors that are easy to get wrong.
print(bool(re.fullmatch(r"\d+", "12345")), bool(re.fullmatch(r"\d+", "12345a")))
print(bool(re.match(r"\d+$", "12345\n")), bool(re.fullmatch(r"\d+", "12345\n")))
# $ matches before a trailing newline; \Z does not. fullmatch avoids both.
print(bool(re.match(r"\d+\Z", "12345")), bool(re.match(r"\d+\Z", "12345\n")))

Applies to: atomic grouping (?>...) and possessive quantifiers *+, ++, ?+ since 3.11; inline flag scoping (?i:...) since 3.6; re.Pattern and re.Match as the public types since 3.7. Requirements: compile a pattern once and reuse the Pattern; the re module keeps a cache of 512 patterns, so a pattern built with string interpolation misses the cache on every call and the cache is silently overwritten. Caveats: $ matches before a final newline, so re.match(r"\d+$", "123\n") succeeds; \Z does not, and fullmatch avoids the question. re.sub interprets backslash escapes in the replacement string, so a replacement containing \1 or \g is a group reference; a callable replacement is the fix. re.VERBOSE makes both # and unescaped whitespace significant, and both are needed in any real pattern. A lookbehind must be fixed-width, so variable-width lookbehind is a re.error and the workaround is a non-capturing group plus an explicit length check. re is single-threaded-safe for compiled patterns; a Match object is not reusable and a Pattern is immutable and shareable. Third-party: the regex module adds a timeout parameter, atomic grouping on older versions, possessive quantifiers, recursive patterns and variable-width lookbehind; it is a drop-in for most patterns and is the correct answer when a pattern over untrusted input cannot be made unambiguous.

11.6 json, configparser and tomllib

json is the default interchange format for structured data in Python, and it has four round-trip limitations that are properties of the format rather than defects in the implementation. All four are silent, and all four are avoidable only by knowing them.

import json
from dataclasses import asdict, dataclass, field

# The four round-trip losses. Each is silent.
data = {
    "tuple": (1, 2, 3),              # becomes a list
    "int_keys": {1: "a", 2: "b"},    # keys become strings
    "set": {1, 2},                   # becomes a list, order unspecified
    "bytes": b"\x00\x01",            # raises TypeError
    "complex": 1 + 2j,               # raises TypeError
    "nan": float("nan"),             # becomes the literal NaN
    "inf": float("inf"),             # becomes the literal Infinity
}

encoded = json.dumps(data)
print(encoded)
decoded = json.loads(encoded)
print(type(decoded["tuple"]), decoded["tuple"])        # list, not tuple
print(decoded["int_keys"])                            # keys are '1', '2'
print(sorted(decoded["set"]))                          # a list
try:
    json.dumps(b"\x00")
except TypeError as exc:
    print("TypeError:", type(exc).__name__, "- bytes are not JSON")

# Non-standard JSON is emitted by default and accepted on input.
print(json.dumps(float("nan")), json.loads("NaN"))
print(json.dumps({"a": 1}, indent=None, separators=(",", ":")))

# The options that matter.
print(json.dumps({"b": 1, "a": 2}, sort_keys=True))
print(json.dumps({"a": {"b": 1}}, indent=2))
print(json.dumps([1, 2], ensure_ascii=True))     # escapes, never raw bytes
print(json.dumps({"a": 1}, default=str))        # a fallback for odd types

# A dataclass is not serialisable without help.
@dataclass
class Point:
    x: int
    y: int
    tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)


p = Point(1, 2, ["a"])
try:
    json.dumps(p)
except TypeError as exc:
    print("TypeError: a dataclass needs `default` or a custom encoder")
print(json.dumps(asdict(p)))


class PointEncoder(json.JSONEncoder):
    def default(self, o):
        if isinstance(o, Point):
            return asdict(o)
        if isinstance(o, set):
            return sorted(o)
        return super().default(o)


print(json.dumps(p, cls=PointEncoder))
print(json.dumps({"s": {3, 1, 2}}, cls=PointEncoder, sort_keys=True))

# Deterministic output, which is what a content hash or a cache key needs.
print(json.dumps({"b": 1, "a": [3, 2]}, sort_keys=True, separators=(",", ":")))

The int-key conversion is the one that bites hardest, because a dictionary keyed by integer identifiers is common and the round trip produces a dictionary keyed by strings that compares unequal to the original while appearing correct when printed. The fix is to make the key type a string at the boundary, not after: encode identifiers as strings in the schema, so that the JSON document and the in-memory object agree. The NaN and Infinity literals are not JSON at all — they are a CPython extension, emitted by default and accepted on input, which means a document produced by Python is not parseable by a strict JSON implementation. allow_nan=False makes the violation an error instead.

import configparser
import tomllib

# configparser: INI format. Every value is a string; the parser provides
# the typing, the interpolation and the defaults.
INI = """
; a comment
[server]
host = localhost
port = 8080
debug = false
tags = a, b, c

[server.tls]
enabled = true
cert = /etc/ssl/server.pem

[client]
host = %(host)s          # interpolation, using a value from the same section
port = 9999
timeout = 3.5
"""

cp = configparser.ConfigParser()
cp.read_string(INI)
print(cp.sections())
print(cp["server"]["host"], type(cp["server"]["port"]))
print(cp.getint("server", "port"), cp.getboolean("server", "debug"))
print(cp.getfloat("client", "timeout"))
print(cp.get("client", "host"))              # interpolated: localhost
print(cp.get("server", "tags").split(", "))
print(cp.has_section("server.tls"), cp.has_option("server", "host"))
print(cp.defaults(), cp.options("server"))

# The env-var interpolation that makes a config file deployable.
cp2 = configparser.ConfigParser({"host": "from-default"})
cp2.read_string("[client]\nhost = %(host)s\n")
print(cp2["client"]["host"])

# Conversion failures are a distinct exception type.
try:
    cp.getint("server", "port_bad")
except Exception as exc:
    print(type(exc).__name__, "(no such option)")
try:
    cp2.getint("client", "port") if "port" in cp2["client"] else None
    cp.getint("client", "host")
except ValueError as exc:
    print("ValueError: not an integer:", exc)

# tomllib: read-only TOML, in the standard library since 3.11. It
# rejects everything malformed, which is the whole value of TOML over
# JSON for configuration.
TOML = """
title = "example"
enabled = true
ratio = 0.75
ports = [8000, 8001]

[owner]
name = "Ada"
active = true

[database]
server = "db.internal"
ports = [5432]
connection_max = 5000

[database.options]
timeout = 15
"""

doc = tomllib.loads(TOML)
print(doc["title"], doc["enabled"], doc["ratio"], doc["ports"])
print(doc["owner"], doc["database"]["options"]["timeout"])
print(doc.get("missing", "default"), doc["database"].get("nope"))

# Dotted keys, inline tables, arrays of tables, and dates.
print(tomllib.loads('a.b.c = 1\n')["a"])
print(tomllib.loads('t = { x = 1, y = 2 }\n')["t"])
print(tomllib.loads('[[items]]\nn = 1\n[[items]]\nn = 2\n')["items"])
print(tomllib.loads('d = 2026-08-05\n')["d"], type(tomllib.loads('d = 2026-08-05\n')["d"]))

# There is no toml writer in the standard library. Writing TOML is a
# packaging concern, not a runtime one.

# The format decision, stated once.
FORMATS = {
    "JSON":  "interchange; typed on the wire; strict; json",
    "TOML":  "configuration; typed; comments; stdlib READ only (3.11+)",
    "INI":   "configuration; untyped; comments; configparser",
    "YAML":  "configuration; untyped; third-party; PyYAML is not stdlib",
}
for k, v in FORMATS.items():
    print(f"{k:5} {v}")

The three configuration formats differ in one dimension that decides almost every choice: whether the parser enforces a type. tomllib gives an int for port = 8080 and a bool for enabled = true, with a TomlDecodeError for anything malformed. configparser gives a str for everything and requires an explicit getint, which means a typo in a key name is a silent None unless the access is wrapped. json gives a type but permits no comments. For a configuration file that operators edit by hand, TOML’s typed-with-comments combination is the strongest of the three, and the absence of a standard-library writer is a packaging-time concern solved by a build dependency, not a runtime one.

Applies to: tomllib in the standard library since 3.11, read-only by design. configparser interpolation with %(name)s since 2.x; the ${} form requires ConfigParser(interpolation=ExtendedInterpolation()). Requirements: json.dumps(..., allow_nan=False) is required for output that a non-Python implementation must read, and json.loads will accept NaN, Infinity and -Infinity regardless, so a strict parser on the other end is the only real defence. Caveats: json preserves dict insertion order and sort_keys is the only way to get a canonical form; a dictionary with mixed key types raises TypeError on dumps because sorting is undefined. configparser lowercases every option name by default (optionxform), which silently merges Host and host; override it with optionxform = str to preserve case. configparser interpolation is applied on every get, so a value containing a bare % raises InterpolationSyntaxError unless interpolation is disabled. tomllib accepts a datetime, date or time for a TOML date literal, which is a datetime in the resulting document and interacts with the naive/aware rule in section 11.4. Security: yaml.load without a safe loader constructs arbitrary objects; it is not in the standard library and must never be used with untrusted input. json.loads on untrusted input is safe, but its recursion depth is the attack surface — deeply nested input raises RecursionError, not a clean error.

11.7 logging

The logging package is a hierarchy of named loggers, each with a level, connected to zero or more handlers, each with a formatter. A record logged on a child logger propagates to every ancestor’s handlers until propagate is False. That single mechanism explains both the duplication and the silence that make configuration the hardest part of this module.

import logging
import sys

# The four pieces, named explicitly.
logger = logging.getLogger("app.service")
logger.setLevel(logging.DEBUG)          # the logger decides what is RECORDED

handler = logging.StreamHandler(sys.stdout)
handler.setLevel(logging.INFO)          # the handler decides what is EMITTED

handler.setFormatter(logging.Formatter(
    fmt="%(asctime)s %(levelname)-8s %(name)s: %(message)s",
    datefmt="%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S%z",
))

logger.addHandler(handler)              # idempotent only if checked
logger.propagate = False                # stop the root logger duplicating

logger.debug("not emitted: the handler is INFO")
logger.info("service started on port %d", 8080)
logger.warning("retrying in %.1fs", 2.5)
try:
    1 / 0
except ZeroDivisionError:
    logger.exception("division failed")   # ERROR level, with traceback

print("--- the record attributes ---")
print(logging.LogRecord("n", logging.INFO, "p", 1, "msg", None, None).__dict__.keys())
print(sorted(logging.getLevelNamesMapping()))
print(logging._nameToLevel["CRITICAL"] if hasattr(logging, "_nameToLevel")
      else logging.getLevelName(50))

The hierarchy is a dotted namespace and nothing more: app.service.db has app and app.service as ancestors, and logging.getLogger("app") is the same object every time it is called. The default logging call configures a handler on the root logger, which is why a library that logs without configuring anything produces output on stderr that the application cannot control — and why a library should log through its own named logger and add no handlers.

import logging
import logging.config
import sys

# A library: named logger, NullHandler, no configuration, level unset.
# NullHandler means "this library logs nothing unless the application
# says so", which is the correct default for a reusable package.
lib = logging.getLogger("mylib")
lib.addHandler(logging.NullHandler())
lib.propagate = True                   # so the application's config applies

# The application: one configuration, applied once, at startup.
LOGGING = {
    "version": 1,
    "disable_existing_loggers": False,
    "formatters": {
        "brief": {"format": "%(levelname)s %(name)s: %(message)s"},
        "full": {
            "format": "%(asctime)s %(levelname)-8s %(name)s "
                      "%(filename)s:%(lineno)d %(funcName)s: %(message)s",
            "datefmt": "%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S%z",
        },
    },
    "filters": {
        "no_healthchecks": {"()": "logging.Filter",
                            "name": "mylib.health"},
    },
    "handlers": {
        "console": {
            "class": "logging.StreamHandler",
            "level": "WARNING",
            "formatter": "brief",
            "stream": "ext://sys.stdout",
        },
        "file": {
            "class": "logging.handlers.RotatingFileHandler",
            "level": "DEBUG",
            "formatter": "full",
            "filename": "app.log",
            "maxBytes": 1_048_576,
            "backupCount": 5,
            "encoding": "utf-8",
        },
    },
    "loggers": {
        "": {"handlers": ["console"], "level": "INFO"},
        "mylib": {"handlers": ["file", "console"], "level": "DEBUG",
                  "propagate": False},
        "app": {"handlers": ["console"], "level": "INFO", "propagate": False},
    },
}

logging.config.dictConfig(LOGGING)

app_log = logging.getLogger("app")
app_log.info("started")
lib.info("into the file only")
app_log.info("into the console")
print("configuration applied")

The RotatingFileHandler entry in that configuration is the reason dictConfig is worth the extra dependency on logging.config: it names logging.handlers.RotatingFileHandler by its importable path, so a rotation policy is declared in data rather than in code. The two settings that are most often wrong are disable_existing_loggers — True silences every logger not named in the configuration, including a third-party library’s, and is the reason a reconfiguration appears to break an unrelated dependency — and propagate, which must be False on a configured child exactly when the root also has the same handler, or every record is emitted twice.

import logging

# The attributes that make a record useful, and how to add more.
class RequestIdFilter(logging.Filter):
    """Attach a per-request identifier. Filters are per-handler, not
    per-logger, so a filter must be attached to every handler that
    needs the attribute."""

    def __init__(self, name: str = "") -> None:
        super().__init__(name)
        self._ids: dict[int, str] = {}

    def filter(self, record: logging.LogRecord) -> bool:
        import threading
        self._ids[threading.get_ident()] = "req-" + str(id(record))
        return True

    def new_id(self) -> str:
        return next(iter(self._ids.values()), "req-none")


class ContextFilter(logging.Filter):
    def filter(self, record):
        import contextvars
        record.request_id = contextvars.ContextVar(
            "request_id", default="-"
        ).get()
        return True


record = logging.LogRecord(
    name="app", level=logging.INFO, pathname=__file__, lineno=1,
    msg="user %s signed in", args=("ada",), exc_info=None,
)
record.request_id = "req-42"
print(record.getMessage())            # lazy % formatting applied here
print(record.levelname, record.name, record.lineno, record.pathname)
print(record.asctime if hasattr(record, "asctime") else "(set by the fmt)")
print(record.__dict__["args"], record.exc_info, record.stack_info)

# Lazy formatting: the arguments are stored, not interpolated, until a
# formatter needs the text. A disabled level therefore costs almost
# nothing, and %-style formatting is the only safe option -- f-strings
# in the message are evaluated before the level check.
log = logging.getLogger("demo")
log.setLevel(logging.INFO)
log.addHandler(logging.NullHandler())
log.debug("expensive %s", "".join(str(i) for i in range(100000)))

# exception vs exc_info vs error.
log.error("failed")                   # no traceback
log.error("failed", exc_info=True)     # uses sys.exc_info()
log.exception("failed")               # ERROR level, exc_info=True
LevelNumericDefault visibilityUse for
CRITICAL50AlwaysThe process cannot continue
ERROR40AlwaysAn operation failed and was not recovered
WARNING30AlwaysSomething unexpected that was handled
INFO20AlwaysNormal, high-level progress
DEBUG10Suppressed by the root defaultDiagnostic detail, off in production
NOTSET0Inherits the effective levelDo not set it; use it as the absence of a decision

Five defects in logging configuration that are invisible in development and expensive in production. (1) Double emission. A child logger with a handler and propagate = True under a root that has the same handler prints every record twice; the two lines have different formats, which makes it obvious once seen and invisible in a log sample. (2) Silence. A logger whose effective level is WARNING because an ancestor set it cannot be rescued by setting the level on the child alone — but it can be silenced by any ancestor’s handler filter, because the level check happens in the ancestor’s handle. (3) Library logging. A library that calls basicConfig or adds a StreamHandler takes over the application’s output; a library must add a NullHandler and nothing else. (4) Unbounded files. FileHandler with no rotation fills a disk; RotatingFileHandler is the minimum and TimedRotatingFileHandler is usually the right answer. (5) f-strings in the message. log.info(f"{expensive()}") evaluates expensive() even when INFO is disabled; pass the value as an argument and let the level check do its job.

Applies to: logging.config.dictConfig and logging.config.fileConfig since 2.7 and 3.2 respectively; getLevelNamesMapping since 3.11. Requirements: a logger needs a level; a handler needs a level; and the record is created only if the logger’s effective level permits it, then emitted by every handler whose level also permits it. A handler with level NOTSET handles everything, so a logger at DEBUG with an NOTSET handler will emit debug records even in production. Caveats: adding the same handler to the same logger twice is not deduplicated, because addHandler compares by identity and a fresh instance is a different object; re-running a configuration in a test therefore doubles the output. logging.raiseExceptions controls whether a handler’s own failure is printed to stderr; setting it to False silently loses records, which is a legitimate choice in a process that must not die of a logging failure. Formatting is not thread-safe for a Formatter shared across threads in CPython 3.12 and earlier; ThreadingLock in the handler or one handler per thread is the repair. The asctime field is only present after a formatter has set it, so reading record.asctime in a filter raises AttributeError. Free-threaded builds: Handler.emit holds a per-handler lock, so concurrent logging to one handler is serialised and safe; a custom handler that mutates the record in place is not, because the record is shared across handlers in the same chain. Cross-reference: structured, delay-rendered log data is the t-string form described in section 1.5.

11.8 subprocess

subprocess runs a program. Its one dangerous parameter is shell=True, which hands a string to a command interpreter and is therefore a command-injection vector whenever any part of that string is not a literal. Everything else in this section follows from avoiding it.

import subprocess
import sys

# The safe form: a list of arguments, no shell.
result = subprocess.run(
    [sys.executable, "-c", "print('hello from a child process')"],
    capture_output=True,       # stdout and stderr as bytes
    text=True,                 # ... decoded as str using the locale
    check=True,                # raise CalledProcessError on a non-zero exit
    timeout=10,                # kill the child if it overruns
    cwd=None,
    env=None,
)
print(result.returncode, result.stdout.strip(), result.stderr.strip())
print(result.args)

# check=False is the default; check=True converts a non-zero exit into
# an exception with the output already captured on the exception.
try:
    subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", "raise SystemExit(3)"],
                   capture_output=True, text=True, check=True)
except subprocess.CalledProcessError as exc:
    print("CalledProcessError:", exc.returncode, exc.cmd)
    print("  stdout:", repr(exc.stdout), "stderr:", repr(exc.stderr))

# capture_output is mutually exclusive with stdout/stderr, and it
# overrides them. Without it, the child inherits the parent's streams
# and the output appears interleaved with the parent's.
proc = subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", "import sys; print(sys.argv[1])",
                       "visible"], text=True)
print("inherited stdout above")

# shell=True: the injection, demonstrated rather than asserted.
SAFE = "world"
BAD = "world; echo INJECTED"
print("list form, safe argument:")
print(subprocess.run(["echo", SAFE], capture_output=True,
                     text=True).stdout.strip())
print("shell form, attacker-controlled argument:")
print(subprocess.run(f"echo {BAD}", shell=True, capture_output=True,
                     text=True).stdout.strip())
# The second runs TWO commands. On Windows the separator is `&` and on
# POSIX it is `;`, and both are in the payload here, so the
# demonstration is not platform-specific.

The rule for shell=True is absolute: use it only when the command string is a literal in the source with no interpolation, no environment expansion and no variable of any kind in it. Everything else uses the list form, which passes each argument to execve directly and gives the interpreter no opportunity to re-parse it. The list form is also more portable, because a list has no quoting rules to get wrong, and it is the only form that works reliably with an executable path containing spaces.

import os
import shutil
import subprocess
import sys
from pathlib import Path

# shutil.which: the only correct way to find an executable. It applies
# PATHEXT on Windows and returns None rather than raising.
print(shutil.which("python3") is not None or shutil.which("python") is not None)
print(shutil.which("definitely-not-an-executable"))
print(shutil.which("python3", path=os.environ.get("PATH", "")))

# The secure path when the executable is not on PATH.
print(isinstance(shutil.which("cmd.exe"), str))


# The full parameter set, with the two that matter most marked.
def run_tool(args: list[str], *, timeout: float = 30.0) -> str:
    exe = shutil.which(args[0])
    if exe is None:
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"{args[0]!r} is not on PATH")
    return subprocess.run(
        [exe, *args[1:]],           # resolved path, list form
        capture_output=True,
        text=True,
        encoding="utf-8",           # explicit: never the locale
        errors="replace",
        check=True,
        timeout=timeout,
        stdin=subprocess.DEVNULL,   # a child that reads stdin must not
                                    # inherit the parent's terminal
    ).stdout


# Popen, for the cases run() cannot express.
proc = subprocess.Popen(
    [sys.executable, "-u", "-c", "print('line'); print('line')"],
    stdout=subprocess.PIPE,
    text=True,
    bufsize=1,                       # line buffered: required for reading
)                                     # a stream without deadlock
with proc:
    for line in proc.stdout:
        print("  streamed:", line.strip())
    proc.wait(timeout=5)
print("returncode:", proc.returncode)

# The communicate() contract: read both pipes concurrently, or the
# child deadlocks on a full pipe buffer.
proc2 = subprocess.Popen(
    [sys.executable, "-c",
     "import sys; sys.stdout.write('o'*100000); sys.stderr.write('e'*100000)"],
    stdout=subprocess.PIPE, stderr=subprocess.PIPE, text=True,
)
with proc2:
    out, err = proc2.communicate(timeout=15)
    print("communicate lengths:", len(out), len(err))

# Two ways to get output to a file rather than a pipe.
with open("out.txt", "w", encoding="utf-8") as handle:
    subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", "print('to a file')"],
                   stdout=handle, check=True)
print(Path("out.txt").read_text(encoding="utf-8").strip())
Path("out.txt").unlink()

# Group management: start_new_session detaches the child from the
# terminal's process group, so Ctrl-C reaches only this process.
proc3 = subprocess.Popen(
    [sys.executable, "-c", "import time; time.sleep(30)"],
    start_new_session=True,
)
with proc3:
    proc3.terminate()
