    try:
        proc3.wait(timeout=5)
    except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
        proc3.kill()
        proc3.wait(timeout=5)
print("detached child reaped:", proc3.returncode)

Four properties of subprocess that cause real incidents. A timeout kills the child, not its descendants, so a shell wrapper that spawned a grandchild leaves the grandchild running; start_new_session=True plus an explicit process-group kill is the only reliable cleanup. text=True without encoding decodes with the locale encoding, which makes output non-deterministic across machines and is the cause of intermittent UnicodeDecodeError in CI. Capturing both pipes and reading them sequentially deadlocks as soon as either buffer fills, which is 64 KiB on Linux and much less on Windows; communicate() exists for exactly this. And a child that reads stdin inherits the parent’s terminal, so it can block forever waiting for input that will never arrive; stdin=subprocess.DEVNULL makes that failure immediate.

Applies to: subprocess.run, Popen and CompletedProcess since 3.5; the text, encoding and errors parameters since 3.6 and 3.5; capture_output since 3.7. Requirements: a library that shells out must validate every argument against an allowlist where the value is not a fixed set, and must never interpolate into a shell string. The list form does not perform any expansion, so a path with ~ or an environment variable must be expanded by the caller — with os.path.expanduser and os.path.expandvars — and the result is then still untrusted input for the purposes of argument validation. Caveats: shell=True on Windows with a string invokes cmd.exe, whose quoting rules differ entirely from POSIX shells; a command assembled for one platform is not safe on the other. timeout raises TimeoutExpired after killing the child, and the child’s output is available on the exception, so a partial result is recoverable. A negative returncode on POSIX is the signal number (-9 for SIGKILL); on Windows the codes come from the Win32 API and do not follow that convention. os.system, os.popen and commands are superseded by this module; the first two are not thread-safe. Cross-platform: the executable name, the argument quoting and the process-group semantics all differ, so any code that shells out is a portability surface in its own right and belongs behind one small wrapper module.

Exercises 11

  1. Benchmark list.pop(0) against deque.popleft() at 103, 104 and 105 elements with timeit, and plot the growth to show the quadratic against the linear.
  2. Use groupby on an unsorted list and then on a sorted copy of the same list, and record the group counts in each case; then implement the grouping with a defaultdict and confirm the two approaches agree on the sorted input.
  3. Measure the memory held by itertools.tee when one branch is consumed 105 elements ahead of the other, using tracemalloc, and compare with materialising the source once.
  4. Walk a temporary tree with Path.walk and with os.walk, prune one subtree with dirnames[:] in each, and confirm the traversal orders match.
  5. Show the four json round-trip losses on one dictionary, then write an encoder that preserves tuples, integer keys and sets, and verify the round trip with ==.
  6. Construct a log configuration with dictConfig that emits to a rotating file, then add a second handler to the same logger at run time and demonstrate the double-emission, followed by a removeHandler that fixes it.
  7. Run a child process that writes 200 KB to both stdout and stderr and read them with sequential readline calls to show the deadlock, then with communicate() to show it does not occur.
  8. Run a child that spawns its own child, apply a timeout to the parent only, and show the grandchild surviving; then repeat with start_new_session=True and a process-group kill and show it does not survive.

12 · Data Access and Databases

12.1 The DB-API 2.0 model12.2 sqlite3 in depth12.3 Injection mechanics and the binding defence12.4 Schema design and constraints12.5 Indexes and query plans12.6 SQLAlchemy Core and the Engine12.7 The declarative ORM and the unit of work12.8 The N+1 query problem12.9 Schema migrations with Alembic12.10 Asynchronous database access

Database access from Python is a three-layer stack. The bottom layer is PEP 249, the DB-API 2.0 specification that defines a connection object, a cursor object, a placeholder syntax and an exception hierarchy, and which every mainstream Python database driver implements to some degree. The middle layer is a concrete driver — sqlite3 in the standard library, or a third-party driver such as psycopg, pymysql, asyncpg or oracledb. The top layer is a toolkit: SQLAlchemy Core, which compiles Python expressions to SQL, and the ORM built on it, which adds an identity map, lazy loading and a unit of work. This chapter starts at the specification, because the portability claims of the layers above it are only as wide as it.

12.1 The DB-API 2.0 model

DB-API 2.0 (PEP 249) specifies a deliberately small surface: a module-level connect() factory, a connection that owns a transaction, a cursor that owns a result set, and four placeholder syntaxes. Everything else is an optional extension, which is precisely the portability problem — code that uses only the core works on every driver, and code that uses an extension works until it meets a driver that does not have it.

# The whole mandatory DB-API surface, exercised against sqlite3.
# Every mainstream driver supports exactly these members.
import sqlite3

conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")   # module-level factory
print(conn.apilevel)                  # "2.0"   -- always "2.0" for a 2.0 driver
print(conn.threadsafety)              # 0/1/2/3, see table below
print(conn.paramstyle)                # "qmark" for sqlite3, "pyformat" for psycopg
print(sqlite3.dbapi2)                 # the module implementing PEP 249

cur = conn.cursor()
cur.execute("CREATE TABLE t (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, label TEXT)")
cur.execute("INSERT INTO t (label) VALUES (?)", ("first",))
cur.execute("INSERT INTO t (label) VALUES (?)", ("second",))
conn.commit()                          # DB-API does NOT autocommit by default

# A cursor is a result set. It is iterable exactly once.
cur.execute("SELECT id, label FROM t ORDER BY id")
print(cur.description)                # (('id', None, None, None, None, None, None), ...)
print(cur.fetchone())                 # (1, 'first')
print(cur.fetchmany(1))               # [(2, 'second')]
print(cur.fetchall())                 # [] -- exhausted, NOT an error
cur.close()

# The connection is a transaction. Context management on it means:
# commit on clean exit, rollback on exception. It is an OPTIONAL extension.
try:
    with conn:                        # optional in PEP 249, universal in practice
        conn.execute("INSERT INTO t (label) VALUES (?)", ("third",))
        raise RuntimeError("boom")
except RuntimeError as exc:
    print("rolled back:", exc)
print(conn.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM t").fetchone())   # (2,)

# Exceptions form a documented hierarchy (see table below).
print(sqlite3.IntegrityError.__mro__)

Three properties of this model drive everything above it. First, a connection is a transaction, and it does not commit until told to; the implicit transaction in PEP 249 was only formalised as the autocommit attribute in 2013, and drivers disagree about what connect(..., autocommit=True) means. Second, a cursor is a stream, not a list: fetchall() on an exhausted cursor returns an empty list rather than raising, and iterating a cursor twice yields nothing the second time. Third, placeholders are values, not text, and the syntax for writing them varies by driver, which is the subject of 12.3.

DB-API memberRoleNotes and portability
connect(dsn, **kwargs)Open a connectionDriver-specific keyword arguments; the DSN syntax is not standardised. close() is mandatory; commit(), rollback() and cursor() are mandatory.
Connection.cursor()Create a cursorMandatory. Most drivers allow several cursors on one connection; none of them are independently transactional.
Cursor.execute(sql, params)Run one statementMandatory. Must return the cursor. params is a sequence or a mapping, or omitted.
Cursor.executemany(sql, seq)Run one statement per parameter setMandatory. Implementations may batch or loop; do not assume one round trip.
Cursor.executescript(sql)Run several statementsOptional, and not in PEP 249 proper. sqlite3 has it; psycopg does not. Multi-statement strings are a driver extension and should not appear in portable code.
Cursor.fetchone/fetchmany/fetchallConsume the result setMandatory. fetchmany uses Cursor.arraysize (default 1 in most drivers, which is almost never what you want).
Cursor.descriptionColumn metadataMandatory. A sequence of 7-tuples, or None for a statement with no result set.
Cursor.rowcountRows affectedMandatory as an attribute, but its meaning for SELECT is undefined; -1 means "do not know". Never branch on it for reads.
Cursor.lastrowidGenerated keyDB-API extension, universally present. Only meaningful after a single-row INSERT on an autoincrement column.
Connection.__enter__/__exit__Transaction blockOptional in PEP 249, present in every current driver. Commit on clean exit, rollback on exception. It is not a connection pool context and does not close the connection.
Cursor.__enter__/__exit__Cursor blockOptional. Closes the cursor on exit. Mostly a convenience for generators.
paramstylePlaceholdersDriver
qmark... WHERE id = ?, positionalsqlite3, Oracle (oracledb), ODBC
numeric... WHERE id = :1, positional by indexOracle, IBM DB2
named... WHERE id = :id, by namesqlite3 (supported alongside qmark), SQL Server (pyodbc)
pyformat... WHERE id = %(id)s, by namepsycopg (PostgreSQL), mysqlclient, pymysql
format... WHERE id = %sNot a DB-API style. The raw DB-API 2.0 original draft used ANSI C %s; it was withdrawn because it collides with printf-style identifiers. Do not use it.

paramstyle is a property of the module, not of a connection, and it is a string you can branch on. Portable code either queries it or, far more commonly, delegates placeholder syntax to a toolkit that compiles it for you.

ExceptionRaised when
WarningBase of the warning classes. Rare in practice.
ErrorBase of every error the module raises. Catching this is usually too broad, but it is the correct base for a retry wrapper.
InterfaceErrorThe database itself is not involved: wrong argument count, bad cursor state, connection already closed. Retrying never helps.
DatabaseErrorBase for errors the database reported.
DataErrorValue out of range for the column type, division by zero in SQL, malformed input for the target dialect.
OperationalErrorThings outside the programmer’s control: connection lost, deadlock, serialisation failure, disk full, table missing. This is the class to retry on, and only after distinguishing the retryable codes.
IntegrityErrorConstraint violation: unique, foreign key, not-null, check. Retrying the identical statement always fails again.
InternalErrorThe database is in an invalid state. Restart the connection; do not retry the statement.
ProgrammingErrorProgramming error: table does not exist, syntax error, wrong number of parameters, wrong parameter type. A bug, not a fault.
NotSupportedErrorAn optional DB-API extension or a SQL feature the database does not implement, such as Cursor.nextset() on SQLite.

The hierarchy is the single most useful thing PEP 249 provides, because it is the only part of the specification that every driver implements consistently. A retry loop that catches OperationalError works unchanged across PostgreSQL, MySQL and Oracle; a retry loop that catches sqlite3.OperationalError does not exist outside SQLite.

Applies to: every DB-API 2.0 driver. Requirements: none beyond the driver. Caveats: the optional extensions are the trap. executescript, callproc, nextset, scroll, setinputsizes, setoutputsize, autocommit, Connection.messages and Cursor.connection are all optional, and several popular drivers omit some of them. Code that must run on more than one engine should use only execute, executemany, fetch*, description, commit, rollback, close and the context-manager protocol. See 12.6 for how SQLAlchemy Core absorbs these differences.

PEP 249 defines no standard for quoting identifiers, no standard for the result of a DDL statement, no standard for binding a placeholder in a LIMIT clause, no standard for multiple statements in one execute() call, and no standard for a server-side cursor. Every one of these has a different answer in SQLite, PostgreSQL, MySQL and SQL Server. Any code that depends on them is engine-specific, whatever the driver documentation says.

12.2 sqlite3 in depth

sqlite3 is the only database driver in the standard library, and it is not a toy. It is a complete embedded engine with a real query planner, WAL journalling, foreign keys, a type system with affinities, and window functions. Its two defining limitations for server work are that it serialises writers and that its concurrency model is process-level rather than connection-level; its two defining advantages are that it has no daemon and no network.

import sqlite3
import sys

# --- Connecting -----------------------------------------------------------
# A path creates the file. ":memory:" is a private in-process database.
# "file:memdb1?mode=memory&cache=shared" shares one in-memory database
# between connections in the same process.
conn = sqlite3.connect("inventory.db")
print(type(conn), conn.isolation_level)      # isolation_level is "" -> implicit BEGIN

# --- isolation_level controls transaction management ------------------------
# ""            (default) sqlite3 opens a BEGIN before DML and commits on
#               commit(). DDL no longer implicitly commits as of 3.6.
# "DEFERRED"    BEGIN is issued explicitly, not implicitly; you control it.
# "IMMEDIATE"   acquire a RESERVED lock at the first statement, so a second
#               writer fails fast instead of failing at COMMIT.
# "EXCLUSIVE"   acquire an EXCLUSIVE lock immediately.
conn.isolation_level = "IMMEDIATE"
print(conn.isolation_level)

# --- PRAGMAs are per-connection, not per-database -------------------------
cur = conn.cursor()
cur.execute("PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON")        # OFF by default since 3.6.0
cur.execute("PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL")       # better read/write concurrency
cur.execute("PRAGMA synchronous = NORMAL")     # safe with WAL, faster than FULL
cur.execute("PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000")      # ms to wait on a locked DB
print(cur.fetchall())
for pragma in ("foreign_keys", "journal_mode", "synchronous", "busy_timeout"):
    cur.execute(f"PRAGMA {pragma}")
    print(pragma, "=", cur.fetchone()[0])

# --- DDL and DML through the same execute ---------------------------------
conn.executescript("""
    DROP TABLE IF EXISTS product;
    CREATE TABLE product (
        id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        sku         TEXT    NOT NULL UNIQUE,
        name        TEXT    NOT NULL,
        price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
        created_at  TEXT    NOT NULL DEFAULT (datetime('now'))
    );
    CREATE TABLE stock (
        product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES product(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
        warehouse  TEXT    NOT NULL,
        qty        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
        PRIMARY KEY (product_id, warehouse)
    );
""")

# --- executemany is the only bulk path SQLite has -------------------------
rows = [(f"SKU-{i:05d}", f"Product {i}", i * 137) for i in range(1, 1001)]
conn.executemany(
    "INSERT INTO product (sku, name, price_cents) VALUES (?, ?, ?)",
    rows,
)
conn.commit()
print(conn.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM product").fetchone())   # (1000,)

# --- row_factory controls what a row is -----------------------------------
conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
row = conn.execute("SELECT * FROM product WHERE id = 1").fetchone()
print(type(row), row["sku"], row.keys())
print(dict(row))
print(tuple(row))                      # a Row is also a tuple
row = conn.execute("SELECT 1").fetchone(); row  # Row behaves like a tuple

# A callable row_factory is a lightweight record construction step.
from collections import namedtuple
Product = namedtuple("Product", "id sku name price_cents")
conn.row_factory = lambda cur, r: Product(*r[:4])
p = conn.execute("SELECT * FROM product WHERE id = 2").fetchone()
print(p, type(p).__name__)

# --- Returning clause: INSERT ... RETURNING (SQLite 3.35, 2021) ----------
conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
cur = conn.execute(
    "INSERT INTO product (sku, name, price_cents) VALUES (?, ?, ?) RETURNING id, sku",
    ("SKU-X", "Returned", 500),
)
print(cur.fetchone())                  # no separate lastrowid round trip

# --- lastrowid and total_changes ------------------------------------------
print(conn.execute("SELECT last_insert_rowid()").fetchone()[0])
print(conn.total_changes)              # INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE since open
print(conn.changes)                    # rows changed by the last statement
conn.close()

Three SQLite behaviours surprise people who arrive from a client/server engine. Foreign keys are off by default and must be enabled per connection with PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON, which is why ON DELETE CASCADE sometimes appears to do nothing. The default isolation level is the empty string, which means implicit transaction control: sqlite3 issues BEGIN before the first INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE and leaves it open. And busy_timeout defaults to zero, so a concurrent writer gets sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked immediately instead of waiting — a frequent source of production incidents on network filesystems.

For anything beyond a single-file application, use WAL mode plus busy_timeout and set foreign_keys = ON in one place, immediately after connecting. Wrap the three pragmas in a function that returns a configured connection, so no call site can forget one of them.

import sqlite3
from pathlib import Path

SCHEMA = """
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS product (
    id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    sku         TEXT    NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    name        TEXT    NOT NULL,
    price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS stock (
    product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES product(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    warehouse  TEXT    NOT NULL,
    qty        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    PRIMARY KEY (product_id, warehouse)
);
"""

def connect(db: str | Path = "app.db", *, timeout: float = 5.0) -> sqlite3.Connection:
    """Return a connection with the pragmas this application relies on."""
    conn = sqlite3.connect(str(db), timeout=timeout, isolation_level="IMMEDIATE")
    conn.execute("PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON")
    conn.execute("PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL")
    conn.execute("PRAGMA synchronous = NORMAL")
    conn.executescript(SCHEMA)
    return conn

def transfer(conn: sqlite3.Connection, src: str, dst: str, qty: int) -> None:
    """Move stock between warehouses atomically, or move nothing."""
    if qty <= 0:
        raise ValueError("qty must be positive")
    with conn:                                    # BEGIN ... COMMIT / ROLLBACK
        conn.execute(
            "UPDATE stock SET qty = qty - ? WHERE warehouse = ? AND qty >= ?",
            (qty, src, qty),
        )
        moved = conn.total_changes                # not reliable; use the cursor below
        cur = conn.execute(
            "UPDATE stock SET qty = qty + ? WHERE warehouse = ?",
            (qty, dst),
        )
        if cur.rowcount != 1:
            raise LookupError(f"no such warehouse: {dst}")
        if conn.execute(
            "SELECT 1 FROM stock WHERE warehouse = ? AND qty < 0", (src,)
        ).fetchone():
            raise RuntimeError("source would go negative")

conn = connect(":memory:")
conn.executemany(
    "INSERT INTO product (sku, name, price_cents) VALUES (?, ?, ?)",
    [(f"S{i}", f"Item {i}", i) for i in range(1, 4)],
)
conn.executemany("INSERT INTO stock VALUES (?, ?, ?)", [(1, "a", 10), (1, "b", 5)])
transfer(conn, "a", "b", 4)
print(conn.execute("SELECT warehouse, qty FROM stock ORDER BY warehouse").fetchall())
try:
    transfer(conn, "a", "b", 100)                 # rolls back, nothing changes
except RuntimeError as exc:
    print("aborted:", exc)
print(conn.execute("SELECT warehouse, qty FROM stock ORDER BY warehouse").fetchall())
conn.close()

conn.total_changes counts statements, not rows; do not use it to detect whether an update took effect. Use the cursor’s rowcount, and for a conditional write add the condition to the WHERE clause and check rowcount == 1. A conditional UPDATE ... WHERE qty >= ? is atomic in SQLite and in every server database, which makes it the correct way to implement a non-blocking decrement without a SELECT first.

12.3 Injection mechanics and the binding defence

SQL injection is not an esoteric attack. It occurs whenever a value that originated outside the program text is concatenated into a statement before it reaches the driver, and it is fully prevented by passing that value as a bound parameter instead. Understanding the mechanics matters because the defence is structural rather than procedural: it removes an entire class of bug by construction, and it does not depend on a reviewer noticing a dangerous string operation.

import sqlite3

conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
conn.execute("CREATE TABLE user (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT, password TEXT)")
conn.executemany("INSERT INTO user VALUES (?, ?, ?)",
                 [(1, "alice", "hashed1"), (2, "bob", "hashed2")])
conn.commit()

# --- The vulnerability ----------------------------------------------------
def login_vulnerable(name: str, password: str) -> bool:
    sql = ("SELECT * FROM user WHERE name = '" + name +
           "' AND password = '" + password + "'")
    cur = conn.execute(sql)          # the SQL text is now attacker-controlled
    return cur.fetchone() is not None

# Three classic payloads, all of which are one string and no special tooling.
print(login_vulnerable("alice", "x' OR '1'='1"))        # tautology
print(login_vulnerable("' OR 1=1 --", "anything"))      # comment out the rest
print(login_vulnerable("alice' UNION SELECT 1,2,3 --", "x"))   # UNION exfil
conn.execute("DROP TABLE IF EXISTS secret")
print(conn.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM sqlite_master").fetchone())
# ... and DDL, because execute() is not restricted to SELECT.

# --- The defence: bind the value ------------------------------------------
def login_safe(name: str, password: str) -> bool:
    cur = conn.execute(
        "SELECT id FROM user WHERE name = ? AND password = ?",
        (name, password),
    )
    return cur.fetchone() is not None

for payload in ("alice", "x' OR '1'='1", "' OR 1=1 --", "'; DROP TABLE user; --"):
    print(repr(payload), "->", login_safe(payload, "x"))

# What the driver actually sends, for the curious.
import logging
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.DEBUG)
logging.getLogger().setLevel(logging.DEBUG)
print(conn.execute("SELECT id FROM user WHERE name = ?", ("alice",)).fetchone())

The reason binding works is not that the driver filters the string. The client library builds the statement and the parameter value into separate parts of the protocol message; the value never passes through the SQL parser, so a quote in it is a quote in a string and nothing more. Every injection primitive — terminating a string, starting a comment with --, stacking statements with ;, or appending a UNION SELECT — requires the parser to see the payload as SQL, and it never does.

Hand-written escaping is not a substitute. quote()-style helpers, str.replace("'", "''"), HTML escaping, URL encoding and ad-hoc regexes all fail on at least one of: character sets with alternative encodings, backslash handling in MySQL, the difference between string and identifier context, second-order injection through a value that is written to a table and re-read later, and prepared-statement support that is silently absent on some driver code paths. Parameter binding is not an optimisation; it is the only complete answer, and it is free.

Escaping is not merely insufficient — it is a sign the design is wrong. If a value must be escaped, it means it is in a position the parameter mechanism cannot reach, and the places it cannot reach are the interesting ones.

import sqlite3
import re

conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
conn.executescript("""
    CREATE TABLE sales (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, region TEXT, amount INTEGER);
    CREATE TABLE region_total (region TEXT PRIMARY KEY, total INTEGER);
""")
conn.executemany("INSERT INTO sales (region, amount) VALUES (?, ?)",
                 [("emea", 10), ("emea", 5), ("apac", 7)])

# --- Values: bind. Always. -------------------------------------------------
def total_for_region(region: str) -> int:
    row = conn.execute(
        "SELECT coalesce(sum(amount), 0) FROM sales WHERE region = ?", (region,)
    ).fetchone()
    return row[0]

print(total_for_region("emea"), total_for_region("nope"))

# --- Identifiers: placeholders cannot reach them. Whitelist instead. ------
SORTABLE = ("amount", "region", "id")
DIRECTION = {"asc": "ASC", "desc": "DESC"}

def fetch_sorted(sort: str, direction: str, limit: int) -> list[tuple]:
    if sort not in SORTABLE:                       # deny by default
        raise ValueError(f"unsortable column: {sort!r}")
    if direction.lower() not in DIRECTION:
        raise ValueError(f"bad direction: {direction!r}")
    if not isinstance(limit, int) or not 1 <= limit <= 1000:
        raise ValueError("limit out of range")
    # f-string interpolation is correct HERE, because both substituted values
    # came from a closed set of literals in this module.
    sql = f"SELECT id, region, amount FROM sales ORDER BY {sort} {DIRECTION[direction.lower()]} LIMIT {limit}"
    return conn.execute(sql).fetchall()

print(fetch_sorted("amount", "desc", 3))
print(fetch_sorted("id", "asc", 2))
for bad in ("amount; DROP TABLE sales", "1", "rowid"):
    try:
        fetch_sorted(bad, "asc", 5)
    except ValueError as exc:
        print("rejected:", exc)

# --- IN lists: expand placeholders, never splice a literal list ------------
def fetch_regions(regions: list[str]) -> list[tuple]:
    if not regions:
        return []
    if len(regions) > 900:                     # SQLite caps variables per stmt
        raise ValueError("too many regions")
    holes = ",".join("?" * len(regions))       # builds placeholders, not values
    return conn.execute(
        f"SELECT id, region, amount FROM sales WHERE region IN ({holes}) ORDER BY id",
        tuple(regions),
    ).fetchall()

print(fetch_regions(["emea", "apac"]))
print(fetch_regions(["emea", "' OR '1'='1"]))   # a harmless, inert string
print(fetch_regions([]))                        # guarded: "IN ()" is a syntax error

# --- LIKE: the escape character is the part people forget ------------------
def search_names(term: str) -> list[tuple]:
    # % and _ are wildcards; without an explicit ESCAPE they cannot be matched
    # literally, so a user searching for "100%" gets everything.
    pattern = term.replace("\\", "\\\\").replace("%", "\\%").replace("_", "\\_")
    return conn.execute(
        r"SELECT id, region FROM sales WHERE region LIKE ? ESCAPE '\'",
        (f"%{pattern}%",),
    ).fetchall()

print(search_names("emea"))
assert search_names("%") == search_names("\\%") or True
conn.close()

The rule that follows: values bind, identifiers whitelist, structure is code. Anything that changes which table, column, sort direction or join type is used is part of the program’s structure, and it belongs in Python — a lookup in a dict, a member of a frozen tuple, or a branch in the source. Only a value that can take any shape at all should reach the driver as a bound parameter, and then any shape at all is safe.

Two more injection surfaces sit next to the SQL one. Any ORDER BY, GROUP BY, LIMIT, table name or column name interpolated with an f-string is an injection point even when every data value is bound. And a LIMIT ? placeholder is not portable: SQLite accepts it, PostgreSQL accepts it through asyncpg and psycopg in limited form, MySQL does not, and the portable form is a whitelisted integer converted with int() before interpolation.

Caveats: parameter binding does not help with second-order injection, where a stored value is safe on the way in and later concatenated into a query on the way out. It also does not help with a LIKE pattern that must match wildcards literally; that needs the escaping shown above. And a prepared statement reused across a connection-pool checkout must be re-prepared per connection unless the driver supports server-side named statements.

12.4 Schema design and constraints

A database rejects bad data; application code does not, reliably, and never on every code path. Constraints declared in the schema are the only enforcement that survives a second deployment, a new service written in another language, a restore from a backup, and a human running psql by hand. Design the constraints first and the code follows.

ConstraintGuaranteesFailure mode when violated
PRIMARY KEYUniqueness and non-nullness; the target of every foreign keyIntegrityError
NOT NULLThe column always has a valueIntegrityError
UNIQUENo duplicate value, separately from the keyIntegrityError
CHECK (expr)The expression is true for every rowIntegrityError
DEFAULTA value when the column is omitted—
FOREIGN KEYReferential integrity: the referenced row exists, or is nullIntegrityError; silently ignored in SQLite unless enabled
REFERENCES ... ON DELETE CASCADEDeleting the parent removes the childrenOrder-of-deletion bug, or an accidental cascade
REFERENCES ... ON DELETE RESTRICTDeleting the parent is refused if children existIntegrityError; the safest default
REFERENCES ... ON DELETE SET NULLDeleting the parent nulls the child key, which requires the key to be nullableOrphan semantics; needs a migration to make the column nullable
REFERENCES ... ON DELETE SET DEFAULTResets the child key to its defaultRarely what is intended
DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERREDThe check runs at COMMIT rather than per statement, which is what cyclic foreign keys needConstraint checked late, so the error message names the commit, not the row
EXCLUDE (PostgreSQL)No overlap on a range, using a GiST or SP-GiST indexExclusionViolationError
import sqlite3

# --- Normalisation, applied to a small domain ------------------------------
# 0NF -> one fact per cell. 1NF -> atomic values. 2NF -> every non-key column
# depends on the whole key. 3NF -> no non-key column depends on another
# non-key column.
#
# Unnormalised orders(id, customer_name, customer_city, product, qty, price)
#   customer_city depends on customer_name, not on id -> a 3NF violation:
#   changing a customer's city requires touching every one of their orders.
#
# Normalised:
conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
conn.executescript("""
    CREATE TABLE customer (
        id        INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        name      TEXT NOT NULL,
        city      TEXT NOT NULL,
        country   TEXT NOT NULL,
        UNIQUE (name, city)
    );
    CREATE TABLE product (
        id     INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        sku    TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
        name   TEXT NOT NULL,
        -- Money as an integer count of minor units. Never REAL, never a float.
        price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0)
    );
    CREATE TABLE "order" (
        id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES customer(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
        placed_at   TEXT    NOT NULL DEFAULT (datetime('now'))
    );
    CREATE TABLE order_line (
        order_id   INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES "order"(id)  ON DELETE CASCADE,
        product_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES product(id)   ON DELETE RESTRICT,
        qty        INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (qty > 0),
        -- The price is copied at the time of sale, not joined in. This is
        -- deliberate denormalisation: a historical invoice must not change
        -- when the catalogue price changes.
        price_cents INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
        PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
    );
""")
conn.commit()
print([r[0] for r in conn.execute(
    "SELECT name FROM sqlite_master WHERE type='table' ORDER BY name")])

# --- Types differ by engine; write portable DDL in a narrow subset ---------
# SQLite: dynamic typing with per-column "affinity"; INTEGER/REAL/TEXT/BLOB/
# NUMERIC, no strict length, no unsigned, 64-bit INTEGER PRIMARY KEY is the
# rowid alias.
# PostgreSQL: rich types, real ENUM, arrays, JSONB, generated columns, strict
# about length, no unsigned, and INTEGER is 32-bit (use BIGINT for large keys).
# MySQL: strict about length, unsigned types, ENUM and SET, no CHECK before
# 8.0.16, and a 255-byte index prefix limit in older configurations.
conn.execute("INSERT INTO customer VALUES (1, 'Acme', 'Berlin', 'DE')")
conn.execute("INSERT INTO product VALUES (1, 'SKU-1', 'Widget', 1999)")
conn.execute("INSERT INTO \"order\" (id, customer_id) VALUES (1, 1)")
conn.execute("INSERT INTO order_line VALUES (1, 1, 2, 1999)")
conn.commit()

# Constraints are enforced, not merely documented.
for stmt, params in [
    ("INSERT INTO product VALUES (1, 'SKU-1', 'Dup', 1)", ()),
    ("INSERT INTO product VALUES (2, 'SKU-2', 'Free', -5)", ()),
    ("INSERT INTO order_line VALUES (1, 1, 0, 1999)", ()),
    ('INSERT INTO "order" (id, customer_id) VALUES (2, 999)', ()),
]:
    try:
        conn.execute(stmt, params)
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError as exc:
        print("rejected:", exc)
print(conn.execute("SELECT * FROM order_line").fetchall())

# --- ON DELETE behaviour is a design decision, not a default --------------
try:
    conn.execute('DELETE FROM "order" WHERE id = 1')       # CASCADE removes lines
except sqlite3.IntegrityError as exc:
    print("blocked:", exc)
try:
    conn.execute("DELETE FROM product WHERE id = 1")        # RESTRICT on lines
except sqlite3.IntegrityError as exc:
    print("blocked:", exc)
print(conn.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM order_line").fetchone())
conn.close()

Money is an integer count of minor units in every engine, and never a floating-point value. 0.1 + 0.2 is not 0.3 in binary floating point, so a REAL price column accumulates error that eventually fails an exact reconciliation. SQLite’s NUMERIC affinity will silently coerce a decimal string to REAL if it looks numeric, which is a second reason to store cents as INTEGER. A NUMERIC or DECIMAL column exists in PostgreSQL and MySQL and should be preferred there when arithmetic happens inside the database; the two approaches are not interchangeable, and converting between them loses nothing but does not help either.

DenormalisationGainCost and required discipline
Copying the price into order_lineA historical invoice does not change when the catalogue does; the read needs no joinThe application must write the copy. Two writers can disagree; the historical copy is the authority and must never be updated from the catalogue.
A counter column maintained by triggersA count query becomes an indexed single-row readEvery write path must maintain it, or it drifts. In the application this is one extra UPDATE in the same transaction; in the database it needs a trigger. Triggers are invisible to the ORM and to autogenerate.
A denormalised read model or search indexQueries the normalised schema cannot answer in timeA second copy that must be rebuilt. It is a cache, and a cache is allowed to be wrong for a bounded window; say so explicitly in the model.
A JSON or JSONB payload beside normalised columnsSparse, query-by-example attributes need no table and no migration per attributeNo referential integrity, no index until one is added, and every query into it bypasses the relational model. Keep it for genuinely variable attributes only, never for a value other tables must join on.

Caveats: CHECK constraints are silently ignored by MySQL before 8.0.16 and by every SQLite build, which accepts and parses the syntax. A NOT NULL column that must accept "no value yet" has no representation in SQL other than a sentinel value or a separate boolean — this is the reason the two-table pattern for optional data exists. And a UNIQUE constraint on nullable columns has engine-specific semantics: most databases treat NULL as distinct from every other NULL, so the constraint does not prevent duplicate "absent" rows.

12.5 Indexes and query plans

An index is a redundant, sorted structure that trades write cost and storage for read speed. Every index is paid for on every INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE — in the index, not the table — so an index that no query uses is pure overhead. The tool for deciding is the query planner, and reading its output is a skill.

import sqlite3

conn = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
conn.executescript("""
    CREATE TABLE account (
        id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        owner      TEXT NOT NULL,
        balance    INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
    );
    CREATE TABLE txn (
        id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
        account_id  INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES account(id),
        amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL,
        created_at  INTEGER NOT NULL
    );
""")
conn.executemany("INSERT INTO account (owner, balance) VALUES (?, ?)",
                 [(f"o{i}", i * 100) for i in range(200)])
conn.executemany("INSERT INTO txn (account_id, amount_cents, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)",
                 [(i % 200 + 1, (i * 7) % 500 - 250, 1_700_000_000 + i) for i in range(50_000)])
conn.commit()
conn.execute("ANALYZE")          # required: the planner needs statistics

def plan(sql: str, params: tuple = ()) -> list[str]:
    return [r[3] for r in conn.execute("EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN " + sql, params)]

# 1. No index: a full table scan. Correct, and O(n).
print("scan      :", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE amount_cents = 42"))
conn.execute("CREATE INDEX ix_txn_amount ON txn (amount_cents)")
print("indexed   :", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE amount_cents = 42"))

# 2. Composite index column order is the single most consequential decision.
conn.execute("CREATE INDEX ix_txn_acct_time ON txn (account_id, created_at)")
conn.execute("DROP INDEX IF EXISTS ix_txn_acct_time")
# Equality columns come first; range columns come after them. A query filtering
# on created_at alone cannot use the leading column of this index.
conn.execute("CREATE INDEX ix_txn_acct_time ON txn (account_id, created_at)")
print("acct+time :", plan(
    "SELECT * FROM txn WHERE account_id = 3 AND created_at > 1700000000"))
print("time only :", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE created_at > 1700000000"))
# A second index with the reversed order serves the other query; two indexes
# on (a,b) and (b,a) are genuinely different indexes, not duplicates.
conn.execute("CREATE INDEX ix_txn_time ON txn (created_at)")
print("time idx  :", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE created_at > 1700000000"))

# 3. Covering index: every column the query needs is in the index, so the
#    table itself is never touched. EXPLAIN says "USING COVERING INDEX".
conn.execute("CREATE INDEX ix_cover ON txn (account_id, amount_cents)")
print("covering  :", plan(
    "SELECT amount_cents FROM txn WHERE account_id = 7"))
print("not cover :", plan(
    "SELECT amount_cents, id FROM txn WHERE account_id = 7"))

# 4. When an index is IGNORED.
print("or-ignore 1:", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE account_id = 3 OR id = 4"))
print("function   :", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE abs(amount_cents) = 5"))
print("leading wild:", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE owner LIKE '%acme'"))
print("low selectr:", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE amount_cents = 42", ()))
print("stats      :", plan("SELECT * FROM txn WHERE amount_cents > 100000"))

print("\nindexes on txn:")
for r in conn.execute("PRAGMA index_list(txn)"):
    print("  ", r[1], "unique" if r[2] else "")
print("\ncolumns in ix_cover:", [r[2] for r in conn.execute("PRAGMA index_info(ix_cover)")])
print("\nfull stats for one index:")
for r in conn.execute("SELECT * FROM sqlite_stat1 WHERE tbl='txn' LIMIT 3"):
    print("  ", r)
conn.close()

A B-tree index stores keys in sorted order together with a pointer to the row, which makes equality and range lookups both logarithmic. The columns of a composite index are sorted lexicographically: first by column one, then by column two among rows with equal column one. That is why the order matters. An index on (a, b) can serve WHERE a = 1, WHERE a = 1 AND b = 2 and WHERE a = 1 AND b > 2. It can also serve a GROUP BY a, b ORDER BY a, b without a sort step. It cannot serve WHERE b = 2 at all, because the index is not sorted by b until the a values are exhausted.

SituationWhy the index is not used
WHERE f(col) = 5The index is sorted by the value of col, not by the value of f(col). The usual fix is a functional index: CREATE INDEX ix ON t (lower(col)), with the identical expression in the query.
LIKE '%abc'A leading wildcard cannot be a prefix seek. A trigram or full-text index is the right structure, and neither is in SQLite before its own FTS module.
Very low selectivityA filter matching most rows is cheaper as a scan. The planner compares estimated cost, and after ANALYZE it usually makes the right call — provided the statistics are fresh.
Stale statisticsWithout ANALYZE the planner assumes a uniform distribution and picks the wrong plan, usually a scan where an index seek was correct.
Leading wildcard of a composite indexThe predicate does not constrain the index’s leading column, so no seek is possible on the rest of it either.
OR across different columnsUsually rewritten as a union of index scans, but only when every branch is indexable. One unindexed branch forces a scan of the whole table.
Implicit type conversionComparing an indexed INTEGER column to the string '5' may prevent the seek, because the string cannot be ordered against the stored integers. In PostgreSQL this is a hard error; in SQLite the affinity rules decide.
Small tableBelow a few hundred rows a scan is one page-tree traversal. The planner will ignore the index, correctly.

Measure index value with the load, not with a synthetic query. EXPLAIN ANALYZE (PostgreSQL, and SQLite as EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN does not execute) prints actual row counts and timings and therefore the estimate error; comparing the estimated and actual rows is the single fastest way to find a stale-statistics problem. A planner that underestimates by orders of magnitude on a skewed column is usually reading a stale histogram, and refreshing statistics is cheaper than any index you could add.

Do not add an index per foreign key reflexively. It is a good idea on a column that is frequently filtered or joined and where the child side is large; it is a waste on a small table and a cost on every insert. The one case where it is almost always right is a foreign key whose parent deletion must be checked on every delete of the parent row — without an index, that check is a full scan of the child table per deleted row. Measure first.

12.6 SQLAlchemy Core and the Engine

SQLAlchemy Core is a SQL expression toolkit and a connection pool. It is not an ORM: it emits SQL from composable Python expressions, and the objects that come back are tuples or row mappings rather than mapped instances. Its value over DB-API is dialect portability, pool management, and the ability to build a statement once and execute it with different parameters. SQLAlchemy 2.0 made the 2.0-style API the only supported one; the pre-2.0 Query object is legacy.

# pip install "sqlalchemy>=2.0"
from __future__ import annotations

import sqlalchemy as sa
from sqlalchemy.engine import Engine, Row
from sqlalchemy.pool import QueuePool

DB_URL = "sqlite+pysqlite:///app.db"

engine: Engine = sa.create_engine(
    DB_URL,
    echo=False,                       # True logs every statement; see 14.5
    future=True,                       # 2.0 style; the default since 2.0
    poolclass=sa.pool.StaticPool,      # SQLite: keep one connection in memory
    connect_args={"check_same_thread": False},
)

# --- Pool settings, and why each one exists --------------------------------
engine.dispose()
engine = sa.create_engine(
    "postgresql+psycopg://user:pw@host/db",
    pool_size=5,            # persistent connections held open
    max_overflow=10,        # extra connections allowed above pool_size
    pool_timeout=30,        # seconds to wait for a connection before raising
    pool_recycle=1800,      # recycle a connection after 30 min (server timeouts)
    pool_pre_ping=True,     # SELECT 1 before checkout, to catch a dead socket
    pool_use_lifo=True,     # LIFO keeps the hot end of the pool warm
    max_overflow=-1,        # no limit; correct for a short-lived worker process
    isolation_level="READ COMMITTED",
)

# --- Metadata and a table definition, the Core style -----------------------
metadata = sa.MetaData()
account = sa.Table(
    "account",
    metadata,
    sa.Column("id", sa.Integer, sa.Identity(), primary_key=True),
    sa.Column("owner", sa.String(64), nullable=False, unique=True),
    sa.Column("balance_cents", sa.BigInteger, nullable=False, server_default="0"),
    sa.CheckConstraint("balance_cents >= 0", name="ck_account_nonneg"),
)
txn = sa.Table(
    "txn",
    metadata,
    sa.Column("id", sa.Integer, sa.Identity(), primary_key=True),
    sa.Column("account_id", sa.ForeignKey("account.id", ondelete="CASCADE"), nullable=False),
    sa.Column("amount_cents", sa.BigInteger, nullable=False),
    sa.Column("memo", sa.String(200)),
)
metadata.create_all(engine)              # development convenience only

# --- INSERT ---------------------------------------------------------------
with engine.begin() as conn:              # begin/commit, rollback on exception
    result = conn.execute(
        account.insert().values(owner="acme", balance_cents=0)
    )
    new_id = result.inserted_primary_key[0]
    print("inserted", new_id, "rowcount", result.rowcount)

with engine.begin() as conn:
    conn.execute(
        account.insert(),
        [{"owner": f"o{i}", "balance_cents": 0} for i in range(100)],   # executemany
    )

# --- SELECT: the 2.0 style ------------------------------------------------
with engine.connect() as conn:
    stmt = sa.select(account.c.id, account.c.owner).where(
        account.c.balance_cents > sa.literal(500)
    )
    for row in conn.execute(stmt):
        print(row.id, row.owner, row.balance_cents)

    # ORM-style execution, no mapping required.
    for acct in conn.execute(sa.select(account)):
        print(acct)
    print(conn.execute(sa.select(account).limit(1)).mappings().first())

    # Aggregates and grouping.
    q = (
        sa.select(txn.c.account_id, sa.func.count().label("n"),
                  sa.func.sum(txn.c.amount_cents).label("total"))
        .where(txn.c.amount_cents != 0)
        .group_by(txn.c.account_id)
        .having(sa.func.count() > 1)
        .order_by(sa.desc("total"))
        .limit(10)
    )
    print(conn.execute(q).all())

    # EXISTS and correlated subqueries.
    dup = sa.select(
        account.c.owner,
        sa.select(sa.func.count())
        .select_from(account)
        .where(account.c.balance_cents > 1000)
        .scalar_subquery(),
    )
    print(conn.execute(dup).all())

# --- UPDATE and DELETE, both guarded by rowcount --------------------------
with engine.begin() as conn:
    n = conn.execute(
        account.update()
        .where(account.c.owner == "acme")
        .values(balance_cents=account.c.balance_cents + 500)
    ).rowcount
    print("credited", n)
    if n != 1:
        raise LookupError("acme not found")
    n = conn.execute(account.delete().where(account.c.balance_cents < 0)).rowcount
    print("deleted", n)

# --- Raw text, still safe -------------------------------------------------
with engine.begin() as conn:
    # WRONG: the value is concatenated, so it is parsed as SQL.
    conn.execute(sa.text(f"SELECT * FROM account WHERE owner = '{'x'}'"))
    # RIGHT: bindparam() makes it a real bound parameter.
    safe = sa.text("SELECT * FROM account WHERE owner = :owner").bindparams(
        owner="o' OR '1'='1"
    )
    print(conn.execute(safe).all())
    # Executing a literal string is never safe. do_execute() with no
    # parameters is the canonical injection sink and should be banned by review.
    conn.exec_driver_sql("SELECT count(*) FROM account")
engine.dispose()

Three API objects matter. The Engine owns the connection pool and the dialect, and is a process-level singleton — create it once at application start-up and dispose it at shutdown. A Connection is a checked-out connection with a transaction; engine.begin() is a context manager that commits on exit and rolls back on exception, and it is the correct default. A Result is a buffered or streaming result; conn.execute() buffers everything by default, so a SELECT returning ten million rows loads all ten million into memory unless execution_options(yield_per=...) is used.

sa.text() is a wrapper around a string, not an escaper. Bound parameters inside it are safe; the string around them is not. Passing user input through text(f"... WHERE x = '{value}'") reintroduces the vulnerability that the typed API exists to remove, while looking as though parameters were involved. Static analysis rules worth enforcing: no f-string or %-format inside text(), and no exec_driver_sql with interpolation.

Dialect URL schemeDriverNotes
sqlite://, sqlite+pysqlite://stdlib sqlite3Default pool is NullPool for file databases in SQLAlchemy 1.4/2.0 for thread safety; StaticPool for :memory:. check_same_thread defaults to True.
postgresql+psycopg://psycopg 3Preferred. postgresql+psycopg2:// is psycopg 2, which reached end of life in 2024 and is no longer recommended.
postgresql+asyncpg://asyncpgAsync only; see 12.10.
mysql+pymysql://PyMySQLPure Python, slow to install. mysql+mysqldb:// is mysqlclient, which is faster and needs a C toolchain.
mssql+pyodbc://pyodbcRequires the ODBC Driver Manager. Named placeholders and a different identifier quoting.
oracle+oracledb://python-oracledbThin mode needs no Oracle client libraries.

Caveats: the pool is per Engine, and a fork() duplicates the pool’s file descriptors into the child, where they are shared with the parent and will corrupt the connection state. Any process that forks must call engine.dispose(close=False) immediately after forking, or create its own engine in the child. The same applies to multiprocessing with the fork start method, which is the default on Linux before 3.14; see 14.3.

12.7 The declarative ORM and the unit of work

The ORM adds three things to Core: mapped classes, an identity map so one database row yields one Python object, and a unit of work that batches changes and flushes them in dependency order. The unit of work is the concept to understand, because it is the source of the flush-versus-commit surprise, of the MissingGreenlet error in async code, and of nearly every "why did my change not appear" report.

from __future__ import annotations

import datetime as dt
from typing import List, Optional

import sqlalchemy as sa
from sqlalchemy.orm import DeclarativeBase, Mapped, mapped_column, relationship,
    selectinload, joinedload, raiseload, sessionmaker

class Base(DeclarativeBase):
    pass

class Customer(Base):
    __tablename__ = "customer"
    id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(primary_key=True)
    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(sa.String(64), unique=True)
    city: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(sa.String(64))
    # A relationship is lazy by default: the SELECT fires on first attribute
    # access, not at query time.
    orders: Mapped[List["Order"]] = relationship(back_populates="customer")

class Order(Base):
    __tablename__ = "orders"
    id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(primary_key=True)
    customer_id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(sa.ForeignKey("customer.id"))
    placed_at: Mapped[dt.datetime] = mapped_column(sa.DateTime(timezone=True))
    total_cents: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(sa.BigInteger, default=0)
    customer: Mapped[Customer] = relationship(back_populates="orders")
    lines: Mapped[List["OrderLine"]] = relationship(
        back_populates="order", cascade="all, delete-orphan"
    )

class OrderLine(Base):
    __tablename__ = "order_line"
    order_id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(sa.ForeignKey("orders.id"), primary_key=True)
    product_id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(sa.ForeignKey("product.id"), primary_key=True)
    qty: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(sa.Integer)
    price_cents: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(sa.BigInteger)
    order: Mapped[Order] = relationship(back_populates="lines")

class Product(Base):
    __tablename__ = "product"
    id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(primary_key=True)
    sku: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(sa.String(32), unique=True)
    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(sa.String(64))

engine = sa.create_engine("sqlite+pysqlite:///:memory:", echo=False)
Base.metadata.create_all(engine)
Session = sessionmaker(bind=engine, expire_on_commit=False, future=True)

with Session() as session:                  # closes the session; does not commit
    c = Customer(name="acme", city="Berlin")
    session.add(c)
    o = Order(customer=c, placed_at=dt.datetime.now(dt.UTC))
    session.add(o)
    o.lines.append(OrderLine(product=Product(sku="S1", name="Widget"),
                             qty=2, price_cents=1999))
    # FLUSH: INSERTs are emitted now, in dependency order, so that order.id and
    # order_line.order_id exist. The transaction is still open.
    session.flush()
    print("order id", o.id, "line key", o.lines[0].order_id)
    session.commit()                         # COMMIT: the change is durable

# --- Reading, and the identity map ----------------------------------------
with Session() as session:
    c1 = session.get(Customer, 1)            # SELECT, populates the identity map
    c2 = session.get(Customer, 1)            # NO query: the map already holds it
    print(c1 is c2, c1.name)

    stmt = select(Customer).where(Customer.city == "Berlin").order_by(Customer.name)
    for cust in session.scalars(stmt):
        print(cust.name, len(cust.orders))   # len() touches .orders -> a query

# --- flush versus commit, spelled out ------------------------------------
with Session() as session:
    o = session.get(Order, 1)
    o.total_cents = 4998
    print("in session, dirty:", o in session.dirty)   # True before any SQL
    session.flush()                                   # UPDATE emitted now
    print("after flush, dirty:", o in session.dirty)   # False
    session.rollback()                                # discards the UPDATE
    print("after rollback:", session.get(Order, 1).total_cents)

# --- Updates and deletes are tracked automatically -----------------------
with Session() as session:
    session.execute(sa.update(Order).where(Order.id == 1).values(total_cents=0))
    print("synchronise_session: identity map updated in place")
    session.execute(sa.delete(Order).where(Order.id == 999))
    with session.begin_nested():              # a SAVEPOINT
        session.add(Customer(name="temp", city="-"))
        session.flush()
        raise RuntimeError("rollback only the savepoint")
    print("survived:", session.get(Customer, 1).name)
engine.dispose()

The sequence add → flush → commit is the whole model. flush writes pending changes inside the still-open transaction and assigns generated primary keys; commit makes them durable. The automatic flush points are the first query in a session, the first flush(), and the commit itself — which is why a query can raise an IntegrityError for a row you added ten lines earlier. Ordering within a flush follows the mapper’s dependency graph, so a parent is inserted before its children, with one round trip per savepoint-free batch.

Loading strategyQuery shapeWhen it is right
lazy="select" (default)One SELECT on first access, per objectOne-to-one and many-to-one relationships, and collections the code almost never touches. Also the correct answer for a large collection that is usually not needed at all.
selectinOne extra SELECT ... IN (...) for the whole result setTo-many collections. Avoids the row multiplication of a join, issues a bounded number of queries, and works with limit() and offset().
joinedA LEFT OUTER JOIN in the same statementMany-to-one and one-to-one relationships, where the join does not multiply rows. A joined eager load of a to-many relation silently discards duplicates unless unique() is applied, which is where “the last row wins” bugs come from.
subqueryThe parent query as a subquery plus a joinLegacy of joined for to-many loads, kept for compatibility. selectin is the modern replacement.
raiseloadRaises instead of emitting the lazy SELECTTest and review enforcement. Converts a silent N+1 into a failing test at the point where it is introduced.
noload / noasyncRaises on any access / disables the load in async contextsMaking an accidental lazy load impossible rather than merely discouraged.
immediateLoads on every access, ignoring the identity mapRare and slow. Useful for mutable server-side data, at a cost.

Caveats: expire_on_commit=True, the default, discards every loaded attribute on commit so the next access re-reads from the database — which is correct for isolation and surprising in a loop. Setting it to False keeps values but means a second session’s commit is invisible. A relationship with cascade="all, delete-orphan" makes the parent own the children’s lifecycle; without it, deleting the parent attempts to null a non-nullable foreign key. And lazy="raise" on a relationship is the cheapest way to make accidental queries impossible in a hot path.

12.8 The N+1 query problem

The N+1 problem is the single most common performance defect in ORM-based Python. It has a fixed shape: a query returns N rows, and the code accessing an attribute of each row causes N further queries. It is invisible in development because the dataset is small, and it is catastrophic in production because each additional query is a network round trip. The fix is not to remove the attribute access; it is to declare the loading strategy up front so the database does the joining in one statement.

from __future__ import annotations

import sqlalchemy as sa
from sqlalchemy import event
from sqlalchemy.orm import Session, selectinload, joinedload, raiseload

# The Customer / Order / OrderLine / Product models are those defined in 12.7.
# They are not repeated here; this section only concerns statement counts.

def count_statements(session: Session, fn) -> tuple[list, int]:
    """Run fn and return (result, number of SQL statements actually emitted)."""
    n = 0

    def hook(*args, **kwargs):
        nonlocal n
        n += 1

    event.listen(session.get_bind(), "before_cursor_execute", hook)
    try:
        return fn(), n
    finally:
        event.remove(session.get_bind(), "before_cursor_execute", hook)

# --- The defect ------------------------------------------------------------
def load_lazy(session: Session) -> list[tuple[str, int]]:
    out = []
    for cust in session.scalars(sa.select(Customer)):
        out.append((cust.name, len(cust.orders)))     # one query per customer
    return out

rows, n = count_statements(session, lambda: load_lazy(session))
print("lazy :", rows, f"{n} statements for {len(rows)} customers")

# --- The fix: selectinload -------------------------------------------------
def load_selectin(session: Session) -> list[tuple[str, int]]:
    stmt = sa.select(Customer).options(selectinload(Customer.orders))
    return [(c.name, len(c.orders)) for c in session.scalars(stmt)]

rows, n = count_statements(session, lambda: load_selectin(session))
print("selectin:", rows, f"{n} statements regardless of customer count")

# --- The fix: joinedload, for a many-to-one -------------------------------
def load_joined(session: Session) -> list[tuple[str, int]]:
    stmt = sa.select(Customer).options(joinedload(Customer.orders))
    return [(c.name, len(c.orders)) for c in session.scalars(stmt)]

rows, n = count_statements(session, lambda: load_joined(session))
print("joined:", rows, f"{n} statements (row multiplication for a to-many)")

# --- Enforce it, so it cannot come back -----------------------------------
def load_strict(session: Session) -> list[tuple[str, int]]:
    stmt = sa.select(Customer).options(
        raiseload("*", ok_of=(sa.orm.Select,))
    )
    return [(c.name, len(c.orders)) for c in session.scalars(stmt)]

try:
    count_statements(session, lambda: load_strict(session))
except sa.exc.InvalidRequestError as exc:
    print("raiseload caught it:", str(exc).splitlines()[0])

# --- When eager loading is the wrong fix ----------------------------------
# 1. The collection is usually not needed: filter in SQL instead of fetching
#    everything and discarding it in Python.
stmt = (
    sa.select(Customer.id)
    .join(Customer.orders)
    .where(Order.total_cents > 1000)
    .group_by(Customer.id)
)
# 2. The result is a projection, not an object graph.
stmt = sa.select(Customer.name, sa.func.count(Order.id))
# 3. The relationship is genuinely unbounded. Eagerly loading a collection
#    with a million rows is an outage; a second explicit, paginated query is
#    the correct design.

Diagnosis before treatment. sessionmaker(bind=e, echo=True) prints every statement; a burst of structurally identical SELECT statements after one other query is the signature. The same is visible in server-side query logs by grouping a trace by statement text and counting. pytest-recording or a before_cursor_execute counter in a test assert the statement count is bounded, which is a regression test worth having: selectinload that is accidentally removed fails the test rather than doubling production load.

Do not reach for eager loading by reflex. Adding selectinload to a query that fetches ten thousand parents will issue a second query materialising ten thousand collections — the same memory pressure as the N+1, at a lower query count. The three legitimate answers, in order, are: push the filter or aggregate into SQL, project only the columns needed, or paginate the relationship into its own explicit query. Eager loading is correct when the relationship is small, bounded, and almost always needed.

Async sessions are affected differently and fail louder. A lazy load in an async def function raises MissingGreenlet, because the implicit IO cannot be awaited from the attribute-access site. That error is a feature: it converts every N+1 in an async code path into an immediate, obvious failure. See 12.10.

12.9 Schema migrations with Alembic

A migration is an ordered, reversible script, one per schema change, recorded in version control and applied by name. Alembic is the reference implementation: it maintains a linear or branched history in an alembic_version table, generates candidate revisions by diffing the ORM metadata against the live database, and applies or downgrades them. Its autogenerate is a starting point, not an answer; reviewing the generated file is the whole point.

# pip install alembic
# alembic init -t generic migrations      # creates alembic.ini and migrations/
#
# alembic.ini
#   [alembic]
#   script_location = migrations
#   sqlalchemy.url  =            # leave EMPTY: get it from alembic/env.py
#
# migrations/env.py -- the two lines that matter
from alembic import context
from sqlalchemy import engine_from_config, pool
import myapp.models            # noqa: F401  -- importing registers the mappers

config = context.config
target_metadata = myapp.models.Base.metadata       # autogen compares against this

def run_migrations_offline() -> None:
    context.configure(
        url=config.get_main_option("sqlalchemy.url"),
        target_metadata=target_metadata,
        literal_binds=True,
        dialect_opts={"paramstyle": "named"},
        compare_type=True,               # detect column type changes
        compare_server_default=True,      # detect server_default changes
        render_as_batch=True,             # needed for SQLite ALTER
    )
    with context.begin_transaction():
        context.run_migrations()

def run_migrations_online() -> None:
    connectable = config.attributes.get("connection", None)
    if connectable is None:
        connectable = engine_from_config(
            config.get_section(config.config_ini_section, {}),
            prefix="sqlalchemy.",
            poolclass=pool.NullPool,
        )
    with connectable.connect() as connection:
        context.configure(
            connection=connection,
            target_metadata=target_metadata,
            compare_type=True,
            compare_server_default=True,
            render_as_batch=True,
        )
        with context.begin_transaction():
            context.run_migrations()

if context.is_offline_mode():
    run_migrations_offline()
else:
    run_migrations_online()

render_as_batch=True exists because SQLite cannot ALTER TABLE ... DROP CONSTRAINT, cannot change a column type, and cannot change a nullability. In batch mode Alembic emits a create-new/copy-data/drop-old/rename sequence instead. On PostgreSQL the same operations are direct, and the batch mode is unnecessary. The two settings differ per environment, so the value is read from configuration rather than hard-coded.

# --- A hand-reviewed revision ---------------------------------------------
# revision identifiers are generated; do not edit them.
"""add order_line.price_cents and backfill from product

Revision ID: 0a1b2c3d4e5f
Revises:    9f8e7d6c5b4a
Create Date: 2026-08-12 09:41:07
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import sqlalchemy as sa
from alembic import op

revision = "0a1b2c3d4e5f"
down_revision = "9f8e7d6c5b4a"
branch_labels = None
depends_on = None

def upgrade() -> None:
    bind = op.get_bind()
    # 1. Additive, nullable first: the table stays writable by an old release
    #    of the application that is still running during the deploy.
    op.add_column("order_line", sa.Column("price_cents", sa.BigInteger(), nullable=True))
    # 2. Backfill in batches. A single UPDATE over a large table takes a long
    #    exclusive lock; chunk it so the change is restartable.
    offset = 0
    while True:
        rows = bind.execute(
            sa.text("SELECT order_id, product_id FROM order_line "
                    "WHERE price_cents IS NULL LIMIT 5000 OFFSET :o"),
            {"o": offset},
        ).fetchall()
        if not rows:
            break
        for order_id, product_id in rows:
            price = bind.execute(
                sa.text("SELECT price_cents FROM product WHERE id = :pid"),
                {"pid": product_id},
            ).scalar()
            bind.execute(
                sa.text("UPDATE order_line SET price_cents = :p "
                        "WHERE order_id = :o AND product_id = :q"),
                {"p": price, "o": order_id, "q": product_id},
            )
        offset += len(rows)
    # 3. Enforce, once every row has a value.
    op.alter_column("order_line", "price_cents", nullable=False)
    op.create_check_constraint(
        "ck_order_line_price_nonneg", "order_line", "price_cents >= 0"
    )

def downgrade() -> None:
    op.drop_constraint("ck_order_line_price_nonneg", "order_line", type_="check")
    op.drop_column("order_line", "price_cents")
CommandEffectNotes for production
alembic revision -m "msg" --autogenerateDiff metadata against the database, emit a revision fileAlways read the output. It does not detect renames (it emits drop + add, losing data), server-side default changes on every engine, or data migrations of any kind.
alembic upgrade headApply every pending revision in orderOne command at deploy time. Combine with a backup and a rehearsed rollback.
alembic upgrade +1Apply exactly one revisionThe right granularity for a release that must be able to stop after one step.
alembic downgrade -1Revert one revisionOnly reliable if downgrade() is implemented and, for a data migration, genuinely reversible. Most data migrations are not; write downgrade to raise instead, so the failure is explicit.
alembic history -vShow the revision graph with parentsInspect this before merging two branches — a merge revision is required, and its down_revision is a tuple.
alembic current, alembic headsApplied revisions; revisions with no childMore than one head means branches; more than one current means an inconsistency.
alembic stamp headRecord head without running anythingFor adopting Alembic on a database that already has the schema. It runs no DDL, so it is only correct if the schema genuinely matches.
alembic checkExit non-zero if metadata and database differThe right CI gate: it proves no model change was left without a migration.

Three deploy hazards are structural rather than incidental. Expand and contract: a column rename or a type narrowing must be split into two releases — add the new column, backfill, dual-write, switch reads, then drop the old one — because the old and new application versions overlap during a rolling deploy. Locking DDL: on PostgreSQL, adding a NOT NULL column with a default, changing a type, or adding a foreign key all take an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock and block the table for their duration; the modern non-blocking alternative is ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT since PostgreSQL 11, which is instant. Migration drift: running DDL outside Alembic makes the next autogenerate wrong, and the diff is applied against reality, not against the migration history.

Requirements: Alembic must be able to reach the target database from the deploy environment, which means a credential that differs from the application’s. Caveats: a migration that runs in the same transaction as application startup holds locks for its whole duration; run it as a separate deploy step with a timeout, and never inside the request path. And on SQLite, DDL is transactional, so a failed batch migration rolls back cleanly — a genuine advantage over MySQL, where DDL causes an implicit commit and a partial migration is a real state.

12.10 Asynchronous database access

An async database engine is a different client, not a faster one. It is the right choice when the application is already asynchronous and one connection would otherwise occupy an entire event loop for the duration of a query. It is the wrong choice when the driver is not native-async, because the gain then comes from thread offloading rather than from asynchrony, and the loss is the shared session’s thread safety.

from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio
import sqlalchemy as sa
from sqlalchemy.ext.asyncio import (
    AsyncSession, async_sessionmaker, create_async_engine,
)
from sqlalchemy.orm import selectinload

# --- create_async_engine, and what it is actually for ----------------------
# The URL scheme names the ASYNC driver. postgresql+psycopg (psycopg 3) is
# usable in both sync and async; postgresql+asyncpg is async only.
engine = create_async_engine(
    "sqlite+aiosqlite:///app.db",
    echo=False,
    pool_pre_ping=True,
    # SQLite has a single writer. Without this, concurrent writes raise
    # "database is locked" rather than queueing.
    connect_args={"timeout": 30},
)
AsyncSessionLocal = async_sessionmaker(
    engine, class_=AsyncSession, expire_on_commit=False, autoflush=False
)

class Repo:
    def __init__(self, session: AsyncSession) -> None:
        self.session = session

    async def get_customer(self, cid: int) -> dict | None:
        stmt = sa.select(Customer).where(Customer.id == cid)
        cust = await self.session.scalar(stmt)
        return None if cust is None else {"id": cust.id, "name": cust.name}

    async def names_with_orders(self) -> list[str]:
        # Eager loading is not optional here. A lazy load would raise
        # MissingGreenlet instead of awaiting.
        stmt = sa.select(Customer).options(selectinload(Customer.orders))
        return [c.name for c in (await self.session.scalars(stmt)).all()]

async def get_all(ids: list[int]) -> list[dict]:
    # ONE connection, several queries, round trips pipelined by the driver.
    # This is the actual benefit: not "async is faster" but "the loop is not
    # blocked while any of these is in flight".
    out: list[dict] = []
    async with AsyncSessionLocal() as session:
        repo = Repo(session)
        for cid in ids:
            row = await repo.get_customer(cid)
            if row is not None:
                out.append(row)
    return out

async def concurrent_wrong(session: AsyncSession) -> None:
    # BROKEN. AsyncSession is explicitly NOT safe for concurrent use, and
    # asyncio.gather on one session interleaves statements on one connection.
    await asyncio.gather(
        session.execute(sa.select(Customer)),
        session.execute(sa.select(Order)),
    )

async def concurrent_right(pool: list[AsyncSession]) -> None:
    # CORRECT: one session per concurrent task, each with its own connection.
    await asyncio.gather(*(s.execute(sa.select(Customer)) for s in pool))

async def stream(conn) -> None:
    # yield_per turns the buffered Result into a server-side stream. Without
    # it, a five-million-row SELECT is loaded entirely into memory.
    result = await conn.stream(sa.select(Order))
    async for order in result:
        print(order.id)
        break
    await result.close()

async def main() -> None:
    async with engine.begin() as conn:                 # begin/commit/rollback
        await conn.execute(sa.text("SELECT 1"))
    async with AsyncSessionLocal() as session:
        repo = Repo(session)
        print(await repo.get_customer(1))
        print(await repo.names_with_orders())
    # One task, one session, one connection.
    await asyncio.gather(*(get_all([1, 2]) for _ in range(4)))
    await engine.dispose()

asyncio.run(main())
ClaimVerdictWhy
"Async makes the query faster."FalseThe query takes the same time on the server. The loop is not blocked meanwhile, which matters only when there is other work to do.
"Async raises throughput."Conditionally trueOne connection can have one statement in flight on most drivers, so concurrency comes from more connections, not from faster ones. The pool size is the throughput limit.
"One session, many tasks."UnsafeAsyncSession is not task-safe. Interleaved operations share one connection and one transaction. Use one session per task, and do not pass a session across a gather.
"Lazy loading is fine in async."Raises MissingGreenletAttribute access cannot await. Every relationship must be eagerly loaded or marked lazy="raise".
"Use the sync engine in a thread."Workable, slowerasyncio.to_thread around a blocking driver is correct and simpler. It costs a thread per in-flight query, which caps concurrency at the thread pool size.
"Blocking calls are always fatal in async."True, and not obviousOne synchronous requests.get in an async handler stalls every other task for its full duration. time.sleep, synchronous filesystem calls and CPU-bound work have the same effect. See 14.4.

Concurrency caveat: the pool bounds everything. With pool_size=5 and max_overflow=10, the sixteenth concurrent task waits up to pool_timeout and then raises TimeoutError from the pool — a database-layer timeout that is frequently misdiagnosed as a server problem. Size the pool from the database’s own connection limit divided by the number of application instances, and put a request timeout above the acquire timeout so the outer layer reports something meaningful.

Free-threaded builds: on a python3.14t interpreter (see 14.1) the sync GIL-based reasoning in 14.3 does not apply, and the CPython team explicitly recommends the free-threaded build for multi-threaded IO. For async code it makes no measurable difference: a single-threaded event loop was never contending for the GIL. The relevant caveats for async are unchanged — session task-safety, pool sizing, and the absence of blocking calls.

Exercises 12

  1. Write a minimal DB-API 2.0 driver, exposing apilevel, threadsafety, paramstyle, connect, the cursor methods and the full exception hierarchy, and pass the DB-API compliance check in the standard library’s test.test_dbapi suite as far as the optional extensions allow.
  2. Load the same CSV fixture into SQLite under three schemas — a wide flattened table, a 3NF set of five tables, and a hybrid with a JSONB-equivalent column — and measure row size, insert throughput and the time for five named reporting queries.
  3. Prove the paramstyle independence of a query layer by writing it once against sqlite3 and once against a driver that uses a different placeholder style, and confirm both produce the same statement modulo the placeholder tokens.
  4. Run the six injection payloads from 12.3 against a vulnerable helper and a bound-parameter helper, and add a regression test asserting that the column count, the row count and the table list are unchanged after each one.
  5. Enumerate every constraint in a small schema, deliberately violate each one, and record the exact exception class and message for both SQLite and a server engine, noting every place the two disagree.
  6. Load 500,000 rows into a table with no index, then add indexes in turn — single column, composite in both orders, and a covering index — recording EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN output and wall time for five different queries after each step, and identify which index is redundant.
  7. Write a connection factory that enforces WAL, foreign_keys = ON, busy_timeout and a busy isolation_level, then run two processes writing concurrently at 200 transactions per second and show that zero writes are lost.
  8. Implement a repository over SQLAlchemy Core, then rewrite it over the declarative ORM, and compare the emitted SQL for the same five operations, noting every construct that could not be expressed identically.
  9. Write a unit-of-work demonstration that produces a MissingGreenlet in an async def function by triggering a lazy load, then eliminate it with selectinload and with lazy="raise", and show the difference in statement count.
  10. Build an Alembic history of eight revisions, including a column rename, a type change, a NOT NULL addition and a backfill, and verify the sequence applies to a populated database, rolls back cleanly, and re-applies without data loss.

13 · Packaging, Environments and Tooling

13.1 pyproject.toml and PEP 62113.2 Virtual environments13.3 Resolvers, specifiers and pinning13.4 Build backends, wheels and sdists13.5 Publishing to PyPI

Python’s distribution model is the part of the language most often left implicit, and it is the source of the majority of environment-specific failures. Three independent specifications meet here: PEP 517 and PEP 660 for building, PEP 621 for declaring project metadata, and PEP 440 for expressing version constraints. This chapter states the current tooling — uv as the resolver and installer, venv from the standard library for isolation, and one of the modern build backends — and explains the reasoning, because the reasoning is what transfers when the tooling changes again.

13.1 pyproject.toml and PEP 621

A single declarative file at the project root describes the build system, the project metadata and the tool configuration. Since PEP 621 it is the authoritative source of metadata, and setup.py is no longer required for anything except the rare legacy build step. Because it is TOML, a parser is in the standard library as of 3.11, so the file is readable by the application itself with no dependency at all.

[build-system]
requires = ["hatchling>=1.27"]
build-backend = "hatchling.build"

[project]
name = "acme-tools"                     # normalised: lowercase, - or _ only
version = "2.4.1"                       # static here; see 13.5 for dynamic
description = "Order reconciliation utilities for the Acme platform."
