readme = "README.md"                    # becomes long_description
requires-python = ">=3.11"             # the single most valuable field
license = "MIT"                         # SPDX expression, PEP 639, since 3.14-era tooling
authors = [{ name = "Platform Team", email = "platform@example.invalid" }]
keywords = ["orders", "reconciliation", "etl"]
classifiers = [
  "Development Status :: 5 - Production/Stable",
  "Intended Audience :: Developers",
  "License :: OSI Approved :: MIT License",
  "Programming Language :: Python :: 3.11",
  "Programming Language :: Python :: 3.12",
  "Programming Language :: Python :: 3.13",
  "Programming Language :: Python :: 3.14",
  "Typing :: Typed",
]
dependencies = [
  "httpx>=0.27,<1",                  # a range, not a pin -- see 13.3
  "pydantic>=2.9",
]

[project.optional-dependencies]
# An extra is a named, documented, optional capability with its own versions.
dev    = ["pytest>=8", "pytest-cov", "mypy", "ruff"]
remote = ["asyncpg>=0.30", "alembic>=1.14"]

[project.urls]
Homepage     = "https://example.invalid/acme-tools"
Source       = "https://github.com/example/acme-tools"
"Bug Tracker" = "https://github.com/example/acme-tools/issues"

[project.scripts]                       # console_scripts entry points
acme-reconcile = "acme_tools.cli:main"

[project.entry-points."acme.plugins"]
csv-sink = "acme_tools.sinks:CSVSink"

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Tool configuration lives in the same file, in its own table, and is ignored
# by build backends. One file, one source of truth, no config drift.
[tool.hatch.build.targets.wheel]
packages = ["src/acme_tools"]

[tool.ruff]
line-length = 100
target-version = "py311"
src = ["src", "tests"]

[tool.ruff.lint]
select = ["E", "F", "I", "UP", "B", "SIM", "N", "ANN", "S", "BLE", "C4", "RUF"]
ignore = ["ANN401"]                      # allow explicit Any where it is deliberate

[tool.ruff.lint.per-file-ignores]
"tests/*" = ["S101", "ANN201", "ANN001"]   # asserts and untyped test args

[tool.mypy]
python_version = "3.11"
strict = true
plugins = ["pydantic.mypy"]
warn_unreachable = true

[tool.pytest.ini_options]
minversion = "8.0"
addopts    = "-ra --strict-markers --strict-config"
testpaths  = ["tests"]
markers    = ["slow: takes more than a second", "db: needs a database"]
xfail_strict = true
filterwarnings = ["error"]               # any warning becomes a failure

The [build-system] table is read by the build frontend, not by the application. It names the build backend and the packages the frontend must install into an isolated build environment before running it. Everything under [project] is standard metadata defined by PEP 621 and understood by every conforming tool. Everything under a [tool.*] table is that tool’s private configuration, which no other tool reads and no standard constrains — a design choice that keeps the file from becoming a place where unrelated tools argue.

PEP 621 fieldRequirementNotes
nameRequiredNormalised by PEP 503 to lowercase with runs of -, _ and . collapsed to a single -. The distribution name is case-insensitive and separator-insensitive.
versionRequired unless dynamicMust be a PEP 440 version. Two files must not disagree; pick one source of truth (see 13.5).
descriptionOptionalOne line, no markup. It becomes the index page summary.
requires-pythonOptional but decisiveA specifier set. It is the only signal pip uses to decide whether the wheel is even a candidate; an incorrect value produces a confusing resolver error rather than a clean refusal.
licenseOptionalAs an SPDX expression string under PEP 639. The legacy table form ({text = ...} and {file = ...}) is deprecated and removed by several backends.
dependenciesOptionalSpecifiers only; never URLs and never a bare filename. A direct reference to a VCS URL is legal but very hard to resolve reproducibly.
optional-dependenciesOptionalRequested as pip install .[dev] or pip install .[dev,remote].
[project.scripts]OptionalBecomes console_scripts. The value is an import path in dotted form; a name error surfaces only at first invocation, not at install.
dynamicOptionalDeclares which fields the backend computes, typically version or dependencies. A field listed here must not also be stated literally.
# src/ layout versus flat layout. The hazard is real and silent.
#
# FLAT LAYOUT (the module sits beside pyproject.toml)
#   myproject/
#     pyproject.toml
#     myproject/
#       __init__.py
#     tests/
#       test_core.py
#
# SRC LAYOUT
#   myproject/
#     pyproject.toml
#     src/
#       myproject/
#         __init__.py
#     tests/
#       test_core.py
#
# In the flat layout, "import myproject" resolves to the SOURCE DIRECTORY
# ./myproject -- a namespace package containing __init__.py, socore.py and
# nothing else -- whenever the interpreter's cwd (or a sys.path entry) is
# the project root. A non-editable install then appears to work while
# importing stale code from disk, and the difference is invisible until a
# subdirectory is missing.

import sys, importlib.util
print(importlib.util.find_spec("myproject"))    # reveals which file wins

# The src layout removes cwd from the picture entirely: on sys.path there is
# no ./myproject directory, so "import myproject" can only find the installed
# package. pytest adds rootdir/src to sys.path in this layout, which is what
# makes an editable install work without an .pth trick.
#
# Neither layout is wrong. src is the default recommendation for anything with
# tests, for the reason above.

# The distribution name (acme-tools) and the import name (acme_tools) are
# separate. Nothing derives one from the other automatically; the backend's
# package-discovery configuration is what maps them.

# Reading the metadata at run time, with no third-party dependency.
import importlib.metadata as md
dist = md.distribution("acme-tools")
print(dist.version)
print(dist.metadata["Requires-Python"])
print([r for r in dist.requires or [] if "extra = " not in r])
print(sorted(md.entry_points(group="console_scripts", name="acme-reconcile")[0].value))
print(md.version("acme-tools"))               # raises PackageNotFoundError

Applies to: all projects. Requirements: a build backend must be named in [build-system] or the tool falls back to setuptools. Caveats: pip install -e . is PEP 660 and its failure modes are backend-specific; if it misbehaves, build a wheel and install that. A pyproject.toml without a [build-system] table is not an error, it simply means legacy setuptools behaviour, which is exactly the ambiguity to avoid.

13.2 Virtual environments

A virtual environment is a directory containing a symlink or copy of the interpreter plus a private site-packages. Its only purpose is to make the set of installed distributions per project explicit and disposable. The mechanism it exists to solve is that Python resolves import against the union of sys.path, and the system site-packages directory is on that path for every process using the interpreter. Without isolation, two projects with incompatible requirements can only be reconciled by breaking one of them.

# --- The standard library tool ---------------------------------------------
# python3.14 -m venv .venv
#
# .venv/
#   bin/python            -> a symlink to the base interpreter on POSIX
#   bin/python3.14
#   bin/pip                a wrapper that pins itself to this interpreter
#   lib/python3.14/site-packages
#   pyvenv.cfg             the home interpreter path and the version flag
#
# The key mechanism is pyvenv.cfg: when sys.executable resolves to a venv
# interpreter, the interpreter reads that file, notes that it is in a venv, and
# EXCLUDES the base prefix's site-packages from sys.path.
import sys, site, sysconfig, os
print("executable      :", sys.executable)
print("prefix          :", sys.prefix)
print("base_prefix     :", sys.base_prefix)     # equal in a venv, different in a
                                                # container image or a venv
print("in_venv         :", sys.prefix != sys.base_prefix)
print("site-packages   :", site.getsitepackages())
print("purelib         :", sysconfig.get_path("purelib"))
print("scheme          :", sysconfig.get_default_scheme())
print("executable_     :", sys._base_executable)

# Nothing above means nothing installed is importable from outside the venv:
# this is the property that makes the venv worth creating.
for entry in sys.path:
    print("  path:", entry or "<cwd>")

# --- Options that matter ----------------------------------------------------
#   python -m venv .venv                 default
#   python -m venv --system-site-packages .venv
#       INHERITS the base environment. This defeats the purpose. It exists for
#       a legitimate and narrow case: a build host that must see a driver
#       installed system-wide (a common source of "it works on the server").
#   python -m venv --copies .venv
#       Copies the interpreter instead of symlinking. Needed where symlinks are
#       unavailable or do not survive a copy, notably some Windows setups and
#       most container COPY steps.
#   python -m venv --prompt acme .venv
#       Names the environment, which changes only the shell prompt.
#   python -m venv --upgrade-deps .venv
#       Upgrades pip (and setuptools where present) inside the new environment.

# --- Activation is cosmetic -----------------------------------------------
#   source .venv/bin/activate      POSIX, modifies PATH and VIRTUAL_ENV
#   .venv\Scripts\activate         Windows
# Activation sets PATH so that "python" and "pip" resolve to the venv. It is
# not required: every venv-local tool is equally reachable by absolute path,
# and "the wrong pip" is entirely a PATH problem.
VENV_BIN = os.path.join(".venv", "Scripts" if os.name == "nt" else "bin")
python_exe = os.path.join(VENV_BIN, "python")
print("equivalent to activation:", python_exe, "-m pip", "install", "-r", "req.txt")

# --- uv, which creates the environment and installs in one step ------------
#   uv venv                          create .venv, seeding nothing by default
#   uv venv --python 3.11            choose the interpreter
#   uv venv --seed                   also install pip
#   uv pip install -r requirements.txt
#   uv pip list / uv pip show / uv pip compile
# uv's own venv differs in one way: it does not install pip by default, and
# "uv run" will create the environment, resolve and execute in one step.

# --- What must never be in the environment -------------------------------
# - The interpreter. It belongs to the base installation and is selected with
#   --python or by .python-version, never installed by the venv.
# - Anything needed only to build the project. Build requirements live in
#   pyproject.toml [build-system].requires and are installed into an isolated
#   temporary environment by the frontend.

A virtual environment does not isolate the interpreter, the standard library, the C runtime or the operating system, and it is not a security boundary. Two environments in the same process share everything that is not in site-packages. It also does not solve dependency conflicts within one project, which is a resolver problem (13.3). And an environment created from a different interpreter keeps that interpreter forever — recreating the venv with the intended Python is the fix, and uv venv --python makes it a one-liner.

ApproachIsolationSpeedWhere it fits
Global pip installNoneBaselineNever for a project. Acceptable for a throwaway interpreter in a container that is discarded.
python -m venvPer projectSecondsThe standard answer. Needs no third-party tool.
uv venvPer projectSub-second typicallyThe modern default for a new project. Also manages the interpreter itself.
Container per projectProcess plus filesystemImage buildDeployment and CI. Orthogonal to the venv, and a container is the only real isolation boundary.
Poetry / PDM / Hatch environmentsPer projectVariesUseful when the tool already owns the environment; otherwise it is a second source of truth alongside uv.
Dependency injection (no environment)Per test, not per processSlowpip install --target plus PYTHONPATH. Reproducible in a lockfile-driven build; awkward interactively.

13.3 Resolvers, specifiers and pinning

A resolver takes a set of declared requirements and produces a set of installed distributions. The hard part is that it must find a version of every transitive dependency that satisfies every declared constraint simultaneously, or report that no such set exists. Three tools do this: pip with its vendored resolver, uv with a resolver written in Rust, and pip-tools which compiles requirement files. They agree on PEP 440 semantics; they differ in speed, in output format and in how strictly they protect the result.

# --- PEP 440 version specifiers, in full ------------------------------------
import packaging.specifiers as sp
import packaging.version as pv

S = sp.SpecifierSet
examples = [
    (">=3.11",        "3.11, 3.14, 4.0"),
    (">=3.11,<3.15","3.14 yes, 3.15 no"),
    ("~=3.11",          "compatible release: >=3.11, ==3.*  (allows 3.14)"),
    ("~=3.11.2",        ">=3.11.2, ==3.11.*  (allows 3.11.9, not 3.12.0)"),
    ("==3.14.*",        "3.14.0 through 3.14.99"),
    ("!=3.13.5",        "exclusion"),
    ("===3.14.7",       "arbitrary equality: accepts non-PEP440 strings verbatim"),
]
for spec, meaning in examples:
    s = S(spec)
    print(f"{spec:16} contains 3.14.0 = {s.contains('3.14.0')!s:5}  ({meaning})")

# Ordering is defined by PEP 440, not by string comparison.
order = ["1.0", "1.0.post1", "1.0.1", "1.1.dev1", "1.1a1", "1.1rc1", "1.1", "2.0"]
print(sorted(order, key=pv.Version))
print("2.0" == "2.0.0", pv.Version("2.0") == pv.Version("2.0.0"))   # True

# Pre-releases are excluded from ranges unless asked for explicitly, which is
# what stops a resolver silently installing 3.15.0b1 for ">=3.11".
s = S(">=3.11")
print(s.contains("3.15.0b1"), s.contains("3.15.0b1", prereleases=True))
print(S(">=3.15.0b1").contains("3.15.0b1"))                  # explicit opt-in

# --- constraints versus requirements --------------------------------------
# requirements.txt: what you want.  constraints.txt: what you are allowed.
# A constraint file is never installed directly; it narrows the search space.
print(open("constraints.txt").read() if False else """
# constraints.txt -- transitive only. Never list a direct dependency here:
# the application declares it, and a constraint can contradict it silently.
requests==2.32.3
urllib3==2.2.3
""".strip())

# --- Lock files ------------------------------------------------------------
# A lock file records the FULL resolved set with exact versions and, usually,
# hashes. It turns a resolver run into a verification run.
print("""
# uv.lock (excerpt, TOML)
version = 1
requires-python = ">=3.11"

[[package]]
name = "acme-tools"
version = "2.4.1"
source = { editable = "." }

[[package]]
name = "pydantic"
version = "2.9.2"
source = { registry = "https://pypi.org/simple" }
dependencies = [
    { name = "annotated-types" },
    { name = "typing-extensions", marker = "python_full_version < '3.13'" },
]
sdist = { url = "...", hash = "sha256:...", size = 3456789 }
wheels = [
    { url = ".../pydantic-2.9.2-py3-none-any.whl", hash = "sha256:...", size = 254713 },
]
""".strip())

# --- Commands --------------------------------------------------------------
#   uv lock                resolve and write uv.lock
#   uv sync                install EXACTLY uv.lock, removing anything extra
#   uv sync --frozen       fail if uv.lock would change
#   uv add "httpx>=0.27"  add a direct dependency and re-lock
#   uv remove httpx        remove a direct dependency and re-lock
#   uv run pytest          ensure the environment matches the lock, then run
#
#   pip install .          resolves and installs, no lock
#   pip install -r requirements.txt
#   pip install -c constraints.txt -r requirements.txt
#   pip install --require-hashes -r requirements.txt
#       refuses any package without a hash; the strongest reproducibility
#       guarantee available with pip alone.
#
#   pip-compile requirements.in --generate-hashes -o requirements.txt
#       resolves once, writes a pinned file. The .in file is the source;
#       the generated file is a build artefact and belongs in .gitignore or
#       in CI only.
#
#   pipx install ruff     an application tool, installed into its own venv
Project kindWhat to pinWhy
Application, deployedEverything exactly, in a committed lock, with hashesThe artifact you ship must be byte-reproducible from the lock. This is the entire point of the lock and the entire reason for --require-hashes. A pin is only useful if it is enforced at install time — a commented-out line in a text file is not a pin.
Application, under active developmentLoose direct constraints, exact lock>=0.27,<1 in pyproject.toml states the supported range; the lock pins the instance. Re-lock deliberately, review the diff, and never let a build silently re-resolve.
LibraryLower bounds only, and as few as possibleAn exact pin in a library propagates into every application that installs it and makes the application impossible to install alongside anything else. State what you have tested against, not what you happened to use.
Library, with a hard requirementA lower bound, plus an upper bound with a documented reasonAn upper bound (<2) is legitimate and becomes a habit other projects copy. If the bound exists, put a comment in the source saying which version broke.
CIThe lock, plus --frozenCI must never re-resolve. A build that resolves is a build that can differ from the one that was tested.

Pin drift is the failure mode that gets missed. A pin in pyproject.toml that is not in the lock is a comment; a lock that is not committed is a suggestion; a lock that is regenerated by a pipeline step that also runs tests means the tests ran against something that was never reviewed. The invariant to enforce is that the only thing installed in CI and in production is the lock, and that a lock change is a reviewable commit with its own diff.

Caveat: a version constraint is a claim about compatibility that no tool can verify. A resolver finds a combination satisfying the arithmetic, not the semantics. A transitive dependency that declares >=2.0 when it is incompatible with 2.3 of everything else will be installed happily. This is why an upper bound on a widely used library is often a temporary measure, and why the release notes of every direct dependency are part of the dependency-update process rather than an optional extra.

13.4 Build backends, wheels and sdists

A build frontend (pip, uv, build, python -m build) creates an isolated environment, installs the packages named in [build-system].requires, and calls the backend’s build_wheel or build_sdist hook. The backend’s only job is to turn a source tree into two artifacts: a wheel, a zip with a standardised layout that installs without executing project code, and an sdist, a tarball of the source that a backend will rebuild.

# --- The wheel layout ------------------------------------------------------
# my_package-2.4.1-py3-none-any.whl
#   my_package/                 the import package, with __init__.py
#     __init__.py
#     core.py
#   my_package-2.4.1.dist-info/
#     METADATA                  PEP 566 metadata, generated from [project]
#     WHEEL                     Wheel-Version, Generator, Root-Is-Purelib
#     RECORD                    every file with its SHA-256 and size
#     entry_points.txt          [console_scripts] and friends
#     licenses/LICENSE          PEP 639 licence text
#     top_level.txt             optional, legacy
#
# The .dist-info directory name is the distribution name with - replaced by _
# and the version. The wheel filename encodes compatibility tags:
#   py3-none-any    pure Python 3, no platform, no ABI
#   cp314-cp314-linux_x86_64   CPython 3.14, CPython ABI, CPython 3.14 linux amd64
#   cp314t-...                 the free-threaded ABI (see 14.1)
#   pp314-...                   PyPy 3.14
# An installer selects a wheel whose tags are a subset of its own; a package
# with no compatible wheel must be built from the sdist, which requires a
# build toolchain on the target machine. Publishing only an sdist is the most
# common cause of a slow or failing installation for users.

# --- Backends --------------------------------------------------------------
# setuptools    the historical default. Mature, ubiquitous, and a large
#               surface with a long history of surprising default behaviour.
#               Still the safest choice for a package that must work
#               everywhere including very old pip.
# hatchling     fast, configuration-light, first-class PEP 621 support. The
#               modern default for new projects.
# pdm-backend   PEP 621 native, also backs PDM itself. Good if PDM is the tool.
# flit-core     extremely small, requires the module to have a docstring and a
#               __version__, and nothing else configurable. Ideal for a single
#               pure-Python module.
# scikit-build-core  the reference for anything with compiled extensions; drives
#               CMake or meson and produces correct platform wheels.
# maturin       Rust extensions via PyO3/maturin, with a bridge package for
#               mixed Python/Rust projects.

# --- Hatchling configuration ----------------------------------------------
# [build-system]
# requires = ["hatchling>=1.27"]
# build-backend = "hatchling.build"
#
# [tool.hatch.build.targets.wheel]
# packages = ["src/acme_tools"]        # explicit is better than discovery
#
# [tool.hatch.build.targets.sdist]
# include = ["src", "tests", "README.md", "LICENSE", "pyproject.toml"]
# exclude = ["/.github", "/.venv", "**/__pycache__"]
#
# [tool.hatch.version]
# path = "src/acme_tools/__init__.py"   # single source of truth for __version__

# [tool.hatch.build.hooks.vcs]
# version-file = "src/acme_tools/_version.py"

# --- Building, and inspecting the result ----------------------------------
#   python -m build              build an sdist, then a wheel FROM the sdist
#   uv build                     the same, in about a second
#   python -m build --wheel      wheel only
#
# Building the wheel from the sdist, which is what the first command does, is
# not redundant: it proves the sdist is complete. A file present in the wheel
# but absent from the sdist is the classic packaging bug, and this ordering
# catches it.
#
#   python -m zipfile -l dist/*.whl
#   python -m zipfile -e dist/*.whl /tmp/unpacked
#   python -c "import pathlib,zipfile; \
#     z=zipfile.ZipFile(next(pathlib.Path('dist').glob('*.whl'))); \
#     print([n for n in z.namelist() if n.endswith('METADATA')])"
#   twine check dist/*
#     validates the metadata renders on PyPI. Run it before uploading.
#   check-wheel-contents dist/*.whl
#     catches the SOURCES.txt trap and similar.

# --- Editable installs (PEP 660) -------------------------------------------
#   pip install -e .        uv pip install -e .
#
# An editable install is NOT a copy. The backend produces a .pth file or an
# import hook that redirects the import name to the source directory, so edits
# to the source take effect with no reinstall.
#
# Consequences that are easy to forget:
#   - Changes to pyproject.toml [tool.*] or to metadata do NOT take effect
#     until the install is repeated. Changing entry points, dependencies or
#     the version requires `pip install -e .` again.
#   - An editable install does not put the project on sys.path for other
#     environments, and it makes sys.path contain an absolute path to a
#     developer's machine.
#   - Some backends execute a build step on import, which makes the first
#     import after an edit slow (Hatchling's "dev mode" does this for
#     compiled extensions).
#   - A backends' editable implementation may be a different code path from its
#     wheel build, so a project can work in editable mode and fail when
#     installed from a wheel. Test the wheel.

# Verify the editable model explicitly:
#   python -c "import my_package, sys; print(my_package.__file__)"
#   python -c "import my_package; print(my_package.__version__)"
ArtifactContainsInstalled byUse it for
Wheel (.whl)Built package plus .dist-infoUnzipped by the installer; no project code runsDistribution. The only artifact that can be installed without a build toolchain.
Sdist (.tar.gz)Source tree plus pyproject.tomlA frontend rebuilds it into a wheelSource distribution, reproducibility, and a fallback when no wheel matches.
Editable wheel (.pth plus hook)A redirect to the source treepip or uvLocal development only. Never published, never deployed.

Free-threaded wheels: a free-threaded CPython has a distinct ABI tag, cp314t, and therefore needs a distinct wheel. A pure-Python wheel tagged py3-none-any is compatible with every interpreter and needs no special build; a package with a compiled extension must publish both the cp314 and cp314t builds or free-threaded users must compile from source. This is the single most common reason an application cannot simply switch to a python3.14t interpreter.

13.5 Publishing to PyPI

Publishing is a metadata operation, a trust decision and a repeatable pipeline. The metadata is already correct if [project] is, and the whole operation reduces to uploading two files. The trust decision is delegated to PyPI rather than owned by the project, which is the subject of the final two subsections.

# --- One source of truth for the version -----------------------------------
# The classic failure is a version in three places: pyproject.toml,
# __init__.py and a git tag. Two of them will disagree.
#
# Option A: the module is the source, and the backend reads it.
#   pyproject.toml:  dynamic = ["version"]
#                    [tool.hatch.version] path = "src/acme_tools/__init__.py"
#   __init__.py:    __version__ = "2.4.1"
# Then importlib.metadata.version("acme-tools") and __version__ can never
# disagree, because the installed metadata is generated from the module.
#
# Option B: a generated file the module imports.
#   [tool.hatch.build.hooks.vcs] version-file = "src/acme_tools/_version.py"
#   __init__.py:  from ._version import __version__
# The file is written at build time from the git tag, is not committed, and
# there is nothing to keep in sync by hand.
#
# Option C: the tag is the only source.
#   version = {attr = "acme_tools._version.__version__"}   # setuptools
# and CI asserts that the tag, the metadata and any docs agree.
import importlib.metadata as md
from acme_tools import __version__
assert md.version("acme-tools") == __version__, "metadata and module disagree"

# --- Uploading -------------------------------------------------------------
#   python -m build
#   twine check dist/*
#   twine upload --repository pypi dist/acme_tools-2.4.1-py3-none-any.whl \
#                            dist/acme_tools-2.4.1.tar.gz
#
#   uv build
#   uv publish                  reads dist/ and pyproject.toml
#
# A filename is immutable. PyPI refuses to accept a second upload of the same
# filename, ever, for any reason, including a corrupted upload. The recovery
# is a new version number; deleting and re-uploading the same version is not
# possible.
#
# Upload the sdist and the wheel, always both. The sdist is what makes the
# project auditable and buildable; a wheel-only project cannot be verified by
# anyone but its author.

# --- Trusted publishing (PyPI) ---------------------------------------------
# The old flow -- an API token stored in CI secrets -- is being retired.
# Trusted publishing issues a short-lived token automatically to a named
# workflow in a named repository, scoped to one project and one environment,
# with no long-lived secret anywhere.
#
#   1. On PyPI, for the project, create a pending publisher:
#        owner, repository, workflow name, environment name.
#   2. In .github/workflows/release.yml, declare the matching environment and
#      add pypa/gh-action-pypi-publish.
#
# name: Publish
# on:
#   release:
#     types: [published]
# permissions:
#   id-token: write          # the entire authorisation
# jobs:
#   publish:
#     runs-on: ubuntu-latest
#     environment: pypi      # must match the pending publisher exactly
#     steps:
#       - uses: actions/checkout@v4
#       - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
#         with: { python-version: "3.14" }
#       - run: python -m build
#       - run: twine check dist/*
#       - uses: pypa/gh-action-pypi-publish@release/v1
#   test:
#     # The gate: build, test and audit BEFORE the publish job is reachable.
#     runs-on: ubuntu-latest
#     steps:
#       - uses: actions/checkout@v4
#       - uses: astral-sh/setup-uv@v5
#       - run: uv sync --frozen
#       - run: uv run pytest -q --cov --cov-report=term-missing
#       - run: uv run pip-audit
#       - run: uv build
#       - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
#         with: { name: dist, path: dist/ }

# --- What must not be committed --------------------------------------------
#   .venv/            environments are disposable and machine-specific
#   dist/, build/     build outputs; reproducible from a tag
#   *.egg-info/       generated; a stale one causes an install of stale code
#   __pycache__/      generated
#   .python-version   commit it, but treat it as a hint; CI pins its own
# uv.lock            COMMIT for an application; do NOT commit for a library
# requirements.txt   generated output; commit only if the project has chosen
#                    pip-tools as its resolver of record
# .env, *.pem, secrets/  never, under any circumstances (see 16.4)

A package name is not reserved by having the name in pyproject.toml. Anyone can upload any name, and squatters register popular names precisely to intercept the typo. A project that is not yet published should not build tooling around a name it does not control: verify the name’s exact spelling on PyPI, prefer an organisation-scoped name for internal tools, and treat a homograph or missing project as a stop condition rather than a detail. See 16.7.

CredentialScopeStatus
API token (project scope)One project, upload or download onlyLegacy. Use only as a temporary bridge, never in a repository, and rotate on any suspicion.
API token (account scope)Every project on the accountTo be deprecated. An account token is a single value whose compromise affects everything.
Trusted publisherOne project, one workflow, one environmentRecommended. The token is minted per job, expires in minutes, and is not a secret that can leak from a log or a fork.

Exercises 13

  1. Convert a project from setup.py to PEP 621 with hatchling, choose a version source of truth, and prove by a clean uv build that the wheel and the sdist produce byte-identical installed module files.
  2. Reproduce the import-shadowing defect: create the same package in flat and src layouts, import it in each with a sys.path dump, and record the __file__ that wins in each case.
  3. Build ten wheels of the same project with setuptools, hatchling, pdm-backend and flit-core, list the files in each, and explain every difference in terms of what each backend includes by default.
  4. Add a compiled extension to a pure-Python package, build it for cp314 and cp314t, and record the tag difference in the resulting filenames and the WHEEL file.
  5. Write a release workflow that builds, runs the test suite, runs pip-audit, executes twine check and publishes through trusted publishing, then confirm the package page shows the expected metadata and licence.
  6. Construct a dependency conflict that no version combination can satisfy, capture the resolver’s error message, and add a constraint that resolves it, recording both outputs verbatim.
  7. Using pip-compile and uv lock on the same requirements.in, diff the two resolutions and explain each disagreement in terms of a marker, a yanked release or an extra.
  8. Write an editable install of a package with a console script, add a new entry point, and demonstrate that the script is stale until the editable install is repeated.

14 · Concurrency and Performance

14.1 The GIL and free-threaded CPython14.2 Threads, processes or async14.3 concurrent.futures14.4 Event-loop overhead14.5 Profiling CPU time14.6 Memory and the garbage collector14.7 Optimising measured hot paths

Python’s concurrency story changed materially in 3.13 and 3.14. Free-threaded CPython is production-ready in 3.14 (PEP 779), and the experimental JIT is now available as an opt-in build. Both change the performance arithmetic, and neither removes the need to measure. This chapter documents the current state of all three execution models, then treats performance as a measurement problem: profiling, memory attribution, and the specific optimisations that are actually worth making in Python.

14.1 The GIL and free-threaded CPython

The global interpreter lock is a mutex held by whichever thread is currently executing bytecode. It exists because CPython’s reference counting manipulates objects from arbitrary bytecode, and making that safe without it would require either a per-object lock or an atomic reference count. It does not protect operating system resources, it does not serialise I/O, and it is released around a blocking system call. What it does guarantee is that no two threads execute Python bytecode at the same instant, which caps CPU-bound parallelism at one core per process.

import sys, sysconfig, threading, time
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

# --- Detecting the build you are actually running ---------------------------
print("version           :", sys.version)
print("GIL enabled       :", not getattr(sys, "_is_gil_enabled", lambda: True)())
print("_is_gil_enabled() :", getattr(sys, "_is_gil_enabled", None) and sys._is_gil_enabled())
print("Py_GIL_DISABLED   :", sysconfig.get_config_var("Py_GIL_DISABLED"))
print("cache_tag         :", sys.implementation.cache_tag)   # cpython-314 vs cpython-314t
print("config vars       :", sysconfig.get_config_var("Py_GIL_DISABLED") is not None)

# A GIL build: two threads doing pure-Python arithmetic are serialised.
def burn(n: int) -> int:
    total = 0
    for i in range(n):
        total += i * i
    return total

N = 3_000_000
t0 = time.perf_counter(); [burn(N) for _ in range(4)]; serial = time.perf_counter() - t0
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as ex:
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); list(ex.map(burn, [N] * 4)); threaded = time.perf_counter() - t0
print(f"serial {serial:.3f}s  4 threads {threaded:.3f}s  speedup {serial/threaded:.2f}x")
# GIL build: speedup ~1.0x.  Free-threaded build: ~3-4x on 4 cores.

# A GIL build: I/O-bound threads scale, because the GIL is released around
# the blocking call.
import socket
def io_wait(_):
    with socket.create_connection(("127.0.0.1", 9), timeout=0.05):
        pass
t0 = time.perf_counter(); [io_wait(i) for i in range(50)]; s = time.perf_counter() - t0
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=16) as ex:
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); list(ex.map(io_wait, range(50))); p = time.perf_counter() - t0
print(f"serial {s:.3f}s  16 threads {p:.3f}s  speedup {s/p:.2f}x")   # ~16x either way

# --- What the GIL does NOT protect, on any build ---------------------------
# - I/O in the operating system. The GIL is released for read, write, recv,
#   connect, sleep. Two threads waiting on sockets overlap fully.
# - C extensions that release the GIL themselves. numpy releases it around
#   the inner loop, so threaded numpy is genuinely parallel for large arrays.
#   This is why "the GIL makes numpy single-threaded" is wrong.
# - Subprocesses. They have their own interpreter and their own GIL entirely.
# - The filesystem. Two threads writing the same file do not serialise.
# - The event loop. asyncio is single-threaded by construction, and has never
#   used the GIL for anything.

# --- The free-threaded build ----------------------------------------------
# Built from source:  ./configure --disable-gil   &&   make
# Distributed by python.org as a separate installer, a "t" ABI tag, and a
# distinct executable name:
#
#   python3.14t      3.14 with the GIL disabled
#   python3.14       3.14 with the GIL
#
# Both are installed side by side and both build the same source.
#
# sys._is_gil_enabled() returns True in a free-threaded build when the GIL has
# been re-enabled at run time, which happens automatically if an extension
# module without thread-safety declarations is imported:
#
#   import _tkinter      # some extension modules are not marked safe
#   sys._is_gil_enabled()   # -> True again, and a warning was emitted
#
# The env var PYTHON_GIL=0 forces the GIL off and causes an ImportError
# instead of silently re-enabling it, which is what a production deployment
# should use so the failure is loud.

# --- Migration cost, honestly ---------------------------------------------
# Single-threaded: the free-threaded build is 3-10% SLOWER in 3.14. There is
# per-object locking and biased-reference-count bookkeeping on every access.
# It is a cost paid for a capability, not a regression, and it is why the
# GIL build remains the default.
# Multi-threaded: single-threaded code inside a threaded workload is usually
# FASTER, because the serialising effect of the GIL is gone, provided the
# shared state is actually protected.
# C extensions: a single-threaded extension forces the GIL back on. As of
#   3.14 the list of such extensions is short but non-empty. Check with
#   sys._is_gil_enabled() after the application's own imports.
# JIT: the experimental JIT in 3.14 is a copy-and-patch tier-2 design and is
#   opt-in. It is not a reason to change a deployment; measure it on
#   representative code with the workload in production, not a microbenchmark.
InterpreterWhere the bytesCPU-bound threadsNotes
CPython 3.14 (GIL)One at a time, process-wideNo parallelism; the fastest single threadThe default. Correct for every workload that is not CPU-bound-parallel.
CPython 3.14t (free-threaded)Many at once, with per-object locks on the mutable internalsNear-linear to core countPEP 703, production-ready in 3.14 (PEP 779). Distinct ABI tag, distinct wheel tag, single-thread overhead of a few percent.
PyPy 3.14Many at a time; a tracing JIT, no GILNear-linear to core countHistorically 1-3x slower on cold pure-Python loops and 10-100x faster on long-running hot loops once the JIT warms up. A different memory profile and different C-extension compatibility; test before deploying.
GraalPyMany at a timeNear-linearFast interpreter start-up; incomplete C-API coverage.
CPython with the experimental JITOne at a time, with inline cachesNo parallelism; faster per core on some workloadsOpt-in build. Measure on the real workload. Not a default in 3.14.

Free-threading does not make shared mutable state safe. It removes the accidental serialisation, not the requirement for synchronisation. Under a GIL, a compound operation such as d[k] = d.get(k, 0) + 1 is a single bytecode-dispatched step in many cases and appears to work; on a free-threaded build it is a genuine race. Code that was correct only because the GIL hid an interleaving must be audited, and the audit is the work — not the flag. A counter guarded by a Lock, a cache protected by a lock, and a queue fed by several threads all remain correct and all get faster.

Caveat: free-threaded builds are a separate download and a separate set of wheels. A virtual environment created by python3.14 is not usable by python3.14t: the ABI tag differs, the compiled extensions differ, and the site-packages contents are not interchangeable. Give each interpreter its own environment, and let uv create both with uv venv --python 3.14t.

14.2 Threads, processes or async

Three models exist and the choice is not a matter of taste. Threads share an address space and are cheap to create; processes are isolated and expensive; coroutines are cheaper still and require an event loop. Each is optimal for a different shape of work, and the shape of the work is decided by where the time is spent: in the interpreter, in the operating system, or while waiting.

WorkloadModelRationaleFailure mode if chosen wrongly
Blocking I/O to a network or database, on a GIL buildThreads, or asyncio with an async driverThe GIL is released around the blocking call, so threads overlap. asyncio wins on scale because each connection costs a coroutine rather than an 8 MB stack.Threading a few hundred concurrent connections: a thread per connection is a memory and scheduling problem. asyncio for CPU-bound work: total serialisation plus per-task overhead.
Blocking I/O on a free-threaded buildThreads are now viable at high concurrency, but asyncio still wins on memoryThe GIL is not the constraint, thread count isThousands of threads regardless of interpreter: the OS scheduler, not Python, becomes the bottleneck.
Pure-Python CPU work, GIL buildProcessesOnly processes give real parallelismThreads: no speedup at all, plus contention overhead that can make it slower than serial.
Pure-Python CPU work, free-threaded buildThreads or processesThreads now scale and avoid the serialisation cost of picklingAssuming the scaling is linear; it is not, because the allocator and the interpreter loop are still shared.
CPU work in a C extension that releases the GIL (numpy, PIL)ThreadsThe extension parallelises internally; the GIL is not heldProcesses: the work is copied across a process boundary, which costs more than it saves.
Code that blocks for seconds and cannot be rewritten as a coroutineThreadPoolExecutor or a dedicated processThe event loop must never be blocked by a synchronous callasyncio without to_thread: the entire loop stalls and every other connection times out.
Untrusted or crash-prone code, or a hard memory ceilingProcessesIsolation in address space; a leak is reclaimable by killing the childThreads: one runaway allocation takes down the process.
import asyncio, time, threading, os, math
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, ProcessPoolExecutor

# --- The decision procedure, in order ------------------------------------
# 1. Is the time spent in C code that releases the GIL?  -> threads.
# 2. Is it in the operating system (a syscall)?          -> threads or async.
# 3. Is it in Python bytecode?                          -> processes, or
#    threads on a free-threaded build.
# 4. Do you need ten thousand concurrent connections?   -> async.

# --- Threads: shared address space, cheap creation -----------------------
# threading.Thread is a thin object. The default stack size (8 MB on Linux) is
# virtual, and only touched pages are resident, so a few hundred threads are
# usually fine. A few thousand are a scheduling problem.
print("thread stack default (bytes):", threading.stack_size() or "platform default")
print("active threads:", threading.active_count())
print("main thread   :", threading.main_thread().name, threading.main_thread().daemon)

results: dict[str, int] = {}
lock = threading.Lock()                        # required; the GIL is not a lock

def worker(n: int) -> None:
    with lock:                                  # guard the shared dict
        results[f"w{n}"] = n * n

ts = [threading.Thread(target=worker, args=(i,), name=f"w{i}") for i in range(8)]
t0 = time.perf_counter()
for t in ts: t.start()
for t in ts: t.join()                           # join IS the synchronisation
print(f"{len(results)} threads in {time.perf_counter()-t0:.4f}s")

# A Lock is NOT reentrant. A Condition is built on one, and a RLock allows
# re-entry from the owning thread. Deadlock is the failure mode of getting
# this wrong, and it is silent: the process stops making progress with no
# error and no traceback.
lock2 = threading.RLock()
counter = 0
def increment_twice() -> None:
    global counter
    with lock2:
        with lock2:                            # legal only with an RLock
            counter += 1
ts = [threading.Thread(target=increment_twice) for _ in range(100)]
for t in ts: t.start()
for t in ts: t.join()
print("counter:", counter)

# Event is a one-shot flag: set from anywhere, waited on by anyone.
done = threading.Event()
def worker2() -> None:
    time.sleep(0.01); done.set()
threading.Thread(target=worker2).start()
print("waited:", done.wait(timeout=1.0))

# --- Processes: separate address space, expensive creation ----------------
# The top-level function is the unit of pickling. A closure or a lambda is
# NOT picklable, which is the most common ProcessPoolExecutor error.
def cpu_bound(n: int) -> float:
    return math.fsum(math.sin(i) * math.cos(i) for i in range(n))

if __name__ == "__main__":                     # REQUIRED on Windows and macOS
    with ProcessPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as ex:
        t0 = time.perf_counter()
        list(ex.map(cpu_bound, [2_000_000] * 8))
        print(f"process pool: {time.perf_counter()-t0:.3f}s")
    print("default start method:", mp_start_method())

def mp_start_method() -> str:
    import multiprocessing as mp
    return mp.get_start_method()

# Fork safety: an SQLAlchemy Engine or a database connection inherited through
# fork() is SHARED with the parent and will corrupt. Dispose immediately after
# forking, or use the "forkserver" or "spawn" start method.
import multiprocessing as mp
# mp.set_start_method("forkserver")   # Linux default is still "fork" before 3.14
# mp.set_start_method("spawn")        # always safe, always slower to start

# --- Coroutines: one thread, many tasks ----------------------------------
async def fetch(n: int) -> int:
    await asyncio.sleep(0.001)                 # stands in for real IO
    return n * n

async def main() -> None:
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    r = await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(i) for i in range(1000)))
    print(f"{len(r)} coroutines in {time.perf_counter()-t0:.3f}s")

asyncio.run(main())

# Never block the loop. This is the single most important rule of async code.
async def wrong() -> None:
    time.sleep(1.0)                            # blocks EVERY task for 1 second
    with open("f.txt") as fh:                  # blocking syscall: same effect
        fh.read()
    requests.get("https://example.invalid")    # the classic, and the worst

async def right() -> None:
    await asyncio.sleep(1.0)
    data = await asyncio.to_thread(sync_function, "arg")   # offloaded
    data = await anyio.Path("f.txt").read_text()           # if anyio is present

The most reliable way to detect an accidental blocking call in production is to enable PYTHONASYNCIODEBUG=1 in staging. It logs every task that takes more than 100 ms to yield, with the source location, which turns a mysterious latency spike into a single actionable log line.

fork() in a process that has threads is unsafe for the child, and Python 3.12 deprecated os.fork() in a multi-threaded process outright. Python 3.14 changes the default start method on Linux from fork to forkserver for newly created processes, which avoids the inherited-lock problem; code that depends on the old behaviour explicitly asks for fork, and should be reviewed rather than assumed.

14.3 concurrent.futures

concurrent.futures is a small, well-specified library over the same two models: a thread pool and a process pool. Both return Future objects, both support the same three submission methods, and both are a reasonable default when the choice is genuinely uninteresting. What it does not offer is cancellation, back-pressure, or per-item result streaming — those require asyncio or a queue.

from __future__ import annotations

import time
import threading
from concurrent.futures import (
    Future, ThreadPoolExecutor, ProcessPoolExecutor, as_completed,
    wait, FIRST_COMPLETED, ALL_COMPLETED, TimeoutError as FutureTimeoutError,
)

# --- Submissions ----------------------------------------------------------
def work(i: int) -> int:
    time.sleep(0.05)
    if i == 3:
        raise ValueError(f"item {i} is bad")
    return i * 10

# ThreadPoolExecutor: shared address space, I/O-bound work.
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=8, thread_name_prefix="io") as pool:
    a = pool.submit(work, 1)              # returns a Future immediately
    b = pool.submit(work, 2)
    print("submit returns at once:", a.done(), b.done())
    print("result(2.0):", b.result(timeout=2.0))
    print("all results:", [f.result() for f in (a, b)])

    # map(): ordered, lazily consumed, raises on iteration.
    try:
        list(pool.map(work, range(5)))
    except ValueError as exc:
        print("map re-raises the worker's exception:", exc)

    # as_completed(): unordered, results as they finish, one at a time.
    for fut in as_completed(pool.submit(work, i) for i in range(6)):
        try:
            print("  done:", fut.result())
        except ValueError as exc:
            print("  failed:", exc)      # per-future handling, not per-batch

    # wait(): block for a subset.
    fs = [pool.submit(work, i) for i in range(10)]
    done, not_done = wait(fs, timeout=0.2, return_when=FIRST_COMPLETED)
    print(f"first batch: {len(done)} done, {len(not_done)} pending")
    for f in not_done:
        f.cancel()                        # works only if not yet started
    print("cancelled:", sum(f.cancelled() for f in not_done))

# --- The Future API -------------------------------------------------------
f: Future[int]
f.cancelled(); f.running(); f.done()
f.result(timeout=None)                    # blocks; re-raises the worker's error
f.exception(timeout=None)                 # blocks; returns the exception or None
f.add_done_callback(lambda fut: print("callback:", fut.exception()))
f.set_result(1); f.set_exception(ValueError())   # for tests and adapters

# --- ProcessPoolExecutor --------------------------------------------------
def picklable_top_level(x: int) -> int:
    return x * 2

if __name__ == "__main__":
    with ProcessPoolExecutor(max_workers=4, mp_context=mp_context()) as pool:
        print(list(pool.map(picklable_top_level, range(8))))

    # Pickling constraints, all of them real:
    #  - the function and every argument must be picklable;
    #  - a closure, a lambda, a functools.partial over a closure, an open
    #    file, a socket, a logger, a DB connection and a thread lock are not;
    #  - __main__ is re-imported in the child under spawn and forkserver, so
    #    module-level side effects run again; the if __name__ guard is not
    #    optional;
    #  - a bound method is picklable only if the instance is;
    #  - the arguments and the return value are copied twice each way.
    # The cheapest way to cross a process boundary is a small, plain
    # dataclass or tuple -- pass identifiers, fetch the data in the child.

# --- Tuning ---------------------------------------------------------------
#   max_workers:  ThreadPoolExecutor defaults to
#                 min(32, (os.cpu_count() or 1) + 4) -- deliberately more than
#                 the core count, because the tasks are assumed to be
#                 I/O-bound. For CPU-bound work set it to the core count.
#   initializer / initargs: runs once per worker. A per-process database
#                 connection pool belongs here; creating connections in the
#                 parent and hoping they survive is incorrect.
#   chunksize:     for ProcessPoolExecutor.map, the number of items per task
#                 sent to a child. Larger reduces IPC overhead and increases
#                 tail latency. 1 for uneven work.
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor as PPE
import multiprocessing as mp
ctx = mp.get_context("forkserver")

_POOL: object = None
def init_worker(dsn: str) -> None:
    global _POOL
    _POOL = make_engine(dsn)              # one engine per child process

def make_engine(dsn: str):
    import sqlalchemy as sa
    return sa.create_engine(dsn, pool_size=1)   # small: one process, one session

def query(conn_id: int) -> int:
    return conn_id * 2

if __name__ == "__main__":
    with PPE(max_workers=4, initializer=init_worker, initargs=("sqlite://",),
             mp_context=ctx) as pool:
        print(list(pool.map(query, range(4))))

# --- Cost, measured --------------------------------------------------------
def bench(executor_factory, label: str) -> None:
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    with executor_factory() as pool:
        list(pool.map(lambda x: None, range(200)))     # trivial work
    print(f"{label}: {time.perf_counter()-t0:.4f}s for 200 no-ops")

bench(ThreadPoolExecutor, "threads  ")     # thread creation dominates
# bench(ProcessPoolExecutor, "processes")   # process creation dominates:
#                                            hundreds of ms, and each is a fork

An exception raised inside a task is stored in the Future and re-raised by result(). It is never printed if the future is never awaited, so a failing task in a fire-and-forget submit() is completely silent. Always attach a add_done_callback that reports fut.exception(), or collect the futures and resolve them. Note also that map() raises the first exception encountered while iterating, which means the remaining work is still running and the remaining results are discarded; when partial success matters, use as_completed and handle each result on its own.

A ProcessPoolExecutor worker that dies abruptly — killed by the OOM killer, segfaulting in C code, or calling os._exit() — raises BrokenProcessPool for every pending future, and the pool cannot be reused. This is a frequent consequence of a fork that inherits a database socket. The remedy is prevention: forkserver or spawn, and engine disposal after fork.

14.4 Event-loop overhead

An event loop is a single-threaded scheduler with a cost per task. That cost is small and it is not zero, and the point at which it stops being small depends on how much work each task performs. The two questions that decide whether an event loop is the right tool are therefore: how many concurrent tasks, and how much work does each one do?

import asyncio, time, threading, statistics
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

# --- The per-task cost ----------------------------------------------------
async def noop() -> None:
    pass

async def measure(n: int) -> float:
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    for _ in range(n):
        await noop()                        # schedule and reschedule, no work
    return (time.perf_counter() - t0) / n

for n in (1_000, 100_000):
    print(f"await round trip, n={n:<7}: {asyncio.run(measure(n))*1e6:.3f} us/task")

# Creating a Task is more expensive than awaiting a coroutine, because it
# allocates a Task, binds the context and schedules a callback.
async def measure_create(n: int) -> float:
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    tasks = [asyncio.create_task(noop()) for _ in range(n)]
    await asyncio.gather(*tasks)
    return (time.perf_counter() - t0) / n
print(f"create_task + gather      : {asyncio.run(measure_create(100_000))*1e6:.3f} us/task")

# A thread, for comparison. Creation is far more expensive, which is the whole
# reason async exists.
def spawn_thread(n: int) -> float:
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    ts = [threading.Thread(target=lambda: None) for _ in range(n)]
    for t in ts: t.start()
    for t in ts: t.join()
    return (time.perf_counter() - t0) / n
print(f"thread create+join        : {spawn_thread(5_000)*1e6:.1f} us/thread")

# --- When the event loop is not worth it ---------------------------------
# Rule of thumb: asyncio pays off above roughly a few hundred concurrent
# tasks, and loses below a few dozen. A blocking pool of 20 threads handling
# 20 requests has less overhead and simpler code.
async def cpu_in_loop(n: int) -> int:
    # Pure Python in a coroutine: the loop is blocked for the whole duration.
    # No amount of awaiting makes this parallel.
    return sum(i * i for i in range(n))

async def compare(n: int) -> None:
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); await cpu_in_loop(n); serial = time.perf_counter() - t0
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); await asyncio.to_thread(cpu_in_loop, n); th = time.perf_counter() - t0
    print(f"cpu in loop {serial:.3f}s   same work in a thread {th:.3f}s (serial either way)")

# --- The overhead of the alternatives ------------------------------------
# Threading a blocking library: one thread per concurrent call. Cheap to
# reason about, expensive per unit of concurrency, and correct.
# Running a blocking library in to_thread: same cost, plus a handoff to the
# loop's default executor, which is itself a ThreadPoolExecutor bounded by
# min(32, cpu+4). Past that bound, tasks queue silently.
# Running a blocking library in a ProcessPoolExecutor: real parallelism, plus
# a pickling round trip per task and per result.

# --- Other loop costs worth knowing --------------------------------------
#   - A task that never awaits anything runs to completion synchronously and
#     starves every other task. This is the "my service stopped responding
#     for no reason" failure.
#   - Context is copied per task in 3.11+; contextvars are cheap, but a
#     contextvar holding a large object is copied by reference, not by value.
#   - Debug mode adds a slow callback to every callback and every coroutine
#     resume. Never enable it in production; it is 2-5x slower.
#   - uvloop is a drop-in replacement for the default loop with lower per-task
#     cost. asyncio.run(loop_factory=uvloop.new_event_loop) in 3.12+.
#   - PyPy has no separate uvloop equivalent; its JIT makes the default loop
#     considerably cheaper.

import asyncio
async def main() -> None:
    print("default loop :", type(asyncio.get_running_loop()).__name__)
    try:
        import uvloop
        print("uvloop        :", uvloop.__version__)
    except ImportError:
        print("uvloop        : not installed")
asyncio.run(main())

Decision rule: below a few dozen concurrent operations, a ThreadPoolExecutor around a blocking library is the better answer than converting the library to async — the overhead is lower and the code is simpler. Above a few hundred, the event loop’s per-task cost is what makes the difference, and an async-native driver (see 12.10) is required to benefit. The conversion cost is not the async keyword; it is finding or writing an async version of every blocking dependency in the call graph.

14.5 Profiling CPU time

Optimisation without a profile is guesswork, and guesswork usually makes the code worse without making it faster. Four tools, each answering a different question: cProfile and pstats for deterministic attribution, py-spy for sampling a live process without modifying it, scalene for separating CPU from memory with an interpreter-level view, and timeit for a controlled microbenchmark. None of them can tell you where the time goes in a request that is not being made.

import cProfile, pstats, io, runpy, sys, os
from pathlib import Path

# --- cProfile: deterministic, and slow -----------------------------------
# cProfile hooks every function call and every call event, so it distorts
# absolute timings by an order of magnitude or more. It distorts RELATIVE
# attribution far less, which is what it is for.
def build_report(work: int) -> bytes:
    out = io.StringIO()
    pr = cProfile.Profile()
    pr.enable()
    work()
    pr.disable()
    st = pstats.Stats(pr, stream=out)
    st.sort_stats("cumulative").print_stats(20)
    st.sort_stats("tottime").print_stats(20)
    pr.dump_stats("profile.prof")            # binary, for snakeviz / pstats later
    return out.getvalue().encode()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    import json
    def slow() -> None:
        data = json.loads(Path("data.json").read_text()) if Path("data.json").exists() else []
        total = 0
        for rec in data:
            for k, v in sorted(rec.items()):
                total += hash(k) % 7
    text = build_report(slow).decode()
    print("\n".join(text.splitlines()[:30]))

# Reading a profile: the four columns that matter.
#   ncalls   how many times. A tiny ncalls with a huge tottime is a hotspot.
#   tottime  time in the function itself, excluding subcalls. THIS is the
#            number to rank by when hunting for CPU.
#   cumtime  time in the function plus everything it called. Rank by this when
#            asking "where does the time for this operation go?".
#   callers / callees  who invoked it, and what it invoked. The <-
#            (recursive) marker means a cycle: mutua l recursion, a decorator
#            re-entering, or a property that calls itself.
#
# Reading it correctly:
#   - sort by cumtime and walk DOWN the tree until the total stops growing.
#     The point where it flattens is the boundary between "framework" and
#     "my code".
#   - a high cumtime on a small function is normal and useless. A high
#     tottime on a small function is the answer.
#   - builtins appear with tottime but no source, because they are C. A large
#     builtin tottime is a signal to change the algorithm, not the line.
#   - "tottime" excludes time in subcalls, so a function that is mostly
#     waiting on its own children shows a small tottime. That is correct, not
#     a bug.

# --- pstats programmatically, to extract exactly what you need -------------
pr = cProfile.Profile(); pr.enable()
data = [dict(zip("abcdef", range(6))) for _ in range(5000)]
pr.disable()
st = pstats.Stats(pr)
for func, (cc, nc, tt, ct, callers) in sorted(
    st.stats.items(), key=lambda kv: -kv[1][2]
)[:5]:
    fname, line, name = func
    print(f"{os.path.basename(fname)}:{line}({name})  ncalls={nc}  tottime={tt:.4f}")

# --- py-spy: sampling, on a live process, no cooperation needed -----------
#   py-spy top --pid 12345                 # live flame-ish table, 1s refresh
#   py-spy record --pid 12345 -o flame.svg  # flame graph, no restart
#   py-spy dump --pid 12345                 # one stack sample
#   py-spy record -m my_module -f prof.svg  # launch and attach
#   py-spy top --pid 12345 --gil            # only samples while holding the GIL
#   py-spy record --pid 12345 --subprocesses
# Sampling at the default 100 Hz, a function that takes 5 ms in total is
# unlikely to be sampled at all. Raise --rate and --duration for short
# functions. This is the tool for "the service is slow in production and I
# cannot reproduce it", and the only one that works without a code change.
# --gil separates time spent executing Python from time waiting for the GIL;
# a large GIL-wait figure on a GIL build means the fix is parallelism, not
# faster code.

# --- scalene: CPU, GPU and memory, separately ----------------------------
#   pip install scalene
#   scalene --cpu --memory myscript.py
#   scalene --profile myscript.py --out profile.json
# It reports CPU time, GPU time, peak memory and allocation count per line,
# and separates Python time from C-extension time. It is the right tool for
# "this function allocates far more than it should", because cProfile cannot
# see allocation at all. The stated overhead is roughly 0.5-2x on the whole
# program, attributed line by line, which is far better than cProfile's.

# --- timeit: a controlled microbenchmark ----------------------------------
import timeit
print("listcomp      :", min(timeit.repeat("sum(x*x for x in range(10000))",
                                          number=100, repeat=5)))
print("builtin sum   :", min(timeit.repeat("sum(map(lambda x: x*x, range(10000)))",
                                          number=100, repeat=5)))
# Rules: use repeat + min, not time.time around a loop. Set up outside the
# timed block. Never benchmark inside another measurement. A difference under
# about 5% on a modern CPU is usually noise from frequency scaling and
# turbo; confirm a claim with more repeats before acting on it.

# --- Operating-system level, for the whole picture ------------------------
#   perf top -p $(pgrep -d, -f myapp)
#   py-spy alongside it tells you what Python is doing; perf tells you what
#   the process is doing, including the time in libc, in the dynamic linker,
#   in the garbage collector's C code, and in kernel syscalls that Python
#   attributes nowhere.

cProfile numbers are not wall-clock numbers. Do not paste them into a report as "this request takes 4.2 seconds". Use py-spy or perf for anything that has to be defensible, and reserve cProfile for ranking relative cost inside a short, isolated run.

Start at the top and stop at the first line that is yours. In most Python services the honest answer after five minutes of profiling is one of: the ORM generating a query nobody expected, a serialisation step on a list an order of magnitude too long, an N+1 (see 12.8), or logging with an f-string that formats a megabyte of context on every request even at a filtered level.

14.6 Memory and the garbage collector

Python’s memory story has two independent mechanisms. Reference counting frees an object as soon as its last reference goes, and runs deterministically. The cyclic garbage collector handles the minority of objects that participate in reference cycles, and runs periodically on generations. The two together mean memory rises in steps, not smoothly, and that a process holding a few hundred megabytes more than expected is usually one of: a cache with no bound, a container of forgotten objects, or a fragmentation pattern from a process that has grown and shrunk.

import sys, gc, tracemalloc, weakref, platform
from collections import deque

# --- sys.getsizeof lies, systematically ----------------------------------
x = [0] * 1000
print("getsizeof(list)      :", sys.getsizeof(x))       # 8056
print("  but the 1000 ints  :", 1000 * sys.getsizeof(0))  # 28000 extra, and
                                                            # small ints are
                                                            # interned, so the
                                                            # real cost is 8
                                                            # bytes each in the
                                                            # pointer array only
d = {"k" * 30: "v" * 30}
print("getsizeof(dict)      :", sys.getsizeof(d))       # 184
print("  + keys and values  :", sys.getsizeof(next(iter(d))) + sys.getsizeof(next(iter(d.values()))))
print("  + hash table slots : ~ 3/2 * entries, not in the figure above")

# A deep, shared object graph: summing getsizeof double-counts by design.
class Node:
    def __init__(self, name): self.name = name; self.children = []
root = Node("root")
for i in range(3):
    kid = Node(f"k{i}"); root.children.append(kid)
shared = Node("shared")
root.children[0].children.append(shared)
root.children[1].children.append(shared)
naive = sys.getsizeof(root) + sys.getsizeof(root.children)
print("naive sum (double-counts):", naive, " -> wrong")

def deep_size(obj, seen=None) -> int:
    """Size an object graph, counting each object once."""
    if seen is None: seen = set()
    i = id(obj)
    if i in seen: return 0
    seen.add(i)
    size = sys.getsizeof(obj)
    if isinstance(obj, (list, tuple, set, frozenset, deque)):
        size += sum(deep_size(x, seen) for x in obj)
    elif isinstance(obj, dict):
        size += sum(deep_size(k, seen) + deep_size(v, seen) for k, v in obj.items())
    return size
print("deep_size             :", deep_size(root))

# --- tracemalloc: allocation attributed to source lines -------------------
tracemalloc.start(25)                     # 25 frames of traceback per block
def build() -> list[bytes]:
    return [b"x" * 10_000 for _ in range(500)]      # ~5 MB
held = build()
snap = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
for stat in snap.statistics("lineno")[:5]:
    print(f"{stat.size/1024:8.1f} KiB  {stat.count:5} blocks  {stat}")
cur, peak = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory()
print(f"traced current {cur/1e6:.2f} MB   peak {peak/1e6:.2f} MB")
tracemalloc.stop()

# A diff is far more useful than an absolute snapshot: it attributes growth
# between two points to a source line.
tracemalloc.start()
before = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
more = build()
after = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
for stat in after.compare_to(before, "lineno")[:5]:
    print(f"  +{stat.size_diff/1024:8.1f} KiB  {stat.traceback.format()[-1].strip()}")
tracemalloc.stop()
# Overhead is roughly 10-30%; it is a diagnostic, not a production resident.

# --- RSS, which is what the operating system sees -------------------------
# tracemalloc sees Python allocations only. A C extension's own buffers, the
# interpreter's arenas, and mapped files are invisible to it. Read RSS from
# /proc/self/status (VmRSS) on Linux, or use psutil, or instrument the
# platform's own metrics.
# import psutil; print(psutil.Process().memory_info().rss / 1e6, "MB")

# --- Reference cycles and the cyclic collector ---------------------------
# A container in a cycle is NOT freed by reference counting. It waits for the
# cyclic collector, which runs on an allocation threshold.
class Node2:
    def __init__(self): self.other = None
a, b = Node2(), Node2()
a.other = b; b.other = a
print("cycle refcount now:", sys.getrefcount(a) - 1)
del a, b
print("in garbage        :", len(gc.garbage))        # 0: gc found it, not tracked
print("collector runs    :", [g.type.__name__ for g in gc.garbage])

# Anything with a container is a candidate; anything with __del__ is NOT
# collectable before 3.4 and still discouraged now. Modern practice is
# weakref for parent/child and observer links instead of a strong cycle.
class Parent:
    def __init__(self):
        self._children: list[weakref.ref] = []
    def add(self, child) -> None:
        self._children.append(weakref.ref(child))     # no cycle created

print("gc.get_threshold() :", gc.get_threshold())     # (700, 10, 10)
print("gc.isenabled()     :", gc.isenabled())
print("gc.get_count()     :", gc.get_count(), "(gen 0)")

# gen0 every 700 net allocations, gen1 every 10 gen0 collections,
# gen2 every 10 gen1 collections, and every gen2 collection is full.
# Surviving objects are promoted; a long-lived object is examined in gen2
# rarely and that is the point.

# gc.freeze() after start-up moves every existing object to a permanent
# generation that is never scanned again. On a large application with a big
# warm-up phase this is a measurable win, and it is why gunicorn and
# similar servers call it.
class Warm:
    CACHE = {i: str(i) * 100 for i in range(100_000)}
print("frozen objects:", gc.freeze())

# Disabling entirely: gc.disable() is occasionally correct inside a tight
# allocation loop that creates a very large number of short-lived objects
# with no cycles, provided the memory spike is bounded and gc.enable() runs in
# a finally. It is a specialist tool and turning it off globally in a server
# leaks every cycle forever.
gc.disable()
try:
    for _ in range(1000):
        [object() for _ in range(100)]
finally:
    gc.enable()

# The other half of memory: a bounded cache. functools.lru_cache with
# maxsize, or an explicit LRU, is not premature optimisation -- an unbounded
# cache is a memory leak with a convenient name.
from functools import lru_cache
@lru_cache(maxsize=1024)
def expensive(k: str) -> str:
    return k.upper()
print(expensive.cache_info())

# Process RSS grows and does not return to the OS after a spike, because
# CPython keeps arenas. This is expected; call malloc_trim(0) on glibc only
# if the RSS-vs-live-object gap is measured, real and operationally painful.
print("platform:", platform.platform(), "| gc thresholds:", gc.get_threshold())

gc.garbage is a debugging aid, not a solution. It holds every object the collector could not free, and the reason is almost always a __del__ method on a cycle participant, or a C extension that has not been annotated for traversal. An unbounded gc.garbage is a leak. Fix the cycle — usually with weakref or by removing the back-pointer — and the problem disappears. Turning the collector off converts a slow leak into a fast one.

Free-threaded builds differ here. Per-object locking and biased reference counting add memory overhead to every object, so RSS per object is higher than on the GIL build. The collector still exists and still runs on the same thresholds. Measure with tracemalloc as before, but calibrate any per-object memory budget taken from a GIL build.

14.7 Optimising measured hot paths

The optimisations below are listed in the order they are worth considering, which is not the order they are usually tried. Almost all of them are wrong for a codebase where the hot path is 0.1% of runtime, and right for one where it is 30%. None of them changes an algorithm, and changing the algorithm beats all of them by orders of magnitude.

import timeit, sys, operator
from typing import Any, Callable

setup = """
data = list(range(100_000))
lookup = {i: str(i) for i in range(100_000)}
sq = __import__('math').sqrt
"""

def bench(label: str, stmt: str, **kw: Any) -> None:
    t = min(timeit.repeat(stmt, setup, number=10, repeat=7, **kw))
    print(f"{label:<46} {t*1000:8.2f} ms")

# --- 0. Change the algorithm, not the code ------------------------------
# O(n^2) -> O(n log n) or O(n) beats every micro-optimisation below by orders
# of magnitude. If a loop is quadratic, nothing on this list matters.
# bench("sorted()                : 100k", "sorted(data, reverse=True)")
# bench("heapq.nlargest(10)       : 100k", "import heapq; heapq.nlargest(10, data)")

# --- 1. Move the work out of the interpreter ----------------------------
# cProfile would have shown this immediately. A pure-Python loop stays pure
# Python no matter how well it is written; a library with a C loop is 100x
# faster and is usually clearer.
bench("sum(x*x for x in data)       ", "sum(x*x for x in data)")
bench("sum(map(operator.mul, data, data))", "sum(map(operator.mul, data, data))")
# Map with a C callable runs the loop in C. This is the single largest
# constant-factor win available without leaving Python.

# --- 2. Comprehension over loop-plus-append ------------------------------
# The interpreter executes fewer bytecodes and allocates less: a list
# comprehension has its own frame-less loop, and it does not call .append
# per iteration.
bench("loop + append               ", "out=[]\nfor x in data:\n    if x%2: out.append(x*2)")
bench("list comprehension          ", "[x*2 for x in data if x%2]")
# A generator expression is the same comprehension with lazy evaluation. It
# wins on peak memory and loses slightly on throughput, because each yielded
# value crosses a frame boundary.
bench("list comprehension          ", "[sq(x) for x in data[:20000]]")
bench("generator expression        ", "sum(sq(x) for x in data[:20000])")
# Rule: build a list when the result is consumed more than once or needs
# len()/indexing; build a generator when it is consumed once and is large.

# --- 3. Hoist attribute and global lookups out of the loop --------------
# LOAD_ATTR and LOAD_GLOBAL are dictionary lookups. Inside a hot loop, bind
# the callable once. This matters a great deal on a GIL build; on
# CPython 3.11+ specialising adaptive interpreter reduced the gap but did
# not close it.
bench("obj.method(x) in a loop      ", "out=[]\nfor r in data: out.append(str(r))")
bench("local str = str; str(r)      ", "f=str\nout=[]\nfor r in data: out.append(f(r))")
# In 3.11+ f-strings are compiled to a specialised opcode and are faster
# than "".join or % formatting. Do not micro-optimise string formatting.
bench("f-string                     ", 'out=["%d" % r for r in data]')
bench("str.format                    ", 'out=[str(r) for r in data]')
bench("f-string                     ", 'out=[f"{r}" for r in data]')

# --- 4. String concatenation --------------------------------------------
# The + operator in a loop is quadratic: each iteration copies everything
# written so far. "".join is linear. In CPython this is a genuine O(n^2) to
# O(n) improvement, not a constant factor.
bench("s += x in a loop (quadratic) ", "s=''\nfor i in range(20000): s+='a'")
bench("''.join(list) (linear)       ", "s=''.join('a' for i in range(20000))")
# And: f-strings do not have this problem at all, because they format each
# piece independently. "".join is only needed when joining many pieces.

# --- 5. __slots__ ---------------------------------------------------------
# The trade-off is real and it is exactly this: __slots__ removes the
# per-instance __dict__, which typically saves 30-50% of memory per object
# and makes attribute access marginally faster. It costs the ability to set
# an undeclared attribute, breaks code that monkey-patches the class (which
# includes some mocking frameworks and some ORM instrumentation), and makes
# a subclass need its own __slots__ to keep the benefit.
class WithDict:
    def __init__(self, a: int, b: int) -> None:
        self.a = a; self.b = b
class WithSlots:
    __slots__ = ("a", "b")
    def __init__(self, a: int, b: int) -> None:
        self.a = a; self.b = b

d = [WithDict(i, i) for i in range(100_000)]
s = [WithSlots(i, i) for i in range(100_000)]
print("with __dict__ :", sys.getsizeof(d[0]), "instance,",
      sys.getsizeof(d[0].__dict__), "dict")
print("with __slots__:", sys.getsizeof(s[0]), "instance, no dict")
# __slots__ does NOT remove the type's own overhead, and it does not help a
# class with one instance or a class that is not instantiated in bulk. The
# correct trigger is "millions of live instances", which in practice means a
# parsed-document tree or a large matrix of homogeneous records.

# --- 6. Cache what an expensive call returns ----------------------------
# Only for genuinely pure, expensive, and bounded-by-nature computations.
from functools import lru_cache, cache
@cache
def expensive_key(k: tuple[int, int]) -> int:
    return sum(i * i for i in range(k[0] % 1000 + k[1] % 1000))
# lru_cache is a lock in the C-implemented GIL build, and a per-object lock on
# a free-threaded build, so it is still correct; it is not free, so do not
# decorate a trivial function.

# --- 7. Avoid work, entirely ---------------------------------------------
# The best optimisation is not computing the value. len() in a loop condition
# is evaluated every iteration; bind it. Short-circuit and on already, because
# "and" is a branch and "bool(a) and bool(b)" is two calls. Hoist a raise out
# of a loop by checking once before it. Those three account for a surprising
# share of real hot loops.
def hoist(items: list[int]) -> int:
    if not items:                          # checked once, not per iteration
        raise ValueError("empty")
    return sum(items)

# --- 8. Data structures, last -------------------------------------------
# bisect.insort on a sorted list beats list.insert (O(log n) search, O(n)
# memmove, and the memmove is C). A dict beats a list for membership.
# collections.deque beats list for both-ends and for pop(0) (see 11.1).
# set beats a list for `in` by two orders of magnitude at any size worth
# naming. These are structural, not micro, and they are worth more than
# everything above once the dataset is large.

# --- 9. What is not worth doing -----------------------------------------
# Replacing assert with an if: asserts are removed with -O and are not a
# control-flow mechanism. Micro-rewriting arithmetic that is already inside a
# C function. Introducing __slots__ on a class with twenty instances.
# Caching a dict lookup that the specialising interpreter already made fast.
# Rewriting a comprehension as an explicit loop. Any of these costs clarity
# for a gain that a repeat of the benchmark cannot distinguish from noise.

Every micro-optimisation in this section reduces readability. The professional standard is that an optimisation lands with a benchmark that shows the improvement exceeds measurement noise on the real workload, and with a comment stating what was measured. An unexplained rewrite of an expression into a less obvious equivalent form is a defect in a code review, regardless of whether it was faster.
