The failures are the same three: not constraining the result to a base directory, trusting a separator that is not the platform’s, and creating a file in a shared or predictable location. pathlib resolves all three cleanly, because it separates path arithmetic from filesystem access and because resolve() follows symlinks.

from __future__ import annotations

import os, tempfile, shutil, uuid, stat
from pathlib import Path

# --- The vulnerability -----------------------------------------------------
BASE = Path("/srv/data")
def read_unsafe(user_path: str) -> bytes:
    return (BASE / user_path).read_bytes()      # /srv/data/../../etc/shadow
# Payloads:   ../../etc/passwd      absolute: /etc/passwd
#             ..%2f..%2fetc%2fpasswd (if the URL is decoded before this call)
#             a/../../../etc/passwd     a/..\\..\\windows\\system32  (Windows)
#             /proc/self/environ            a named pipe, a device, a socket
# A symlink inside BASE pointing outside it defeats every check that does not
# resolve.

# --- The defence: resolve, then check containment -------------------------
def safe_read(base: Path, user_path: str) -> bytes:
    base_r = base.resolve(strict=True)            # canonical, symlinks followed
    # PurePosixPath treats a backslash as an ordinary character; on Windows,
    # PureWindowsPath does not. Choose deliberately, or use the platform
    # default and then verify.
    candidate = (base_r / user_path).resolve(strict=True)
    # is_relative_to is the correct test. startswith is NOT: "/srv/data-evil"
    # starts with "/srv/data" and is a different directory entirely.
    if not candidate.is_relative_to(base_r):
        raise PermissionError(f"path escapes the base directory: {user_path!r}")
    if not candidate.is_file():
        raise ValueError(f"not a regular file: {user_path!r}")
    return candidate.read_bytes()

# strict=True in resolve() is the point: it raises FileNotFoundError rather
# than returning a non-existent path, so the containment check operates on a
# path that demonstrably exists.

# Rejecting the suspicious inputs as well is defence in depth, not the
# primary control, and it is worth doing because it makes the intent explicit:
def validate_component(name: str) -> str:
    if name in {"", ".", ".."}:
        raise ValueError("invalid path component")
    if "/" in name or "\\" in name or "\x00" in name:
        raise ValueError("path separator or NUL in a single component")
    if os.path.isabs(name) or (len(name) > 1 and name[1] == ":"):
        raise ValueError("absolute path")
    if name.startswith("."):
        raise ValueError("dotfile")
    if len(name) > 255:
        raise ValueError("component too long")
    return name

# --- Never trust a client-supplied filename -------------------------------
def store_upload(raw_name: str, data: bytes, dest: Path) -> Path:
    ext = Path(raw_name).suffix.lower()          # extension only, for the type
    if ext not in {".png", ".jpg", ".txt", ".pdf"}:
        raise ValueError(f"unsupported type: {ext}")
    name = uuid.uuid4().hex + ext               # a name WE generate
    target = dest / name
    # O_EXCL: fail rather than follow an existing symlink at that path.
    fd = os.open(target, os.O_WRONLY | os.O_CREAT | os.O_EXCL, 0o600)
    with os.fdopen(fd, "wb") as fh:
        fh.write(data)
    return target
# The client's name is used for nothing except possibly an extension from an
# allowlist, and the extension does not make the content an image. If the type
# matters, verify it: python-magic, or a real decode of the header.

# --- Symlinks -------------------------------------------------------------
# A symlink is a capability, and following one is how a check on the path
# string is bypassed. Three policies, in increasing strictness:
#   1. Follow them (default). Correct for a data directory you own.
#   2. Refuse them:  Path.is_symlink(), and O_NOFOLLOW on open where the
#      platform provides it. Correct for a directory holding executable or
#      configuration files.
#   3. Refuse to traverse out of the tree at all, which is a policy for a
#      jail and belongs in the kernel (a container, chroot, bwrap) rather than
#      in Python.
for p in (BASE).rglob("*"):
    if p.is_symlink():
        print("symlink:", p, "->", p.readlink(), "stays inside:",
              p.resolve().is_relative_to(BASE.resolve()))

# --- Temporary files -------------------------------------------------------
# The three requirements are unique, unpredictable and restrictive.
with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as td:
    p = Path(td) / "input.json"
    p.write_text("{}")
    print("temp dir perms:", oct(Path(td).stat().st_mode & 0o777))
# TemporaryDirectory / NamedTemporaryFile / mkdtemp create with mode 0700,
# which is correct and is the reason to prefer them over a fixed path.
# tempfile.mkstemp() returns (fd, path) and the fd is open -- use os.fdopen.
# Never build a temporary name from os.getpid() or a counter: both are
# predictable, and a predictable name in a shared directory is a symlink
# attack, which is why the sticky bit exists on /tmp and why it does not
# save you if you are the one creating the link.

# --- One more: the destination directory must exist and be trusted --------
# write_text() does not create parent directories, which is correct: a
# caller that could make the program create /srv/app/data/uploads/../../etc
# is a caller that has already won.

str(path).startswith(str(base)) is the most common defect in hand-written path validation and it is wrong: /srv/data-old starts with /srv/data as a string and is a different directory. Path.is_relative_to(), added in 3.9, is the correct test and it handles the trailing separator and the relative-path case correctly. The second most common is validating the path string and then opening it, which means a symlink created between the check and the open, and after the resolve, is not covered.

16.6 XML entity attacks

XML is a document format with a language for what a document may refer to. That language is the problem. An external entity makes the parser read an arbitrary URI, which is a local file disclosure and a server-side request forgery primitive. A billion-laughs payload nests a few hundred entity definitions to expand a few kilobytes into gigabytes of memory. Both are attacks on the parser, not on the application’s logic, and both are fixed by not using a parser that resolves entities.

import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET
from xml.sax import make_parser
from xml.dom import pulldom

# --- XXE: external entity expansion ---------------------------------------
# A classic payload, as data. Never evaluate this; this is a shape, not a test.
XXE = """<?xml version="1.0"?>
<!DOCTYPE root [ <!ENTITY xxe SYSTEM "file:///etc/passwd"> ]>
<root><name>&xxe;</name></root>"""

# --- Billion laughs --------------------------------------------------------
BILLION = """<?xml version="1.0"?>
<!DOCTYPE lolz [
  <!ENTITY lol "lol">
  <!ENTITY lol1 "&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;&lol;">
  <!ENTITY lol2 "&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;&lol1;">
  <!ENTITY lol3 "&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;&lol2;">
]>
<lolz>&lol3;</lolz>"""
# Ten levels of this is about a gigabyte of text from about a kilobyte of
# input, and it is a denial of service in one function call.

# --- The parser matrix ----------------------------------------------------
#   xml.etree.ElementTree
#       Expands internal general entities. Does NOT resolve external entities
#       and does NOT fetch remote DTDs. NOT vulnerable to the classic XXE.
#       IS vulnerable to billion laughs, and to quadratic entity expansion in
#       the default expat configuration. ReDoS-style and resource-exhaustion
#       defences are absent.
#   xml.dom.minidom, xml.sax
#       expat-based. The classic guidance is that external entity resolution
#       depends on the parser setup, and the setup is easy to get wrong.
#   xml.etree.ElementTree.XMLParser
#       The same, with the entity handling hookable. Defining a no-op for
#       EntityDeclHandler is the mitigation that works across the stdlib.
#   lxml
#       Resolves external entities by default. VULNERABLE unless
#       resolve_entities=False, no_network=True and huge_tree=False.
#       defusedxml.lxml is the correct way to use it.
#   pyexpat
#       Low level. ExternalEntityRefHandler and EntityDeclHandler are the
#       hooks.

# --- The defence: defusedxml ----------------------------------------------
# pip install defusedxml
from defusedxml.ElementTree import fromstring as safe_fromstring
def parse_xml(data: bytes):
    """Parse XML with external entities and DTD retrieval disabled."""
    return safe_fromstring(data)              # forbids DTDs and entities outright

# defusedxml replaces xml.etree, minidom, sax and lxml with versions that
# raise on a DTD, an entity declaration, an external reference and an
# undefined entity. It is the correct answer and it is three lines of change:
#     from defusedxml.ElementTree import parse, fromstring, iterparse
# For a large file, iterparse is the streaming form and needs the same import.

# --- Hand-rolled mitigation, when a dependency is not permitted -----------
def parse_no_entities(data: bytes):
    parser = ET.XMLParser()
    def reject_doctype(name, sysid, pubid, has_internal):  # noqa: ANN001
        raise ValueError("DTDs are not accepted")
    def noop_entity(*args, **kwargs): pass
    parser.parser.StartDoctypeDeclHandler = reject_doctype   # expat hook
    parser.parser.EntityDeclHandler = noop_entity
    parser.parser.ExternalEntityRefHandler = lambda *a: False
    return ET.fromstring(data, parser=parser)
# This is a mitigation, not a guarantee: it depends on the underlying expat
# hooks, and a parser that changes hooks silently loses them. Prefer
# defusedxml, which is maintained as a security library specifically because
# the hand-rolled version keeps being wrong.

# --- lxml, configured correctly -------------------------------------------
# from lxml import etree
# parser = etree.XMLParser(resolve_entities=False, no_network=True,
#                          huge_tree=False, load_dtd=False)
# root = etree.fromstring(data, parser)
# The four flags are all needed. Setting only resolve_entities=False still
# allows a DTD to be fetched, which is the SSRF half.

# --- The general rule ------------------------------------------------------
# Prefer a data format with no entity language. JSON, YAML (safe_load),
# MessagePack, CBOR and TOML cannot expand a document into gigabytes, cannot
# reference a local file, and cannot cause a network fetch, because the format
# has no concept of a reference. XML's entity mechanism is a feature of XML
# 1.0 and cannot be removed from the specification; every parser must
# therefore implement an extension, and every extension must be enabled by
# someone. That someone is you.
#
# If you must accept XML, add a size limit on the request body, a timeout on
# the read, and reject the content type before parsing. Entity expansion
# attacks are also a decompressed-bomb class of problem: a 10 MB request that
# expands to 10 GB is a denial of service that no parser setting prevents if
# the parser chooses to expand.

The highest-frequency XXE in production Python is not ElementTree — it is lxml used for convenience, and it is vulnerable by default. The second highest is a hand-rolled ET.XMLParser with the hooks set incorrectly, or set for one expat version and not another. Both are found by one review step: search for lxml and for XMLParser, and check the configuration. defusedxml exists because this is the class of bug that keeps recurring, and its presence in a dependency list should be the default rather than the exception.

16.7 Dependency and supply-chain risk

A Python application executes code from every distribution it installs, transitively, at import time and with the full privileges of the process. The trust boundary is not the direct dependency list; it is the resolved lock file, which is typically three to ten times longer. This section covers the practical controls, and the honest assessment of what each one buys.

# --- Counting what you actually trust -------------------------------------
#   uv tree                              the full resolved dependency graph
#   uv tree --invert --package requests   what depends on a specific package
#   pipdeptree -r -p requests
# The number that matters is the transitive count of a small application.
# A typical one is between 50 and 300 distributions, most of them not
# installed because the developer uses them but because something else does.

# --- Typosquatting --------------------------------------------------------
# Names registered to be one edit away from a popular package: python-dateutil
# vs python-dateutils, beautifulsoup4 vs bs4 (the reverse case, a genuine
# package squatting on an abbreviation), request vs requests, PyYAML vs
# pyaml, colourama vs colors. The defence is entirely procedural:
#   - read the resolved list, once, before deploying, as a deliberate step
#   - a pre-merge dependency check: a new direct dependency is a review event
#   - a lock file, so a new package enters the project only when the lock
#     changes, and the lock is a reviewable diff (see 13.3)
#   - no install scripts, and no unpinned Git URLs: a VCS dependency is
#     resolved at install time and can point anywhere tomorrow
# There is no automated defence against a name that was chosen to be
# confusing, which is why the human review step is the control.

# --- Install-time code execution ------------------------------------------
# A setup.py can run arbitrary code during installation, and every backend
# that predates PEP 517 has hooks that do. Mitigations:
#   - uv and pip both support --no-build-isolation-package, and pip has
#     --only-binary :all: to refuse an sdist entirely
#   - uv build, which uses a declared, isolated build environment from
#     [build-system].requires, rather than an inherited one
#   - installing from wheels only: --only-binary :all: means a package with
#     no compatible wheel cannot be installed at all, which is exactly the
#     desired behaviour
#   - a container image built in CI, with the build stage and the run stage
#     separate, so build-time code never reaches production
# Note that a wheel CAN contain code that runs at import time, which is the
# ordinary case. The distinction is that a wheel's code is visible and its
# files are hashed in the lock.

# --- Scanning -------------------------------------------------------------
#   pip-audit                 queries the OSV and PyPA advisory databases;
#                            --strict pins to the exact vulnerable version;
#                            a lock file is the right input
#   uv run pip-audit          the same, in the project environment
#   osv-scanner               the OSV database, for many ecosystems at once
#   safety                    commercial alternative with its own database
#   pip install safety-db     a local copy of the advisory set, for air-gapped
#
# Run them in CI on every build, and on a schedule even with no commits: a
# CVE published today can apply to a lock file that has not changed at all.
# An audit that only runs on pull requests has a detection latency measured in
# months.

# --- Reviewing what a new dependency brings -------------------------------
# Before adding a direct dependency, establish:
#   1. Does the standard library do it? json, sqlite3, tarfile, zipfile,
#      email, urllib, logging, argparse, decimal, difflib, csv, configparser
#      cover more of the common needs than most third-party packages.
#   2. What is the transitive cost? uv add --dry-run, or uv tree after.
#   3. How many maintainers, how recent is the last release, how many open
#      issues, what does the licence actually permit.
#   4. Does it execute at import time, and what does that code do?
#   5. What happens if it disappears? An unmaintained transitive dependency
#      is a future incident; two of them are an incident now.
# A well-chosen dependency is one with a small transitive closure, a
# permissive licence, a maintenance history visible in the changelog, and a
# behaviour that is documented rather than discovered.

# --- Licences -------------------------------------------------------------
# A licence is a legal constraint, and an incompatible one discovered during
# release is a release blocker. Tools: pip-licenses to enumerate, and a
# policy file for what is acceptable (MIT, BSD, Apache-2.0 are permissive;
# GPL and AGPL impose copyleft obligations that change what a linked
# application must be). Dual-licensing, a "commons clause", and a
# "non-commercial" restriction that is not an open-source licence at all
# all appear in this space and all need a human decision.

# --- Hashes, provenance and the limits of each ----------------------------
#   --require-hashes   integrity: the bytes installed are the bytes pinned.
#                      This prevents a compromised mirror or a tampered
#                      upload. It does not say the package is safe.
#   uv.lock hashes    the same, and by default
#   PyPI attestations provenance: signed statements about how and where a
#                      release was built. Adopt them; do not assume them.
#   RECORD in the wheel  per-file hashes, verified at install
#   Reproducible builds  the sdist rebuilds to the same wheel bit for bit.
#                      A supply-chain property, and rarely checked.
# A hash proves the artifact has not changed. It does not prove the artifact
# is not malicious -- a package published with a vulnerability has a perfectly
# valid hash. Confusing the two is the most common error in dependency
# security reasoning, and the practical consequence is that a hash-pinned
# lock file plus an audit, not one or the other, is the control.

# --- A minimal, defensible baseline ---------------------------------------
#   1. A committed lock file, installed with --frozen in CI and in production.
#   2. --only-binary :all: unless a build is genuinely required.
#   3. pip-audit (or equivalent) in CI and on a schedule.
#   4. pre-commit detect-private-key plus a secret scanner.
#   5. A reviewed dependency-diff in every pull request that changes the lock.
#   6. Pin the build toolchain itself: the base image by digest, the
#      interpreter version, the build frontend version.
#   7. A documented incident procedure: how to rotate, how to rebuild the
#      image, how to know what is affected.
# That is a short list, and it is the list. Everything beyond it is
# proportional to the cost of being wrong in a specific deployment.

Requirements: a lock file, a scanner, and a review step for dependency changes. Caveats: the three most common statements in this area are each wrong in a specific way. "We audit, so we are safe" — the audit reports what has been published; a package with a zero-day that has not been reported is not in the database. "We use hashes, so we are safe" — a hash proves the bytes are unchanged, not that they are benign. "We pin everything, so we are safe" — a fully pinned application cannot receive a security fix without a deliberate, reviewed change, which is a feature and a delay, and the delay is where incidents actually happen. The controls work because they are combined and because the combination is reviewed, not because any one of them is sufficient.

Exercises 16

  1. Write a validator for a request payload with a denylist, then rewrite it as an allowlist, and enumerate every input the denylist accepts and the allowlist rejects, including Unicode confusables and a 10 MB value.
  2. Reproduce command injection against all four vulnerable forms from 16.2 with the same seven payloads, then confirm the argument-vector form is inert for each, and measure the wall-clock difference over 1000 iterations.
  3. Build a pickle payload that writes a file, using __reduce__, and demonstrate json and yaml.safe_load refusing the equivalent structure, then add an allowlist of types to a restricted unpickler and record which payload survives it.
  4. Benchmark hashlib.pbkdf2_hmac, hashlib.scrypt, bcrypt and argon2 at production parameters, and compute the number of hash evaluations a single GPU performs per second for each.
  5. Write a path validator with startswith, then with is_relative_to, and show the /srv/data-evil bypass that the first one permits.
  6. Parse the XXE and billion-laughs payloads with ElementTree, with lxml default, with lxml configured, and with defusedxml, and record the file read, the memory consumption and the exception for each of the twelve combinations.
  7. Run pip-audit and uv tree against a small project, and produce a table of the transitive packages that the project never imports, then identify which of them are required by a package the project does import.
  8. Set up a pre-commit secret scanner with a baseline, add a fake private key to a commit, and confirm the commit is blocked and that git log still contains it afterwards.

Appendix · Reference Tables

A.1 Syntax and operator precedenceA.2 Standard library map, by taskA.3 Common exceptions and their causesA.4 Notable changes, 3.8 to 3.14A.5 Official documentation and reference links

Five reference tables. The syntax card is for the reader who knows the material and needs to check a detail; the standard library map is organised by task rather than by module name, because the module name is the thing being looked up; the exception table lists causes rather than names; the migration table names the versions where behaviour actually changed; the resource list points at the primary sources. Everything here cross-references the chapter that carries the discussion.

A.1 Syntax and operator precedence

Precedence is listed highest binding first, as in the language reference. Bind it in your head as: exponent, then unary, then multiplicative, additive, shift, bitwise and, bitwise xor, bitwise or, comparisons, not, and, or, then the conditional expression, then lambda, then assignment, then the tuple and the comma. See 1.1 for why indentation is part of the grammar, and 1.4 for the pattern grammar.

LevelOperatorsAssociativityNotes
1f(x), x[i], x.attr, (...), [...], {...}Left to rightCall, subscript, attribute, and the display forms
2**RightRight-associative: 2**3**2 is 512, not 64. await x binds as ** does
3+x, -x, ~xRightUnary. Applies after **, so -2**2 is -4
4*, /, //, %, @Left@ is the matrix product, overloaded by the operands
5+, -LeftAlso sequence concatenation and tuple addition
6<<, >>LeftArithmetic shift, not logical
7&LeftSet intersection, and dict intersection from 3.9
8^LeftSet symmetric difference
9|LeftSet union, and dict union from 3.9
10<, <=, >, >=, ==, !=, in, not in, is, is notLeftChaining is allowed: 0 < x < 10, and it is short-circuiting, so it costs at most two comparisons
11not xRightLower than every comparison, so not a == b is not (a == b)
12x and yLeftShort-circuits; returns an operand, not a boolean
13x or yLeftShort-circuits; returns an operand, not a boolean
14a if c else bRightThe condition is evaluated first and once
15lambda—Always lowest-binding of the expressions
16x = y = z, x += y, x: T = yRightAssignment; the right-hand side is evaluated once and bound to each target
17yield, yield from, await xRightawait at this level in a comprehension, in the enclosing function elsewhere
18x, y (bare tuple), f(*a, **k)LeftTuple packing and unpacking; the comma is the lowest-precedence operator that exists
# Precedence, demonstrated. Each print states the grouping it relies on.
print(2 ** 3 ** 2)              # 512: right-associative
print(-2 ** 2)                 # -4: unary binds looser than **
print(2 + 3 * 4)               # 14
print(2 * 3 + 4)               # 10
print(2 * 3 ** 2)              # 18: ** first
print((2 * 3) ** 2)            # 36
print(-3 // 2, -3 % 2)         # -2, 1: floor division, not truncation
print(3 / 2, 3 // 2, 3 % 2)    # 1.5, 1, 1
print(1 < 3 < 5)             # True: chaining, short-circuiting
print(1 < 3 > 2)             # False: NOT (1 < 3 > 2), which is
                                 # (1 < 3) and (3 > 2) == True and False
print(not 1 == 2)              # True: not (1 == 2)
print(1 & 3 | 4)             # 5:  & (1) then | (4)
print(True and 0 or "fallback") # fallback: (True and 0) is 0, falsy, so or runs
print(0 or [] or None)         # None: returns the last falsy OPERAND
print([x := 10, x * 2])        # [10, 20]: the walrus binds tighter than
                                 # the comma, so x exists afterwards

# The and / or rule: they return an operand. Write the test explicitly.
def present(v: object) -> bool:
    return v is not None and v != ""     # returns a bool
def first_default(a: list, default: object = None) -> object:
    return a[0] if a else default        # returns an object, which is intended

# The augmented-assignment operators, all one statement, all equivalent to
# x = x op y except that x is evaluated once and the type may differ:
#   +=  -=  *=  /=  //=  %=  **=  &=  |=  ^=  >>=  <<=  @=
# x += y on a list mutates in place (extend); on an immutable type it rebinds.
# A list, tuple, str and bytes support +=; a set does not.

# Unpacking forms:
a, b = 1, 2                                     # tuple
(a, b), c = (1, 2), 3                           # nested
a, *b, c = [1, 2, 3, 4]                         # a=1, b=[2,3], c=4
[a, b], c = [1, 2], 3                           # mixed
first, *rest = "abc"                            # str and bytes too
*d, last = range(4)                             # d=[0,1,2], last=3
x = y = z = 0                                   # chained: one value, three names
a, b = b, a                                     # simultaneous, so this swaps

# Slicing, in full. Every component is optional:
#   seq[start:stop:step]
#   seq[:]      a shallow copy
#   seq[:n]     first n          seq[-n:]     last n
#   seq[n:]      from n           seq[:-n]     all but the last n
#   seq[::2]     every second      seq[::-1]    reversed
#   seq[1:8:2]   1, 3, 5, 7        (stop is exclusive)
# Negative start and stop count from the end; a negative step reverses; an
# out-of-range index raises IndexError, and an out-of-range slice bound is
# silently clamped.

# f-string form, in one place:
name, n, ratio = "widget", 3, 0.5
print(f"{name}")                     # widget
print(f"{name!r}")                   # 'widget'   -- !r calls repr()
print(f"{name!s}")                   # widget     -- !s calls str()
print(f"{name:>10}")               # right-aligned in 10
print(f"{name:<10}|")              # left
print(f"{name:^10}|")               # centred
print(f"{name:10.3f}")               # precision for a float
print(f"{n:04d}")                    # zero-padded width 4
print(f"{n:,}")                      # thousands separator
print(f"{n:#x}")                     # hex with prefix
print(f"{n:+d}")                     # explicit sign
print(f"{ratio:.1%}")                # percentage
print(f"{name=}")                    # name='widget', the = form (3.8)
print(f"{name=:<10}")                # name='widget'  left aligned
print(f"{name!r:>12}")             # repr, right aligned
print(f"{value:{width}}")            # nested: the spec comes from a variable
print(f"{value:{width}.{prec}f}")    # two nested fields
print(f"{'literal braces need doubling'}")   # -> literal braces need doubling

A.2 Standard library map, by task

Organised by the problem being solved rather than by module name. Each entry names the module and the chapter that covers it in depth. Where a standard library module and a third-party package compete, the standard library is listed and the reason for preferring it is noted.

I need toUseNotes and chapter
Read, write, resize a file by pathpathlib.PathResolves, globs, walks, and is the safe choice for untrusted paths (11.3, 16.5)
Write a file atomicallyos.replace into placeWrite to a temporary name in the same directory, then rename. Rename within a filesystem is atomic; the same name in another directory is not
Handle temporary files safelytempfileMode 0700, unpredictable names (16.5)
Read CSV, TSV or fixed-widthcsvNever str.split(","): quoting, embedded newlines and dialects
Read or write structured datajson, tomllib, configparserData formats only; tomllib is read-only and is in the standard library as of 3.11 (11.6)
Read or write binary formatsstruct, csv, zipfile, tarfiletarfile for tar, and check the member paths before extracting (16.5)
Hash or checksum a filehashlibStreaming, in chunks; never read the whole file (16.4)
Compress or decompressgzip, bz2, lzma, zlibAlways decompress with a size limit; a compression bomb is a denial of service
Search or replace textstr methods, reCompiled patterns reused; a pattern that backtracks on hostile input is a ReDoS (11.5)
Parse a numberint(), float(), decimal.DecimalDecimal for money; never float (2.1, 12.4)
Work with dates and timesdatetime, zoneinfoTimezone-aware objects only; zoneinfo reads the system database and needs tzdata on Windows (11.4)
Random sampling or simulationrandom, secretssecrets for anything that authenticates (16.4)
Work with UUIDs or identifiersuuid, secretsuuid4() is 122 random bits from os.urandom
Queue or buffercollections.deque, queue.Queue, asyncio.Queuedeque for a process-local FIFO, queue for threads (11.1)
Group or countcollections.Counter, defaultdictReplaces most hand-written counting loops (11.1)
Iterate lazily over combinationsitertoolsproduct, permutations, groupby, islice, zip_longest (11.2)
Sort with a key or a total ordersorted, functools.cmp_to_keysorted is stable; list.sort is in-place and returns None
Binary search or an ordered insertbisectbisect.insort on a sorted list; O(log n) search, O(n) memmove in C
Cache a function resultfunctools.cache, lru_cacheBounded with maxsize; an unbounded cache is a leak (4.4, 14.6)
Total a float accuratelymath.fsumCorrectly rounded; sum accumulates error
Run a subprocesssubprocess.run with a listNever shell=True with untrusted input (11.8, 16.2)
Parse command-line argumentsargparseSubcommands, type converters, generated help
LogloggingConfigure once with dictConfig; a handler added twice logs twice (11.7)
Emit warningswarningsNot exceptions; filterwarnings = ["error"] in pytest turns them into failures
Testunittest, unittest.mockpytest is the usual runner; both are standard library and neither is deprecated (15)
ProfilecProfile, pstats, tracemallocIn the standard library; py-spy and scalene are not (14.5, 14.6)
Speak the date, or measure ittime, perf_counter_nsMonotonic; time.time is not, and can jump
Read a URL or POST oneurllib.requestVerify TLS, set a timeout, and follow redirects deliberately
Query a databasesqlite3The only driver in the standard library; a complete engine (12.2)
Speak to the operating systemos, shutil, socket, signalos for stat and environment, shutil for copy/move/rmtree
Inspect Python itselfsys, gc, inspect, dis, importlib.metadata3.11 added sys.monitoring, which is the supported successor to sys.settrace
Scan source for a propertyast, tokenizeBoth standard since 3.8 and 3.8 respectively; neither is deprecated by the typing work in 3.12

A.3 Common exceptions and their causes

Organised by cause, because the exception name is what the reader already knows and the cause is what they need. See 10.1 for the hierarchy and 10.4 for designing new exception types. A traceback names the line where the exception was raised, which is frequently not the line where the bad value entered the program; the values in the frame below are the evidence to read.

ExceptionUsual causeWhat to do
TypeErrorWrong type for an operation; too many or too few positional arguments; a keyword that does not exist; an unhashable object used as a key; None where an object was expectedRead the message: it names the operation and both types. The most common cause is None from a function whose result was not checked
ValueErrorThe right type, the wrong value: int("x"), json.loads on malformed input, unpacking the wrong number of elementsValidate at the boundary (16.1) so this becomes a rejected request rather than a 500
KeyErrorA missing mapping key. On a dict subclass it also means __missing__ did not handle itUse get with a default, setdefault, or a defaultdict when absence is expected (11.1)
IndexError, StopIterationAn index past the end, or a generator consumed once more than it yieldsCheck the length, or use next(it, default). StopIteration inside a generator body becomes RuntimeError, by design
AttributeErrorA missing attribute, or a property that raised internally — read the message, which distinguishes the twoCheck the version of the type. A property that raises AttributeError from a __getattr__ fallback is indistinguishable at the call site without the message
NameErrorAn undefined name, a missing global, or a use-before-assignment at module levelModule-level code runs top to bottom once (8.5), so a name defined later does not exist yet
UnboundLocalErrorUnboundLocalError is a NameError: a local assigned in one branch and read in anotherInitialise the variable, or use a dict with a default
ImportError, ModuleNotFoundErrorA missing module, a missing name within a module, or an import-time exception in the module being importedCheck the environment. An import-time failure in a dependency surfaces as ImportError with the real traceback in the chained exception
ZeroDivisionErrorDivision or modulo by zero, including x % 0Guard the denominator, or use fractions.Fraction
OverflowErrorAn integer operation too large for the type, or a float too large to representIntegers do not overflow; a float operation converting to int does
AssertionErrorAn assert failed. Vanishes under -ONever use assert for a runtime check (10.6). -O is also the reason a check that "always passed in production" is not evidence
RecursionErrorThe recursion limit, default 1000, was reached. Usually infinite recursion rather than deep recursionFind the cycle. For genuinely deep recursion, raise sys.setrecursionlimit and raise the thread stack size, or rewrite it iteratively (4.5)
MemoryErrorAn allocation failedA leak, an unbounded container, or a request that exceeded what the process may hold
FileNotFoundError, PermissionError, IsADirectoryErrorFilesystem conditions. A permission error on a file the process created is almost always a read-only mount or a wrong umaskCheck the path, the mode and the user. In a container, the UID is frequently not the one the file was created with
sqlite3.OperationalError: database is lockedAnother connection holds a write lock and busy_timeout is zero, which is the defaultSet busy_timeout, enable WAL, and separate readers from writers (12.2)
sqlite3.IntegrityErrorA UNIQUE, NOT NULL, CHECK or foreign key constraintA real data problem. The message names the constraint on most engines
sqlalchemy.exc.InvalidRequestErrorSession misuse: an operation outside a transaction, a detached instance, an illegal eager loadRead the message; it is specific and it is the ORM telling you the unit of work was violated (12.7)
sqlalchemy.exc.MissingGreenletA lazy load in an async def functionLoad eagerly, or mark the relationship lazy="raise" (12.10)
TimeoutError, concurrent.futures.TimeoutErrorPool acquisition or a future timed outDistinguish the pool timeout from a query timeout; they have different fixes and the message distinguishes them
BrokenProcessPoolA process-pool worker died abruptly: OOM, segfault, os._exitPrevention: forkserver or spawn, and no inherited sockets (14.3)
json.JSONDecodeErrorMalformed JSON, or a body that is empty because an error page was returned insteadCheck the status code and the content type before parsing
UnicodeDecodeErrorBytes that are not valid in the declared encoding, or a guess that was wrongDeclare the encoding explicitly; never guess, and never use the platform default
subprocess.CalledProcessErrorA command with check=True returned a non-zero statusInspect .stdout and .stderr; both are captured and both are usually the real answer
ExceptionGroupSeveral exceptions from concurrent or batch work; split with except*Handle each sub-exception; catching the group as one hides the specific failures (10.5)
TypeError from a TypedDict or a Protocol at runtimeNothing: the annotation was correct and nothing was checked at run timePython does not enforce annotations. The type error comes from the code that did check (9.1)

A.4 Notable changes, 3.8 to 3.14

Behaviour that changes between versions, in order of how often it breaks a codebase. See the What’s New index for the full record, and 2.5 for the conversion functions in the table below. The versions after 3.8 are listed because a project supporting 3.8 needs all of them.

VersionChangeConsequence
3.8Assignment expression := (PEP 572)Unparenthesised use in a comprehension binds in the enclosing scope, which is deliberate and surprising. See 1.3
3.8Positional-only parameters / (PEP 570)Renaming a positional-only parameter is not a breaking change; renaming a keyword one is. Inspect the signature to check
3.8f"{x=}" in f-stringsSelf-documenting form, from 3.8
3.9Dictionary union |, |=, and the d removal operator (PEP 584)d1 | d2 requires 3.9. d1.update(d2) remains the portable spelling
3.9Type-hint generics: list[int] instead of List[int] (PEP 585)Runtime-subscriptable built-ins. typing.List is deprecated, not removed
3.10Structural pattern matching (PEP 634) — match, caseA match statement is a soft keyword, so a variable named match still works. See 1.4
3.10zip(strict=True)Raises when the iterables differ in length, which is the correct default for the cases that matter
3.11Exception groups and except* (PEP 654)Group members require the group to propagate. See 10.5
3.11tomllib, a read-only TOML parserThe first standard-library TOML support. tomli for 3.10 and earlier
3.11Fine-grained error locations and the except notes syntax (PEP 678)add_note() makes a traceback readable. See 10.3
3.11Specialising adaptive interpreterA measurable single-threaded speedup, roughly 10-20% on a typical suite, with no code change. A pure-Python build sees none of it
3.12Type parameter syntax: def f[T](x: T), class C[T], type Alias = ... (PEP 695)The new syntax is a runtime feature; TypeVar remains. See 9.4
3.12typing.override (PEP 698)A checker-enforced marker. No runtime effect
3.12Per-interpreter GIL (PEP 684) and the interpreters subcommandThe groundwork for 3.13's free-threading and 3.14's multiple interpreters in the standard library. See 14.1
3.12SQLite autocommit attribute (PEP 249 amendment)Finally standard, and the previous default was implicit transactions. See 12.2
3.13Experimental free-threaded build (PEP 703)Single-threaded overhead of roughly 40% in the 3.13 initial release, reduced to a few percent by 3.14. See 14.1
3.13Improved interactive interpreter, including a new PyREPL and colourised tracebacksUser-visible only
3.13copy.replace() and copy.replace_with() (PEP 667-adjacent work)Immutable-style update for any type implementing __replace__
3.14t-strings, t"..." (PEP 750)Template strings producing a structured object, for use with the template engine of your choice. See 1.5
3.14Deferred evaluation of annotations (PEP 649, PEP 667)__annotations__ is computed on access rather than at definition, and annotationlib exposes the metadata. Type checkers are unaffected. See 9.1
3.14Multiple interpreters in the standard library (PEP 734, concurrent.interpreters)True sub-interpreters with isolated state, removing the global-state argument for a process pool in some cases. See 14.2
3.14Free-threaded build officially supported (PEP 779)No longer experimental. A few percent of single-threaded overhead against near-linear multi-threaded scaling. See 14.1
3.14Experimental JIT, opt-in (PEP  4)Copy-and-patch tier 2. Off by default. Measure on the real workload
3.14Improved error messages, including suggestions for a misspelled keyword argumentUser-visible. Note that a corrected suggestion in a message is not a guarantee that the suggestion is correct
3.15 (pre-release)Final release expected October 2026Not for deployment. Test for forward compatibility only, and pin the 3.14 line in production until the 3.15 series has a patch release with a known issue list
# Version-conditional code, and the two mistakes made with it.
import sys
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING, assert_never

if sys.version_info >= (3, 10):
    # Match statement: 3.10+
    def classify(v: int | str) -> str:
        match v:
            case int() if v > 0:  return "positive int"
            case int():             return "non-positive int"
            case str() as s if s:   return "non-empty str"
            case str():             return "empty str"
            case _:                 return "other"
else:
    def classify(v):                 # the 3.9 fallback
        if isinstance(v, int) and v > 0: return "positive int"
        if isinstance(v, int):          return "non-positive int"
        if isinstance(v, str) and v:    return "non-empty str"
        if isinstance(v, str):          return "empty str"
        return "other"

# MISTAKE 1: version-checks scattered through the business logic. Better to
# isolate them in one compatibility module and import the right thing.
# from ._compat import parse_args, Path_walk

# MISTAKE 2: a version check that is never exercised, because every
# developer and every CI job runs on the newest interpreter. If a 3.9
# fallback exists, it needs a test that runs on 3.9 in CI, or it is untested
# code that only runs for users who cannot report the problem.
if TYPE_CHECKING:
    # A block a type checker treats as True and runtime skips: an import that
    # only exists for the checker, or that would be expensive at run time.
    from collections.abc import Sequence
    type Modern = Sequence[int]        # 3.12+ alias syntax

# The one rule for version support: state the floor in
# requires-python, test on the floor, and do not use a construct newer than
# the floor without a guard. Everything else follows from those two.

# Version numbers as data, for a compatibility matrix:
REQUIRES = {"acme-tools": ">=3.11", "legacy-adapter": ">=3.8,<3.13"}
for pkg, spec in REQUIRES.items():
    import packaging.specifiers, packaging.version
    s = packaging.specifiers.SpecifierSet(spec)
    print(pkg, spec, "current interpreter ok:", s.contains(
        ".".join(map(str, sys.version_info[:2]))))

A.5 Official documentation and reference links

The Python documentation is unusually good and unusually dense. These are the primary sources; where a third-party page and the official documentation disagree, the documentation is right and the third-party page is out of date. Every claim in this guide that concerns language or standard-library behaviour is traceable to one of these.

ResourceWhat it is forNotes
docs.python.org/3The language reference, the standard library reference and the tutorialThe reference is normative. The tutorial is an introduction, not a specification
The Python Language ReferenceGrammar, execution model, expressions, compound statements, the data modelThe grammar file in the CPython source is the same grammar, machine-readably. The data model chapter (5.7 here) is the single most useful page in the language
The Standard Library ReferencePer-module documentation, alphabeticalSearch by task, not by name; A.2 here is the task-oriented view. Each module page states its own version changes at the end
What’s New in PythonPer-release changes, including removalsThe "Removed" and "Deprecated" sections are the ones to read before raising a floor version. See A.4
peps.python.orgEvery Python Enhancement Proposal, with status and numberRead the status: Accepted, Final, Deferred, Rejected. Several prominent features are Draft. PEP 649, PEP 750 and PEP 779 are the 3.14 ones referenced in this guide
typingThe typing module, protocols, generics, type narrowingSee 9.1 to 9.6. The runtime behaviour and the static contract are documented separately here and that separation is the point
sqlite3The stdlib driver and the SQLite features it exposes12.2. Read the autocommit and transaction-control notes; they changed in 3.12
PEP 249The database API specification, including the 2013 amendment12.1. The specification is short, and the optional extensions section is the part that matters in practice
PEP 621 and PEP 517 and PEP 660Project metadata, build frontends, editable installs13.1 and 13.4
PEP 440Version identifiers and specifiers13.3. The normalisation and ordering rules are the part that surprises
PEP 703 and PEP 779Free-threading, and its graduation to officially supported14.1. PEP 779 defines what "officially supported" excludes, which is the section to read
asyncioEvent loop, tasks, queues, timeouts, the debug mode6.4, 12.10 and 14.4. The Debug mode section documents the slow-callback mechanism referenced in 14.2
venvVirtual environments, pyvenv.cfg, the options13.2
The CPython sourceObjects/, Python/, Modules/_sqlite, Lib/The authoritative answer when the documentation is ambiguous. Grammar/python.gram and Lib/pydoc_data/topics.py are two of the most useful files in it
The Python Development GuideStyle, versioning, typing policy, documentation standardsShip a py.typed marker for a typed package. The typing policy section explains the three marker variants
docs.astral.sh/uvuv: resolver, lock file, build, publish13.3 and 13.5. The resolution documentation explains the fork and the marker evaluation, which is the part that explains a surprising lock
SQLAlchemy 2.0 documentationCore, the ORM, the AsyncIO extension, Alembic12.6 to 12.9. The 2.0 series is a rewrite of the query interface, not a compatibility layer over 1.4
AlembicMigrations, autogenerate, batch mode, multi-database12.9. The cookbook documents the render_as_batch and naming-convention cases that the tutorial omits
pytest documentationFixtures, marks, parametrisation, conftest, plugins15.1 and 15.2. The pytest_generate_tests and request fixture pages are the two that repay reading
HypothesisStrategies, shrinking, state machines, profiles15.4. The documentation explains the health checks, which are the first confusing thing a new user encounters
secrets and hashlibSecure randomness, hashing, PBKDF2, scrypt16.4. Neither is a password hashing library; the documentation says so and it is the most important sentence in the module
Python Cheat Sheet and Short CutsQuick syntax recallCommunity resources. Useful for orientation; the reference is authoritative wherever they disagree

A working order for using this guide as a reference: read the chapter, then the reference page it names, then the PEP if the behaviour is new enough to have one. The PEP is where the rejected alternatives are recorded, and the rejected alternative is usually the reason the design surprises you.

Appendix Exercises

  1. Verify the precedence table by evaluating each row’s examples in a REPL and recording any case where the grouping is ambiguous, then add the ambiguous case to the table.
  2. Exercise every row of the standard library map against the interpreter and replace any entry whose module has been renamed, moved or removed, noting the version in which it happened.
  3. For each row of the exception table, write the smallest snippet that raises it, and confirm the message matches the description in the table.
  4. Build a matrix of the 3.8 to 3.14 changes in this guide against the release notes, and identify any change documented as a deprecation that is now a removal.
  5. Check every URL in the resource table resolves, and record any that have moved, since official documentation URLs are stable but project documentation sites are not always.
  6. Write a script that asserts the requires-python floor of this guide’s own target, 3.11, is consistent with every construct used in it, by scanning for the syntax introduced in each later version.

Python 3.14 — The Complete Language Guide. One-page interactive textbook. Progress is saved in your browser (theme, last chapter). Open in any modern browser. No internet needed. Chapters 1-16 plus a reference appendix cover the language, the standard library, data access, packaging, concurrency, testing and security.
